“Edith!” said Margaret, soft as the rustle of the white muslin draped over the sofa cushions. “Edith!”
As she half-expected, her cousin was fast asleep. Edith lay curled on the crimson damask sofa in the back Harley Street drawing room, her blue ribbons askew and her silk curls spilling over the arm, looking so soft and glowing that Titania herself, if ever caught napping in muslin and blue ribbons on a velvet sofa, might have been mistaken for her instead. Margaret’s breath caught a little at the sight. They had grown up side by side from childhood, and every acquaintance save Margaret had always gushed over Edith’s famous prettiness — but Margaret had never truly registered how much she cherished that charm until the last few days, when the looming loss of her constant companion sharpened every sweet, silly little quirk of Edith’s into something unforgettable.
They had been chattering nonstop only minutes before: of lace wedding dresses, of the ceremony, of Captain Lennox’s stories of their new posting at Corfu, of the near-impossibility of keeping a piano in tune in that salt-heavy Mediterranean air (a trial Edith had insisted was the worst fate that could befall a married woman), of the gowns she would need for their immediate post-wedding trip to Scotland. But their whispers had grown slower, sleepier, until Margaret, after pausing to watch her cousin, realized that Edith had rolled herself into a little ball of fabric and hair and drifted off into a peaceful post-dinner nap, even over the low buzz of guests in the front room.
Margaret had been just about to tell Edith of all the quiet plans she had made for her own return to the Helstone parsonage, the country home where her father was the clergyman, the place where she had spent every holiday of her childhood — even though for the last ten years, Aunt Shaw’s Harley Street house had been her official home, after her parents had decided London would give her a better education. With no one left to listen, she leaned back in her chair and brooded over the coming upheaval, soft and happy even as a faint sting of regret pricked at her at the thought of leaving her gentle aunt and dearest cousin behind for who knew how long. As she daydreamed of taking up her rightful place as the only daughter of the parsonage, snatches of conversation drifted in through the door from the front drawing room.
Aunt Shaw was talking to the half-dozen ladies who had joined them for dinner, their husbands still lingering over port in the dining room. These were their closest London acquaintances — neighbors Mrs. Shaw called friends, people who dropped in unannounced before luncheon to borrow sugar or return a book, who dined with the Shaws far more often than they did anyone else. They had all been invited to this farewell feast in honor of Edith’s upcoming nuptials, a plan Edith had hated at first, for she was expecting Captain Lennox on a late-evening train that very night. But spoiled, easygoing Edith had never been strong-willed enough to fight her mother for long, and she had caved the moment she saw Mrs. Shaw had ordered every rare, expensive seasonal delicacy supposed to soften the grief of a farewell dinner. She had contented herself with slumping in her chair, picking at her food and looking distant and grave, while the rest of the party laughed at the witticisms of Mr. Grey, the gentleman who always took the foot of Mrs. Shaw’s dinner table and always asked Edith to play for the company after the meal. Mr. Grey was in particularly fine form that night, and the gentlemen stayed downstairs far later than usual — a lucky thing, Margaret thought, as she caught the fragments of gossip drifting through the wall.
“I suffered so terribly myself, you know,” Mrs. Shaw was saying, her soft, plaintive voice carrying clear. “Not that I wasn’t happy with dear departed General Shaw, of course — but a disparity in age is such a terrible drawback. I swore Edith would never have to face that. No maternal partiality, I swear: I knew from the moment she turned sixteen she would marry very young. I always said she’d be wed before she turned nineteen. I had a perfect premonition the moment Captain Lennox —”
Her voice dropped to a whisper, and Margaret could easily fill in the rest. Edith’s romance had run far smoother than any in the acquaintance’s circle. Mrs. Shaw had leaned into that “premonition” of hers, actively pushing the wedding along even though many of Edith’s friends had thought a pretty, wealthy young heiress could aim far higher than a penniless army captain stationed half a world away. But Mrs. Shaw insisted her only child would marry for love, and sighed so heavily after the words that anyone not in on the secret might have thought her own marriage to the rich, respected General had been a grand great love match, rather than a practical arrangement she had entered into with nothing but cool respect for his position. She far more enjoyed the romance of Edith’s engagement than Edith herself did, in fact. Edith was certainly fond enough of Captain Lennox, but she would have traded all his stories of Corfu’s sunlit cliffs and impromptu barrack dances for a fine townhouse in Belgravia in a heartbeat. The same wild, gipsy-style makeshift life that made Margaret’s face glow with excitement when she heard of it, Edith pretended to shudder at — half to make her captain coax her out of her silly “dislike” with kisses and promises of grand furniture, half because she truly hated the thought of living without all the small comforts she was used to. If a duke with a grand estate and a title had come courting her, she might have wavered for a moment, but she would always have chosen Lennox in the end — though she would no doubt have pined quietly, later, that he could not be both the man she loved and the owner of a Belgravia house and a title. She was her mother’s daughter, after all: Mrs. Shaw, who had deliberately married the General for nothing warmer than respect for his character and his fortune, spent most of her quiet days softly bemoaning her terrible, unfortunate fate to be tied to a man she had never loved.
“I spared no expense on her trousseau,” were the next words Margaret caught, clear as day. “She’s got all the beautiful Indian shawls the General gave me, the ones I’ve not had the heart to wear since he died.”
“She’s a very lucky girl,” gushed Mrs. Gibson, whose daughter Helen had married only three weeks prior, and was thus far more invested in the conversation than the rest. “Helen has been pining after an Indian shawl for months, but when I saw the prices they ask for those things now I simply had to say no. She’ll be green with envy when she hears Edith has a whole set. Are they Delhi shawls? The ones with the tiny stitched borders?”
Margaret heard Aunt Shaw’s voice rise again, like she was pushing herself up from her couch to peer through the doorway into the dimmer back drawing room. “Edith! Edith!” she called, before sinking back against the cushions like the effort had tired her out completely.
Margaret stepped out of the shadow of the doorframe. “Edith’s asleep, Aunt Shaw. Is there anything I can help you with?”
All the ladies gasped “poor little dear!” at the news, and Mrs. Shaw’s tiny lapdog Tiny began yapping frantically, overexcited by the sudden burst of sympathetic noise. “Hush, you naughty little thing, you’ll wake your mistress,” Mrs. Shaw chided the dog. “I only meant to ask Edith to send Newton down with the shawls to show everyone. Would you run and do that for me, Margaret dear?”
Margaret climbed the three flights of stairs to the old top-floor nursery, where Newton, Edith’s lady’s maid, was hunched over a table mending lace for the wedding gown, grumbling under her breath about how the light up there was so bad she could barely see to stitch. Newton huffed and complained the whole time she was undoing the shawls, which had already been pulled out to show off four or five times that day, while Margaret wandered around the little room, her chest tight with nostalgia. This was the first room she had come to know in the Harley Street house nine years prior, when her father had brought her up from the New Forest, wild and sunburned and used to running barefoot through the woods, to live and learn and play alongside her cousin Edith. She remembered the dim, dark London nursery, ruled over by a strict, formal nurse who fussed endlessly over clean hands and torn frocks, her first tea eaten up there while her father and aunt dined somewhere what had felt like miles below, in the bowels of the earth as she had thought at the time. Back in Helstone, her mother’s dressing room had been her nursery, and she had always eaten every meal with her parents. She remembered the wild, bitter tears she had cried her very first night in that room, hiding under the bedclothes, the nurse telling her to be quiet so she would not wake Edith — how she had cried all the harder, quieter, until her pretty grand new aunt had come upstairs with her father to check on his little sleeping daughter. Nine-year-old Margaret had immediately hushed her sobs, pretending to be fast asleep so she would not make her father sad, so he would not think he had made a mistake leaving her here. Now, at eighteen, she loved the old dismantled nursery more than any other room in the house, and looked around it with a cat-like soft regret, knowing she would never step foot in it again three days from now.
“Ah, Newton,” she said. “I think we’ll all be sorry to leave this dear old place.”
“Not me, miss, not one bit,” Newton huffed. “My eyes aren’t what they used to be, and the light up here’s so bad I can barely mend lace unless I’m right by the window, and there’s a draught there strong enough to give you your death of cold. I’ll be right glad to get to Naples, where there’s proper warmth and proper light.”
“Well, I’m sure you will,” Margaret smiled. “Thank you, Newton, I can carry the shawls down myself. You get back to your mending.”
She trundled back down the stairs with the bundle of silk draped over her arms, breathing in their sharp, spicy Eastern scent. With Edith still fast asleep on the sofa, Aunt Shaw had Margaret stand in her place as a living mannequin, draping the long, gorgeous shawls over her tall, slender figure. Margaret was wearing her black mourning silk for a distant relative of her father’s that day, and the dark fabric made the bright, rich colors of the shawls glow far better than they ever could have on petite Edith, who would have been half-smothered under their length. She stood perfectly still under the chandelier, quiet and passive, as her aunt turned her this way and that, and once or twice she caught a glimpse of herself in the mantel mirror, smiling softly at the absurd, princess-like figure staring back. She stroked the soft silk gently, enjoying the texture and the bright colors like a small child, a quiet pleased smile playing on her lips.
Then the door opened, and the servant announced Mr. Henry Lennox.
Several of the ladies jumped back half-embarrassed, caught red-handed fawning over pretty fabric. Mrs. Shaw held out a hand to him, chattering a mile a minute, peppering him with questions about his brother the bridegroom, his sister Janet who was travelling down from Scotland to be bridesmaid, and every other distant member of the Lennox family she could think to ask after. Margaret stood still for a moment, not wanting to disturb the drapery and ruin the display, meeting Henry’s amused, bright gaze with a knowing smile, certain he would see how absurd the whole scene was. It was clear no one needed her as a shawl-bearer any longer, so she drifted to the edge of the room to keep the other guests entertained while her aunt was distracted.
Almost immediately, Edith wandered in from the back drawing room, winking and blinking in the bright gaslight, shaking her rumpled curls back from her face like Sleeping Beauty just startled awake from her enchanted nap. Even half-asleep, she had apparently sensed a Lennox in the house worth waking up for, and she immediately swarmed Henry with dozens of questions about her soon-to-be sister-in-law Janet, a woman she had never met but claimed to already adore so fiercely Margaret would have felt jealous, if she had not known full well it was all performative sentiment.
As Margaret drifted toward the far wall, out of the main circle of conversation, she saw Henry glance pointedly at the empty chair next to hers, and knew he would make his way over the moment Edith released him from her interrogation. She had not been sure he would make it that night, given his busy lawyer schedule, and she was glad to see him — he liked and disliked almost all the same things she did, and talking to him always felt like talking to someone who understood. Her face lit up into an open, unselfconscious grin when he finally sat down beside her.
“I take it you are all deep in ladies’ wedding business,” he said, laughing. “Very different from my line of work, where I spend all day drawing up dry marriage settlements. Playing dress-up with shawls is far more fun than that, I’m sure.”
“I knew you’d laugh the moment you walked in and saw me swathed in silk,” Margaret laughed back. “But truly, Indian shawls are perfect things, in their own way.”
“I don’t doubt that. Their price tags are perfectly enormous, too, nothing wanting.”
The rest of the gentlemen drifted in one by one from the dining room, and the hum of conversation swelled louder around them.
“This is your last dinner party before the wedding, isn’t it?” he asked. “No more of these before Thursday?”
“No,” Margaret said. “I think after tonight we can all finally rest. I’ve not had a quiet minute for weeks now. I can’t wait for all the hands-on work to be done, all the arrangements finished, and have a moment to just think. Edith will be glad for that, too, I’m sure.”
“I wouldn’t count on that for her, but I can very well imagine you are exhausted,” he said. “Every time I’ve seen you lately, you’ve been swept up in a whirlwind someone else made.”
“I have,” Margaret sighed, thinking of the endless stream of trivial little crises that had occupied every minute for the past month. “I wonder if every wedding has to be preceded by that kind of chaos, or if it could ever be calm and peaceful, right before the day.”
“Cinderella’s godmother ordering the trousseau, the wedding breakfast, writing all the invitations, that sort of thing?” he teased.
“But are all those troubles really necessary?” Margaret asked, looking him straight in the eye. She was tired to the bone of all the silly arrangements Edith had obsessed over for the past six weeks, all for the sake of a pretty effect, and she was craving someone to talk to about what a wedding could actually be, beyond the fuss.
“Oh, they’re necessary enough,” he said, his tone turning serious. “We have forms and ceremonies to get through, not so much for our own sakes, but to stop the rest of the world from gossiping. No one can be truly happy if the neighbors are whispering about you. But how would you arrange your perfect wedding, then?”
“Oh, I’ve not thought about it much,” Margaret said. “Only that I’d want it to be on a fine bright summer morning, and I’d want to walk to church through the shade of the trees, not drive to a stuffy London church on a paved street. No hordes of bridesmaids, no fancy wedding breakfast. I expect I’m just rebelling against all the nonsense I’ve been dealing with the last few weeks.”
“I don’t think you are,” he said. “That idea of quiet, stately simplicity fits your character perfectly.”
Margaret winced a little, not liking the compliment. He had a habit of trying to draw her into conversations about her own personality, with him taking the endlessly flattering side, and she always found it awkward. “It’s only natural I think of Helstone church, and the walk to it, rather than a London church,” she said, cutting him off. “Tell me — I’ve never properly described Helstone to you, have I? You’ll be coming to visit us once this house is shut up and 96 Harley Street is all dusty and dull. Is it a village or a town, first of all?”
“Hardly even a village, just a tiny hamlet,” Margaret said, her face lighting up. “There’s the church, and a handful of little cottages on the green, with roses climbing all over their walls.”
“Roses blooming all year round, even at Christmas, I suppose, to make the story perfect?” he teased.
Margaret frowned, a little annoyed. “I’m not making up a fairy tale, I’m describing the place exactly as it is. You shouldn’t have said that.”
“I’m sorry, I’m penitent,” he laughed. “It just sounded like something out of a storybook, not real life.”
“And it is a storybook sort of place,” Margaret said eagerly. “Every other part of England I’ve seen is so hard and prosaic, after the New Forest. Helstone is like a village out of a Tennyson poem. But I won’t describe it anymore. You’ll just laugh at me if I tell you how much I love it.”
“I promise I won’t. But since you’re determined not to tell me about the village, tell me about the parsonage — the home you’re going back to.”
“I can’t describe my home,” Margaret said softly. “It’s home. I can’t put the charm of it into words.”
“Very well, I yield,” he said. “You’re rather severe tonight, Margaret.”
“Am I?” she said, turning her big soft eyes to him in confusion. “I didn’t mean to be.”
“I made one silly joke, and now you won’t talk to me about Helstone or your house, even though I told you how much I want to hear,” he said, pretending to pout.
“But you can’t talk about a home properly unless you’ve been there,” Margaret said. “You won’t get it until you see it for yourself.”
“Then tell me what you do all day there, then,” he said. “Here in London you read, or do lessons, until midday, take a walk before lunch, drive out with your aunt in the afternoon, and go to some party or another in the evening. What does a typical day in Helstone look like? Will you ride, or drive, or walk everywhere?”
“Walk, definitely,” Margaret

“By the soft green light in the woody glade,
On the banks of moss where thy childhood played
By the household tree, thro’ which thine eye
First looked in love to the summer sky.”

Margaret had slipped back into her plain, comfortable morning dress, riding the railway home in quiet, slow contentment beside her father, who had travelled up to London to stand witness at Edith’s wedding. Her mother had stayed behind at the parsonage under a flurry of half-explained excuses no one but Mr. Hale could fully parse: he knew well his gentle argument that her soft grey satin, worn but perfectly respectable, was more than appropriate for a family wedding had fallen on deaf ears, and that with no spare coin to outfit her from bonnet to slippers in new finery, she would not suffer the embarrassment of appearing at her only sister’s only child’s celebration. Had the wealthy, long comfortable Mrs. Shaw guessed the true cause of her sister’s absence, she would have showered gowns and bonnets and trinkets on her without a second thought; but nearly twenty years had passed since she was the poor, pretty Miss Beresford, and the only grievance she could still summon any passion for was the misery of marriages with wide gaps in age, a topic she could hold forth on for half an hour at a stretch. To her mind, dearest Maria had married perfectly: a man only eight years her senior, with the sweetest temper and that rare striking blue-black hair, and Mr. Hale was the most delightful preacher she had ever heard, a perfect model of a country parish priest. It was no great logical leap for her to conclude, as she mused over her sister’s lot, “Married for love, what more could dearest Maria ever wish for in the world?”
Mrs. Hale, if she had spoken honestly, could have rattled off a list in reply: a silver-grey glacé silk gown, a fine white chip bonnet for the wedding, dozens of small luxuries for the celebration, hundreds more little comforts for the parsonage home.

Margaret only knew her mother had found the trip inconvenient, and she was secretly glad their reunion would unfold at Helstone, rather than amid the chaotic rush of the last few days in Harley Street, where she had played the part of Figaro, called on by every guest and family member at once. Her mind and body ached with the memory of the last forty-eight hours of running errands, soothing overwrought bridesmaids, and rushing last minute wedding preparations. The hurried farewells to all the people she had lived alongside in London for two years hung over her, soft and sad, for times that were gone and never could be brought back — no matter how happy those years had been, or how mundane, they were finished now. It was strange to feel such a heavy heart, on the way to the home and quiet country life she had ached for all those years in London, in that drowsy lull before tired senses blur into sleep. She wrenched her thoughts away from the past, turning instead to the bright, serene future she had dreamed of, and looked up to see her father leaned back asleep in the railway carriage.
That once vivid blue-black hair was now streaked thin with grey, lying soft over his brows, the bones of his face sharp and clear beneath his skin — almost too sharp for conventional beauty, were his features not so fine and gentle, holding a quiet grace all their own. The repose on his face was not the soft calm of a man who leads a placid, untroubled life, but the heavy rest of someone worn thin by long weariness. Margaret was startled by the faint, drawn anxiousness etched into his features, and turned over the known details of their life to find a cause for those deep lines of habitual distress.
“Poor Frederick,” she thought, sighing to herself. “If only he had chosen to be a clergyman instead of running off to the navy, he would not be lost to us all. I wish I knew the full story — Aunt Shaw would never tell me the whole of it, only that he can never come back to England after that terrible affair. Poor dear papa, how sad he looks. I am so glad I am home now, to be here to comfort him and mamma.”
When he woke, she turned to him with a bright, unfatigued smile, and he smiled back, faint as though the small effort cost him something. The lines of quiet anxiety settled back over his face the moment he was still, and he had a little habit of half-opening his mouth as if to speak that left his expression perpetually undecided. He shared the same large, soft slow-moving eyes as his daughter, framed by delicate white eyelids. Margaret took far more after him than her mother; strangers often wondered that two such conventionally handsome parents could have a daughter so far from regular beauty, as some unkind souls put it. Her mouth was wide, no tiny rosebud suited only for murmuring “yes” and “if you please, sir”, but those wide lips curved soft and full, a rich deep red. Her skin was not the bright pink-and-white English fair of society belles, but smooth and delicate as polished ivory. If her usual bearing was too dignified and reserved for a girl of nineteen, right now, talking to her father, her face was bright as morning, dimples showing all through cheeks that lit up with childish glee and unbound hope for the years ahead.

It was late July when they arrived back at Helstone. The forest trees stood heavy with dark, dusky full green, the ferns beneath catching every slanted beam of sunlight, the air sultry and still and thick with growing things. Margaret tramped along the forest paths at her father’s side, crushing the ferns under her light feet with a giddy, almost cruel glee, breathing in that sharp, earthy scent that rose up as they yielded to her step. She wandered out across the wide sun-warmed commons, teeming with wild bees and butterflies and all the small living things revelling in the July sun, and this life — these long, unstructured walks — exceeded every one of her old anticipations. She took a fierce, proud joy in her forest, and the folk who lived there were her folk: she made hearty friends with every cottage family, learned to speak their soft country turns of phrase naturally, nursed their sick babies, read the Bible and old stories slow and clear to their half-blind grandparents, carried pots of jellied broth and spiced fruit to their ill, and resolved to start teaching at the village school very soon. She was forever being lured away from the schoolroom to drop in on some friend, man, woman or child, tucked away in a cottage in the green shade of the oak trees. Her life out of doors was perfect. But the peace indoors had sharp thorns.
With the unguarded honesty of a child, Margaret blamed her own sharp eyes for noticing that all was not as it should be at home. Her mother, always so tender and kind to her, was now often discontented with their lot, convinced the bishop was shockingly neglecting his duties in not granting Mr. Hale a larger, better paid living, and almost reproached her husband for never bringing himself to say he wanted to leave small Helstone to take charge of a bigger, busier parish. He would sigh softly in reply, saying he would be thankful enough if he could tend his small flock in Helstone as he ought, but every day he felt more overwhelmed, the world around him growing more bewildering. Every time his wife pressed him to make an effort to seek church preferment, Margaret saw him shrink further and further into himself, and she would step in to try and reconcile her mother to their quiet life. When Mrs. Hale complained the mist and trees of Helstone were ruining her health, Margaret would tempt her out for walks across the sun-dappled, wind-swept common, sure her mother had simply grown too used to staying indoors, rarely venturing further than the church, the schoolhouse, and the nearest neighbouring cottages. The trick worked for a little while, but when autumn drew near and the weather grew changeable, Mrs. Hale’s conviction that the place was unhealthy deepened, and she began to complain even more often that her husband, far more learned than their old neighbour Mr. Hume, and a far better parish priest than Mr. Houldsworth, had never received the preferment those two men had been granted.
This slow, constant gnawing discontent that marred the peace of the parsonage was something Margaret had never been prepared for. She had known, and even revelled in the knowledge, that she would be leaving behind all the frivolous luxuries of London society that had felt like chains to her, and she had prided herself on being able to do without all of them if need be. But the storm cloud never rises from the corner of the horizon you are watching. In her previous short holidays at Helstone, she had brushed off her mother’s small passing complaints about their position as trivial little annoyances, forgotten amid all the happy memories of home.

In the last weeks of September, the autumn rains and gales set in, and Margaret was forced to stay indoors far more than she liked. Helstone was miles away from any neighbours of their own social standing. “It is unquestionably one of the most out-of-the-way spots in all England,” Mrs. Hale said one afternoon in a plaintive mood. “I cannot help regretting constantly that your papa has no one at all of equal station to speak to here; he is quite thrown away, seeing no one but farmers and labourers week after week. If only we lived on the other side of the parish, we could walk over to the Stansfields’ — the Gormons would be an easy stroll too, at the very least.”
“The Gormons?” Margaret said. “Are those the Gormons who made their fortune in trade in Southampton? I am glad we do not visit them. I do not care for ‘shoppy’ people. I think we are far better off, knowing only cottagers and labourers, and people with no pretensions at all.”
“You must not be so fastidious, Margaret dear,” her mother chided, privately thinking of the handsome young Mr. Gormon she had once met years ago at Mr. Hume’s dinner party.
“My tastes are very broad, I think!” Margaret laughed. “I like all people whose work ties them to the land. I like soldiers, and sailors, and the three learned professions, as they call them. You would not have me admire butchers and bakers and candlestick makers, would you, mamma?”
“The Gormons were none of those, they were very respectable coach builders.”
“Very well. Coach building is still a trade, and I think it is far more useless than butchering or baking. Oh, how tired I grew of driving around in Aunt Shaw’s carriage every single day, how I longed to just walk!”
And walk Margaret did, rain or shine, nearly dancing for joy as she tramped the paths at her father’s side, the soft west wind at her back carrying her across the heath as light and easy as the fallen red and gold leaves wafting along the autumn breeze. But the long evenings were hard to fill pleasantly. Right after tea, her father would slip away to his small, cluttered library, leaving her alone with her mother. Mrs. Hale had never cared much for books, and early in their marriage had discouraged Mr. Hale’s habit of reading aloud to her while she sewed. For a little while they had played backgammon together, but as Mr. Hale grew more devoted to his school and his parishioners, every interruption from his duties had come to feel like a hardship to his wife, not a natural part of his work, something to be resented and struggled against. So he had withdrawn to his library while their children were still small, to spend his evenings reading the metaphysical and speculative books he loved best.
The old bookshelves in the drawing room held no new volumes for Margaret, only the well-bound, little-read English classics that had been cleared out of her father’s library long ago: Thomson’s Seasons, Hayley’s life of Cowper, Middleton’s Life of Cicero, the lightest, newest, most amusing titles on the shelves. Margaret would tell her mother every little detail of her life in London, and Mrs. Hale listened with great interest, amused and curious at some stories, at others apt to compare her own sister’s comfortable, easy life in Harley Street to their narrow, constrained circumstances at the parsonage. On those evenings, Margaret would often fall quiet mid-sentence, listening to the monotonous drip-drip-drip of rain on the lead panes of the small bow window. More than once she found herself mechanically counting the drops, summoning the courage to ask the question she had carried in her heart for months: where was Frederick now? What was he doing? How long had it been since they last heard from him? But the knowledge that her mother’s delicate health, and all her discontent with Helstone, stretched all the way back to the time of the naval mutiny her brother had been mixed up in, a story Margaret had never heard told in full, and one that everyone seemed determined to bury in sad oblivion, made her hold her tongue. She thought to ask her father when her mother was not around, then decided she could speak more easily of it to her mother later — and likely there was no new information to learn anyway. Only one letter from her father, sent before she left London, had mentioned that they had heard from Frederick: he was in Rio de Janeiro, in good health, and sent his love to her. It was dry, bare news, none of the warm, full details she ached for.
“Poor Frederick” was the only name they used for him on the rare occasions his name came up. His bedroom was kept exactly as he had left it, dusted and set to rights every week by Dixon, Mrs. Hale’s lady’s maid, who had been hired decades ago by Lady Beresford to care for the two pretty Miss Beresfords, the belles of Rutlandshire. Dixon still considered Mr. Hale the great blight that had fallen on her young lady’s prospects, convinced that if Maria had not rushed into a marriage with a poor country parson, no one could say how grand and important she might have become. But she was far too loyal to abandon her through the quiet hardships of married life, and considered herself her mistress’s protecting fairy, whose job it was to fend off the malignant giant of Mr. Hale’s inconvenient, low income clergyman ways. Master Frederick had been her favourite and her pride, and when she went into his room every week to tidy it, her staid, dignified expression softened, as if she half expected him to walk through the door that very evening.

Margaret was convinced some new, troubling news about Frederick, hidden from her mother, was the source of her father’s quiet unease. Mrs. Hale noticed no change in his behaviour at all. His spirits were gentle and tender, easily cast down for days after sitting with a dying parishioner, or hearing of some local tragedy. But now Margaret saw a constant absence of mind, as if his thoughts were preoccupied by some heavy weight no amount of comforting mourners, or teaching schoolchildren to build a better future, could lift. Mr. Hale no longer wandered the parish visiting his people as often as he once had, and spent far more time shut up in his study, lingering by the front gate on fine mornings to wait for the postman, whose arrival at the parsonage was marked by a sharp rap on the back kitchen shutter. Once, the postman’s knock often went unanswered for minutes, while the household went about their work unaware of the time. Now, Mr. Hale would loiter in the garden by the sweetbriar hedge if the weather was fine, or stand staring out his study window on grey days, until the postman had walked all the way down the lane, giving the parson a quiet, half-respectful, half-confidential little shake of his head before he turned back inside to start his day’s work, his face heavy with unspoken worry.
Margaret was still young, though, and all vague unproven worries blew away like thistledown the moment the first bright, golden days of October arrived. Two whole weeks of cloudless crisp autumn sun unfolded, all the rain gone, opening up scores of deep, shadowy forest glades Margaret had only caught glimpses of through the summer green. She had learned to draw alongside Edith in London, and had regretted wasting the fine summer days wandering idly through the woods, so now she was determined to sketch every glen and great oak before winter set in. She was just fastening the lid of her paint box and sketch board one bright morning, when the housemaid Sarah threw wide the drawing room door, and announced, “Mr. Henry Lennox.”

*"Learn to win a lady’s faith
Nobly, as the thing is high;
Bravely, as for life and death—
With a loyal gravity.
Lead her from the festive boards,
Point her to the starry skies,
Guard her, by your truthful words,
Pure from courtship’s flatteries."

“Mr. Henry Lennox.”

Margaret Hale had been turning him over in her thoughts only a minute prior, remembering his question before he’d left London for her cousin Edith’s wedding, about how she would fill her days once they moved to the quiet Hampshire parsonage of Helstone. It was that old French proverb come true: parler du soleil et l’on en voit les rayons — speak of the sun, and you see its light. A bright flush spread over Margaret’s face as she set down her embroidery board and hurried forward to shake his hand. “Go tell Mamma he’s here, Sarah,” she called over her shoulder to the maid. “Mamma and I have a hundred questions to ask you about Edith. I can’t thank you enough for making the journey out to see us.”

“Did I not promise I would?” he replied, his tone softer than his usual crisp barrister’s register. “I’d heard you were settled so deep in the New Forest I never dreamed Hampshire would be so easy a trip from the Highlands.”

“Oh!” he went on, lighter now, “that foolish young couple you’re related to were up to every reckless prank imaginable — scaling unmarked mountains, sailing rickety little dinghies across lochs that should have swallowed them whole. I soon realised they needed a Mentor at their heels to keep them alive, and my uncle was far too frazzled chasing after them to manage it. By the time I saw how little sense the two of them had between them, I knew it was my duty to see them safely embarked on their ship for the Continent from Plymouth.”

“Plymouth?” Margaret’s eyes went wide. “Edith never wrote a word of that. She’s been dashing off her letters in such a hurry lately, half the details slip right past. Did they really sail on Tuesday, like she wrote?”

“Bright and early Tuesday morning, and off my hands at last,” he laughed, pulling a crumpled, tiny folded note from his waistcoat pocket. “Edith stuffed this in my coat before I left, full of scribbled messages just for you.”

Margaret thanked him breathlessly, half desperate to read the note in private, and made the excuse that Sarah must have forgotten to pass the word to her mother, so she would go fetch her herself. The moment she slipped out of the room, Lennox glanced slowly around the little drawing room. The morning sun streamed full tilt through the open bow window, where climbing roses and scarlet honeysuckle curved around the glass to peep inside. The small lawn outside blazed with verbenas and crimson geraniums, their brightness making the room’s furnishings look faded by contrast: the carpet was worn thin at the edges, the chintz curtains had been washed so many times their pattern had softened to a blur, the whole space far smaller and shabbier than he had expected for a girl as regal, as poised as Margaret Hale. He picked up a book from the side table: Dante’s Paradise, bound in fine old white vellum and gold, a dog-eared Italian dictionary tucked next to it, Margaret’s neat slanted handwriting filling a scrap of paper with lists of translated vocabulary. He sighed as he set it down. The living here was every bit as small as she’d described it, he thought, and it felt strange to see the daughter of a Beresford, from such a good old family, making her home in this quiet, modest parsonage.

Meanwhile Margaret had found her mother in the pantry, fretting through one of her bad days where every small task felt like an impossible burden. She was secretly flattered Henry Lennox had made the effort to call, but all she could see was the inconvenience of it. “This is such bad luck,” she moaned. “We’re dining early today, and all we have is cold roast beef — the servants are behind on their ironing, and I didn’t plan for guests. Of course we have to invite him to stay, though, he’s practically family to Edith. And your father has been so low-spirited all morning, I found him in his study just now with his face buried in his hands. I told him Helstone air was far too damp and relaxing to agree with any of us, and he snapped his head up and begged me never to say a word against the place again — he says it’s the only spot on earth he loves.”

Margaret felt a thin cold shadow fall over the bright mood she’d had since Lennox arrived. She let her mother ramble on for a little while, hoping it would ease her distress, before gently steering her back to the drawing room. “Papa liked Mr. Lennox enormously at the wedding breakfast,” she said. “His coming will cheer him up, I promise you. And cold beef will do perfectly for a two o’clock dinner — he’s a London barrister, he’ll think of it more as a late luncheon anyway. We’ll go out sketching for a few hours, he and I, and we’ll be out of your hair while you finish getting the house in order.”

Mrs. Hale smoothed the creases out of her black silk apron and adjusted her cap, and emerged a perfectly gracious, gentlewomanly hostess to greet Lennox with all the warmth due to a near relation. He accepted the invitation to stay the day with such obvious gladness that Mrs. Hale immediately regretted not having a fancier spread to offer him. He was delighted by Margaret’s suggestion that they go out sketching, said he wouldn’t dream of disturbing Mr. Hale before dinner, and the two of them set off in the highest of spirits with their tins of watercolour paints and sketchbooks slung over their arms.

“Stop here a moment,” Margaret said, pulling him to a halt before a cluster of tumbledown thatched cottages half hidden in the oak wood. “I spent all that rainy fortnight after we moved here staring at these, and feeling guilty I’d never stopped to draw them.”

“Better draw them now than wait for them to tumble down entirely, that’s for certain,” Lennox grinned. “They’re far too picturesque to waste. Where can we sit?”

“Right there!” She pointed to a broad, gnarled tree stump the woodcutters had left perfectly positioned to catch the golden morning light. “I’ll spread my plaid over it, and it’s a proper forest throne.”

“With your feet in that puddle for a footstool? No, come stand this way, I’ll shift a few stones for you. Who lives in these cottages?”

“Squatters built them fifty or sixty years ago. One’s empty, the foresters are going to tear it down as soon as the last old man in the other one passes on. Look — there he is! Old Isaac. I’ll go speak to him, he’s so deaf he won’t hear a word of any secrets we might whisper.”

The old man stood bareheaded in the sun, leaning on his walking stick at his cottage gate, his stiff rheumatic face softening into a slow smile when Margaret approached. Lennox worked quickly, sketching the two of them into the background of his landscape before she noticed, and Margaret laughed and blushed bright pink when they packed up their supplies later and compared drawings. “That’s treacherous!” she protested. “I had no idea you were making the two of us the subject, when you told me to go ask him the history of the cottages.”

“I couldn’t resist,” he said, his voice softer than she’d ever heard it. “You’ve no idea how strong the temptation was. I hardly dare tell you how much I’m going to love keeping this sketch.” She wandered off to the nearby brook to wash the paint out of her palette, flushing hot, and Lennox was glad she didn’t seem to have caught the full weight of what he’d said — it was rare for a man as deliberate, as planned in all his actions, to let such an unguarded line slip out.

When they got back to the parsonage, all the earlier shadows had cleared: a neighbour had dropped off a brace of fresh-caught carp, and Mrs. Hale’s good mood was fully restored. Mr. Hale was waiting for them by the wicket gate leading into the garden, looking every inch the gentle scholar even in his threadbare old coat and well-worn hat. Margaret was always tenderly proud of how favorably her father impressed strangers, but her sharp eye caught the faint traces of some deep unspoken disturbance on his face, pushed aside but not erased.

They all went inside to dine, the conversation flowing easily as they traded stories of Edith’s adventures in Italy, of General Shaw’s widow Mrs. Shaw’s plans to travel further south, and Lennox soon forgot the small flicker of disappointment he’d felt when he realised how modest the Hales’ home was — in Margaret’s company, the plain, unpretending warmth of the parsonage felt like far more luxury than any drawing room in Harley Street.

Halfway through the meal, Mr. Hale set down his wine glass and said, “Margaret, my dear child, run out to the south wall and pick us those few brown beurré pears I’ve been tending to, the ones the sun’s been ripening. They taste better than any foreign preserve or fruit you could buy in a London shop.”

Lennox jumped to his feet before Margaret could move. “Let us all go out to the garden and eat them there,” he said. “There’s nothing more delicious than biting into a pear warm from the sun, crisp and juicy, with the scent of the leaves still on it.” Mrs. Hale would have preferred to finish dinner properly, to bring out the finger glasses she’d stored in the pantry for guests, but when both Mr. Hale and their guest moved toward the door, she had no choice but to follow.

Margaret laid the picked pears out on a large fresh beetroot leaf that made their golden brown skins glow, and she and Lennox strolled slowly along the terrace walk under the south wall, where bees hummed around the hives tucked against the hedge. “What a perfect, serene life you lead here,” Lennox said, pausing to look up at the deep unbroken blue of the sky. “I used to mock the poets who longed for a cottage in the country, thought they were all being ridiculous — I see now I was nothing but a London cockney, blind to all this beauty. I feel like twenty years of slaving away at law in cramped chambers would be well rewarded for just one year of a life as peaceful as this.”

“Our skies aren’t always this bright, you know,” Margaret laughed. “We have weeks of rain where the leaves turn sodden and the fog hangs so thick you can’t see the wood ten paces away. Though I do think Helstone is the loveliest place on earth. Don’t you remember the night in Harley Street you laughed when I called it a ‘village out of a fairy tale’?”

“Scorned it?” he said, warm and earnest. “I’ll never speak of it like that again.” They rounded the far end of the terrace walk, and he hesitated, stopping short. Margaret looked up at him, suddenly, unaccountably uneasy — she could see he was about to say something she had no answer for. Her pride kicked in a moment later, steadying her: she was a grown woman, of course she could face whatever he had to say with dignity.

“Margaret,” he said, and caught her hand before she could step away, holding it firm in his. “I’ve waited three months to tell you this. I wish you didn’t love Helstone quite so much, didn’t seem so perfectly happy here, that you missed London a little — missed your old friends — enough to listen more kindly to what I have to say. I have very little to offer you, nothing but prospects for the future, but I love you, Margaret, almost in spite of myself.” He saw her lips tremble, and let go of her hand for half a second. “Have I startled you too much? Say something.”

She mastered her voice with an effort, her gaze steady and open, full of quiet regret. “You did startle me. I’ve only ever thought of you as a friend, nothing more. I don’t want you to be upset, but I can’t answer you the way you want. I can’t think of you as a lover.”

Lennox stared at her, struck dumb for a moment. He had expected hesitation, not a flat, gentle, firm rejection. “There’s no rush,” he said, at last. “Will you not even give me hope, that one day you might change your mind?”

Margaret was quiet for a long moment, searching her own heart, before she shook her head. “I never will. Please, let us both forget this conversation ever happened.”

He laughed, a sharp, bitter little laugh that fell back on his usual worldly barrister’s tone. “Forget it? That’s all very well in theory, but I daresay it will be hard for me, at least. A struggling barrister, with barely enough money to keep chambers in the Temple, thinking he could marry — what a fool I’ve been.”

Margaret felt a flicker of disdain stir in her chest, tangled up with her sorrow at hurting him. That sharp, cynical edge was exactly the part of him that had sometimes made her pull back even back in London. The two of them rounded the hedge and stumbled straight into Mr. Hale, still sitting on the garden bench, peeling his pear in one long unbroken silver strip of rind. They made forced small talk for the next fifteen minutes, Margaret grave and quiet, Lennox forcing himself to chatter lightly about London society, full of quiet sarcasm that made Mr. Hale frown in puzzlement, wondering what had come over the pleasant young man from the wedding breakfast.

Lennox was as desperate to leave as Margaret was to be alone with her thoughts. He said he must go at once if he meant to catch the five o’clock train back to London, and they all filed back into the house to say goodbye to Mrs. Hale. At the very last moment, as he picked up his hat from the hall table, he leaned in close to Margaret, his voice dropping so no one else could hear.

“Don’t despise me, Margaret. Underneath all this sharp, worldly talk, I have a heart. I think I love you more now than I did before, even for the flash of that quiet disdain I saw in your face a few minutes ago. Goodbye. Margaret.”

He was gone before she could think of a single word to say in reply.

“Cast me upon some naked shore,
Where I may tracke
Only the print of some sad wracke,
If thou be there though the seas roare,
I shall no gentler calm implore.”

He was gone. The vicarage doors were latched for the evening, the soft glow of the southern sunset fading fast from Helstone’s hedgerows. No more of the deep, bright blue skies Margaret loved to paint, no more streaks of crimson and amber spilling over the ancient beech woods. She climbed the stairs to change for early tea, to find Dixon in a sharp ill humour, the visitor’s unexpected call having thrown off her schedule of chores for the day. The maid made her irritation plain by brushing Margaret’s hair far harder than necessary, all under the pretense of rushing to attend to Mrs. Hale.

Even so, Margaret was left waiting alone in the drawing room for long stretches after she finished dressing. She sat by the crackling hearth, the candles on the table behind her left unlit, turning the events of the day over in her mind: the happy walk through the woods that morning, the quiet pleasure of sketching the gnarled old oak at the edge of the common, the warm, easy dinner with their guest, then the sharp, miserable tension of their talk in the garden after.

How very differently men and women were made, she thought. Here she was unmoored, wretched, because every instinct of hers had made anything but a refusal impossible, and her heart ached at the thought of what she had turned down. Meanwhile, the man who had just had the most sacred, life-altering proposal of his life rejected could speak moments later of court briefs, professional success, fine houses and clever society, as if those superficial prizes were the whole of his desire. Oh, how she could have loved him, if only he had been different—if that light, careless exterior had not run so deep. Then she softened, wondering if his levity was only a mask, to hide the sharp, bitter disappointment that would have settled like a stone in her own chest had she been the one to love and be turned away.

Her mother swept into the room before her tangled thoughts could settle to any order, and Margaret had to shake off every last trace of that garden conversation to listen sympathetically to Mrs. Hale’s stories of Dixon burning the ironing blanket for the third time that month, and of Susan Lightfoot being spotted wearing artificial flowers in her bonnet, proof positive that the girl was vain and giddy beyond redemption. Mr. Hale sipped his tea in distant silence, leaving Margaret to do all the conversing. She wondered how both her parents could be so forgetful, so wrapped up in their own small concerns, that they had not even mentioned their visitor’s name. It did not occur to her that neither of them knew he had made her an offer at all.

When the tea things were cleared, Mr. Hale rose to his feet, leaned his elbow on the mantelpiece, and rested his head on his hand, sighing deeply every few moments as he turned over some heavy thought. Mrs. Hale left the parlour to consult Dixon over winter clothing for the parish poor, and Margaret set to sorting out the worsted her mother used for knitting, shrinking from the thought of the long empty evening ahead, wishing bedtime would come fast so she could re-trace every moment of the day in quiet.

“Margaret!” Mr. Hale said at last, in a sudden, desperate tone that made her start. “Is that tapestry mending you hold urgent right now? Can you set it aside and come to my study? I need to speak to you about something that is very serious for all of us.”

For one wild moment Margaret thought the news concerned Mr. Lennox. She felt guilty and hot-cheeked, abruptly aware that she was old enough now for people to think of her as a woman to be married, and feared her father might be angry she had turned down a perfectly good suitor on her own authority. But it was soon clear the matter had nothing to do with a recent proposal. He made her sit in a chair close beside his, stirred the fire to a bright blaze, snuffed the candles, and sighed two or three times before he forced the words out, sharp and sudden:
“Margaret! I am leaving Helstone.”

“Leaving Helstone, papa? Why ever would we leave here?”

Mr. Hale did not answer for a minute or two. He fumbled nervously with the loose papers scattered across his desk, opening his mouth to speak half a dozen times only to close it again, too cowardly to say what was on his mind. Margaret could not bear the suspense, which she saw was distressing him far more than it distressed her. “Tell me, dear papa! Please tell me what it is.”

He looked up at her all at once, and spoke with a slow, forced calm. “Because I can no longer serve as a minister in the Church of England.”

Margaret had been daydreaming that her mother’s long-held wish for a grander church preferment had finally come true, that they would move to one of those stately, quiet cathedral closes she had visited once or twice—places noble and imposing, yes, but leaving beloved Helstone forever would have been a long, lingering pain. This new shock was far worse. She went cold all over. Could it have something to do with Frederick? Her brother was an outlaw, run off to serve in the Spanish navy after a mutiny aboard his ship. Could her father have hidden him away, or aided him in some way?
“Oh—what is it? Speak, papa, tell me everything! Surely if the bishop knew all the unfair, unjust things that happened to Frederick—”

“It has nothing at all to do with Frederick. The bishop has no business with that matter. This is all my own doing, Margaret. I will answer every question you have this once, but after tonight, we will never speak of it again. I can bear the consequences of my painful, long-held doubts, but to talk of the years of suffering they have caused me is a burden I cannot carry twice.”

“Doubts, papa? Doubts about religion?” Margaret asked, more horrified than she had ever been in her life.

“Not doubts about religion—never that. Nothing to shake my faith in God, only my faith that I can keep my vows to the church honestly.” He paused, and Margaret sighed, feeling as if she stood on the edge of some new, unnameable horror. He spoke fast now, as if rushing through a painful task he had put off for months. “You could not possibly understand all of it—the years I have spent fretting over whether I had any right to hold this living, the ways I tried to smother my smouldering doubts by leaning on the authority of the church. Oh, Margaret, how I love the holy Church I am soon to be shut out of!”

He could not speak for a moment or two, and Margaret did not know what to say. The news felt as impossibly, terrifyingly alien as if he had told her he intended to convert to Islam.
“I was reading today of the two thousand clergymen who were ejected from their parishes in 1662,” he went on, with a faint, wobbly smile, “trying to steal some of their bravery for myself. But it is no use. I still feel every sharp stab of this grief.”

“But papa—have you truly thought this all the way through? It feels so terrible, so shocking,” Margaret burst out, tears spilling over. The steady foundation of her home, the image of her gentle, beloved father she had carried her whole life, was reeling and rocking right under her.

Her distress steeled Mr. Hale, and he swallowed down the dry, choking sobs that had been rising in his throat. He crossed to his bookshelf and pulled down a well-worn volume he had been re-reading for months, the book he said had given him the courage to take this step. “Listen, dear Margaret,” he said, slipping an arm around her waist. She gripped his hand tight in hers, but could not lift her tear-stained face, nor could she properly attend to the words he read, for the chaos of her own thoughts. “This is the soliloquy of a country parish clergyman just like me, written by Mr. Oldfield, who was minister of Carsington in Derbyshire more than a hundred and sixty years ago. His trials are long over. He fought the good fight.” He murmured those last two sentences to himself, then read aloud:
“When thou canst no longer continue in thy work without dishonour to God, discredit to religion, foregoing thy integrity, wounding conscience, spoiling thy peace, and hazarding the loss of thy salvation; in a word, when the conditions upon which thou must continue (if thou wilt continue) in thy employments are sinful, and unwarranted by the word of God, thou mayest, yea, thou must believe that God will turn thy very silence, suspension, deprivation, and laying aside, to His glory, and the advancement of the Gospel’s interest. When God will not use thee in one kind, yet He will in another. A soul that desires to serve and honour Him shall never want opportunity to do it; nor must thou so limit the Holy One of Israel, as to think He hath but one way in which He can glorify Himself by thee. He can do it by thy silence as well as by thy preaching; the laying aside as well as thy continuance in thy work. It is not pretence of doing God the greatest service, or performing the weightiest duty, that will excuse the least sin, though that sin capacitated or gave us the opportunity for doing that duty. Thou wilt have little thanks, O my soul! if, when thou art charged with corrupting God’s worship, falsifying thy vows, thou pretendest a necessity for it in order to a continuance in the ministry.”

As he read, and glanced over the pages he did not speak aloud, his own resolve strengthened, and he thought he could be brave and firm in doing what his conscience bid him. But when he finished, he heard Margaret’s low, convulsive sobs, and his courage dissolved in the sharp, keen sense of her suffering.
“Margaret, dear,” he said, pulling her closer, “think of the early martyrs. Think of the thousands who have suffered far worse for their conscience.”

“But father,” she said, lifting her flushed, wet face at last, “the martyrs suffered for the truth they believed. You—oh, dearest papa!”
“I suffer for conscience’ sake, my child,” he said, with a quiet dignity that only shook from the sharp, tender sensitivity of his character. “I must do what my heart tells me is right. I have borne this burden of self-reproach for years, long enough to have roused any mind less torpid and cowardly than mine.” He shook his head sadly. “Your poor mother’s fondest wish, which came true in the mocking, bitter way such over-great wishes often do—Sodom’s apples, crumbling to ash in your mouth—brought this crisis to a head not a month back. The bishop offered me another, better living then. If I had accepted it, I would have had to make a fresh declaration of conformity to the Liturgy at my institution. Margaret, I tried to do it. I tried to be content with turning down the preferment quietly, staying on here, strangling my conscience the same way I had strained and twisted it for years before. God forgive me for that.”

He rose and paced the room, murmuring low words of self-reproach Margaret was grateful not to overhear most of. At last he stopped. “Margaret, I come back to that old, sad truth. We must leave Helstone.”

“Yes. I see that. But when?”
“I already wrote to the bishop,” he said, sinking back into his usual depressed, distant tone now that he was speaking of practical arrangements. “I told him I intended to resign this vicarage. He has been endlessly kind, argued with me, tried to persuade me out of it, but nothing he said could shift me. It is all the same arguments I have already run through my own head, to no avail. I will take my deed of resignation to him myself, to bid him farewell. That will be a hard trial. But far worse, far far worse, will be parting from my people here. A curate named Mr. Brown has already been appointed to read prayers. He will come stay with us tomorrow. Next Sunday I will preach my farewell sermon.”

Margaret thought it would be far better to leave all at once, no lingering to drag out every last sting of pain, no chance to grow attached to the familiar walls and lanes all over again. She drew a deep, heavy breath. “What does mamma say?”

To her surprise, her father started pacing the room again, before he stopped to answer, looking wrung out. “Margaret, I am a poor coward after all. I cannot bear to give pain. I know your mother’s married life has never been half all she hoped for, all she had a right to expect. This will be such a terrible blow to her that I never had the heart, the strength, to tell her. But someone must tell her now,” he said, looking at his daughter with a wistful, helpless gaze.

Margaret was almost overpowered by the realization that her mother had no clue at all, even though every arrangement was nearly finalised. “Yes, of course she must be told. She will be shocked, that is certain.” A new thought struck her. “Where are we going to live?”

“Milton-Northern,” he answered, dull and indifferent, for he had seen that for all Margaret’s love was trying to soothe him, her own grief was still raw.

“Milton-Northern? That big smoky manufacturing town up in Darkshire?”
“Yes.”
“Why ever there, papa?”
“Because there I can earn bread for my family. Because no one there knows Helstone, no one here knows Milton. No one will ever remind me of the life I have lost.”

“Bread for our family? I thought you and mamma had independent means apart from the living,” Margaret stopped herself, seeing the dark, heavy gloom settle over his brow. But he read her concern in her face like a mirror, and forced himself to soften. “I will tell you everything, Margaret. Only help me break this to your mother. I will do anything but that part. The thought of her distress turns me sick with dread. I am going out all day tomorrow to say goodbye to Farmer Dobson and all the poor families on Bracy Common. Could you break the news to her tomorrow? Would you mind it very much?”

Margaret did mind it. She shrank from the task more than anything she had ever been asked to do in her life. She could not speak at first.
“You hate the thought, don’t you, Margaret?” her father said.

She conquered herself in that moment, and looked up at him with a bright, steady, brave face. “It is a painful thing, but it has to be done. I will do it as well as I can. You have so many far more painful things to get through yourself.”

Mr. Hale shook his head despondently, and squeezed her hand in gratitude. Margaret nearly burst into tears all over again, so she turned to practical plans to distract herself. “Tell me, what are our finances? You and mamma have about a hundred and twenty pounds a year of your own, don’t you, apart from the living? Aunt Shaw gave that to you, I know.”

“Yes. Seventy of that has always gone to Frederick, ever since he was exiled abroad. I do not know if he needs all of it,” he added, hesitating. “He must get some pay serving in the Spanish army.”

“Frederick will not go short,” Margaret said firmly. “He is in a foreign country, treated so unjustly by his own. That leaves a hundred a year. Could the three of us not live on that in some quiet, cheap little village in the south? I am sure we could manage.”

“No,” Mr. Hale said. “That would never work. I need something to do, to keep busy, so I do not sink into morbid, brooding thoughts. Besides, if I stayed in the south, every lane, every church I passed would remind me of Helstone. I could not bear it. And a hundred a year would not stretch far enough to give your mother all the small comforts she has been used to, and deserves. We have to go to Milton. It is settled. I can make far better decisions on my own, without the people I love arguing with me. I cannot stand objections. They make me too undecided.”

Margaret held her tongue. Compared to the terrible, life-altering change they were facing, it hardly mattered where they went.
“A few months ago, when my doubts grew too heavy to bear alone, I wrote to Mr. Bell,” he went on. “You remember Mr. Bell, Margaret? Frederick’s godfather, my old tutor at Oxford?”

“I never met him, but I know who he is. He is a Fellow at Plymouth College, right?”
“Yes. He was born in Milton-Northern, I believe, and he owns property there that has doubled and tripled in value now the town has grown into such a huge manufacturing hub. I knew he would sympathise, even if he has lived a soft, easy life at college and never had to face such a trial himself. It is all thanks to him we are going to Milton.”

“How so?”
“He has mills, houses, tenants there. Even though he hates the bustling, noisy place, he has to keep an eye on his investments. He tells me there is a very good opening for a private tutor there. Many of the mill owners are self-made men, painfully aware of their own gaps in education. Some are determined to learn classics and literature even as grown men, others want their children to get the education they never had. He has recommended me to a Mr. Thornton, one of his tenants, a very intelligent man, from what I have seen of his letters. In Milton, Margaret, we will find people and scenes so different from Helstone, we will never be reminded of what we left behind.”

Margaret recognised that secret motive all too well, from her own aching heart. The north was a foreign, bleak, discordant place, all she had ever heard of its smoky mills and rough population filled her with dread—but it was not Helstone. That was its only, and its greatest, recommendation.
“When do we leave?” she asked, after a short, heavy silence.

“I do not know exactly. I wanted to talk it over with you first. Your mother still knows nothing, but I think in a fortnight. Once my resignation is formally accepted, I will have no right to stay on in the vicarage.”

Margaret was stunned. “In a fortnight!”
“Not exactly to the day. Nothing is set in stone

“I ask Thee for a thoughtful love, Through constant watching wise, To meet the glad with joyful smiles, And to wipe the weeping eyes; And a heart at leisure from itself To soothe and sympathise.” — ANON.

Margaret sat and listened gently as her mother chattered through all her little plans to bring small, much-needed comforts to the poorest of Helstone’s parishioners. She could not do otherwise than pay attention, though each new project landed like a quiet stab to her heart. By the time the first frost settled sharp on the ground, they would be hundreds of miles away from the village. Old Simon’s rheumatism would gnaw at his joints, his eyesight would dim even further, and there would be no one to pop in and read him his favourite psalms, leave a steaming porringer of broth on his windowsill, or wrap his aching limbs in that soft red flannel he had long complained he could not afford. Even if some stranger stepped in to tend to him, he would watch in vain through the forest edge for Margaret’s familiar step. Mary Domville’s little crippled boy would drag himself to the cottage door day after day, waiting for her to bring the carved wooden animals she whittled for him, and she would never come. None of these dear, simple friends would ever understand why she had vanished — and there were dozens more besides.
“Your father has always spent every penny of his living’s income back on this parish,” Mrs. Hale was saying. “I may be dipping a little into the next quarter’s dues to cover these little extras, but the winter is shaping up to be a harsh one, and our poor must not go without.”
“Oh, mamma, let us give them all we possibly can,” Margaret burst out eagerly, not stopping to weigh the practical prudence of the cost, only clinging tight to the thought that every kindness they offered now was the last they would ever be able to give. “We may not be here much longer, after all.”
“Darling, are you unwell?” Mrs. Hale asked, her face softening with anxious concern as she misinterpreted Margaret’s quiet hint at their coming upheaval. “You look so pale and worn. It’s that soft, damp, unhealthy Helstone air, I’m sure of it.”
“No, no, mamma, it is not that,” Margaret said, smiling faintly as she kissed her mother’s cheek. “This air is delicious. It smells of wild pine and hawthorn, sweeter by far than all the soot and smoke of Harley Street. I am only tired. It must be close to bedtime, surely?”
“Half-past nine, nearly. Off you go to bed, dear, ask Dixon to bring you up a basin of warm gruel. I’ll come tuck you in the second you’re settled. I do fear you’ve caught a chill, or breathed in the foul fumes rising off those stagnant ponds at the edge of the common —”
Margaret slipped up the stairs, and meekly ate the gruel Dixon brought her to soothe her mother’s worries. She lay languid under the covers until she heard her mother’s bedroom door click shut and lock, then she sprang out of bed, threw on her wool dressing gown, and paced the length of her small room until a creak from the loose floorboard by the wardrobe reminded her she must not make a sound. She curled herself up on the deep, recessed window seat, and pressed her forehead to the cold glass. That same morning, she had stood in this very spot and laughed to see the sun glint bright off the square stone church tower, promising a day of clear, golden sunlight. Now, barely sixteen hours later, her heart was too heavy for tears, a cold, dull ache leaching all the youth and buoyancy out of her chest as if it would never return. Henry Lennox’s visit, his unexpected proposal, felt like a half-remembered dream, something entirely separate from her real life. The harsh, unyielding truth was that her father had let temptation fester into deep, unshakable doubt until he could no longer bear to serve as a priest in the Church of England — a schismatic, an outcast — and every single terrible change coming for them all bloomed out from that one blighting fact.

She stared out at the dark grey outline of the church tower, sharp and straight against the endless, deep blue of the night sky. She gazed and gazed, feeling as if she could fall forever into that transparent vastness, seeing further and further into the dark silence with no end in sight, but no sign of God anywhere. For a wild, despairing moment, it felt worse than if the whole earth was sealed under an iron dome — at least beyond that dome you might imagine the Almighty’s peace and glory waiting. This infinite, quiet stretch of space felt like a mockery: all the cries of the world’s suffering poor might soar up into it and vanish completely, lost forever before they ever reached His throne.
Lost in that dark spiral, she did not hear her father come in. The moonlight was bright enough to show him her huddled, bowed form by the window, and he touched her shoulder gently before she realised he was there.
“Margaret, I saw your light under the door. Come, pray with me — just the Lord’s Prayer. It will do us both good, I think.”
Mr. Hale and Margaret knelt side by side on the carpet before the window seat, he looking up toward the stars, her head bowed low in humbled shame. God was there, close around them, hearing her father’s soft, whispered words. Her father might have abandoned his clerical post, but only minutes before, Margaret had tangled herself in far more bitter, unthinking doubt than he ever had. She did not speak a word once the prayer was done, but slipped back to her bed like a small child ashamed of a fault, resolved that no matter how tangled and perplexing the world around her grew, she would trust to take only the one small step laid out in front of her that hour.

That night, the memory of Henry Lennox’s proposal, rudely pushed aside by the day’s upheaval, haunted all her fevered dreams. He was climbing a tree of impossible, mythic height to reach the branch where her bonnet had got stuck, he lost his footing and fell, she screamed and struggled to save him but an invisible, iron-strong hand held her back. He was dead. Then the scene shifted without warning, and she was back in the Harley Street drawing room, chatting with him as easily as she always had, all while the cold, unshakable certainty lingered in her bones that she had watched him plummet to his death.
It was a miserable, restless night, no rest at all for the day ahead. She woke with a start, unrefreshed, and the full weight of their reality pressing down on her heavier than any bad dream. It was not only the sorrow of leaving Helstone, but the terrible sharp discord woven through it: how far had her father wandered, led by doubts that had always felt to her like temptations of the Devil? She ached to ask him, and yet she would not have borne to hear the answer for all the world.

The bright, crisp morning put Mrs. Hale in an unusually light, happy mood at breakfast. She chattered on nonstop, laying out all her village kindness plans, paying no mind to her husband’s silence or Margaret’s short, monosyllabic replies. Before the table had even been cleared, Mr. Hale stood up, leaned one hand flat on the wood to steady himself, and did not look at either of them as he spoke.
“I will not be home till evening. I am going out to Bracy Common, and will ask Farmer Dobson to put me up for dinner. I shall be back for tea at seven.”
Margaret knew exactly what that meant. By seven o’clock, he planned to break the full truth of his resignation to Mrs. Hale. But she could not bear to let that heavy, impending weight hang over all of them for a whole long day. It would take every hour left to comfort her mother, once the news was out. While she stood by the window turning over how to start the conversation, and waiting for the servant to leave the parlour, Mrs. Hale had already slipped upstairs to pull on her bonnet and shawl, ready to head to the village school. She came back down brisker than usual, in unusually high spirits.
“Mamma, come take just one turn round the garden with me,” Margaret said, slipping her arm tight around her mother’s waist.
They stepped out the open French window. Mrs. Hale prattled on about something Margaret barely heard, her eyes fixed on a bumblebee rummaging deep in the bell of a foxglove. She would not speak until that bee flew out with its spoil, she decided — that would be her sign.
The bee buzzed free, and Margaret blurted the three hard, unvarnished facts out before she could second-guess herself.
“Mamma! Papa is going to leave Helstone. He’s going to leave the Church, and we are going to live in Milton-Northern.”
“What nonsense is this?” Mrs. Hale asked, her voice sharp with shocked disbelief. “Who has been filling your head with such ridiculous stories?”
“Papa told me himself,” Margaret said, reaching for the soft, comforting words that would not come. They were right by the old wooden garden bench, and Mrs. Hale sank down onto it and began to cry.
“I do not understand you,” she said. “Either you have made some terrible mistake, or I have missed everything.”
“No, mamma, I have made no mistake.” Margaret sat down beside her, and told her every last detail: the letter to the bishop, the consultation with Mr. Bell, Frederick’s godfather, the plan to resettle in the northern manufacturing town. Mrs. Hale never took her eyes off Margaret’s face as she spoke, and the shadow that fell over her features told Margaret she had accepted it was all true.
“It cannot be real,” Mrs. Hale said at last. “He would have told me long before it ever came to this.”
Margaret felt a sharp rush of anger on her mother’s behalf. Whatever her mother’s small faults of discontent and complaining, her father had been wrong to leave her to learn of his life-altering choice from their daughter. She pulled her mother’s unresisting head to rest on her breast, and stroked her hair softly.
“Dearest, darling mamma, we were both so afraid of causing you pain. Papa suffered so terribly, all those months he was wrestling with his doubts — you know your health is frail, and he did not want to put you through all that awful suspense.”
“When did he tell you, Margaret?” Mrs. Hale asked, a faint edge of wounded jealousy in her voice.
“Only yesterday,” Margaret said quickly, sensing that sting. “Poor papa — think of all the quiet pain he has carried alone all this time.”
“But what does he mean by ‘doubts’?” Mrs. Hale asked, half impatient. “Surely he cannot think he knows better than the whole Church? Cannot the bishop talk some sense into him, set him right?”
“I am afraid not,” Margaret said, her own eyes filling with tears as her mother prodded the raw nerve of regret she had been carrying all day. “I did not dare ask him for the details. It is all settled now, in any case. He plans to leave Helstone in a fortnight. I think he has already sent in his formal deed of resignation.”
“A fortnight?” Mrs. Hale cried out. “This is monstrous. It is so unfeeling of him! He has these doubts, gives up his post, and makes all these plans without ever once consulting me. I am sure if he had come to me the very first day he started questioning things, I could have nipped the whole silly idea right in the bud.”
Margaret could not bear to hear her father blamed, even when she thought he had acted foolishly. She knew his long, stubborn silence had come not from cruelty, but from a soft, cowardly tenderness for his wife’s delicate health.
“I almost hoped you would be glad to leave Helstone, mamma,” she said after a quiet pause. “You have never been well in this soft, relaxing air, you know.”
“You cannot possibly think the smoky, sooty air of a manufacturing town, all chimneys and factory grime, is better than this sweet pure air?” Mrs. Hale said, almost indignant. “Fancy living in the middle of factories, surrounded by coarse factory workers! And of course, once word gets out your father has left the Church, no decent society will receive us anywhere. It will be a terrible, terrible disgrace for our whole family! Poor dear Sir John! Thank God he is not alive to see what your father has come to! When I was a girl staying with your Aunt Shaw at Beresford Court, he would toast ‘Church and King, down with the Rump’ every single night at dinner without fail.”
Margaret was glad her mother’s thoughts had drifted away from the hurt of being kept in the dark. “We barely see any society here anyway, mamma. The Gormans, our nearest neighbours, who we never even visit, make their living as carriage-trade merchants — just as much in trade as any of the Milton manufacturers.”
“Yes, but the Gormans made carriages for half the county’s gentry, and mixed in proper society! These factory folk — who on earth wears cotton when they can afford linen, anyway?”
“Very well, mamma,” Margaret said, smiling faintly. “I will not defend the cotton spinners, or any other tradespeople, at all. We will hardly see much of them, in any case.”
“Why on earth did your father pick Milton-Northern of all places to settle?”
“Partly,” Margaret sighed, “because it is so very different from Helstone — partly because Mr. Bell says there is steady work for a private tutor there.”
“A private tutor in a factory town? Why can’t he go up to Oxford, and tutor gentlemen’s sons like he ought to?”
“You forget, mamma,” Margaret said gently. “He left the Church over his religious opinions. His doubts would make him a pariah at Oxford, no one would hire him.”
Mrs. Hale fell quiet for a long time, crying softly. At last she looked up, her face streaked with tears.
“And the furniture? How on earth are we supposed to manage a whole removal? I have never moved house in my whole life, and we only have a fortnight to sort it all out!”
Margaret felt an enormous, unnameable relief. Her mother’s distress had shifted away from the great, unanswerable spiritual and social grief, to a practical problem she could help her fix.
