She planned, promised, and steered her mother through listing every item that needed packing, sorting, or selling, until the woman was almost fully occupied with household logistics. All day long, Margaret never left her mother’s side, bending all her energy to soothe every shifting turn of her feelings. As evening drew near, she was desperate to make sure her father walked in to a soft, welcoming home after a day of draining, tearful goodbyes to every last parishioner.
When she heard his heavy, tired step in the hall, Margaret’s heart sank. She dared not go to meet him, to admit she had broken the news early, for fear of stoking her mother’s jealous, hurt feelings. She saw her mother’s lips twitch, her face flush and pale in turns, as she registered he was home too. Mr. Hale opened the parlour door, and stood in the threshold, uncertain if he should step in. His face was grey and drawn, his eyes wide with a timid, beaten look that made him look pitiful, far older than his years. The sight of that weary, defeated uncertainty tugged straight at his wife’s heart. She walked over to him, and flung herself into his arms, sobbing against his shoulder.
“Oh Richard, Richard, you should have told me sooner!”
Margaret did not wait to hear more. She slipped out of the room, ran up the stairs, threw herself face down on her bed, and buried her face in the pillows to muffle the heaving, hysterical sobs that burst loose after the whole day of rigid, careful self-control.
She had no idea how long she lay there. The housemaid Charlotte crept in to tidy the room, saw her sobbing, and fled on tiptoe to tell Dixon that Miss Hale was crying so hard she was sure to make herself deathly ill. A few moments later, Margaret felt a hand touch her shoulder, and jolted upright. Dixon stood a little ways back, holding a candle behind her so the light would not sting Margaret’s swollen, tear-blinded eyes.
“Oh Dixon, I did not hear you come in,” Margaret said, quickly forcing her trembling self back under control, lifting her feet off the floor as if she had only just woken from sleep. “Is it very late?”
“I can scarcely tell what time it is,” Dixon said, in a deeply aggrieved tone. “Ever since your mamma told me this terrible news when I dressed her for tea, I have lost all count of the hours. I do not know what is to become of all of us. I told Mrs. Hale as much, that poor Sir John would have raged worse than ever if he knew what Mr. Hale had done, leaving the Church at his time of life! I had a cousin once, miss, who became a Methodist preacher at fifty after working as a tailor his whole life — but then he could never even sew a pair of trousers that fit, so it was no surprise. But for Mr. Hale, respected by the whole parish —”
“Dixon.” Margaret’s voice was low and sharp, the kind of quiet tone that carried the weight of a distant, coming storm. She stood upright, tall and steady, and fixed the waiting-maid with a firm, unyielding stare. “Dixon, you forget to whom you are speaking. I am Mr. Hale’s daughter. That kind of talk will not be tolerated in this house. Go now. You have made a great mistake, and once you think on it, I know your own good heart will make you sorry for it.”
Dixon hovered irresolutely by the door for a minute or two. Margaret spoke again, her tone unwavering. “You may leave me, Dixon. I wish to go to bed now.”
Dixon did not know if she ought to take offense and storm off, or burst into tears. Later, she would mutter to Charlotte that Miss Margaret had the same fierce, proud streak in her that poor Master Frederick had always had, and no one else could have spoken to her like that and gotten away with it. She who would have resented such firm words from any other servant or acquaintance found herself subdued entirely, and mumbled in a half-humbled, half-injured tone, “Shall I unfasten your gown and do your hair for you, miss?”
“No

“Unwatch’d the garden bough shall sway,
The tender blossom flutter down,
Unloved that beech will gather brown,
The maple burn itself away;
Unloved, the sun-flower, shining fair,
Ray round with flames her disk of seed,
And many a rose-carnation feed
With summer spice the humming air;
* * * * *
Till from the garden and the wild
A fresh association blow
And year by year the landscape grow
Familiar to the stranger’s child;
As year by year the labourer tills
His wonted glebe, or lops the glades;
And year by year our memory fades
From all the circle of the hills.”

The final day of the Hales’ time at Helstone dawned, and the parsonage hummed with a restlessness that curdled into quiet grief. Stack upon stack of wooden packing cases crowded the front hall, the men carting them out one by one to load onto the wagon bound for the nearest railway station. Straw drifted in through open doors and windows, littering the once immaculate side lawn till its bright green blades were half hidden under chaff, making the pretty space look unkempt and forlorn. Inside, the stripped rooms echoed with every footfall, and harsh, slanting November sunlight poured unfiltered through bare, curtainless windows, making every familiar corner feel strange, already half-alien.

Mrs. Hale’s dressing room was the last spot left untouched by the chaos of packing. There, she and Dixon sorted through piles of clothing, pausing every few minutes to exclaim over some long-forgotten trinket: a scrap of Frederick’s baby embroidery, a scrap of ribbon Margaret had worn to her first village party, a chipped porcelain toy left behind after one of their childhood games. They lingered over each relic so long they barely made headway on their work, soft tearful laughs mingling with the rustle of linen.

Downstairs, Margaret stood calm and steady, directing the hired porters, answering every question cook and Charlotte posed, her face set and grave. The two maids sniffling between loads of dishes marvelled that their young lady could stay so composed, and whispered to each other that surely she did not grieve much to leave Helstone, having spent so many years in the bustle of London. They could not see the heavy, throbbing weight pressing on her chest, a ache so sharp it would have made her cry out if she let her focus slip for even a moment. She kept her eyes fixed on every small task, every last detail, because if she gave way to tears, who would steer them all through? Her father was still at the parish vestry sorting through registers, old sermons, and decades of church papers with the clerk, and when he returned he would need someone to see to his books, which no one but he could pack to his satisfaction. She would not break down before strange workmen, not even before her old familiar maids.

When the four packers trundled off to the kitchen for their tea, Margaret stepped stiffly away from her post in the hall, and wandered through the bare, echoing drawing-room out into the soft twilight of the early November evening. A thin, silvery mist draped the garden, tinting every hedge and branch a soft lilac; the sun had not quite set, and a plump robin sang from an apple bough—Margaret thought it must be the very same bird her father had doted on for years, the one he had built a little wooden nesting box for outside his study window with his own hands. The autumn leaves glowed brighter than ever, amber and burnished gold drifting down one by one in the low sun, soon to be swept flat by the first frost.

She walked along the shaded path under the pear-tree wall, the same path she had strolled down only a fortnight before at Henry Lennox’s side. Right here, next to the bed of wild thyme, he had begun to speak of his wish to marry her, and she had stared at a single late-blooming half-frozen rose as she struggled to find the words to turn him down. Only fourteen days ago, and everything had unravelled so completely. Where was he now? Back in London, no doubt, wrapped up in the familiar round of his law work, dining with his Harley Street relatives or laughing with his group of bright young legal friends. Even now, as she wandered through the damp fading Helstone garden, he might be closing his law books after a long satisfying day’s work, slipping out for the quick after-dinner walk he had told her he loved so much through Temple Gardens, breathing in the low, vast roar of tens of thousands of unseen Londoners, catching glimpses of gaslit lamps flickering up from the surface of the Thames.

Here at Helstone there was no such hum. The robin fell silent, vanishing into the vast soft hush of the coming night. Now and then a cottage door clicked shut in the far distance, a sound that echoed faint and far away. A soft, crunching rustle rose from the crisp fallen leaves in the wood beyond the garden fence, so close it made Margaret’s skin prickle. She knew it was a poacher—she had sat up many autumn nights in her bedroom, candle blown out, watching them leap noiselessly over the garden wall, run across the moonlit lawn, and slip into the shadows, and she had always felt a secret, fond admiration for their wild, free life. But tonight, for no reason she could name, she was afraid. She heard Charlotte inside shutting all the ground floor windows and bolting them for the night, unaware Margaret had slipped outside. When a dry dead bough came crashing down just inside the edge of the wood, Margaret ran swift as a deer to the drawing room window, rapping at it hard, her hands shaking.

“Let me in! Charlotte, it’s only me!” Her heart did not stop fluttering till she was safely inside, the window bolted behind her, the familiar walls wrapping around her once more. She sank down onto an empty packing case, the dismantled drawing room cold and cheerless, no fire lit, only Charlotte’s long untrimmed candle casting wavering yellow light. Charlotte stared at her in surprise, and Margaret half-smiled to explain. “I was afraid you’d lock me out entirely. The lane and churchyard gates were locked hours ago, no one would have heard me call from the kitchen.”

“Oh miss, I’d have noticed you were gone soon enough,” Charlotte fussed. “The men would’ve needed you to tell them where everything went. I’ve laid tea out in master’s study, it’s the only room that still feels like home right now.”

“Thank you, Charlotte,” Margaret said softly. “You’ve been so good. I shall hate to leave you. You must write to me whenever you can, and I promise to send you our new address the moment we know where we are going. I’ll always be glad to hear any news of Helstone.”

The study was lit with a roaring, cheerful fire, unlit candles set out on the table. Margaret sank down on the rug by the hearth to warm herself, the damp of the evening seeping through her thin dress, leaving her chilled to the bone. She sat with her arms wrapped tight around her knees, her head drooping, when she heard her father’s step on the gravel outside. She jumped up at once, shaking back her loose heavy black hair and wiping away the few stray tears that had slipped down her cheeks before she opened the door for him.

Mr. Hale looked far more broken and dejected than she did. She tried to draw him out, talking of all the small parish things he loved, but he barely answered, and refused to touch a bite of food. “Did you walk far today, father?” she asked at last.

“All the way to Fordham Beeches,” he said. “I went to see Widow Maltby. She’s heartbroken she couldn’t come say goodbye to you. Little Susan’s been waiting down by the lane for three days now, hoping to catch one last glimpse of you before you left. Oh, Margaret, my dear—what is it?”

That final image of the small child waiting patiently for her, day after day, was the last fragile thread holding Margaret’s composure together. She burst into great heaving sobs, as if her heart would break. Mr. Hale paced up and down the study helplessly, distraught at the sight of her pain. “I cannot bear it,” he muttered to himself. “I cannot bear to see everyone I love suffer. I could have endured my own grief patiently enough. Is there no way to turn back?”

“No, father,” Margaret said, lifting her head, her voice steady and low even through her tears. “It would be hard enough to learn you had made a mistake. But it would be a thousand times worse to know you had stayed, and lived a hypocrite all your days.” She dropped her voice, almost whispering the last words, as if even speaking of her father being false felt like a kind of irreverence. “Besides, it’s only that I’m tired tonight,” she added, swiping at her cheeks. “Don’t think this is any fault of yours, dear papa. We won’t speak of it any more tonight. I’ll take this cup of tea up to mamma—she had hers very early, when I was too busy to bring it to her, and I know she’ll be glad of it now.”

The next morning, the railway cart wrenched them away from the beloved Helstone parsonage, inexorable as fate. The last sight they had of the long low cottage was its walls half draped in blooming china roses and glossy pyracanthus, gilded by the bright morning sun that glinted off every window belonging to some well-loved, memory-filled room. Before they had even settled into the hired carriage that was to take them to the station, they were already moving away, never to return. Margaret’s chest ached so sharply she leaned forward to try to catch one last glimpse of the old church tower peeking over the crest of the forest at the bend in the road—then she saw her father’s eyes fixed on that very same view, and she sank back, giving him the window, for he had earned that last look far more than she had. She shut her eyes, and the tears welled up, glistening on her dark eyelashes for a moment before they rolled down her cheeks and dropped unheeded onto her dress.

They were to stop one night in London at a quiet, unassuming hotel. Poor Mrs. Hale had cried softly almost the entire day, while Dixon vented her own grief in sharp, cross irritability, pointedly pulling her skirts far away from Mr. Hale every time he passed, for she held him entirely to blame for all their upheaval. They drove through the familiar London streets, past shops they had known for years, past houses of old acquaintances, and Mrs. Hale roused up, staring around her like an excited child.

“Oh, that’s Harrison’s, where I bought almost all my wedding finery!” she exclaimed. “Goodness, how they’ve expanded! Their new plate glass windows are bigger than Crawford’s back in Southampton. And look—Margaret, did you see? We just passed Mr. Henry Lennox!”

Margaret jolted forward in her seat, then sank back at once, half-laughing at her own sudden movement. They were already a hundred yards away from him. He felt like a little piece of Helstone, a leftover from that bright, fateful day when he had asked her to marry him, and she ached to catch one small glimpse of him without him seeing her, without any need to speak.

That long evening, spent in a high, bare hotel sitting room with nothing to occupy their hands, dragged on endlessly. Mr. Hale went out to call on his bookseller and a few old clerical friends, while Margaret and her mother sat by the window, watching the crowds hurry past far below. Everyone they saw on the streets was rushing to a meeting, a dinner, a warm welcome from someone who cared. They alone felt like strangers, friendless and adrift. Margaret knew there were dozens of houses within a mile where they would be welcomed as guests—houses belonging to her aunt Shaw’s old friends, her own London acquaintances. But those busy, bustling London households had no room for the heavy, wordless, complicated grief they carried. London life spun too fast, too bright, to make space for the seven days of silent, shared mourning the friends of Job had once given him, the deep quiet where no one speaks, because they see that the grief of the grieving is far too great for any words to mend.

“Mist clogs the sunshine,
Smoky dwarf houses
Hem me round everywhere.”

The next afternoon, roughly twenty miles outside of Milton-Northern, their train pulled onto the narrow little branch line that ran straight to the seaside town of Heston. Heston itself was no more than one long, straggling street stretched parallel to the shore, with a character entirely its own: as different from the small genteel bathing spots of southern England as those were from the holiday towns of the continent. To borrow a well-loved Scots phrase, every last thing about the place felt “purpose-like.” Country carts had far more iron reinforcement and far less flimsy wood and leather on their horse tackle, even people out for a leisurely day at the beach carried the sharp, preoccupied edge of folks who never fully turn off their work mode. All the colours around them were muted gray, sturdy and long-wearing, none of the bright, frilly frippery Margaret was used to back home in Hampshire. No one wore smock frocks anymore, even the local farm labourers: the loose, billowing garments got caught in machinery too easily, and the habit had died out generations before. Down south, Margaret had often seen shop clerks with a spare minute lolling in their doorways, breathing fresh air and watching passersby. But here, if a shopkeeper had no customers to wait on, they invented tiny unnecessary tasks to keep busy—Margaret swore she spotted more than one unrolling and re-rolling the same spool of ribbons just to occupy their hands—while she and her mother set out the next morning to hunt for temporary lodgings.

Their first two nights at Heston’s inn had cost far more than Mr. Hale had budgeted for, so they jumped at the first clean, cheery set of rooms they found available. For the first time in days, Margaret felt fully at rest. There was a soft, dreamy haze to the quiet that made the rest feel almost luxurious: the low, steady lap of the distant sea against the sand, the high, clear calls of the donkey boys trotting the beach, new strange sights passing before her like half-remembered paintings that she was too lazy to press for full context before they slipped away. Even late in November, the sea air blowing off the shore was soft and warm enough to wander the waterfront at her leisure, the long misty horizon blurring the dark line of the ocean into the tender pale blue of the wintry sky, the white sail of a distant fishing boat glinting silver through a stray sliver of sun. For a little while, she was half convinced she could dream her days away in that soft pensiveness, refusing to look back on the life they’d left in Helstone, refusing to peer forward into the uncertain future waiting in Milton.

But the future would not wait to be avoided. One evening, the family agreed that Margaret and her father would travel to Milton-Northern the next day to hunt for their permanent new home. Mr. Hale had received several letters from his old university friend Mr. Bell, and two more from Mr. Thornton, the local mill-owner who was acting as their liaison on the ground, and he was desperate to meet Thornton face to face to sort out all the practical details of his new tutoring work. Margaret was loath to make the trip: she already had a deep, unspoken distaste for the very idea of a smoky manufacturing town, and was sure her mother was growing stronger by the day breathing Heston’s sea air. She would happily have delayed the expedition indefinitely.

Miles before their train even pulled into Milton’s station, a thick lead-coloured cloud hung low over the horizon in the direction of the town, far darker than the pale frosty blue of the wintry sky that had still held traces of Heston’s first light frost that morning. The closer they drew to the town limits, the more the air carried a faint taste and scent of smoke—more the absence of the sweet grass and hedgerow fragrance she was used to, than a sharp, acrid fume. They were whirled past miles of straight, identical, unrelenting streets lined with tiny uniform brick houses, dotted at regular intervals with great boxy factory blocks, hulking over the small terraces like hens over their chicks, belching great plumes of thick black “unparliamentary” smoke that fully explained the great far-off cloud Margaret had mistaken for a rain storm. As their carriage drove from the station to their hotel, they were forced to stop every few minutes: heavy, overloaded cotton lorries choked the narrow thoroughfares, leaving barely enough space for a horse and cab to squeeze past. Even her visits to the busy streets of London with her aunt Edith had never looked like this: every single van, waggon, and truck on the roads carried nothing but cotton, either raw and bulging in rough sacks, or woven into finished calico stacked in heavy bales. The footpaths were thronged with people wearing clothes of good sturdy material, but cut loose and slovenly, nothing like the shabby, threadbare smartness Margaret was familiar with among similar working and middle class crowds in London.

“This is New Street,” Mr. Hale said, leaning out the window. “The main thoroughfare in Milton. Bell talked of it constantly. When they widened it from a narrow lane into a proper public road thirty years ago, his property along here more than tripled in value overnight. Mr. Thornton’s mill can’t be far off—he’s Bell’s tenant, though I believe he runs most of his operations out of his main warehouse nearby.”

“Where is our hotel, papa?” Margaret asked.

“Just at the far end of this street, as I recall. Shall we have lunch first, or head straight out to see the list of houses we marked in the Milton Times?”

“Oh, let’s get the work done first,” Margaret said.

“Very well then. I’ll just check the desk for any note Mr. Thornton may have left for me—he said he’d send word of any good properties he heard of. We’ll keep the cab waiting, so we don’t lose our way wandering and miss our train back to Heston this afternoon.”

There were no notes waiting for them at the front desk, so they set off house-hunting. Thirty pounds a year was the absolute maximum they could afford in rent—money that would have bought them a roomy cottage with a pretty walled garden back in Hampshire. But in Milton, even the most basic accommodation of two sitting rooms and four bedrooms seemed completely out of reach. They went through every property on their list, rejecting every last one as they visited it, before staring at each other in quiet dismay.

“I think we have to go back to the second house we saw,” Margaret said at last. “The one in Crampton suburb, don’t you remember? It had three sitting rooms, we laughed at how many there were compared to only three bedrooms. But I’ve mapped it all out. The front downstairs room can double as your study and our dining room, poor papa, so mamma can have the sunniest back sitting room in the house just like we agreed. The upstairs front room, even with its horrid blue and pink rose wallpaper and the heavy overdone cornice, has a pretty view out over the plain, with that great bend in the river or canal down below. I can take the tiny little bedroom tucked over the kitchen at the top of the first flight of stairs, and you and mamma can have the room behind the drawing room. That little closet nook in the roof will make you a perfect dressing room.”

“But what about Dixon, and the young housemaid we’re planning to hire to help her?” her father asked.

“Wait, I’ve worked that out too,” Margaret said, grinning at her own sudden burst of domestic genius. “Dixon can have the back sitting room down stairs, so she doesn’t have to climb half a dozen flights of stairs all day. She complained nonstop about the stairs at Heston, you know. The young girl can have the sloped attic above yours and mamma’s bedroom. Won’t that work?”

“I’m sure it will,” Mr. Hale sighed. “But that wallpaper. What appalling taste. To smother such a small, plain house in bright patterns and such heavy trim!”

“Never mind papa,” Margaret said. “Surely you can charm the landlord into re-papering the drawing room and your bedroom at least, for mamma will spend most of her time in those two rooms. And your bookshelves will cover almost all of that gaudy print in the dining room, anyway.”

They agreed it was the best they could possibly do, so Mr. Hale walked Margaret back to the hotel, left her at the foot of the stairs, and headed off to find the landlord, Mr. Donkin, at his address on Canute Street. Margaret’s hand was just on the door of their hotel sitting room when a harried waiter came hurrying up behind her.

“Beg your pardon, ma’am. Your husband left so quickly I didn’t have a chance to flag him down. A Mr. Thornton called not five minutes after you two stepped in the door, then came back again just now, and I told him you said you’d be back in an hour, so he’s waiting in your sitting room for Mr. Hale.”

“Thank you. My father will be back very soon, just tell him that when he arrives,” Margaret said. She opened the door and stepped inside with the straight, unhurried, dignified poise that was habitual to her, no trace of awkwardness: she was far too used to formal social company to be flustered by a stranger waiting in her sitting room on business for her father.

Mr. Thornton was far more surprised and discomfited than she was. He had expected a quiet, mild middle-aged clergyman to walk through the door, and instead a young lady stepped forward with frank, unapologetic dignity—entirely different from the type of woman he was used to meeting in Milton’s industrial circles. Her dress was perfectly plain: a fine, well-shaped straw bonnet trimmed with nothing but white ribbon, a large soft Indian shawl draped in long heavy folds over her shoulders, which she wore with the easy grace of an empress wearing royal drapery. He fumbled for words, utterly taken aback: he had only ever heard Mr. Hale mention that he had a daughter, and had assumed the girl was still small.

“Mr. Thornton, I presume?” Margaret said, after a short quiet pause where he failed to find his voice. “Do sit down. My father dropped me at the door barely a minute ago, but no one had the chance to tell him you were here, so he stepped out on an errand. He will be back almost immediately. I am so sorry you’ve had the trouble of calling twice.”

Thornton was a man well used to being the one in charge, but he found himself sitting down unthinkingly at her bidding. He had been fuming a minute before, annoyed at wasting precious time on a busy market day waiting for a man who never showed up, but now he found himself calm, unhurried, in no rush to leave. “Do you know where Mr. Hale has gone? I might be able to go find him for you, to save him the extra walk.”

“He has gone to see a Mr. Donkin in Canute Street,” Margaret told him. “He is the landlord of the house we are hoping to rent in Crampton.”

Mr. Thornton knew that house well. He had seen the advertisement himself, and gone to inspect it on Mr. Bell’s direct request, to do what he could to help his old friend’s new protégé settle into Milton. He had also been curious about the clergyman who had given up a comfortable, respected country living to start a quiet new life in the industrial north. Back then he had thought the Crampton house was ideal for the Hales, but now, seeing Margaret move with that quiet, elegant poise of a woman raised on soft country gentility, he suddenly felt ashamed he had ever thought that gaudy, overdecorated house would be good enough for them.

Margaret was tired after a morning traipsing the hard pavement, and would have happily sat quiet and rested, but she knew she had a duty to play the gentlewoman and make polite small talk to their guest. But Mr. Thornton was distracted, his short curled upper lip, strong squared upturned chin, and the unyielding set of his shoulders making him look every inch the resolute, no-nonsense manufacturer he was. He misread her quiet, careful politeness as haughty coldness, even contempt for his rough, unpolished northern ways. He found himself caught in two conflicting feelings: he could not repress a sharp, unlooked-for admiration for her pale clear beauty, her soft dark eyes, the graceful line of her throat as she spoke, but he resented what he assumed was her supercilious disdain for a man he considered himself to be: a rough, plain working man with no fine southern graces. He was half ready to storm out in irritation when Mr. Hale finally stepped through the door, full of warm, gentlemanly apologies for missing him, and smoothed over the awkward tension between them at once.

The two men spent nearly an hour chatting about their mutual old friend Mr. Bell, while Margaret slipped over to the window to lose herself watching the crowds move past on New Street, barely hearing her father when he called her over to tell her that the landlord was refusing point blank to re-paper the Crampton house, insisting the pink and blue rose print was a very expensive, fashionable new design. Margaret sighed, mentally mapping out if she could hang her watercolour sketches to cover the worst of the gaudy patterns, before Mr. Hale warmly pressed Thornton to stay and share luncheon with them. Thornton would have accepted in a heartbeat if Margaret had so much as smiled to second her father’s invitation, but she was too lost in thought to notice, and said nothing. He left with a stiff, formal bow, and walked out onto New Street feeling more awkward and self-conscious in every limb than he ever had in his entire life.

As soon as he was gone, Margaret laughed softly. “I was so relieved you walked in when you did. He never once elaborated on any subject, just gave me little short, abrupt answers to every remark I made. I was all out of small talk.”

“But he was very much to the point, wasn’t he?” Mr. Hale said, defending his new friend. “He is a remarkably clear-headed fellow. He told me you were right about Crampton being the best place for us—it’s built on gravelly soil, far and away the healthiest suburb within miles of Milton.”

When they arrived back in Heston, they sat around the tea table recounting every last detail of the day to Mrs. Hale, who was full of endless eager questions. She asked Margaret to describe Mr. Thornton, and Margaret, still tired from the long day, roused herself to paint a quick portrait: tall, broad-shouldered, around thirty years old, face neither plain nor handsome, with an expression of such sharp unyielding resolution that no amount of rough edges could make him seem common or vulgar at all, exactly the sort of strong, sagacious man who was born to run a great manufacturing operation. She warned her mother to prepare herself for the hideous pink and blue wallpaper in their new drawing room, and the absurdly heavy overdone cornice running around the ceiling.

But when their moving van pulled up to the Crampton house a few days later, the obnoxious bright patterned paper was gone entirely, replaced with a soft, plain neutral print that suited their furniture perfectly. The landlord accepted their thanks very calmly, and let them believe he had relented out of kindness, choosing not to mention that he had only agreed to take on the extra work after one short, sharp remonstrance from Mr. Thornton, the wealthiest and most respected man in the whole town of Milton.

Not even the fresh, pale sprigged wallpaper hung in each of their rooms was enough to reconcile the Hales to their new life in Milton. Far more than pretty decor was needed—far more than they could ever hope to get. The thick, rank yellow fogs of November had settled over the town, swallowing the broad sweep of valley and river plain that would have stretched out below their windows on a clear day; when Mrs. Hale stepped over their threshold for the first time, all she could see was murky, smoky mist pressed right up against the glass, great choking white wreaths of unwholesome damp curling through every open door.

Margaret and Dixon had been unpacking for two full days, but the house still reeked of moving chaos, crates stacked in hallways, furniture half pushed into place, linens stacked on every available surface. “Oh Margaret! Are we truly to live here?” Mrs. Hale asked, her voice thick with blank dismay. Margaret’s own ached in echo of that dreary tone, and she could barely steady herself enough to reply that London fogs were often far worse. “But London had friends behind it,” Mrs. Hale whispered. “Here—we are desolate. Oh Dixon, what a place this is!”

“Indeed ma’am, this damp will be the death of you before the winter’s half out, and then I know exactly who’ll—wait a minute, Miss Hale! That crate is far too heavy for you to be lifting,” Dixon fussed, darting forward to take the weight from Margaret’s arms. “Not at all, thank you,” Margaret replied coolly. “The best thing we can do for Mamma is get her room fully made up for her to rest in, while I run and bring her a cup of hot coffee.”

Mr. Hale was equally low-spirited, and turned to Margaret for comfort as soon as he saw her. “Margaret, I swear this place is unhealthy. Only think if your mother or you fell ill over this muck. I wish I had moved us to some quiet corner of Wales instead—this is dreadful.”

There was no comfort to be given. They were settled in Milton, and they would have to endure the smoke and fog for many months yet. The night before, Mr. Hale had tallied up their moving costs and their fortnight’s stay at the inn in Heston with a sinking heart, and found it had eaten through nearly all his small stock of ready cash. There was nowhere else to go. That heavy smoky curtain of fog was not just weather, Margaret realized late that night—it was a barrier of circumstance that had shut them completely off from the old soft life they had known at Helstone. She sank down onto a wooden crate in her narrow back bedroom, half ready to slump into a stupor of despair. The window looked out onto the blank brick wall of the adjoining house, barely ten feet away, looming through the murk like a solid wall blocking off all hope. The address label stuck to the crate under her fingers was written in the familiar looping hand of the Helstone village grocer, and that tiny detail was almost enough to make her cry.

She forced herself to pull her mind away from the present, and fished out the half-read letter from Edith she had abandoned that morning in the chaos of unpacking. Edith wrote giddily of their arrival in Corfu, of their Mediterranean voyage, the dancing and late-night music on deck, the new villa they shared with another newly married officer’s family, perched high on rocky cliffs looking out over a sea so deep and blue it looked like crushed sapphire. Her days were nothing but boat trips and land picnics, copying the latest popular dance tunes for the regimental bandmaster; her life was a perfect stretch of cloudless blue sky, with not a single shadow of care to mar it. She wrote that she hoped Margaret could come visit them for a long stay if the regiment was posted another year on the island.

Margaret smiled sadly, remembering the rainy day a full year prior that Edith described, when she had refused to wear her new silk gown to a stuffy London dinner, only to meet Captain Lennox there for the very first time. All the easy luxury of the Harley Street drawing rooms, the grand dinners, the quiet ease of her old London life rose up before her, a sharp contrast to the cold damp chaos of her current bedroom. That old smooth world had closed over without her, going on just the same even though Aunt Shaw and Edith were gone, even though she was hundreds of miles north in a smoky mill town. She thought of Henry Lennox, who had once asked her to marry him, and knew he would do all he could to forget her now, given how she had rejected him. She could not help but wonder what life would have been like if she had accepted him—she knew his sharp lawyer’s pride would have chafed terribly at the scandal of her father leaving the church. But in that moment she was almost glad it had not come to pass. They were at the very bottom now, she thought. Things could not get any worse. She undressed slowly, falling asleep with a faint, unspoken hope that some brightness would find them soon. She had no idea just how long she would have to wait for it.

The cold damp settled over the household quickly. Mrs. Hale caught a severe, lingering chest cold, Dixon herself grew worn and haggard with all the extra work, and they could not find a single local girl willing to come work as their maid. All the young women in Milton preferred the higher wages and greater independence of working in the factories, and the few who did apply for the post answered Dixon’s sharp inquiries about their qualifications with blunt, independent cheek, even questioning her right to put on airs as a lady’s maid in a household that lived in a thirty pound a year rental house. Dixon’s standard for a good servant was rooted in the reverent, quiet village girls of Helstone’s Sunday school, who treated the parsonage staff with near awe, and she was utterly scandalized by their rough, unapologetic northern manners. The Hales were forced to hire a charwoman to come in almost every day to help with the heavy work.

Mr. Hale had soon picked up several pupils, recommended to him first by his old college friend Mr. Bell, and then through the influence of the prominent local mill owner Mr. Thornton. Most of them were sons of Milton’s manufacturing families, who held that a boy had to be introduced to the work of the mill or warehouse by the age of fourteen or fifteen if he was ever to become a good tradesman—any boy sent off to an English university would come back too unsettled for commercial life, or so the common wisdom ran. There were a handful of older men, though, past their youth but in their prime, who knew they had missed out on an education in their rush to start work, and came to Mr. Hale for lessons. Mr. Thornton was the oldest of these, and quickly his favourite. Mr. Hale praised his intelligence and good sense so often at the dinner table that it became a quiet family joke that he barely ever did any actual tutoring at their lessons, the two of them spending the whole hour talking. Margaret encouraged that light, teasing tone, because she noticed Mrs. Hale was growing quietly jealous of her husband’s new friend, hurt that he seemed to look forward to their meetings more than spending his evenings with her.

Margaret eventually grew weary of Dixon’s endless tales of insolent potential servants, and offered to go out herself to the butchers and grocers around town to ask after a suitable girl. It was a trial at first, walking the bustling streets of the manufacturing town completely unaccompanied. All her life in London, Aunt Shaw had insisted a footman walk with her and Edith anywhere beyond the immediate bounds of Harley Street, and her long free rambles through the wild Helstone woods, where she could run or stand still for an hour to watch a bird at her own pace, made the slow, decorous pace required of her in the crowded mill streets feel stifling. For the first few weeks she kept accidentally stumbling into the great streams of workers pouring out of the mills at shift change, loud, boisterous, completely unapologetic about their frank curiosity. The mill girls would lean in to touch the soft wool of her shawl, exclaim over her dress, and ask where she had bought such pretty fabric, and Margaret soon found herself smiling back, charmed by their uncomplicated honesty. The mill men flustered her far more, calling out open, blunt compliments on her face and figure, that at first made her cheeks burn hot with indignation—until she realized none of them had any intention of being rude, they simply had no use for the polished false politeness of southern drawing rooms. One ragged, careworn middle-aged worker called out to her once on a grey drizzling day, “Your bonny face, my lass, makes the whole day look brighter,” and Margaret gave him a soft, warm smile back. After that they would nod quietly to each other whenever they passed on the road, never exchanging another word.

One mild early spring day, Margaret walked a little way out of town, past the mills, to the hedgerows at the edge of the fields, gathering small bunches of dog violets and lesser celandines, her heart aching a little for the thick carpets of wild spring flowers that would be blooming in the Hampshire woods that same week. Her father left her to run a quick errand in the town, and on the walk home she ran straight into that quiet nodding acquaintance, Nicholas Higgins, walking with his pale, thin young daughter Bessy, who could barely keep pace on her frail legs. Bessy stared longingly at the posy in Margaret’s hand, and on a sudden impulse Margaret offered it to her. The girl’s pale blue eyes lit right up. Nicholas told her he was from further north, near Burnley, and that it did his heart good to see a southern lady be so kind to a pair of ragged factory folk. Margaret spoke to Bessy, her voice soft with pity, and the girl told her quietly that no spring or summer on earth would ever make her well again, that the mill dust had eaten away at her lungs, but she dreamed of a heavenly spring where she would walk in fields of immortal amaranth flowers, no pain or breathlessness to trouble her.

Margaret was shocked, but not repelled. She asked where they lived, and Nicholas at first grumbled that he did not care for strangers poking around his small cottage, but softened when he saw how sorry Margaret was for his sick daughter, and told her she could call on them any time she liked. As they turned towards their street, Bessy looked over her shoulder and whispered to Margaret not to forget her promise to visit. Nicholas laughed, shaking his head, and said he could read that proud soft face of hers like a book—she would not let them down.

Margaret walked home smiling, and for the first time since they had arrived in Milton, the foggy smoky town did not feel like a cold prison. Brightness had not come with the sun, or some grand lucky turn of fortune. It had come in the shape of two ragged, honest strangers, who had crossed her path on a grey mill road, and given her a real, living human stake in the strange northern town that had once felt so utterly alien.

“Let China’s earth, enriched with coloured stains,
Pencil’d with gold, and streaked with azure veins,
The grateful flavour of the Indian leaf,
Or Mocha’s sunburnt berry glad receive.”

The day after their meeting with Higgins and his daughter, Mr. Hale drifted up to the small upstairs drawing room at an unaccustomed hour. He lingered by the mantelpiece, plucking at the edge of a shell ornament, then running a finger along the spines of the few books stacked on the side table, as if he were carefully inspecting every object in the room. Margaret saw at once it was no more than a nervous tic: a way of staving off something he longed to say, yet feared to voice. At last, the words came out in a soft rush:
“My dear! I’ve asked Mr. Thornton to come to tea tonight.”

Mrs. Hale was leaned back in her plush easy chair, eyes half-shut, the faint, habitual line of pain that had settled on her face these last months deepening. At her husband’s announcement she roused at once, sharp and querulous.
“Mr. Thornton! And tonight? What on earth is the man thinking of, dropping by unannounced like that? And Dixon is mid-wash of all my muslins and laces, and these horrid east winds have turned all the water hard—no soft water anywhere, I suppose, and we shall be stuck with this wretched Milton wind all year round.”
“The wind’s veering round, my dear,” Mr. Hale said, glancing out the window at the plumes of factory smoke drifting steadily due east. He had not yet learned to read Milton’s compass, and adjusted the points to suit whatever pleasant fiction he wanted to believe at the time.
“Don’t tell me such nonsense!” Mrs. Hale said, shuddering and pulling her shawl tighter around her shoulders. “East or west, this man is coming, I suppose.”

“Oh mamma, you’ve never met Mr. Thornton, or you’d know he’s exactly the sort of man who delights in fighting every adverse thing he meets—enemies, gales, inconvenient arrangements. The harder it blows and rains, the surer he is to show up. I’ll go help Dixon, never you fear. I’ve got quite a knack for clear-starching these days. And he won’t want any fancy amusements, he’ll only want to talk to Papa. Papa, I’ve been dying to meet the Pythias to your Damon! You know I only saw him once before, and we were both so awkward and flustered we had nothing sensible to say to each other, so we didn’t get on at all well.”
“I’m not sure you’ll ever take to him, Margaret. He is not the sort of man who fusses over ladies, that’s all.”
Margaret curved her throat in a little, scornful half-smile. “I don’t care overmuch for men who make a profession of being agreeable to every lady they meet, papa. Mr. Thornton is your friend—the only person in Milton who properly appreciates your work.”
“The only decent person in Milton, at all,” Mrs. Hale put in.
“So we’ll give him a proper welcome, and make coconut cakes. Dixon will be so flattered if we ask her to whip up a batch, and I’ll iron all your lace caps for you, mamma, before he arrives.”

Margaret found herself wishing Mr. Thornton a hundred miles away a dozen times that morning. She had planned a quiet, pleasant day: a long letter to Edith back in the south, a stretch of reading Dante, a visit to the Higgins household to check on Bessy. Instead, she stood bent over the ironing board, pressing linen until her arms ached, listening to Dixon rail at the hard water that had spotted her best lace, at the smoke that drifted in through the scullery window and grimed the clean muslin. She had to pour on extra sympathy to keep Dixon from carrying her litany of grievances straight up to Mrs. Hale, who was already too unwell to be fretted. Every few minutes she had to remind herself of her father’s quiet fondness for his new friend, to beat back the rising irritation and chase away the threat of the throbbing headache that had begun to plague her lately. When she finally set the iron down, she was so tired she could barely speak, and joked to her mother that she was no longer Peggy the laundry maid, but Margaret Hale the lady. To her dismay, Mrs. Hale took the jest completely seriously.
“Yes! If anyone had told me, when I was Miss Beresford, one of the belles of the whole county, that my own daughter would stand half a day in a poky little scullery working like a common servant, just so we could properly receive a tradesman—and that this tradesman would be the only person in the whole town who treats us with any kindness—”
“Oh mamma!” Margaret said, lifting her head, “don’t scold me for a silly careless joke. I don’t mind ironing, or any sort of work for you and papa. I was born and bred a lady, and that doesn’t vanish the moment I have to scrub a floor or wash dishes. I’m just tired for a little while, that’s all. In half an hour I’d do it all over again, no complaint. And as for Mr. Thornton being in trade, poor fellow, he can’t help that. I doubt his education ever left him any other path to take, out here in Milton.” She walked slowly off to her own bedroom after that, too frayed to bear any more cross words just then.

Across the town, in the Thorntons’ solid, substantial home, a similar but far stiffer scene was unfolding that very same hour. A large-boned woman long past middle age sat at work in the grim, richly furnished dining room. Her features, like her broad frame, were strong and unyielding, not heavy or soft. Her face shifted slowly from one firm, decided expression to another, no faint or wavering emotion ever crossing it. Passersby on the street could not help turning their heads for a second glance at her when she walked past: the firm, severe, dignified woman who never stepped aside for small courtesies, never deviated from her straight, steady path toward whatever clear, defined goal she had set for herself. She was dressed in stout black silk, every thread intact, no spot or fade anywhere on the fabric. She was mending a vast, fine linen tablecloth, holding it up to the light every few minutes to spot the thin worn patches that required her careful, delicate stitches. There were almost no books in the room, save for the six volumes of Matthew Henry’s Bible commentaries stacked in the center of the massive sideboard, flanked by a polished silver tea urn on one side and a brass oil lamp on the other. From a distant back room, the tinny trill of a piano drifted through the door: someone was rushing through a flashy salon piece, playing so fast that every third note was indistinct or missed entirely, and the loud crashing chords at the end were half wrong, though the performer was perfectly satisfied with their work.
A step as firm and decisive as her own passed the dining room door.
“John! Is that you?”
Her son pushed the door open, and stepped inside.
“What on earth brings you home so early? I thought you were going to tea at that Mr. Hale’s, the friend of Mr. Bell’s.”
“So I am, mother. I’ve come home to dress for it.”
“Dress! Humph! When I was a girl, young men thought dressing once a day was more than enough. Why on earth do you need to dress up just to drink a cup of tea with an old country parson?”
“Mr. Hale is a gentleman. His wife and daughter are ladies.”
“Wife and daughter? Do they work as teachers too? What do they do with their days? You never mentioned them before.”
“No, mother, because I’ve never even met Mrs. Hale. I only spoke to Miss Hale for half an hour once, some weeks back.”
“Mind you don’t get tangled up by some penniless girl looking to catch a rich husband, John.”
“I am not easily caught, as you well know,” he said, a sharp edge to his voice. “And I will not have Miss Hale spoken of in that light—you know that offends me. I have never seen a young woman show the slightest interest in me for my money, and I do not believe any of them would waste their time trying to snare me.”
Mrs. Thornton was not about to cede the point. “Well, I’m just saying take care. Maybe the Milton girls have too much pride to go angling after husbands, but this Miss Hale comes down from those soft southern aristocratic counties, where, if all the stories are true, any man with a decent fortune is treated like a prize to be won.”
Mr. Thornton’s brow tightened, and he stepped a little further into the room. “Mother,” he said, with a short, sharp, scornful laugh, “you will force me to confess. The only time I met Miss Hale, she treated me with such haughty, distant courtesy it reeked of contempt. She held herself apart from me as if she were a queen, and I some ragged, unwashed vassal of hers. You have nothing to worry about, rest easy.”
“Easy? I am not easy, and I am far from content! Some renegade clergyman’s daughter, daring to turn her nose up at you! If I were you, I would not dress an inch for any of that saucy set.”
As he left the room, he paused to say: “Mr. Hale is kind, and gentle, and far more learned than any man you will meet in Milton. He is not saucy. I will tell you what Mrs. Hale is like, if you care to hear, when I get back tonight.”
He shut the door behind him, and was gone.
“Dare despise my son! Dare treat him like some low vassal, indeed!” Mrs. Thornton muttered, stabbing her needle hard through the linen tablecloth. “Humph! I’d like to see her find half as good a man anywhere in this country. Boy and man, he is the noblest, stoutest heart I have ever known. I am his mother, but I am not blind to what is right and wrong, not like some folk. I know what Fanny is, with her silly little vanities, and I know what John is. Despise him! I hate that girl.”

“We are the trees whom shaking fastens more.” — GEORGE HERBERT

Mr. Thornton left his home without stepping back into the dining room, already running late as he walked at a rapid pace toward Crampton. He had no wish to slight his new friend with disrespectful unpunctuality, and the parish church clock struck half-past seven just as he knocked at the Hales’ door, waiting patiently while Dixon dragged her feet—always doubly tardy when forced to stoop to the indignity of answering a visitor’s bell.

He was ushered straight into the tiny drawing room, where Mr. Hale greeted him warmly before leading him over to his wife: pale, swathed in a soft wool shawl, her bearing a silent, gentle excuse for the languid coldness of her greeting. Margaret was lighting the central lamp as dusk gathered, the soft golden glow spilling out across the dusky edges of the room, where the Hales kept their windows open to the night sky and the cool dark air, out of long-held country habit.

The little space stood in stark contrast to the dining room he had just left back at his own house: heavy, handsomely furnished, with no trace of soft domestic warmth save the spot where his mother sat, and no purpose for the room other than eating and drinking. It was true his mother preferred to spend her evenings there, and her will was unchallenged household law, but his drawing room back home was nothing like this. Twenty times the size, a hundred times finer, it did not hold a quarter of this room’s comfort. There were no gilded mirrors here, not even a small scrap of glass to catch and reflect the light the way a pond catches the sun in a wild landscape—only a warm, soft breadth of muted colour, offset by the faded, beloved Helstone chintz that draped the curtains and chair seats. An open writing desk stood under the window opposite the door, while the other window held a stand with a tall white china vase spilling over with English ivy, pale green birch fronds, and the rich copper hue of autumn beech leaves. Neat work baskets sat tucked in corners, and books stacked casually on a side table, left half-read and set down only moments before. Behind the door, a second table was laid for tea under a crisp white cloth, piled high with crumbly coconut cakes, a basket of bright oranges, and ruddy American apples heaped on fresh green leaves. It struck Mr. Thornton that all these small, graceful, thoughtful touches were second nature to the Hales, and perfectly in keeping with the quiet grace of Margaret.

She stood by the tea table in a pale muslin gown streaked soft pink, seeming not to attend to the conversation at all, her whole focus on arranging the tea cups, her round ivory hands moving over them with quiet, noiseless daintiness. A thin bracelet on her taper arm kept slipping down over her round wrist, and Mr. Thornton watched her push the troublesome ornament back up over her soft flesh, far more absorbed by that small movement than anything her father was saying. He could barely stop himself from exclaiming out loud when it slipped down her wrist again. There was so little left to do to finish preparing tea by the time he arrived, that he was almost sorry the ritual of eating and drinking broke his chance to keep watching her. She handed him his cup of tea with the proud air of an unwilling queen tending to a guest, but her eyes caught the exact second he was ready for a refill without him asking. He almost longed to watch her tend to him the way he watched her tend to her father, who playfully took her small finger and thumb between his own larger, rougher hands to use them as makeshift sugar tongs. Margaret’s beautiful eyes lifted to meet her father’s then, bright with equal parts laughter and soft love, the small private moment entirely unobserved by anyone else, or so the pair thought.

Margaret’s head still ached, her pale face and quiet bearing testimony to that fact, but she was resolved to step in to fill any awkward long pause before their guest could feel himself neglected. But the conversation flowed easily, and once the tea things were cleared away, she slipped into a corner next to her mother with her needlework, relieved she could let her mind wander back to her old Helstone home without fear of being called on to speak. Mr. Thornton and Mr. Hale were both fully absorbed picking up the thread of a discussion they had started at their last meeting.

A soft remark from her mother pulled Margaret back to the present, and she looked up from her work to suddenly notice the sharp contrast between the two men’s faces, a physical marker for how entirely different their natures were. Her father was slight of build, appearing taller than he really was until he stood next to Thornton’s broad, tall, sturdy frame. The lines on Mr. Hale’s face were soft and undulating, a faint tremor passing over them whenever his emotions shifted, every fleeting feeling written clearly on his features. His eyelids were large and arched, giving his eyes a dreamy, languid beauty almost feminine in its softness, his fine arched brows set high above those heavy lids. On Thornton’s face, by contrast, straight dark brows fell low over clear, deep-set, earnest eyes that were never unkindly sharp, but focused enough to pierce straight through to the very core of whatever he was looking at. His face bore few lines, firm as if carved out of pale marble, gathered mostly around his lips, which were slightly compressed over a set of perfect, gleaming teeth. When his rare, bright smile broke out in an instant, it flooded his whole face with light, shifting that severe, determined expression of a man ready to dare anything into a moment of unguarded, honest joy— the kind of open, unfiltered feeling you almost only ever see in small children. Margaret found she liked that smile; it was the first thing she had ever admired about her father’s new friend, and the clear contrast in their characters made perfect sense of the obvious warm attraction the two men felt for each other.

She rearranged the tangled skein of her mother’s worsted wool, and drifted back into half-thought, so completely forgotten by Mr. Thornton as he explained the magnificent, terrible power and delicate precision of the new steam hammer that she might as well have not been in the room at all. He described the way the machine could stretch from earth to sky one minute, filling the whole horizon with its force, and then shrink small enough to crack a walnut without breaking its shell the next, so obedient it might have been one of the genii from the Arabian Nights. “That great leap of imagination, that practical realisation of a gigantic thought, came from the brain of a man right here in our Milton,” Thornton said. “And that same man will keep climbing, step by step, from one wonder to the next, and there are dozens of men in this town who could step straight into his place if he fell, carrying on the work that makes all brute material power bow to science.”

“Your boast reminds me of the old ballad lines,” Mr. Hale laughed, “‘I’ve a hundred captains in England,’ he said, ‘As good as ever was he.’”

Margaret looked up in sudden bewilderment at the quotation—how on earth had they jumped from cog wheels and steam hammers to Chevy Chase?

“It is no boast,” Thornton replied, “it is plain, unvarnished fact. I will not deny I am proud to belong to a district whose very necessities give birth to that kind of grand, uncompromising imagination. I would far rather toil here, suffer, even fail and fall short, than spend a soft, dull, prosperous life in the worn old grooves of what you call the aristocratic South, where days slip by slow and careless, and people get clogged thick with honey so they can never rise and fly.”

“You are wrong,” Margaret burst out, stung to vehement defense of the Hampshire home she loved so well. Colour flooded her cheeks, angry tears pricked the corners of her eyes. “You do not know anything of the South. If there is less of the gambing, restless trade spirit that forces out these brilliant inventions, and less rapid progress, there is far less suffering too. I see men on the streets of Milton ground down by pinching care, who are not just suffering, but bitter, full of hate. In the South we have our poor, but you never see that terrible, sullen look of a man convinced the world has cheated him, that I see on every other face here. You do not know the South at all, Mr. Thornton,” she finished, collapsing into determined silence, furious at herself for speaking so much out of turn.

“Then may I say you do not know the North?” he asked, his tone soft with unmistakeable gentleness, the second he realised he had truly hurt her feelings. She stayed resolutely quiet, her heart aching for the woods and lanes of Helstone hundreds of miles away, certain her voice would shake and break if she tried to speak.

“At any rate, Mr. Thornton,” Mrs. Hale cut in smoothly, “you must admit Milton is far smokier, dirtier, than any town you will ever find in the South.”

“I will not argue that point,” he said, his quick bright smile breaking out. “Though Parliament has bidden us burn our own smoke, so I suppose we will all behave like good little children and obey the edict — sooner or later.”

“But you told me you had already altered your factory chimneys to consume all smoke, did you not?” Mr. Hale asked.

“I paid for the alterations out of my own pocket, long before Parliament ever meddled with the business. It was a steep immediate outlay, but it paid me back in saved coal, in the long run. I doubt I would have done it, if I had waited for the act to pass. I would have dragged my feet, waited to be reported, fined, and dragged my heels every step of the way before complying. All laws that rely on informers and petty fines turn inert, because the whole system is odious to everyone who has to work with it. I doubt if a single factory chimney in Milton has ever been reported for violating the smoke act, not in the last five years — even though half of them send a third of their coal bill billowing out in what we call ‘unparliamentary’ smoke.”

“All I know is that it is impossible to keep muslin blinds clean here for more than a week, when at Helstone we kept the same blinds up for a month or more, and they never looked so much as dusty at the end. And Margaret — how many times did you wash your hands before noon today? Three times, was it not?” Mrs. Hale asked.

“Yes, mamma.”

“You seem to have quite a strong objection to all acts of Parliament and government interference in the cotton trade,” Mr. Hale observed.

“I do, and a great many other masters do too, and with good reason. The whole machinery of the cotton trade is so new, is it any wonder it does not run perfectly smooth in every part all at once? Seventy years ago, what was it? Nothing. Now look at it. Rough, untrained men of exactly the same birth and station found themselves suddenly split into masters and workers, all because some of them had the sharp wit to see the enormous potential hidden in Arkwright’s clunky little spinning frame. That sudden, explosive growth gave the first generation of cotton lords almost unlimited wealth and power, over their workers, over purchasers, over the entire global market. I can show you an old newspaper advertisement from fifty years back, run by one of the half dozen calico printers in Milton at the time, that announced he would close his warehouse sharp at noon every day — and every customer had better show up before then, or be turned away. Imagine a man dictating what hours people could buy his goods! Now? I would get out of bed at midnight, hat in hand, to take an order from a good customer.”

Margaret’s lip curled a little, but she found she could not tune him out, could not drift back to her own private thoughts any longer.

“I only say this to show you the almost unlimited power the first generation of manufacturers held at the start of this century. It went to their heads. Success in trade did not mean a man was balanced or just in every other part of his life. Their sense of fairness, their simple decency, got smothered entirely under the flood of sudden wealth that poured down on them. You hear wild stories of the absurd, reckless extravagance those early cotton lords indulged in, on feast days and holidays. No one can deny how tyrannical they were over their workpeople, either. You know the old proverb, Mr. Hale: ‘Set a beggar on horseback, and he’ll ride to the devil.’ Some of those early manufacturers rode straight there in grand style, crushing human bone and flesh under their horse’s hooves without a flicker of remorse. But then the reaction came. More factories opened, more masters, more hands were needed. The power balanced out between masters and men, and now the fight is waged on fair, equal ground between us. We will not submit to an umpire we did not choose, let alone the interference of some meddler who knows nothing of the real facts of the work — even if that meddler calls itself the High Court of Parliament.”

“Must you call it a battle between the two classes?” Mr. Hale asked. “I can see from the way you use the word it is the truest description of the state of things you know.”

“It is true. It is as necessary a battle as the one that prudence and decency are always waging against ignorance and improvidence. It is one of the great beauties of our system that a working man can raise himself up to be a master by his own exertions and good conduct, that anyone who disciplines himself to decency, sobriety, and hard work can join our ranks — if not as a master, as an overlooker, a cashier, a bookkeeper, a clerk, on the side of order and authority.”

“So you consider everyone who never manages to raise themselves up in the world, no matter the reason for their failure, your enemy?” Margaret asked, her voice clear and cold.

“ Their own enemies, most certainly,” he replied, stung a little by the haughty disapproval in her tone. But in a moment, his straightforward honesty told him that was a petty, quibbling answer to what she had said. Proud as she was, scornful as she chose to be, he owed it to himself to explain exactly what he meant, as truthfully as he could. It was not easy to untangle her interpretation from his own meaning, and the simplest way to explain it was to tell them the story of his own life — personal as that felt, to tell strangers. He pushed aside the flicker of shyness that sent a faint flush up his dark cheek, and said:

“I do not speak out of turn. Sixteen years ago, my father died under terrible, miserable circumstances. I was pulled straight out of school, and had to become a man in the space of a few days. I had a mother very few people are blessed to have: a woman of immense strength, unshakable resolve. We moved to a small country town where living was cheaper than Milton, and I got a job in a draper’s shop — a very fine place, by the way, to learn the true value of goods. Week by week, the three of us lived on fifteen shillings. My mother managed our household so that I put three shillings away from that fifteen, every single week. That was the start. That taught me self denial. Now that I can afford to give my mother all the comforts her old age deserves, far more than she ever wanted for herself, I thank her silently every day for that early training. I know from my own life that it is no luck, no special merit, no rare talent that got me here — just the habits of a life that taught me to despise any indulgence I had not earned with my own work. That suffering you say you see written on the faces of Milton’s working people, Miss Hale? It is the natural consequence of chasing easy, unearned pleasure, somewhere earlier in their lives. I do not hate the self-indulgent, the lazy. I simply look on them with contempt for how small and weak their characters are.”

“But you had the rudiments of a good education,” Mr. Hale pointed out. “The eager way you are reading Homer now makes it obvious you are not meeting it for the first time — you read it as a boy, and are only recalling that old knowledge now.”

“That is true. I stumbled through Homer at school, I was even considered a halfway decent classic in those days, though my Latin and Greek have slipped away from me since. But what preparation was any of that for the life I had to lead? None at all. Utterly none. Any man who can read and write started out with just as much genuinely useful knowledge as I had back then.”

“Well, I will not agree with you,” Mr. Hale laughed. “But that is the old schoolmaster in me, I suppose, the old proverb that says ‘there is nothing like leather.’ I wonder if the memory of the heroic, simple life Homer wrote of ever gave you strength in those hard days?”

“Not a single bit!” Mr. Thornton exclaimed, laughing out loud. “I was far too busy fighting for my daily bread, pressed shoulder to shoulder with other living men fighting the same fight, to waste a single thought on long dead heroes. Now that my mother is safe, comfortable, at peace in her old age, properly rewarded for all the work she put in, I can sit back and read those old stories and enjoy them fully.”

When Mr. Thornton rose to leave after shaking hands with Mr. and Mrs. Hale, he held his hand out to Margaret to wish her good-bye the same way, the frank, familiar custom of Milton. But Margaret had not expected it, and simply bowed to him in farewell. The second she saw his half-extended hand quickly drawn back, she was sorry she had not seen his intention. Mr. Thornton, however, never guessed at her regret. He drew himself up to his full height, and walked out of the house muttering to himself:

“The proudest, most disagreeable girl I ever met. Even her great beauty vanishes from your memory, the second you see that scornful look in her eyes.”

“There’s iron, they say, in all our blood,
And a grain or two perhaps is good;
But his, he makes me harshly feel,
Has got a little too much of steel.”

“Margaret!” Mr. Hale called, stepping back into the parlour after seeing their guest, Mr. Thornton, safely down the Milton staircase to the street. “I could not help watching your face no small amount of anxiety when he owned up to having worked as a shop-boy, you know. Mr. Bell told me that whole history long before we moved here, so I was braced for the confession, but half expected you’d leap straight up and quit the room out of shock.”

“Oh papa, you can’t truly think me that silly!” Margaret laughed, shaking her head, the faint furrow between her brows smoothing out. “I liked that part of his story better than anything else he said all evening. Every other word out of him reeked of that hard, sharp-edged Milton grit that rubs one the wrong way—except for that bit, when he spoke of his youth so simply, no trace of the showy pretence that makes so many tradespeople vulgar, with such tender, unshakable respect for his mother. I was far less inclined to leave then than I was when he was boasting of Milton as if no other town on earth could hold a candle to it, or coolly dismissing the working people around him as lazy, improvident wastrels without ever once seeming to think it his duty to lift them up, to give them even a fraction of the stern, loving training his own mother gave him, the thing that built all his success, whatever that success may be. No—his admission he’d been a shop boy was the very best thing he said all night.”

“I am surprised at you, Margaret,” her mother cut in, fanning herself lightly, her expression tight with lingering disapproval. “You who were forever poking fun at people for being ‘too shoppy’ back at Helstone! I do not think you did quite right, Mr. Hale, introducing a man like this to us without a single warning of his origins. I was half-convinced I could not hide how horrified I was at some of his stories. His father ‘dying in miserable circumstances’? Why, he could well have perished in the workhouse.”

“I am not at all sure it was not worse than the workhouse,” Mr. Hale replied gently. “Mr. Bell told me the whole of his history long before we came to Milton. He shared a sliver of it tonight, so I will fill in the gaps. His father gambled wildly with other people’s money, speculating far beyond his means, failed spectacularly, and killed himself rather than bear the public disgrace. Every old acquaintance of the family shrank away when the full extent of his dishonest, desperate gambles to win back his lost fortune came out. No one stepped forward to help the widow, this boy, and their little daughter, then too young to earn a penny. Mrs. Thornton is not the sort of woman to sit around waiting for tardy charity to find her, you may be sure. They vanished from Milton for years. We know he took a job behind a shop counter, and his wages, plus a tiny fragment of property his mother had managed to secure away from the creditors, kept the four of them alive for years. Mr. Bell swears they survived almost entirely on water porridge for months on end, no one quite knows how. Long after the creditors had written off every last penny of the old man’s debts—if they ever held out any hope of being repaid at all, after the suicide—this young man walked back into Milton, and went quietly round to every single creditor, handing them the first instalment of the money he owed them. No grand announcements, no gathering of crowds, no fanfare. He worked and paid every last penny of it off, all the way to the end, and it was that quiet, unyielding integrity that led one of the old creditors, a gruff, stubborn old man, to take him on as a business partner. That is how he built his fortune.”

“That is fine, truly,” Margaret said, her eyes bright. “What a pity a nature so noble has been warped by his life as a Milton manufacturer, though.”

“Warped how?” her father asked.

“Oh papa, everything he touches he measures by the standard of wealth! He spoke of all the new steam-powered machinery in his mill as nothing more than a new way to expand trade and make more money. And the poor labourers all around him—he sees their poverty as proof they are shiftless, outside the reach of his sympathy, simply because they do not have that same iron-hard will he has that let him drag himself out of ruin.”

“He never said vicious, Margaret. His words were improvident, and self-indulgent,” Mr. Hale corrected softly.

Margaret was gathering up her mother’s sewing supplies to tidy away for the night, ready to head up to bed, when she paused in the doorway. She wanted to say something that would please her father, even if it held a small sting of honesty. “Papa, I do think Mr. Thornton a very remarkable man. But personally? I do not like him at all.”

“And I do!” her father laughed. “Personally, as you call it, and all. I do not hail him as a hero, far from it. But goodnight, child. Your mother looks so very tired tonight, Margaret.”

Margaret had noticed her mother’s jaded, drawn face with quiet anxiety for days now, and that small remark sent her up the stairs with a heavy, dim fear settled in her chest like lead. Life in Milton was nothing like the soft, open-air days Mrs. Hale had known at Helstone, where she could wander out among the trees and fields whenever she pleased. The air here was thick with coal smoke, no trace of that sharp, revivifying freshness that had always revived her. Sordid, unaccustomed domestic worries crowded in on all of them, and there was every reason to fear her mother’s health was fraying badly at the edges. There were other small, worrying signs, too. Mrs. Hale and Dixon held long, hush-hush consultations behind closed bedroom doors, and Dixon would emerge red-eyed and cross, as was her habit when her mistress’s distress pulled at all her sympathies. Once Margaret had stepped into the bedroom right after Dixon left, and found her mother on her knees, so quiet Margaret almost did not see her. As she slipped back out to give her privacy, she caught half a dozen words, a quiet prayer for strength and patience to bear whatever sharp, constant pain she was suffering through.

Margaret ached to rebuild the intimate, trusting bond that had frayed during her long years staying with Aunt Shaw in London. She tried every soft caress, every gentle word, to worm her way back into the warmest, most secure spot in her mother’s heart. Though her mother returned every bit of that affection, hugging her close and murmuring sweet old names she had not heard in years, Margaret could feel there was a secret being kept from her—one that bore directly on her mother’s fragile health. She lay awake for hours that night, planning how to soften the harshness of their Milton life for her mother. She would find a second servant to take permanent work alongside Dixon, even if she spent every waking hour searching for one. Then, at the very least, her mother would have all the personal care she was used to, the care she had never gone without a single day before they moved to this smoky town.

For days after that, Margaret’s whole time and attention was swallowed up visiting servant registries, interviewing one unsuitable candidate after another, barely finding a single one even halfway up to the work. One afternoon, walking home from a particularly unpromising interview, she spotted Bessy Higgins on the pavement, and stopped to speak to her.

“Bessy! How are you? I hope you are feeling better, now the wind has shifted away from that sharp east blow that was making everyone ill.”

“Better and no better, if yo’ know what that means,” Bessy coughed, a faint, tired smile tugging at her lips.

“I am not sure I do,” Margaret said, smiling back.

“I’m better off for not being torn to pieces by coughing every hour o’ the night, but I’m weary to the bone of Milton, and I’m longing so bad to get away to the land of Beulah that every time I think I’m further than ever from it, my heart sinks right down, and I’m worse than I was before.”

Margaret turned to walk beside her, keeping pace with the girl’s slow, faltering steps home. She was quiet for a minute or two before she spoke, low and soft. “Bessy—do you wish to die?” She herself clung so fiercely to every small, bright part of life, young and healthy as she was, that the thought of welcoming death felt alien to her.

Bessy was silent for a minute, too. Then she said, quiet, “If yo’d led the life I’ve led, and grown as weary of it as I am, and thought sometimes ‘maybe this’ll drag on for fifty, sixty years, it does for some folks’—and got dizzy and dazed, sick at the thought of each one of those sixty years spinning around you, mocking you with all its endless hours and minutes, all that endless empty time—oh wench! I tell thee, thou’d be right glad when the doctor told thee he feared thou’d never see another winter.”

“Why Bessy, what has your life been, to make you feel that way?”

“Nought worse than many others’, I reckon. Only I fretted against it, and them others didn’t.”

“Tell me, then. I am a stranger here, you know, so I may not catch all the little unspoken things that people who grew up in Milton take for granted.”

“If yo’d come to our house when yo’ said yo’ would, I might ha’ told you long ago. But father says yo’re just like the rest of them—out of sight, out of mind, the second yo’ get busy with your own folk.”

“I don’t know who the rest are, but I have been so busy, and I will own it—for a little while I truly forgot my promise,” Margaret said honestly. “I would have remembered it again, the moment I was less swamped. May I come home with you now?”

Bessy flicked a quick, sharp glance up at her face, to see if the offer was sincere. The hard, wary glint in her eye melted into soft, wistful longing when she met Margaret’s gentle, open gaze. “I’ve not got many to care for me. If yo’ care to come, yo’ may.”

They walked on in silence together. When they turned off the squalid main street into a narrow, cramped little court of worker’s cottages, Bessy spoke again. “Don’t be daunted if father’s at home, and speaks gruff at first.
