He took a right fancy to yo’, you see, and thought so much of your promise to come visit, that he was vexed and put out when yo’ didn’t show, just because he liked you so.”

“Don’t fear, Bessy,” Margaret said, squeezing her arm gently.

Nicholas Higgins was not at home when they stepped through the door. A tall, rawboned girl, younger than Bessy but far stronger, was slamming things around the wash tub, banging furniture and making such a racket that Margaret’s heart ached for Bessy, who sank straight onto the first chair she saw, as if every last scrap of her strength had run out the second she crossed the threshold. Margaret asked the sister for a cup of water, and while she ran to fetch it—knocking over the fire irons and tripping over a chair in her hurry—Margaret untied the strings of Bessy’s bonnet, letting her draw deep, unobstructed breaths.

“Do you think a life like this is worth caring for at all?” Bessy gasped out, finally.

Margaret did not speak, just held the cup of cool water to her lips. Bessy drank a long, feverish draught, then leaned back and shut her eyes. Margaret heard her murmur, half to herself: “They shall hunger no more, neither thirst any more: neither shall the sun light on them, nor any heat.”

Margaret leaned over her, her voice soft. “Bessy, do not be impatient with whatever life has given you. Remember who gave it to you, who made it exactly what it is!”

She nearly jumped out of her skin when she heard Nicholas Higgins speak right behind her. He had come in so quietly she had not heard the door open.

“Now then, I’ll not have my lass preached at. She’s bad enough as it is, with her dreams, and her Methodist fancies, and her visions of cities with golden gates and precious stones. If it amuses her I let it be, but I’ll not have no more of that stuff poured into her ear.”

“Surely,” Margaret said, turning to face him, “you must believe that God gave her life, and ordained exactly what kind of life it would be?”

“I believe what I can see with my own two eyes, and nothing more. That’s my creed, young woman. I don’t believe half the things I hear, not by a long shot. I heard a young lass making a great fuss about knowing where we lived, and swearing she’d come visit us. And my lass there thought of it day and night, blushing every time she heard a strange step on the pavement, hoping it was you. But you’re here at last—and you’re welcome, so long as you don’t go preaching about things you know nothing at all about.”

Bessy had been watching Margaret’s face, and she half sat up now, laying a thin, trembling hand on Margaret’s arm in a gesture of entreaty. “Don’t be vexed with him. Half the folk in Milton think just like him. If yo’ heard all they say, you’d never be shocked at him. He’s a rare good man, is father—but oh!” she fell back against the chair, her voice breaking with despair, “the things he says sometimes make me long to die even more, because I’m so full of questions, so tossed about with wondering at all the things I don’t understand.”

“Poor lass, poor old lass,” Higgins said, his gruff voice softening. “I’m loath to vex thee, I truly am. But a man has to speak the truth, and when I see the whole world going mad these days, fussing over things they know not a single thing about, and leaving all the messy, broken things right in front of their own doors all untended—why I say, leave off all this talk about religion, and set to work fixing the things you can see. That’s my creed. It’s simple, it’s close to hand, it’s not hard to live by.”

But Bessy only pleaded harder with Margaret. “Don’t think hard of him, he’s a good man, he truly is. Sometimes I think I’ll be sad even in the City of God, if father’s not there with me.” A bright, feverish flush rose to her cheeks, her eyes blazing bright. “But you will be there, father! You shall! Oh—!” She clapped a hand to her chest, and her face went ghastly pale.

Margaret caught her, cradling the tired, limp head against her bosom, brushing the fine thin hair back from her temples and dabbing at her forehead with cool water. Nicholas knew every little sign of her distress as fast as a parent can, and even the rough, clumsy younger sister moved with gentle, careful steps the second Margaret whispered “hush.” The spasm, that small warning of how near death Bessy was, passed after a minute, and Bessy stirred, lifting her head.

“I’ll go to bed, it’s the best place for me now,” she said, but her fingers curled tight around the hem of Margaret’s gown. “But yo’ll come again, I know yo’ will—just say it, promise me.”

“I will come see you tomorrow,” Margaret said.

Bessy leaned back against her father, who lifted her carefully to carry her up the narrow staircase to their garret bedroom. As Margaret turned to leave, Higgins lingered at the bottom of the steps, struggling to find the words to say something. “I wish there was a God, y’know. If only to ask Him to bless thee.”

Margaret walked home heavy-hearted, lost in thought, far later than she usually was. At Helstone, unpunctuality at teatime had been one of Mrs. Hale’s greatest pet peeves, but now that small rule, and a dozen other little old household strictures, had faded away completely. Margaret almost found herself longing for those old familiar complaints.

“Did you find a suitable servant, dear?” her mother asked when she walked in the door.

“No, mamma. That Anne Buckley we saw today would never do at all.”

“Suppose I try,” Mr. Hale put in, smiling. “Everyone else has had a turn at this great difficulty. Let me take a crack at it. I might turn out to be the Cinderella who slips the slipper on the right foot, after all.”

Margaret could barely even muster a smile at the little joke, so weighed down as she was by her visit to the Higgins cottage. “What would you do, papa? How would you even start?”

“Why, I would ask some respectable, well-established house-mother to recommend a girl she and her own staff know to be reliable.”

“That’s a very good plan, but first we have to catch our ‘house-mother’,” Margaret said.

“You have caught her. Or rather, she is walking right into our trap, and you will win her over tomorrow, if you’re at all clever.”

“What on earth do you mean, Mr. Hale?” Mrs. Hale asked, her curiosity piqued.

“My paragon pupil, as Margaret calls Mr. Thornton,” Mr. Hale said, “told me that his mother intends to call on Mrs. and Miss Hale tomorrow.”

“Mrs. Thornton!” Mrs. Hale exclaimed.

“The mother he spoke of the other night?” Margaret asked, her head tilting up.

“Mrs. Thornton, the only mother he has, as far as I know,” Mr. Hale said, calm as ever. “I should very much like to meet her. She must be a most uncommon woman,” Mrs. Hale added, her eyes lighting up. “Perhaps she has a relative looking for work who would suit us perfectly, and would be glad of the post. She sounded to be such a careful, economical person, I would be happy to take on anyone from her family circle.”

“My dear,” Mr. Hale said, alarmed. “Pray do not get your hopes up about that. I suspect

“Well—I suppose we must.”

Mr. Thornton had struggled a little to coax his mother into the proper frame of mind for the polite call he’d been urging. She rarely paid social visits, and when she did, she carried out the duty with the heavy, stately formality of someone performing an unavoidable ritual. Her son had long given her a carriage, but she refused to let him keep horses stabled for it year-round: they were hired only for those rare, solemn occasions when she went out to pay morning or evening calls. Only a fortnight prior she’d kept hired horses for three full days, clearing every last social acquaintance off her visit list, so that they might now take their turn putting themselves to the trouble and expense of calling on her.

But Crampton was far too distant to walk, and she had pressed her son repeatedly to confirm that his wish for her to call on the new Hales was strong enough to justify the cost of a cab ride. She would have been no small amount relieved if he’d backed down, for as she said, “I see no use in striking up intimacies with every last teacher and schoolmaster in Milton. Next you’ll be asking me to call on Fanny’s dancing master’s wife, mark my words.”

“And so I would, mother, if the Masons were as friendless and adrift in a strange town as the Hales are now,” John replied evenly.

“Oh, don’t be so hasty. I’ll go tomorrow, never you mind. I only wanted to be quite clear on just how much this matters to you.”

“If you’re going tomorrow, I’ll send word to have the horses brought round,” he said.

“Nonsense, John. Anyone would think you were rolling in gold.”

“Not quite yet, but the horses are non-negotiable. Last time you took a common cab home, you came in with a throbbing headache from all the jolting over Milton’s cobblestones.”

“I never once complained about it, I’m sure.”

“No, my mother is not one for complaining,” he said, a quiet note of pride in his voice. “Which is all the more reason I owe it to you to look after your comfort. As for Fanny, a little bit of hardship would do her a world of good.”

“She is not made of the same tough stuff as you are, John. She could never bear it,” Mrs. Thornton said, and fell silent. Her last words had touched on a subject that mortified her: she held an unconscious, quiet contempt for weak character, and Fanny was soft exactly where her mother and brother were unyielding. Mrs. Thornton was no woman for long, winding arguments with herself; her sharp judgment and firm will served her far better than any overlong deliberation, and she knew instinctively that nothing would ever toughen Fanny up to endure hardship patiently, or face difficulty bravely. Though the admission stung, it only filled her with a pitying, tender softness towards her daughter, the same careful tenderness most mothers reserve for a sickly, delicate child.

A careless stranger might have thought she loved Fanny far more than she loved John, for all the pet names—dear, love, dearest—were saved for her daughter alone. But that stranger would have been deeply mistaken. The unflinching honesty with which mother and son spoke hard, unpalatable truths to each other spoke of a rock-solid trust in the strength of each other’s core, far more secure a bond than the over-anxious softness Mrs. Thornton lavished on Fanny. She was quietly ashamed of how little her daughter possessed the very grit she valued highest in the world, and that shame betrayed the fact that her affection for Fanny had no firm, unshakable resting place, the way her pride in John did. Day and night she gave quiet thanks for him, and walked prouder than any other woman in Milton on his account.

“Fanny, dear,” she said, turning to her daughter. “We’ll have the horses to the carriage today to call on these Hales. Why don’t you pop in to see Nurse while I pay my visit? It’s on the same route, and she’s always so glad to see you. You can stay with her while I’m with Mrs. Hale.”

“Oh mamma, it’s such a terribly long way, and I feel so tired already.”

“Tired of what?” Mrs. Thornton asked, her brow tightening slightly.

“I don’t know—the weather, I think. It’s so relaxing, oppressive, like. Couldn’t you send for Nurse to come here instead? The carriage can fetch her, and she can spend the rest of the day with me, I know she’d love that.”

Mrs. Thornton laid her work down on the table, considering. “It would be a long walk back for her at night, after dark,” she said at last.

“Oh, I’ll send her home in a cab, of course. I’d never make her walk.”

Just then Mr. Thornton stepped in, on his way out to the mill. “Mother, I don’t need to tell you, if there’s any small comfort you can offer Mrs. Hale as an invalid, you’ll be sure to offer it, I know.”

“I will, if I can think of what it might be. But I’ve never been ill a day in my life, so I’m no expert on the little fancies sick people like.”

“Well, Fanny’s the one who’s rarely without some small ailment,” John said, nudging his sister lightly. “You’ll be full of little ideas for Mrs. Hale, won’t you, Fan?”

“I am not always ailing,” Fanny snapped, pouting. “And I’m not going with mamma today. I’ve got a headache, and I shan’t stir out of the house.”

Mr. Thornton’s face fell. His mother’s eyes stayed fixed on her stitching, her needle moving fast across the cloth.

“Fanny, I wish you to come,” he said, his tone firm and authoritative. “It will do you far more good than harm. You will oblige me by going, no more discussion needed.”

He left the room abruptly before she could protest. If he’d stayed a minute longer, Fanny would have burst into tears at the sharpness of his command, even soft as it was with those words “you will oblige me.” Left behind, she grumbled under her breath.

“John always speaks as if I’m making up illnesses for no reason. Who even are these Hales, that he makes such a fuss over?”

“Fanny, do not speak of your brother that way. He would not ask us to go if he did not have good cause. Go and get your things on at once.”

The small squabble between her son and daughter did nothing to soften Mrs. Thornton’s feelings towards these strangers, the Hales. A small, jealous part of her echoed Fanny’s question, over and over, like a persistent refrain: Who are they, that he is so determined we should heap all this attention on them? The thought lingered long after Fanny had forgotten all about it, distracted by the pleasant thrill of checking her new bonnet’s reflection in the glass.

Mrs. Thornton was shy of new company. She had only gained enough leisure to move in society in the last few years, and she took no enjoyment in casual calls. She loved hosting dinner parties, and criticising other people’s dinners, but walking into a stranger’s home to strike up an acquaintance felt awkward and unnatural, and she looked sterner and more forbidding than usual as she stepped across the threshold of the Hales’ small drawing room.

Margaret was bent over a piece of fine cambric, embroidering a little garment for Edith’s coming baby, work Mrs. Thornton immediately judged as flimsy and useless. She approved far more of Mrs. Hale’s steady double knitting, that was sensible work that served a purpose. The whole room was cluttered with tiny knick-knacks that must take hours to dust, and time for a household on a tight income was as precious as money, she thought to herself, as she went through all the expected polite small talk with Mrs. Hale in her stately, formal way.

Mrs. Hale was putting far more effort into her conversation than she usually bothered with, captivated by the wide band of ancient hand-made point lace around Mrs. Thornton’s collar. “That lace,” she later told Dixon, “is old English point, the kind no one has made for seventy whole years, the kind you can’t buy in any shop. It must be an heirloom—shows she has proper old ancestors, after all.” So the wearer of the heirloom lace earned far more than the lazy, half-hearted politeness Mrs. Hale usually extended to new visitors, and before long the two women were deep in the endless, familiar subject of the failings of household servants.

“I suppose you are not musical,” Fanny said to Margaret, “I don’t see a piano anywhere.”

“I love listening to good music, but I don’t play very well myself. Papa and mamma never cared much for it, so we sold our old piano before we left home to move here.”

“I can scarcely imagine existing without one. It feels like an absolute necessity of life to me.”

“Fifteen shillings a week, and three pence saved out of it to feed a whole family,” Margaret thought to herself, and a faint, unspoken coldness crept into her manner. “I believe you have fine concerts here in Milton, though.”

“Oh yes! Delicious, they are. Only problem is they’re far too crowded— the directors let absolutely anyone buy a ticket. But you’re guaranteed to hear all the newest music in London there. The day after every concert I go straight to Johnson’s music shop and place a huge order for all the new pieces.”

“Do you like new music just for the sake of its newness, then?”

“Oh, you know if it’s being performed here, it’s all the rage in London, or the singers would never bother to bring it north. You’ve lived in London, of course, haven’t you?”

“Yes,” Margaret said. “I lived there for several years.”

“Oh! London and the Alhambra are the two places in the whole world I long to see!”

“London and the Alhambra?”

“Yes! Ever since I read *Tales of the Alhambra*. You’ve read that, haven’t you?”

“I don’t think I have. But surely London is a very easy trip from here.”

“Yes, but somehow,” Fanny whispered, lowering her voice, “mamma has never been to London herself, and she can’t understand why I long to go. She’s absurdly proud of Milton, dirty, smoky old thing as I think it is. I believe she likes it more precisely because of the smoke and the grime.”

“If Milton has been your mother’s home for all these years, I can perfectly understand her loving it,” Margaret said, in her clear, bell-like voice.

“What were you saying about me, Miss Hale? May I ask?”

Margaret was caught off guard, and fumbled for a response, but Fanny cut in before she could speak. “Oh mamma, we were just saying we can’t think of why you love Milton so much!”

“Thank you,” Mrs. Thornton said, dry as dust. “I hardly think my natural affection for the town I was born and raised in, that has been my home for decades now, requires any special explaining away.”

Margaret was vexed. It did sound, after Fanny’s clumsy phrasing, as if they had been gossiping impertinently about Mrs. Thornton’s private feelings, and she resented the sharp, offended edge to the older woman’s tone.

After a short pause, Mrs. Thornton went on. “Do you know anything of Milton, Miss Hale? Have you seen any of our factories? Our grand, magnificent warehouses?”

“No,” Margaret said. “I have not seen anything of that sort yet.” She felt that to pretend she cared a whit for such sights would be dishonest, so she added, “I dare say papa would have taken me long ago if I’d had any interest. But I really do not take much pleasure in touring manufactories.”

“They are very curious places,” Mrs. Hale put in gently, “but there is always so much noise and dirt. I remember once I went to see candles being made wearing a brand new lilac silk gown, and it was utterly ruined beyond repair.”

“Very likely,” Mrs. Thornton said, short and displeased. “I merely thought, as new residents of a town that has risen to national fame on the back of its industry and progress, that you might care to visit the sites where that work is done—places that are unique across the whole country, I’ve been told. If Miss Hale changes her mind and condescends to be curious about Milton’s manufactures, I shall be very happy to arrange access to print works, or reed-making workshops, or the simpler spinning operations in my son’s mill. Every single new improvement to textile machinery is on show there, in its most perfect form.”

“I am so glad you do not like mills and manufactories and all that horrid sort of thing,” Fanny whispered to Margaret, as she rose to follow her mother, who was taking her dignified leave of Mrs. Hale in a rustle of silk.

“If I were you, I think I should very much like to learn all about them,” Margaret replied, quiet and steady.

As they drove back through the smoky streets, Fanny’s mother turned to her. “We will be polite to these Hales, but do not go rushing into one of your silly hasty friendships with the daughter. I can see she will do you no good at all. The mother looks very ill, and seems a quiet, nice enough sort of person.”

“I don’t want to be friends with Miss Hale at all, mamma,” Fanny pouted. “I was only talking to her and trying to amuse her because I thought it was my duty.”

“Well, no matter,” Mrs. Thornton said. “At least John will be fully satisfied now.”

“That doubt and trouble, fear and pain,
And anguish, all, are shadows vain,
That death itself shall not remain;
That weary deserts we may tread,
A dreary labyrinth may thread.
Thro’ dark ways underground be led;
Yet, if we will one Guide obey,
The dreariest path, the darkest way
Shall issue out in heavenly day;
And we, on divers shores now cast,
Shall meet, our perilous voyage past,
All in our Father’s house at last!”

The moment their last visitor stepped off the Hale threshold, Margaret flew up the stairs, her skirts swishing around her ankles, to yank on her bonnet and wool shawl, desperate to reach the Higgins cottage before dinner and sit a while by Bessy’s side. As she navigated the narrow, thrumming, smoke-hazed streets of Milton, she realized how much more meaning those grim, row-lined lanes held now that she cared for one of the people who called them home—they were no longer just a strange, uninviting backdrop to her family’s displacement, but spaces where lives of quiet struggle unfolded.

Mary Higgins, Bessy’s slatternly but well-meaning younger sister, had done her best to tidy the small cottage in anticipation of Margaret’s visit. She’d scrubbed a rough patch of clean stone in the center of the floor, though the flagstones pressed under the chairs and tables, and those running along the walls, still bore their dark, unwashed patina of years of factory soot. Though the day hung heavy and hot, a great roaring fire blazed in the grate, turning the tiny room into a sweltering oven. Margaret did not guess that Mary’s lavish pile of coal was a deliberate gesture of welcoming hospitality, and assumed the oppressive heat was being kept up for Bessy’s comfort. The sick girl herself lay stretched on a short squab sofa tucked under the only window, far frailer than she had been the day before, worn out from straining to lift herself at every passing footstep, craning to see if Margaret had finally arrived. Now that she was here, Bessy sank back against the cushions, content to gaze at Margaret’s face and brush her fingers over the fine weave of her visitor’s dress, with the soft, unselfconscious admiration of a child.

“I never understood why folk in the Bible made such a fuss over soft raiment before,” she murmured, her voice thin. “But it must be lovely to go dressed like you do. It’s nothing like the rough common wear I’ve always known. Most fine folks’ bright gaudy colours tire my eyes right out, but yours, somehow, rest them. Where did you get that gown?”

“In London,” Margaret laughed softly, settling her chair closer to the squab.

“London? You’ve lived in London?”
“Yes. We were there for years, before we moved to Helstone—our old country home, deep in the middle of a forest.”
“Tell me about it,” Bessy said, leaning back, closing her eyes, and lacing her hands over her breast as if to cradle every word Margaret offered.

Margaret had barely spoken of Helstone since they’d left it, save for offhand mentions when the place slipped unbidden into her thoughts; she dreamed of it more vividly than her waking life, and her memory wandered its sun-dappled paths every night before she fell asleep. But her heart opened fully to this sick girl at her side. “Oh, Bessy, I loved that home more than I can say. I wish you could see it. I can’t tell you half its beauty. Great old trees stand all around it, their branches stretching long and level to make a deep, restful shade even at noon. And though every leaf looks perfectly still, there’s a soft, rushing sound of wind moving through them, always, far enough away that it never crowds you. The turf there is sometimes as soft and fine as velvet, and sometimes thick and lush with the constant trickle of a little hidden brook that tinkles just out of sight. Then there are wide stretches of rolling ferns, some deep in the green shadow, others streaked with long swathes of golden sunlight—they look just like the sea.”

“I’ve never seen the sea,” Bessy whispered. “Go on.”

“And then there are wide commons, high up, so that you stand above the very tops of all the trees—”
“I’m glad of that. I always feel smothered, down below. When I get a rare hour off work, I run as high as I can climb, just so I can see far away, and take a deep breath of that full, open air. I get smothered enough in Milton. That soft noise of the trees you talk of, going on forever and ever? It would never make my head ache the way the mill’s roar does. I guess on those commons there’s hardly any noise at all?”
“Nothing but a lark, high up in the sky sometimes,” Margaret assured her. “Once in a while I’d hear a farmer shouting sharp and loud at his servants, but it was so far away it just felt nice, like a little reminder that other people were hard at work somewhere distant, while I sat on the heather and did nothing at all.”

“I used to think, once, that if I could just have one whole day of doing nothing, just to rest—one day in a quiet place like that—maybe it would heal me right up. But now I’ve had weeks and weeks of idleness, and I’m just as tired of them as I was of my work. Sometimes I’m so worn out I think I can’t even enjoy heaven unless I get a proper piece of rest first. I’m half afraid to go straight there without a good long sleep in the grave to set me up right.”

“Don’t be afraid, Bessy,” Margaret said, laying her hand over the girl’s. “God can give you a more perfect rest than any idle day on earth, or even the deep sleep of the grave, ever could.”

Bessy shifted restlessly on the cushions. “I wish father wouldn’t talk to me the way he does. He means well, I’ve told you that before, and I’ll say it a hundred times over. But you see, even if I don’t believe a word of what he says by daylight, when the fever’s on me at night, half asleep and half awake, all those dark thoughts come crashing back on me—oh, so bad! I think, what if this is all there is? What if the whole point of me being born is just to work my heart and my life away, to sicken in this dreary place, with the mill’s noise ringing in my ears forever, until I want to scream just to make it stop and let me have one little bit of quiet—with the cotton fluff filling my lungs until I’m thirsting to death for one long deep breath of that clear air you talk about? And my mother’s gone, and I never got to tell her how much I loved her, never got to tell her half my troubles. If this life is all there is, and there’s no God to wipe all tears away—you wench, you!” she cried, sitting bolt upright and clutching Margaret’s hand tight, almost fiercely, “I could go mad, I could kill you, I swear I could.” She fell back then, completely spent by the force of her outburst.

Margaret knelt down beside the squab. “Bessy—we have a Father in Heaven.”

“I know it! I know it,” Bessy moaned, turning her head from side to side restlessly. “I’m so wicked. I spoke so wickedly. Oh, don’t be frightened of me, and never come back again. I wouldn’t harm a hair on your head. And,” she added, opening her eyes to look straight and earnestly at Margaret, “maybe I even believe more of what’s coming than you do. I’ve read the Book of Revelations so many times I know it off by heart, and I never doubt, when I’m awake and in my right senses, all the glory I’m going to see someday.”

“Let’s not talk about the fancies that come to you when you’re feverish,” Margaret said gently. “I’d much rather hear about what you used to do when you were well.”

“I think I was still well when mother died, but I never was rightly strong after that. I went to work in the carding room not long after, and the fluff got into my lungs and poisoned me.”

“Fluff?” Margaret asked, confused.

“Fluff,” Bessy repeated. “Little tiny bits that fly off the cotton when they’re combing it out, fill the whole room until the air looks like fine white dust. They say it wraps itself around your lungs and tightens them up. Either way, there’s dozens and dozens of folks who work in the carding rooms that waste away, coughing and spitting blood, poisoned right by that fluff.”

“But can’t anything be done to stop it?” Margaret asked, aghast.

“I don’t know. Some masters have a great big wheel at one end of the carding room that makes a draught, and pulls all the dust out of the air. But that wheel costs a fortune—five or six hundred pounds, maybe, and it don’t make any profit. So barely a handful of masters will put them up. And I’ve heard of men who didn’t want to work in the mills that had them, said that after years of swallowing fluff, going without it made them ravenously hungry, and that their wages ought to be raised if they had to work in places with those wheels. So between the masters not wanting to spend the money, and the men not wanting to work the new way, the wheels never get put up. But I wish to God our factory had one.”

“Didn’t your father know all this?” Margaret asked.

“He did, and he was sorry. But our mill was one of the better ones, all in all, with a steady set of workers. Father was scared to let me go work at some strange place, and though you’d never guess to look at me now, everyone used to call me a proper, fine lass back then. And I didn’t want to be thought soft and useless. And mother said Mary’s schooling had to be paid for, and father was always buying books, going to lectures of one kind and another—all of that costs money. So I just kept on working, until I’ll never get that whirr of the mill out of my ears, or the fluff out of my throat, not in this world. That’s all.”

“How old are you, Bessy?”
“Nineteen, come July.”
“I’m nineteen too.” Margaret’s throat tightened, her heart heavy with the terrible, unfair contrast between their two lives. She couldn’t speak for a moment, fighting down the swell of tears.

“About Mary,” Bessy said, after a pause. “I wanted to ask you to be a friend to her. She’s seventeen, the last of us, and I don’t want her going to work in the mill. But I don’t know what else she’s fit for.”

Margaret glanced unconsciously at the un scrubbed corners of the small room. “She could hardly take on a servant’s position just yet, could she? We have an old faithful servant, almost a member of the family, who’s looking for an assistant, but she’s very particular about how the house is kept. It wouldn’t be fair to her to send Mary over untrained, and make her lives harder for both of them.”

“No, I see you’re right. Our Mary’s a good girl, but who’s ever been there to teach her how to keep a house? No mother, and me at the mill until I was too sick for anything but snapping at her for doing badly the things I never even knew how to do right myself. But I still wish she could live with you, all the same.”

“But even if she’s not quite ready to come work for us as a servant right now—and I don’t know what may change in time—I will always try to be a friend to her, for your sake, Bessy. I have to go now, but I’ll come back as soon as I can. If I don’t make it tomorrow, or the next day, or even for a whole week or a fortnight, don’t think I’ve forgotten you. I might be busy with things at home.”

“I know you won’t forget me. I won’t mistrust you ever again. But remember—by the time a week or a fortnight passes, I might be dead and buried.”

“I’ll come back as soon as I can, Bessy,” Margaret said, squeezing her hand tight. “And you’ll send someone to let me know if you get any worse, won’t you?”

“Ay, that I will,” Bessy said, pressing her hand back weakly.

From that day forward, Mrs. Hale grew more and more of a suffering invalid. The anniversary of Edith’s marriage was drawing near, and looking back over the whole heap of troubles the past year had piled at her feet, Margaret wondered how they had all managed to bear it. If she could have foreseen every last one of them, she would have shrunk away and hidden herself from the coming time in terror. But day by day, each one had been bearable on its own; small, bright, little sparks of quiet joy had sparkled right in the middle of all her sorrows. A year before, back when they first arrived in Milton, and Margaret had first grown quietly aware of the new fretfulness in her mother’s temper, she would have groaned bitterly at the thought of her mother bearing a long, painful illness in a strange, grim, noisy busy place, with every possible comfort of their old Helstone home stripped away. But now, as her mother’s real pain grew worse, a new, gentle patience had risen up in her. She was softer and quieter through even her worst bouts of bodily suffering, almost in exact proportion to how restless and fretful she had been in the months before, when there had been no real cause for grief at all.

Mr. Hale, for his part, was in exactly that stage of anxious denial that takes hold of men of his gentle, thoughtful temperament: deliberate, willful blindness. He grew more irritated than Margaret had ever seen him when she voiced her worries about her mother.

“Margaret, you are getting far too fanciful! Heaven knows I would be the very first to sound the alarm if your mother was truly unwell. Back in Helstone, we always knew the moment she had one of her headaches, even before she told us. Now look at her—she has a bright, healthy flush in her cheeks, just like she did when I first met her all those years ago.”

“But Papa,” Margaret said, hesitating, “I think that bright colour is the flush of pain.”

“Nonsense, Margaret. I tell you, you’re the one who’s not well. I’ll send for the doctor tomorrow for you, and then, if it will ease your silly mind, he can look in on your mother while he’s here.”

“Thank you, dear Papa. That will make me so much happier.” She stepped forward to kiss his cheek, but he pushed her away—gently, but firmly, as if her worries were unpleasant ideas he wanted to banish as quickly as he wanted her out of the room. He paced restlessly up and down the parlor floor. “Poor Maria,” he muttered half to himself. “I wish one could do what is right without hurting the people you love. I will hate this town, and hate myself too, if she—” He broke off, turning back to Margaret. “Tell me, does your mother often talk to you of the old places? Of Helstone, I mean?”

“No, Papa,” Margaret said, her voice sad.

“Then there, you see! She can’t be fretting for it, can she? It’s always been a comfort to me that your mother is so simple and open, that I know every little grievance she has the moment it troubles her. She would never hide anything serious that was hurting her health from me, would she, Margaret? I am quite sure she would never do such a thing. So don’t let me hear any more of these foolish, morbid ideas. Come, give me a kiss, and run off to bed.”

Margaret kissed him, and climbed the stairs to her room, but long after she had finished her slow, tired undressing, long after she had settled under her covers, she could still hear him pacing back and forth across the parlor carpet, his heavy, unsteady steps sounding through the quiet house, as restless as the unspoken worries he was doing his best to outrun.

“I was used
To sleep at nights as sweetly as a child,—
Now if the wind blew rough, it made me start,
And think of my poor boy tossing about
Upon the roaring seas. And then I seemed
To feel that it was hard to take him from me
For such a little fault.”

It was a small, soft comfort for Margaret Hale in those raw early Milton days to find her mother drawing closer to her with a tender, unguarded intimacy she had not known since Margaret’s childhood. Mrs Hale took her daughter to her heart at last as the trusted confidante Margaret had ached to be for years—long envying Dixon the small, affectionate private talks the housekeeper had always shared with her mistress. Margaret tended to every small call for sympathy her mother made, no matter how trivial the worry, with deliberate care: she fussed over draughts at the window, listened to rants about bad butter and unreliable tradesmen, and checked the flue for sparks at every gust of wind, just as an elephant, unaware of the tiny pin at his feet, will lift it gently when his keeper bids him. She did not know then that this quiet devotion was drawing her close to a moment of raw, unvarnished truth she had long yearned for.

One evening, when Mr Hale was out visiting a distant parishioner, the howl of the gale down their narrow street made Mrs Hale shiver, and she began to speak of Frederick— the subject Margaret had dared never ask after plainly, the one topic that made her naturally open tongue turn shy. The more she hungered for details of her exiled brother, the more afraid she had been to draw them out.

“Oh Margaret, that was a terrible wind last night, wasn’t it? It howled right down our bedroom chimney, loud enough to rattle the bed hangings. I never can sleep through winds like that. I got into the habit of waking fitfully when poor Frederick was at sea, and even now, if I don’t jolt straight awake at once, I dream of him stuck in the middle of a storm: the great glass-green walls of waves rearing on either side of his ship, higher than her very masts, curling over with cruel white foam like some gigantic crested serpent. It is an old, old dream, but it always comes back on windy nights, till I wake sitting bolt upright in bed, stiff with terror, my heart hammering. Poor Frederick! He’s on land now, so wind can do him no harm. Though I did half fear those tall Milton factory chimneys might topple right over in the gusts.”

“Where is he now, mamma?” Margaret asked softly. “I know all our letters are addressed care of Messrs Barbour in Cadiz, but I’ve never known the name of the place he’s hiding in.”

“I can’t keep the Spanish name straight in my head, but he doesn’t go by Hale, you must never forget that. Every letter he sends has F.D. scribbled in the corner of the envelope, and he uses the name Dickenson. I wanted him to take Beresford, my maiden name, the one he had a sort of family right to, but your father said it was far too risky. Someone might recognise the name, and trace it straight back to us.”

“Mamma,” Margaret said, “I was staying with Aunt Shaw when all that happened, and I was far too young to be told the full truth. But I’m old enough now. May I hear the whole story, if it doesn’t hurt you too much to speak of it?”

“Pain? No,” Mrs Hale replied, her cheek flushing hot with a mix of pride and grief. “It only hurts to think I may never see my darling boy again. But he did right, Margaret. They can say what they like in the papers, in the courts, but I have his own letters. I will believe my son before any court-martial on earth. Go to my little black japan cabinet by the window, dear, second drawer on the left, and you will find a packet of his letters tied up with blue silk ribbon.”

Margaret fetched them immediately. They were yellowed at the edges, stained with brine, carrying that sharp, salty, peculiar scent all letters that have spent months at sea carry. Mrs Hale untied the ribbon with trembling fingers, flipping through the dates as she passed them to Margaret, chattering out hurried, anxious little asides before her daughter could even finish reading the first line.

“You see, Margaret, he disliked Captain Reid from the very first day he stepped on board the Orion, his first ship as a midshipman. Poor little fellow, he looked so fine in his uniform, his dirk at his hip, slashing open every newspaper that came his way like it was nothing more than a paper knife. But Mr Reid, as he was then, took an instant dislike to the boy. Later, when Frederick was assigned to the Russell, he found his old enemy Reid made captain. He swore he would bear all the man’s tyranny patiently, you know. Look, that letter right there—he says ‘my father may rely upon me, that I will bear with all proper patience everything that one officer and gentleman can take from another. But from my former knowledge of my present captain, I confess I look forward with apprehension to a long course of tyranny on board the Russell.’ He meant it too. He was the sweetest-tempered boy alive, when no one was pushing him too far. That’s the letter where he writes about Reid flying into rages because the new crew of the Russell couldn’t run through their manoeuvres as fast as the Avenger’s men, isn’t it? He says the Russell was full of raw new hands, while the Avenger had been on station for three years straight, chasing slavers and working their crew till they scrambled up and down the rigging like rats.”

Margaret read the faded, half-illegible scrawl slowly. It was easy to see how Frederick, still hot from the scene he described, might have exaggerated Captain Reid’s petty cruelties a little. Reid had ordered the men aloft in the main-topsail rigging to race back down, threatening to flog the last man down with the cat-o’-nine tails. One sailor, stuck far out on the spar, knew he could never outpace the men ahead of him, and too terrified to bear the shame of a public beating, he threw himself wildly to catch a lower rope, missed, and fell senseless to the deck. He died only a few hours later, and the whole crew was seething with rage by the time Frederick sat down to write.

“We never got that letter till long, long after we saw the first news of the mutiny,” Mrs Hale said, her voice cracking. “Poor Fred, it must have been a comfort to write it, even when he didn’t know how he could ever get it sent home. Long before his letter arrived, we saw the notice in the papers: a brutal, atrocious mutiny had broken out on the Russell, the mutineers had seized the ship and were thought to have turned pirate, and Captain Reid had been set adrift in an open boat with a handful of loyal officers, all of whose names were printed, because they’d been picked up by a West Indian steamer. Oh Margaret! Your father and I turned sick going down that list, over and over, because Frederick Hale’s name wasn’t there. We thought it had to be a misprint. Fred was such a good, kind boy, only a little hot-headed. We hoped the ‘Carr’ printed in the list of mutineers was a typo for Hale—newspapers are so careless, after all. The next day, your father set off at dusk to walk all the way to Southampton to get the late post and the new papers, and I couldn’t bear to wait at home, so I set off to meet him. I sat down under the hedge by the Oldham beech-tree to wait, and he came at last, hours late, his arms hanging loose at his sides, his head sunk, dragging his feet like every step cost him all his strength. I can see him right now, as plain as day.”

“Don’t go on, mamma,” Margaret whispered, leaning against her mother’s side to kiss her hand. “I can guess the rest.”

“No you can’t. No one can who wasn’t there. I could barely stand up to go to him, the whole world was spinning around me. He didn’t even speak, or seem surprised to find me three miles from home in the dark. He just tucked my arm in his, and stroked my hand over and over, like he was trying to soothe me to stay quiet through some terrible blow. When I shook so hard I couldn’t speak, he pulled me into his arms, rested his head on mine, and began to shake with that strange, muffled, groaning cry that scared me so still I could only beg him to tell me what he had heard. Then he handed me that wicked newspaper, all crumpled at the edges, that called our Frederick a ‘traitor of the blackest dye,’ a ‘base ungrateful disgrace to his profession.’ I can’t even repeat all the vile names they called him. I tore that paper to tiny bits, Margaret. I tore it with my teeth, I think. I didn’t cry. My cheeks were burning hot as fire, my eyes stung like they were full of smoke. I told him it was all lies, and it was. Months later, Fred’s letter finally arrived, and you see what provocation he had. He didn’t rebel for his own wrongs. He spoke up for the poor, helpless sailors Reid was torturing to death, and the rest of the crew all stuck by him.” She paused for a long minute, her voice thin and trembling, exhausted from the weight of the memory. “I’m glad of it, you know. I’m prouder of Frederick standing up against injustice than I would ever be if he’d just been a quiet, obedient, unremarkable officer.”

“I am sure I am too,” Margaret said firmly. “Loyalty to wise, just authority is a fine thing, but it is far finer to defy arbitrary, cruel power, not for your own sake, but for the sake of others who cannot defend themselves.”

“Even so, I wish I could see him just once more. He was my first baby, Margaret.” Mrs Hale spoke wistfully, almost as if apologising for the hungry, yearning wish, as if it were a slight to Margaret, the child who was still here. But the thought never crossed Margaret’s mind. She was already wondering if there was any way that wish could come true.

“It’s six or seven years now, mamma. Would they still prosecute him if he came home and stood his trial? Surely he could bring forward evidence to show all the terrible provocation he had?”

“It would do no good,” Mrs Hale sighed, beginning to cry for the first time all evening. “Some of the sailors who went with Fred were caught, and court-martialled on board the Amicia. I believed every word of their defence, it matched Fred’s story word for word. But it didn’t matter. They were hanged at the yard-arm. And the worst part of it all? The court, when they sentenced them to death, said all those poor men had been led astray from their duty by their superior officer—by Frederick.”

They sat in heavy, thick silence for a long time after that.

“So he was in South America for several years, wasn’t he?” Margaret asked at last.

“Yes. Now he’s in Spain, near Cadiz somewhere. If he ever sets foot in England, he will be hanged. I shall never see his face again.” Mrs Hale turned her face to the wall, and lay perfectly still, sunk in the old, familiar despair of a mother who has lost her son to a cruel, unforgiving law. She pulled her hand gently out of Margaret’s, almost impatient for the quiet to be left alone with her memories of Fred. When Mr Hale came home an hour later, Margaret slipped out of the house, her chest tight with unnameable gloom, and could not see a single glimmer of light anywhere on the dark, foggy horizon.

> “Thought fights with thought; out springs a spark of truth
> From the collision of the sword and shield.”

“Margaret,” her father said the next day, “we must return Mrs. Thornton’s call. Your mother is far too unwell to walk the distance, and thinks she cannot manage it; so you and I will go that afternoon, the pair of us.”

As they set out, Mr. Hale began to speak of his wife’s health with a quiet, veiled anxiety Margaret had long hoped he would finally acknowledge.
“Did you send for a doctor, Margaret? Did you consult anyone?”
“No, papa. You talked of having a doctor come to see me, and I was well enough again soon after. But if I could only find a good, reliable physician, I would go and beg him to call today—for I am certain mamma is seriously unwell.”
She spoke plainly, laying the truth before him with firm gentleness, for he had firmly shut his mind against all her previous worries about his wife’s condition. This time, the news landed heavy. He answered in a despondent, trembling tone.
“Do you think she has some hidden complaint? That she is really very ill? Has Dixon said anything? Oh Margaret! I am haunted by the fear our move to Milton is killing her. My poor Maria.”
“Papa, don’t torment yourself with such fancies,” Margaret said, shocked. “She is unwell, that is all. Many people are laid low for a time, and with careful advice, they recover stronger than they were before.”
“But Dixon hasn’t hinted anything of the severity?”
“No. You know Dixon loves to make a grand mystery out of trifles; she has been a little secretive about mamma’s health, which alarmed me at first, but I daresay there is no real cause. You told me only the other day I was growing fanciful, after all.”
“I pray to God you are,” he said. “But do not think for a moment I meant what I said. I will not have you hiding your fears about your mother from me, no matter how fanciful they seem. We will ask Mrs. Thornton if she can recommend a first-rate doctor—we will not waste our money on some quack of no reputation. Now, we turn down this street.”

Marlborough Street stretched before them in long, uniform rows of tiny terraced cottages, broken only by stretches of blank, windowless wall. It seemed impossible a woman as tall, stately, and finely dressed as Mrs. Thornton could live in such a neighbourhood. Margaret had unconsciously imagined her home would match her own grand, imposing bearing.
“He assuredly told me he lived on Marlborough Street,” Mr. Hale said, frowning in confusion.
“Perhaps it is still one of his old thrifty habits, to live in a small house,” Margaret said. “But there are people about; let me ask.”
She stopped a passerby, who pointed them towards the end of the long blank wall, where the factory lodge door stood half-hidden beside great heavy iron gates, built for the passage of wagons and lorries carrying cotton in and finished cloth out. The lodge keeper let them through into a vast oblong yard: one side lined with business offices, the other dominated by the looming, many-windowed mill, where the endless clank of looms and the deep groaning roar of the steam engine thundered so loud it threatened to deafen anyone standing within the enclosure. Facing the street, along the short end of the yard, stood a handsome stone-faced house, blackened with decades of factory smoke, but every window, step, and coat of paint kept scrupulously, almost fiercely clean. It had been built some fifty or sixty years prior, its long narrow windows, twin flights of railed steps leading up to the front door, and heavy stone trim all marking its age. Margaret could not fathom why people who could afford such a fine, well-kept home would choose to live surrounded by the mill’s constant din, rather than a smaller, quieter house out in the country, or even a leafy suburb. Her ears, unaccustomed to industrial noise, could barely catch her father’s voice as they waited on the steps for the door to open.

When they stepped inside, the drawing room struck Margaret as eerily untouched, as if the furniture had been carefully wrapped and bagged in preparation for a volcanic eruption, waiting to be unearthed perfectly preserved a thousand years later. The walls were papered pale pink and gilded, the carpet printed with delicate bunches of flowers on an ivory ground, but its entire centre was covered by a stiff glazed linen drugget to shield it from smoky dust. Lace curtains hung at every window, every armchair and sofa draped with its own custom net or knitted cover. Great heavy alabaster figurines sat on every flat surface, sealed safely from dust under glass domes. A large circular table sat beneath the wrapped, unused chandelier, its polished surface dotted with smartly bound books arranged at perfect equal intervals like the bright spokes of a wheel. Every surface reflected light, none absorbed it. The entire room glinted and glowed with a painful, icy perfection, so spotless and sterile Margaret could not help but feel uneasy, aware only subconsciously of the endless hours of labour required to keep everything so snowy white in the smoky, grimy Milton air. There was no hint of warmth, no sign of daily use: all the endless care lavished on the room went only to preserve its ornaments, never to make it a comfortable space for quiet rest or gentle home work.
They had plenty of time to observe it, speaking to each other in quiet whispers, as if afraid to wake the long-dormant echoes of the unused room, before Mrs. Thornton finally swept in, rustling in her customary fine black silk, her crisp muslin collars and lace cuffs matching the blinding whiteness of the net draperies covering the furniture. Margaret stumbled over her explanation that Mrs. Hale could not join them on their call, too anxious not to rile her father’s fresh fears to give a clear account of her mother’s illness, leaving Mrs. Thornton with the distinct impression that Mrs. Hale’s ailment was nothing more than the fussy, fanciful complaint of a fine lady, who could easily have made the trip if she had cared enough. Remembering that she had sent her own carriage out to visit the Hales, ordering Fanny to come with her to show them all possible respect, Mrs. Thornton drew back slightly, offended, and gave Margaret no sympathy for her mother’s supposed indisposition, barely crediting her story at all.
“How is Mr. Thornton?” Mr. Hale asked. “I feared he was unwell, from his hurried note yesterday.”
“My son is rarely sick, and when he is, he never speaks of it, nor uses it as an excuse to neglect his work. He told me he could not make time to read with you last night, sir. He regrets it deeply; he values the hours spent in your company.”
“They are every bit as precious to me,” Mr. Hale said warmly. “Seeing how much he enjoys and appreciates the finest passages of classical literature makes me feel young again.”
“I do not doubt classics are very pleasant for people with nothing else to fill their days,” Mrs. Thornton said, cool as ice. “But I will confess I was against my son taking them up again. The world he lives in, the work he does, demands every last scrap of his energy and attention. Classics are all very well for men who loiter their lives away in country parishes or college quads—but a Milton manufacturer ought to have every thought and power of his mind fixed entirely on the work of the present day. That is my opinion, at least.”
“But surely,” Margaret put in, “if the mind is fixed too long on only one single object, it grows stiff and rigid, unable to take in any other interest.”
“I do not understand what you mean by a mind growing ‘stiff and rigid,’” Mrs. Thornton replied. “Nor do I admire those flighty, whirligig people who are utterly consumed by one new passion today, and completely forget it exists when the next fancy strikes them. A life full of scattered trivial interests does not suit a Milton manufacturer. It is, or ought to be, enough for him to hold one great, single desire, and bend every purpose of his life to fulfil it.”
“And that desire is?” Mr. Hale asked.
Her sallow cheek flushed bright, her eyes lighting up as she answered.
“To hold and maintain a high, honourable place among the merchants of this country—among the businessmen of this very town. My son has earned that reputation for himself. Go anywhere— I do not say just England, but all across Europe—every man of business knows the name of John Thornton of Milton, and respects it. Of course, no one in idle fashionable circles has ever heard of it,” she added, with a sneer of disdain. “Idle lords and ladies do not trouble themselves about a Milton manufacturer, unless he is elected to Parliament, or marries the daughter of an earl.”
Both Mr. Hale and Margaret felt a strange, awkward amusement as they realised they had never heard of this great renowned name before Mr. Bell wrote to tell them Mr. Thornton would be a good friend to have in Milton. The proud mother’s world of hard industrial renown was entirely separate from their own old world of London gentility, Hampshire squires, and quiet country clergymen. Margaret’s face, for all her efforts to keep it calm and neutral, betrayed that thought to the sharp, sensitive Mrs. Thornton.
“You think you never heard of this wonderful son of mine, Miss Hale. You think I am just an old woman whose whole world is bounded by Milton’s factory walls, who thinks her own crow is the whitest bird alive.”
“No,” Margaret said, with a little spark of spirit. “It is true I had barely heard Mr. Thornton’s name before I came to Milton. But since arriving here, I have heard more than enough to respect and admire him, and to see how completely justified you are in being so proud of him.”
“Who spoke to you of him?” Mrs. Thornton asked, a little mollified, yet jealous that no one else’s account of her son could possibly do him full justice.
Margaret hesitated, not caring for this authoritative, inquisitorial line of questioning. Mr. Hale stepped in to rescue her, as he thought.
“It was what Mr. Thornton himself showed of his character, that let us see what kind of man he is, was it not Margaret?”
Mrs. Thornton drew herself up, stiffening again.
“My son is not the kind of man to boast of his own deeds. May I ask you again, Miss Hale, whose account you formed that good opinion from? A mother is always greedy to hear her children praised, you know.”
Margaret answered. “It was far more what Mr. Thornton did not say about the hard early life Mr. Bell told us of, than anything he did say, that made us realise just how much you have to be proud of.”
“Mr. Bell! What can a man who lazes his days away in a drowsy old college know of John? But I thank you, Miss Hale. Many a flighty young lady would have refused to give an old woman that small pleasure, of hearing her son well spoken of.”
“Why would they refuse?” Margaret asked, looking at Mrs. Thornton in complete bewilderment.
“Why! Because they would know they were making the old mother their advocate, just in case they had designs on the son’s heart,” Mrs. Thornton said, giving a grim little smile. She was pleased by Margaret’s frankness, and aware she had been prying a little too deeply. Margaret laughed outright at the absurd idea, her merriment so bright it grated on Mrs. Thornton’s ear, as if no words could possibly be that funny.
Margaret stopped laughing the moment she saw the older woman’s annoyed look.
“I beg your pardon, madam. But I am very grateful to you for exonerating me from having any kind of designs on Mr. Thornton’s heart.”
“Young ladies have done exactly that, before now,” Mrs. Thornton said, stiffly.
“I hope Miss Thornton is well?” Mr. Hale put in, hastily changing the subject.
“She is as well as she ever is. She is not strong,” Mrs. Thornton answered shortly.
“And Mr. Thornton? I may hope to see him for our readings on Thursday, I suppose?”
“I cannot answer for my son’s engagements. There is trouble brewing in the town, talk of a strike. If that comes, every one of his friends will be coming to him for his experience and judgement. But I expect he can make it on Thursday. At the very least, he will send word if he cannot come.”
“A strike!” Margaret exclaimed. “What are they striking for?”
“To seize control of other people’s property, that is what they always strike for,” Mrs. Thornton said, snorting fiercely. “If my son’s workers walk out, I will say only that they are a pack of ungrateful hounds. And I have no doubt they will do it.”
“I suppose they are asking for higher wages?” Mr. Hale said.
“That is the pretty face they put on it. But the truth is, they want to be masters, and turn the masters into slaves on their own ground. They are always plotting it, always thinking of it, and every five or six years there is a great fight between masters and men. They will be caught out this time, I think—they will find they miscalculated. If they walk out, they may not find it so easy to get their jobs back again. The masters have made plans, and they will teach the men not to be so hasty to strike in future, if they dare try it.”
“Will the town become very dangerous?” Margaret asked.
“Of course it will. But surely you are no coward, are you? Milton is no place for cowards. I remember once I had to walk straight through a crowd of white, furious men, all swearing they would tear Makinson to pieces the moment he stepped foot outside his factory. He had no idea the mob was waiting for him, and someone had to go warn him, or he was a dead man. It had to be a woman, so I went. Once I got inside the factory gates, I could not get back out. It was as much as my life was worth to try. So I climbed up to the roof, where we had piles of heavy stones stacked to drop on any man who tried to break the doors down, and I would have lifted those stones and dropped them as straight and true as any man there, if I had not fainted dead away from the heat and exertion. If you are going to live in Milton, Miss Hale, you must learn to have a brave heart.”
“I will do my very best,” Margaret said, a little pale. “I do not know if I am brave or not, until I am tested. But I fear I might be a coward.”
“Soft south country folks are easily frightened by what we Darkshire folk just call living and struggling. But when you have spent ten years living among people who always hold a grudge against their betters, waiting for their chance to pay it back, you will find out for yourself if you are a coward or not, you may take my word for it.”

That evening, John Thornton turned up at the Hales’ cottage. He was shown straight into their drawing room, where Mr. Hale had been reading aloud to his wife and Margaret.
“I come partly to bring you a note from my mother, and partly to apologise for missing our reading last night. The note has the address of the doctor you asked for, Dr. Donaldson.”
“Thank you,” Margaret said hastily, holding out her hand to take the note, not wanting her mother to overhear that they had been seeking out a physician for her. She was pleased to see Mr. Thornton immediately understood her unspoken wish: he handed her the note without a single word of explanation that might alarm Mrs. Hale.
Mr. Hale soon steered the conversation around to the coming strike. Mr. Thornton’s face hardened, taking on the same fierce, unyielding expression his mother had worn earlier, and Margaret was immediately repelled by it.
“Yes, the fools are determined to strike. Let them. It will suit us well enough. We gave them a fair chance. They think trade is as flourishing as it was last year. But we see the storm gathering on the horizon, and we are taking in our sails to ride it out. Because we will not explain every last detail of our books to them, they refuse to believe we are acting reasonably. They demand we give them a line by line account of every penny we choose to spend or save. Henderson tried a trick with his men out in Ashley, and it backfired. He actually wanted a strike, it would have balanced his books perfectly, so when the men came to ask for their five percent raise, he told them he would think it over and give them his answer on payday—already knowing full well he would refuse. He thought he would string them along and let them get cocky, but they were smarter than he expected. They got wind of the bad trade prospects, and came to him on Friday and withdrew their claim, so now he is trapped into keeping them all employed. But all the Milton masters met today and gave our answer. We will not raise wages a single penny. We told them we may soon have to cut wages, not raise them. So here we stand, waiting for their next move.”
“And what will that move be?” Mr. Hale asked.
“I expect a simultaneous strike. You will see Milton completely smokeless in a few days, Miss Hale, I imagine.”
“But why,” Margaret asked, “could you not just explain the good, honest reasons you have for expecting trade to turn bad? I do not know if I am putting this rightly, but you must understand what I mean.”
“Do you give your full servants a line by line explanation for every penny you spend or save in your own house? We who own the capital have a perfect right to choose what we do with it.”
“A human right,” Margaret breathed, so softly he barely heard it.
“I beg your pardon, I missed what you said.”
“I would rather not repeat it,” she said. “It touches on a feeling I do not think you would share.”
“Won’t you let me try to understand?” he pleaded, his thoughts suddenly fixed on learning what she had said. She was annoyed by his persistence, and did not want to make too much of her own words.
“I said you had a human right. I meant that there is no reason outside of religious principle to stop you doing exactly as you please with your own property.”
“I do not agree with all your religious views,” he said, speaking in a low voice, as if the two of them were alone. “But do you not credit me with having any of my own, even if they are different from yours?”

> And always be for change prepared,

The pale, milky glow of Milton’s smoky afternoon was just fading to grey when Dr. Donaldson arrived for his first formal visit to Mrs. Hale. The fragile intimacy Margaret had hoped had softened the household’s old barriers snapped back into place at once: she was turned away from the sick room, while Dixon was ushered inside at once. Margaret was not quick to show soft affection, but when she loved, she loved fiercely, and a sharp, burning jealousy pricked at her that the two people closest to her mother should shut her out.

She slipped into her mother’s bedroom, separated from the drawing room only by a thin wooden partition, and paced the narrow floorboards back and forth, pausing every few heartbeats to press her ear to the wall. At last she was sure she heard a half-stifled moan, and she clenched her fists so tight her nails dug half-moons into her palms, holding her breath until her chest ached. For several long minutes after that, there was only silence, then the scrape of chair legs, the low murmur of farewells, the turn of the doorknob.

She stepped out into the hall the moment the door opened. “My father is out, Dr. Donaldson—he is attending a pupil at this hour. Will you come down to his study for a moment, if you please?” She cut straight past every petty obstruction Dixon tried to put in her path, stepping into her rightful place as mistress of the house with a quiet, unyielding dignity that quelled the old servant’s officiousness at once. For half a second, even through the thrum of her anxiety, Margaret nearly laughed at the shocked, impressed look on Dixon’s face—she must look absurdly grand, she thought, and the flicker of absurdity carried her all the way down the stairs before the full weight of the moment crashed back, stealing her breath.

She spoke at once, steady and sharp as a command. “What is wrong with my mother? Tell me the plain truth, if you will.” When the doctor hesitated, she leaned forward a little. “I am her only child here. My father is far too unalarmed for his own good, and if the news is grave, it must be broken to him gently. I can do that. I can nurse her. Say it plainly, sir—watching you hold back is a far worse dread than any words you could speak.”

“My dear young lady, your mother has a most attentive, devoted servant who is more like an old friend to her—”
“I am her daughter, sir.”
“But she expressly asked that you not be told—”
“I am not gentle enough to submit to that prohibition. And I am sure you are too wise to have promised to keep the secret.”

Dr. Donaldson half-smiled, his face heavy with sadness. “You are quite right. I made no promise. In fact, I fear the secret will make itself known soon enough, no matter what I do.”

He paused. Margaret’s face blanched to a deathly white, her lips pressing thin, no other feature moving. With the swift insight that had made him the most respected physician in the county, he saw she would not accept half-truths, that any small omission would torture her far worse than the full knowledge. He spoke two quiet, short sentences, never taking his eyes off her, and watched the pupils of her eyes blow wide with black horror, her fair skin turning a livid ash colour. He stopped speaking, waiting for her gasping breath to return.

At last she found her voice. “I thank you for your honesty, sir. This dread has haunted me for weeks. Poor, poor mother.” Her lips began to quiver, and he let her weep for a few minutes, certain her iron self-control would stop the tears before they overwhelmed her. She wiped her face quickly, the questions already tumbling out. “Will she suffer much?”

“No one can say for certain. It depends on her constitution, on a thousand small variables. But modern medical discoveries have given us great power to ease pain, you may be sure.”

Margaret trembled all over. “My father—”
“I do not know Mr. Hale well, so I can only offer gentle advice. Let the knowledge rest with you for a little while, until it no longer feels like a sudden, brutal shock. Then you can break it to him softly, when the time is right. Between now and then, my repeated visits, small changes in the household routine, will wake his alarm little by little, and he will be far more prepared to bear it. I saw Mr. Thornton earlier, by the by— and I honour your father for the sacrifice he made to leave his old parish, even if I think it was ill-advised. And do not think of me as only a doctor now. We meet at times like these, we know each other far better than we ever could over a dozen morning calls.”

Margaret could not speak for crying, but she wrung his hand so hard he noticed the pressure long after he was settled in his carriage. “What a remarkable girl,” he thought to himself, rubbing the faint indent on his ringed hand. “Who would have thought that small soft hand could squeeze so hard? She is queenly, every inch of her—demanding the truth head on, then leaning forward to listen so eagerly. Any other girl, gone as white as that, would have fainted or fallen into hysterics. But not her. She is game to the very backbone. If I were thirty years younger, I would fall in love with her, no doubt of it. Ah, well—we are at the Archers’ now.” He hopped out, his mind already fully turned to the next family waiting for his care, as if no other grief existed in the world.

Back in the study, Margaret sank down by her father’s desk for a moment, to collect herself before going up to her mother. “Oh God, this is terrible,” she whispered to herself. “How shall I bear it? No hope at all. Oh mother, mother, I wish I had never spent all those precious years away from you at Aunt Shaw’s! Poor mamma, how much you have borne alone. Let her pain never be too sharp, too dreadful. How will I stand to watch it? How will I tell papa? It would break him at once, if I told him today. I will not waste a single more moment away from you, my dear mother.”

She ran upstairs. Dixon was not in the room. Mrs. Hale lay propped in her easy chair, wrapped in a soft white shawl, wearing a neat, pretty cap in expectation of the doctor’s visit. A faint flush coloured her cheeks, and the exhaustion of the examination had left her looking peaceful, almost rested. Margaret was surprised to see her so calm.

“Margaret, you look terribly strange. What is the matter?” Mrs. Hale’s face tightened with a faint displeasure as the thought dawned on her. “You didn’t corner Dr. Donaldson and ask him questions, did you, child?”

Margaret did not reply, only looked at her mother with a yearning, helpless expression. Mrs. Hale grew more upset. “He would not break his word to me, surely—”

“Oh no mamma, he did. I made him do it. Blame me.” She knelt at her mother’s side, caught her hand and would not let go, even when Mrs. Hale tried to pull it away, kissing it over and over until her hot tears wet the skin.

“Margaret, that was very wrong. I did not want you to know.” But she was tired, and after a moment she let her hand rest in Margaret’s, and faintly returned the pressure.

Encouraged, Margaret spoke again. “Let me be your nurse. I will learn every trick Dixon can teach me. I am your child—surely I have a right to care for you, to do everything for you. It will be such a comfort to me, mamma.”

“You have no idea what you are asking for,” Mrs. Hale shuddered.

“I do. I know far more than you think. No one in the world will ever try half as hard to take care of you as I will. Please.”

“My poor child. Very well, you may try. Do you know, Dixon and I both thought you would shrink away from me the moment you knew—”

“Dixon thought that?” Margaret’s lip curled. “Dixon did not give me credit for half as much love as she has for you! She thought I was one of those silly soft creatures who want to lie on rose leaves and be fanned all day. Don’t let Dixon’s fancies come between us any more, mamma, please.”

“Do not be cross with Dixon,” Mrs. Hale said anxiously.

Margaret softened at once. “I won’t. I will be humble, and learn all her ways, if only you let me care for you. I used to lie awake at night when I was at Aunt Shaw’s, crying because I was afraid you would forget me.”

“And I used to lie awake at night worrying how you would bear our shabby little makeshift life at Helstone, after all the luxury of Harley Street. I was more ashamed of you seeing our rickety little contrivances there than I would have been of any stranger finding them.”

“Oh mamma, I loved all of that! It was far more fun than the dull routine of Harley Street. The wardrobe shelf with the handles that we used as a supper tray on feast days! The old tea chests we stuffed and covered to make ottomans! Those were the very best parts of Helstone life.”

“I shall never see Helstone again, Margaret,” Mrs. Hale said, and tears welled up in her eyes. Margaret could not find words to reply. “I was always wanting to leave it when we lived there, thinking every other place was better. And now I will die far away from it. It feels like a just penance.”

“You must not talk like that,” Margaret said, half-impatient with grief. “The doctor said you might live for years. We will get you back to Helstone yet, I promise.”

“No, never. But Margaret—Frederick!”

The name burst out of her in a wild, sharp cry, all her calm shattering in an instant. “Frederick! Come to me! I am dying! My first little boy, come see me once more!” She fell into violent, raving hysterics. Margaret ran in terror to call Dixon. Dixon came in in a huff, snapping at Margaret for over-exciting her mother, but Margaret bore every word of it meekly, obeying every direction Dixon gave her to the letter, never making a single excuse for herself. Her quiet submission mollified the old servant. They got Mrs. Hale into bed, and Margaret sat by her until she fell asleep, until Dixon finally beckoned her out of the room, and led her to the drawing room, pressing a cup of hot coffee into her hands with a grumpy, commanding air, making her drink every drop.

“You shouldn’t have been so curious, miss, and you wouldn’t have had to fret ahead of your time. It would have all come soon enough. And I suppose now you will run off and tell master, and we will have the whole household in tears for weeks.”

“No Dixon,” Margaret said sorrowfully. “I will not tell papa. He could not bear it the way I can.” And to prove how well she was bearing it, she burst into tears.

“Ah, I knew it would be so. You will wake your mamma up, just after she fell asleep so quiet. Miss Margaret my dear, I have carried this secret for weeks now. I don’t pretend I love her the way you do, but I have loved her better than any soul alive—no one but Master Frederick ever came close. Ever since Lady Beresford’s maid first took me in to see her all dressed up for a ball, in white crape with corn ears and scarlet poppies in her hair, and I ran a needle right down into my finger and broke it off. She tore up her own fine embroidered handkerchief after the maids had cut my bandage open, and came back from the ball to bathe my finger with lotion herself, when she was the prettiest girl in the whole room. I never loved anyone half as much after that. I little thought I would live to see her brought so low. I don’t mean no blame to no one. Everyone says you are a pretty girl, miss, and they are right, even in this smoky Milton that blinds you so the owls can see clear. But you will never be half as beautiful as your mother was—never, not if you live to be a hundred.”

“Mamma is still beautiful now. Poor mamma.”

“Don’t you start crying again, or I will start blubbering too,” Dixon whimpered. “You will never be able to face master when he comes home and starts asking questions, at this rate. Go take a walk, get some air, come back looking half-way normal. I’ve walked the streets for hours many a time, trying to wear off the weight of this secret, not knowing how it would all end.”

“Oh Dixon,” Margaret said, “how many times I was cross with you, never guessing what a terrible weight you were carrying all this time.”

“Bless you, child! I like to see you show a little spirit. That’s the old Beresford blood. The second last Sir John shot his steward dead on his own doorstep once, just because the man told him he was racking his tenants so hard he couldn’t squeeze any more money out of them than he could get skin off a flint.”

“Well Dixon, I will not shoot you, and I will try not to be cross ever again.”

“You never were. When I said you were, it was only to myself, for the sake of a bit of conversation, since there’s no one else in this smoky hole worth talking to. And when you fire up, you look exactly like Master Frederick. I could half provoke you into a temper every day just to see that same stormy, dark look cloud over your face. But go on out now, miss. I will watch over your mother. Master is so wrapped up in his books, he won’t notice a thing if he comes home before you are back.”

“I will go,” Margaret said. She hovered by Dixon for a moment, half-afraid, half-fond, then suddenly leaned in and kissed her cheek, before hurrying out of the room.

“Bless her,” Dixon whispered to herself, grinning as she wiped at her eye. “She is as sweet as a nut. There are only three people in the whole world I love: missus, Master Frederick, and her. The rest can go hang, for I don’t know what they are even here for. Master was born, I suppose, to marry missus. If I thought he loved her half as much as he ought, I might get to love him in time. But he never paid her half enough attention, always reading, reading, thinking, thinking. Look where it got him. Plenty of men who never open a book in their lives end up Rector, and Dean, and all that. Master could have done it too, if he’d just minded missus, and left all that weary thinking and reading alone.” She leaned to the window as she heard the front door click shut. “There she goes. Poor young lady. Her clothes are all worn thin now, compared to the day we moved from Helstone. Back then she didn’t have a darned stocking or a mended pair of gloves in all her wardrobe. And now—!”

“There are briars besetting every path,
Which call for patient care;
There is a cross in every lot,
And an earnest need for prayer.”

Margaret stepped out of her front door heavy of foot and heavy of heart, dragging her feet as if she had a dozen weights tied to her ankles. But the length of the first Milton street, the sharp, coal-kissed air stinging her cheeks, soon roused the young blood in her veins before she had turned even the first corner. Her step lightened, the cold wind flushed her lips a bright, warm red, and she lifted her gaze from the paving stones to the world around her, no longer trapped entirely within the dark circle of her own anxieties.

She spotted more loiterers in the lanes than was usual for midday: men with their hands stuffed deep in their coat pockets, sauntering slow with no loom to tend, clusters of girls cackling loud, their speech sharp and unguarded, bubbling over with a wild, unshackled high spirits Margaret had never witnessed in the quiet south. The roughest, ragged fringe of the men hung slouched on the steps of every beer-house and gin-shop, pipes dangling from their lips, calling out unapologetic comments to every passerby. Margaret’s plan for a long, quiet walk out to the green fields at the edge of town died in her throat; she turned instead toward the Higgins cottage. It would not be half so refreshing as a country ramble, but it would be far kinder.

Nicholas Higgins was sat by the cottage fire, puffing at his pipe, when she stepped over the threshold. Bessy rocked slow in her chair on the opposite side of the hearth, her shoulders hunched. Nicholas pulled the pipe from his mouth, stood abruptly, and pushed his own armchair toward Margaret before leaning one elbow against the mantelpiece, lazy and at ease, while Margaret bent to greet Bessy.
