“She’s low in spirits, sure enough, but her body’s a touch stronger today,” Nicholas said. “Hates this strike, does our Bessy. Will have peace and quiet at any cost, that lass.”
“This is the third strike I’ve lived through,” Bessy sighed, as if that single line explained every heavy thought weighing on her.
“Third time pays for all, eh?” Nicholas grinned, tapping his pipe against his palm. “This time we’ll beat the masters proper, see if we don’t. They’ll come crawling to us, begging us to come back to work on our own terms. We messed the first two up, I’ll grant you that, but this time our plans are laid deep, desperate deep.”

“Why do you strike, exactly?” Margaret asked, curious. “I never heard of such a thing where I grew up, in the south. Striking is leaving work until you get the wage you ask for, is that right? You mustn’t think me daft for not knowing.”
“I wish I’d grown up there,” Bessy murmured, so tired her voice was barely a breath. “This’ll be the last strike I ever see. Before it’s over, I’ll be in the Great City, the Holy Jerusalem.”
“She’s got her head full of the next world, can’t be bothered with the mess of this one,” Nicholas chuckled. “Me, I’m for making the best I can of what’s in front of me. A bird in the hand’s worth two in the bush, that’s what I say. That’s where we split on this strike, see.”
“But down south, most folk are farm labourers,” Margaret pressed on. “If they decided to strike, no one would sow the seed, no one would bring in the hay, no one would reap the corn come harvest.”
Nicholas puffed at his pipe, lifted an eyebrow in a wordless “well?”
“Then what would the farmers do?” Margaret finished.
He went on puffing for a long minute, the smoke coiling up to the cottage ceiling. “I reckon they’d either give up their farms, or pay a fair day’s wage.”
“Suppose they can’t, or won’t do that,” Margaret said. “They can’t abandon their farms overnight, no matter how badly they want to. They’d have no hay or corn to sell that year, no money to pay their labourers even if they wanted to, come next season.”
Still Nicholas puffed. At last he grunted. “I know nought of your soft southern ways. I’ve heard tell your folk are a pack of spiritless, trodden down wretches, half starved to death, too dazed with hunger to even notice they’re being cheated. Not us. We know when we’re being put upon, and we’ve too much fire in our blood to stand it. We just pull our hands out of our looms and say ‘You can starve us, but you won’t grind us down any further, masters!’ And we’ll be damned if they will, this time.”
“I wish I lived down south,” Bessy repeated.
“There’s hardship down there too, Bessy,” Margaret said gently. “Sorrow lives everywhere. Farm work is backbreaking labour, done on barely enough food to keep your strength up.”
“But it’s out of doors,” Bessy protested. “Away from that endless, endless clatter of the looms, that stinking, suffocating heat in the mill rooms.”
“Sometimes it’s pouring rain, sometimes it’s bitter, biting cold,” Margaret went on. “A young body can stand it, but old men get twisted up with rheumatism, bent and withered long before their time, and they still have to work just the same, or they get thrown in the workhouse.”
“I thought you were all for the south and its quiet green ways,” Bessy said, a little surprised.
Margaret smiled, sheepish. “I am. But I mean, there’s good and bad in every corner of this world. You’ve only seen the bad side of Milton, so I thought it only fair you know the bad side of the country you dream of, too.”
“And your folk never strike, down there?” Nicholas asked, sharp.
“No,” Margaret said. “I think they’ve too much sense.”
“Too much sense?” He smashed the ash out of his pipe so hard against the hearthstone the earthen bowl snapped clean in two. “It’s not sense they’ve got too much of, it’s too little backbone!”
“Oh father!” Bessy cried. “What did the last strike ever get us? Remember the first one, when mother died? We all nearly starved, you worst of all. And every week some poor soul snuck back to work for the old lower wage, till there was no work left for anyone who’d held out, and some of them were beggars for the rest of their days.”
“Aye, that strike was badly run,” he waved her off. “Fools and traitors got their hands on the management of it. This one’s different, you’ll see.”
“You still haven’t told me exactly what you’re striking for,” Margaret reminded him.
“Why, half a dozen of the masters have decided they don’t want to pay the same wages they’ve paid for the last two years, years when they raked in fortunes, got richer and richer off our work. Now they come to us and say we’ve got to take less. We won’t. We’ll starve them out first, see who’ll work for them then. They’ll have killed the goose that laid them the golden eggs, mark my words.”
“So you plan to starve yourselves just to get revenge on them?”
“No,” he shook his head. “I’m planning to stand my post, same as a soldier, before I yield. Folk call that noble and brave when a soldier does it, don’t they? Why can’t a poor weaver get the same honour?”
“But a soldier dies for his country, for other people, not just for himself,” Margaret said.
He gave a grim, dry laugh. “Lass, you’re still young, but do you really think I could feed three people — Bessy, Mary, and me — on sixteen shillings a week? D’you think I’m striking for my own sake? This is as much for other folks as that soldier’s fight. The soldier dies for some man he’s never seen in his life, but me? I’m fighting for John Boucher next door but one, with a sickly wife and eight little bairns not old enough to step foot in a mill. And not just him, though he’s a soft old fool who can only manage two looms at a time. I’m fighting for justice. Why should we get paid less now than we did two years back?”
“Don’t ask me,” Margaret said. “I’m ignorant of all this. Go ask your masters, they must have a reason. They didn’t just pull this pay cut out of thin air on a whim, surely.”
“You’re nothing but a foreigner here, that’s what you are,” he said, half contemptuous, half amused. “You know nothing about it. Ask the masters? They’d tell us to mind our own business and they’ll mind theirs. Our business, that is, to take the cut wage and be grateful. Theirs, to grind us down till we’re half starved, to stuff their own pockets full. That’s how it goes.”
“But the state of trade might be bad enough that they can’t afford to pay you the old wage,” Margaret insisted, not backing down even as she saw she was riling him up.
“State of trade? That’s just a trick the masters made up to fool us,” he scoffed. “It’s nothing but a bogeyman they wave around to scare naughty workers into behaving. Their game is to beat our wages down to swell their own fortunes. Our game is to stand our ground, fight hard — not just for ourselves, but for every man around us — for fair play and justice. We make their profits for them, we’ve a right to our share. This time it’s not even that we’re desperate for cash, like we have been in old strikes. The Union’s got savings put aside. Every man among us has sworn to stand or fall together, not a single one of us will cross the line to work for less than the Union says we’re owed. So I say hurray for the strike, let Thornton, and Slickson, and Hamper and all their lot watch out!”
“Thornton?” Margaret said, perking up. “Mr. Thornton of Marlborough Street?”
“Aye, Thornton who runs the Marlborough Mill, as we call him.”
“He’s one of the masters you’re fighting, isn’t he? What sort of a man is he?”
“Ever seen a bulldog? Set it on its hind legs, dress it in a coat and breeches, and that’s John Thornton, exactly.”
“Nonsense,” Margaret laughed. “Mr. Thornton is plain enough, but he’s nothing like a bulldog, with its short broad snout and snarling lip.”
“Aye, he doesn’t look like one, I’ll grant you. But once John Thornton gets a notion stuck in his head, he’ll hang on to it tighter than any bulldog to a rat. You’d have to pry him off with a pitchfork before he’d let go. He’s a worthy enemy, that Thornton. Slickson? He’ll wheedle his men back soon with soft promises, then cheat them out of every penny the second they’re back under his thumb, you mark my words. He’ll fine them half their wages before the month’s out, see if he doesn’t. Slippery as an eel, that one, sly as a cat. But with Thornton? It’s an honest, up-and-down fight, no tricks. He’s as stubborn as a door-nail, every inch of him, that old bulldog.”
“Poor Bessy,” Margaret said, turning back to the girl rocking in her chair. “You’re sighing over all of it. You don’t care for fighting and struggling the way your father does, do you?”
“No,” Bessy said, her voice heavy. “I’m sick to death of it. I wish the last days I’ve got left could be full of other talk, not just the clashing and clattering that’s worn me out my whole life — talk of work, and wages, and masters, and knobsticks who cross the picket line.”
“Poor lass!” Nicholas said, softening. “Your last days aren’t here yet, you’re looking brighter already for a bit of excitement. And I’ll be around more, to keep you company.”
“The tobacco smoke chokes me,” Bessy said, petulant.
“Then I’ll never smoke indoors again,” he said, tender as anything. “Why didn’t you tell me that before, you daft lass?”
She didn’t speak for a long minute, then whispered so quiet only Margaret could hear: “I reckon he’ll need every bit of comfort he can get from his pipe, or even a drink, before this strike is over.”
Nicholas stepped out the door to finish his smoke in the yard. The second he was gone, Bessy turned to Margaret, wild and upset. “Am I not a fool, Miss? I knew I should have kept him inside, away from all those folk hanging around the pubs who’ll tempt any man in strike time to go drinking. And I had to pick a fight about his pipe, so he’s run off out, and he’ll go and wander the streets whenever he wants a smoke, and no one knows where it’ll end. I wish I’d let myself choke on that smoke before I said a word.”
“Does your father drink, then?” Margaret asked.
“No, not properly,” Bessy said, her voice still raw and overwrought. “But haven’t you ever had days, same as everyone else, when you get up and drag through the hours just craving a little change, a little spark of something different? I’ve done it, gone and bought a loaf of bread from a baker I never use, just because I was sick to death of seeing the same things, hearing the same sounds, tasting the same food, thinking the same empty thoughts day after day, forever. I’ve even wished I was a man, so I could go off wandering somewhere new, looking for work. Men get that restlessness ten times stronger than me. What else have they got to do? It’s no great sin if they pop into the gin shop just to make their blood run a little faster, see something new, look at the pictures on the walls and the big mirrors they’ve got there. Father’s never been a drunkard, not properly, though he’s come home worse for wear once in a while. But strike times? There’s so much to knock the heart out of a man, even if they started out hopeful. Where’s their comfort supposed to come from? He’ll get angry, he’ll get mad, and one day he’ll do something in that rage he’ll wish he could forget. Bless your sweet, pitying face — but you don’t know what a strike is, not really, not yet.”
“Come, Bessy,” Margaret said. “I won’t say you’re exaggerating, I don’t know enough to say that. But you’re unwell, so you’re only looking at the dark side of things. There’s a brighter side to see, too, I promise.”
“That’s easy for you to say,” Bessy said. “You’ve lived in green, pleasant places your whole life, never known want, or care, or wickedness even.”
“Be careful how you judge that,” Margaret said, her cheeks flushing, her eyes bright. “I’m going home to a mother who is so ill — so ill, Bessy — that there’s no way out of the prison of her pain but death. And I have to speak cheerful to my father, who has no idea how bad she is, who will have to be told her condition slow, gentle, bit by bit. The only person who could comfort her more than anyone else in the world, the only one who could understand what we’re going through? He’s been falsely accused of mutiny on his ship. He’d hang if he came home to see his dying mother. I’ve told no one else in Milton, hardly anyone in all of England. Do I not know care? Do I not know anxiety, even if I wear nice clothes and have food on my table? Oh, Bessy, God is just. He portions out our lots fair, even if no one but Him knows how bitter some of our souls feel.”
“I beg your pardon,” Bessy said, quiet and humbled. “Sometimes when I think of my life, how little joy I’ve ever had, I think I must be one of the folk the falling star in Revelations was talking about. ‘And the name of the star is Wormwood; and the third part of the waters became wormwood; and men died of the waters, because they were made bitter.’ It’s easier to bear pain when you think it was written down for you hundreds of years ago, like your suffering is needed to fulfil some grand old prophecy. Otherwise it all feels like it’s for nothing, no meaning at all.”
“Don’t dwell so much on the prophecies, Bessy,” Margaret urged. “Read the clearer, softer, kinder parts of the Bible.”
“Maybe that’s wiser, but where else will I hear such grand, sweeping words of promise, hear about a world so far away from this dreary town? I’ve recited the verses of the seventh chapter of Revelations to myself a thousand times, just for the sound of them. It’s as grand as church organ music, nothing like the dreary sameness of every day. I can’t give it up. It comforts me more than any other book in the Bible.”
“Then let me come back and read you some of my favourite chapters, the ones that give me hope.”
“Aye, do,” Bessy said, eager. “Maybe father will hear you, too. He’s sick of my talking, says all my talk of heaven has nothing to do with the work he’s got to do here on earth.”
“Where’s your sister Mary?” Margaret asked.
“Gone out fustian cutting. I hated to let her go, but we’ve got to eat, and the Union can’t give us much money to live on.”
“Then I must be going,” Margaret said, standing up. “You’ve done me good, Bessy.”
“Me done you good?” Bessy blinked, confused.
“Yes. I came here today so sad, so wrapped up in my own grief I thought no one else in the world could be hurting as bad. Now I know how long you’ve borne your own pain, and it’s made me stronger.”
“Bless you,” Bessy laughed. “I always thought it was the gentlefolk who did all the good. I’ll get a big head if I find out I can do good for you too.”
“You won’t, not if you don’t think about it,” Margaret

“My heart revolts within me, and two voices
Make themselves audible within my bosom.”

When Margaret stepped back through the Hale’s front door after her walk, she found two notes lying on the parlour table. One was a sealed letter addressed to her mother, the other, crisscrossed with smudged foreign postmarks and thin as silver tissue, rustled soft as sea-wind in her fingers: it was unmistakably from her Aunt Shaw, writing from the continent. She was still turning the foreign letter over to trace the stamps when her father burst in, his coat unbuttoned, his face lined with quiet worry.

“Your mother’s gone up to bed early, worn right out, poor thing. That thundery, airless day could not have been worse for the doctor’s visit, I’m sure. What did Donaldson say to you, eh? Dixon told me he pulled you aside to talk through her case.”

Margaret hesitated, and Mr Hale’s face fell sharper, more anxious. “He does not think her seriously ill?”

“Not at present,” she said gently. “He says she only needs careful tending. He was very kind, and promised to call back soon to see how her medicines are settling.”

“Only care? He didn’t suggest change of air? He didn’t say this smoky Milton fog is doing her harm, did he, Margaret?”

“Not a word,” she answered, sober. “Though I think he was more anxious than he let on to you.”

“All doctors put on that grave, fussy manner—professional habit, nothing more,” he said, trying to brush the fear away. But Margaret could see the unease coiled tight under his attempts at lightness: he could not settle to any task that whole evening, kept circling back to the subject of his wife’s health, fighting hard to push away even the faintest hint of bad news that would not be shifted, until Margaret’s chest ached for him.

“Ah, this letter’s from Aunt Shaw,” she said, trying to distract him. “She reached Naples and found the heat unbearable, so she’s rented apartments in Sorrento. Though I don’t think she’s half as fond of Italy as she hoped she would be.”

“Did he say nothing of special diet, then?” he pressed, not taking the bait.

“Nourishing, easy to digest. I think her appetite’s been pretty good lately.”

“Aye, that’s what makes it odd he’d even bother to mention diet at all.”

“I asked him straight, papa,” Margaret said. A long quiet stretched between them, before she picked the letter back up. “Aunt Shaw says she’s sent me a set of coral ornaments, but she’s half convinced Milton’s Dissenters won’t see the beauty in them. She still gets all her ideas of Dissenters from old Quaker tales, doesn’t she?”

“If you ever notice your mother craving anything at all, no matter how small, you must promise to tell me at once,” he said, unsmiling. “I’m always so afraid she won’t own up to what she wants, to spare me extra work. And do see after that new house-servant Mrs Thornton recommended. If we get an efficient girl, Dixon can stay by her side night and day, and I’ll wager proper care will nurse her right back to strength, what with this hot weather and the trouble we’ve had finding help lately. A little rest, that’s all she needs, eh Margaret?”

“I hope so,” Margaret said, so sadly that her father noticed, and pinched her pale cheek playfully.

“Come now, you’re looking as white as a sheet. If you don’t take care of yourself, I’ll be calling the doctor for you next, not your mother.”

But he could not rest. Every few minutes he slipped down the hall on tiptoe, slow as he could move, to peek through the crack of the bedroom door and check his wife was still sleeping. Margaret’s heart ached to watch him: he was fighting tooth and nail to smother the ugly, nameless fear that loomed in the dark corners of his mind. At last he came back, his shoulders loosened a little with relief.

“She’s awake now, Margaret. She smiled the moment she saw me standing by the bed—just her old, familiar smile. Says she feels rested, and is hungry for a cup of tea. Where’s that note for her? She wants to see it. I’ll read it out to her while you make the tea.”

The note was a formal, neatly penned invitation from Mrs Thornton, asking Mr., Mrs. and Miss Hale to dinner at their home on the twenty-first of the month. Margaret was surprised to see both her father and mother already set on accepting it, after all the worrying news the doctor had shared that very day. But the dinner invitation had lit a spark in Mrs Hale long before Margaret even knew what the note said: it was a small, bright event to break the endless monotony of her days stuck inside, and she clung to the idea of going with a fretful, stubborn pertinacity when Margaret tried to object that she should not exert herself.

“Nay, Margaret, if it makes her happy to think of us going, we’ll both go gladly,” Mr Hale said anxiously, sitting down to pen the note of acceptance the next morning. “She would never ask it of us if she didn’t feel stronger, better than we thought she was, isn’t that right, Margaret?”

The comfort he was clinging to so desperately felt too cruel to refuse him. His whole-hearted refusal to acknowledge any possible fear had even seeped into Margaret, warming her own small spark of hope.

“I do think she’s better than she was last night,” she conceded. “Her eyes are brighter, and her complexion is clearer.”

“God bless you, child,” he said, earnest. “Though yesterday was so sultry everyone in town felt unwell. It was the worst possible day for Dr Donaldson to have come round.”

He left then for his daily lessons, which had grown busier now that he was preparing a gratis series of lectures on Ecclesiastical Architecture for the working men at the nearby Lyceum. The subject was far more aligned with his own old clerical tastes and learning than it was with the practical, trade-focused knowledge the mill workers had asked for, but the debt-ridden Lyceum board was only too happy to get a free course from an educated, accomplished man like Mr Hale, no matter what the topic.

---

That same night, at the Thornton house, John Thornton was going over the guest list spread out on the dining room table.

“Mother, who’s accepted the dinner invites for the twenty-first?”

“Fanny, hand me the notes, there,” Mrs Thornton said. “The Slicksons accept, Collingbrooks accept, Stephenses accept, Browns decline. The Hales—father and daughter are coming, Mrs Hale’s too much of an invalid to attend. Macphersons, Mr Horsfall, Mr Young. I was thinking we’d ask the Porters to make up the extra spots, since the Browns can’t make it.”

“Good enough,” Thornton said, his brow creased. “Dr Donaldson told me today I’m afraid Mrs Hale is far from well.”

“Odd that they’d accept a dinner invitation if she’s that unwell,” Fanny put in, tittering her little nervous, weak laugh.

“I didn’t say she was at death’s door,” Thornton snapped, sharper than he meant to be. “Only that she’s far from well. They might not even realise how serious her condition is, not properly.” He stopped short, suddenly remembering what Dr Donaldson had told him: Margaret, at least, knew full well the exact state of her mother’s health.

“Oh, I’m sure they know exactly what you told John yesterday,” Fanny said. “How advantageous it would be for Mr Hale to be introduced to people like the Stephenses and Collingbrooks, all those rich trade families.”

“Those are not the sort of people to curry favour for introductions,” Thornton said shortly. “No, I think I understand how it is.”

“John, you are forever going on about how well you understand the Hales,” Fanny teased, “and you’ll never let the rest of us have a correct thought about them. Are they really so different from every other person you meet in Milton?” She had not meant to vex him, but she could not have done a better job of it if she tried. He chafed in silence, not deigning to dignify the question with a reply.

“They don’t strike me as anything out of the ordinary,” Mrs Thornton pronounced, her voice firm. “The man is a decent sort, but far too simple for trade—no wonder he was a clergyman first, and a teacher now. The wife’s a bit of a fine lady, making a performance out of her invalidism. As for the girl—she’s the only one that puzzles me, when I do bother to think of her, which isn’t often. She carries on like she’s got some high and mighty notion of her own importance, acting like she’s too good for the company around her half the time. And yet they’ve never had a penny to spare, from all I’ve heard.”

“And she’s not even accomplished, mamma,” Fanny added. “She can’t play the piano at all.”

“Go on, Fanny,” Thornton said, turning on her. “What else is she lacking that would make her worthy enough for your standards?”

“Nay, John, that was no harm what Fanny said,” his mother cut in. “I heard the girl myself say she could not play. If you’d stop jumping to her defence all the time, we might even get to see her merits, and like her.”

“I’m sure I never could like her!” Fanny muttered, hidden safe behind her mother’s shoulder.

Thornton heard her, but he did not bother to reply. He paced back and forth across the dining room, wishing his mother would call for candles so he could bury himself in work, and put an end to this conversation once and for all. But he would never interfere with his mother’s small, thrifty domestic habits, the ones she had formed from decades of careful economy when they had nothing. He stopped pacing at last, and spoke out plainly, earnest and soft.

“Mother, I wish you would make an effort to like Miss Hale.”

His mother stared, startled by his tone. “You’re not thinking of marrying her, are you? A girl without a penny to her name?”

“She would never have me,” Thornton said, with a short, bitter laugh.

“Aye, that’s obvious,” his mother agreed. “She laughed straight in my face once, when I praised her for speaking up for you after that story Mr Bell told. I liked her for her frankness then, thought that meant she had no silly notions about you. And the next minute she vexed me so, carrying on like she thought—well, never mind that! But you’re right, she thinks far too highly of herself to ever consider you. That saucy little jade! I’d like to see her find a better man than you anywhere.”

“Since I am as convinced as you are that she’d never look twice at me, and I have no intention of ever asking her to be my wife, you can believe me when I say I speak of her entirely disinterestedly,” Thornton said. “I see trouble coming for that family, trouble that will leave that girl in need of a friend, no motherly care to turn to soon. I only ask that you be ready to step up for her, if she needs it. And Fanny,” he turned to his sister, “I trust you have enough decency to understand that to whisper any silly gossip that I’m sweet on her would do her more harm than it would me—she’d be mortified, to be sure. I only ask you both to show her every kindness you can.”

“I can’t forgive her for her pride,” Mrs Thornton said. “But I’ll befriend her if she needs it, for your sake, John. I’d befriend Jezebel herself if you asked me to. But this girl, who turns her nose up at all of us—who turns her nose up at you—”

“Nay, mother. I have never once put myself in the way of her contempt, and I never mean to.”

“Contempt, indeed!” Mrs Thornton huffed, one of her sharp, expressive snorts. “Don’t speak of Miss Hale to me anymore, John, if I’ve got to be nice to her. When I’m standing right in front of her I can’t tell if I like or dislike her more, but when I think of her, and hear you talk of her, I hate her. I can see she’s been putting on all her high and mighty airs for you, plain as day, even if you won’t admit it.”

“If she has,” Thornton paused for a beat, then went on, “I’m no soft lad to be cowed by a proud look from a woman, or to care if she misunderstands me and what I’ve built here. I can laugh at it.”

“To be sure you can! Laugh at her too, with all her fine notions and haughty little tosses of the head!”

“Then why are we all talking so much about her?” Fanny complained. “I’m sick to death of the subject.”

“Fine,” Thornton said, a sharp edge of bitterness in his voice. “Let’s talk about something more pleasant. What do you say to the workers striking, for a cheerful topic?”

“Have the hands actually walked out?” Mrs Thornton asked, instantly alert, all her attention fixed.

“Hamper’s men are already out. My men are finishing out the rest of the week only because they’re scared of being prosecuted for breach of contract. I’d have every single one of them up before the magistrates the second they step off the job early, to punish them for it.”

“The legal fees would be more than those useless fellows are worth—ungrateful lot of naughts!” his mother scoffed.

“Of course they would. But I’d show them that I keep my word, and I expect them to keep theirs. They know by now what sort of master they’re dealing with. Slickson’s men have all walked out too—serves him right, he’s too mean to spend a penny on punishing them. We’re in for a full turn-out across the whole town, mother.”

“Hope you haven’t got too many large orders on the books?”

“Of course we have. They know that perfectly well, that’s why they picked now to strike. They don’t understand the full picture, even if they think they do.”

“What do you mean, John?”

Candles had been brought into the room now, and Fanny had picked up her endless, slow piece of worsted work, yawning over it, leaning back in her chair every few minutes to stare at nothing.

“The Americans are flooding the market with their own yarn right now,” Thornton explained. “Our only chance to stay in business is to produce it cheaper than them. If we can’t, we can shut up our mills tomorrow, every last one of them, and hands and masters alike will be tramping the roads looking for work. And these fools are still demanding the wages we paid three years ago—some of their leaders even cite Dickinson’s rates, when they know full well those fines they squeeze out of their workers’ pay, no honourable man would ever take, means their real wages are lower than what I pay at my mill. I wish the old Combination Laws were still in force. It’s unbearable, to think a pack of ignorant, silly men, banding their empty heads together, get to rule over the fortunes of men who built this all up with years of hard work, knowledge, thought, and pain. The next step—we’re nearly there already—will be having to go hat in hand to the Spinner’s Union secretary, begging him to be so kind as to give us labour at whatever price they name. They haven’t the sense to see that if we don’t get a fair profit to cover the cost of running these mills, we can pick up and move the whole business abroad, and there’ll be no jobs left for anyone. With home and foreign competition both breathing down our necks, none of us are ever going to make more than a fair profit, and we’ll be lucky to get that even in a good year.”

“Why not bring workers over from Ireland?” Mrs Thornton suggested. “I wouldn’t keep those ungrateful fellows on for a single day more. I’d show them who’s master, who I choose to hire.”

“Aye, I will, if this drags on. It’ll be expensive, and there’s bound to be trouble, danger even, but I’ll do it before I give in to their demands.”

“If we’ve got all this extra expense coming, I’m sorry we arranged a dinner right now,” his mother said.

“So am I—not for the cost, but because I’ll have a thousand things to think of, a hundred unexpected calls on my time. But we had to invite Mr Horsfall, he’s only passing through Milton for a few days, and we owed all the other families return dinners anyway. It’s all the same trouble, whether we do it now or later.”

He paced on, silent after that, only drawing deep, sharp breaths every now and then like he was trying to shake off an annoying, clinging thought. Fanny pestered her mother with a dozen small trivial questions that had nothing to do with the strike, and got nothing but short, sharp answers in return. She was relieved when ten o’clock rolled around, and the servants filed in for the household prayers. Mrs Thornton always read them herself, starting with a chapter from the Old Testament, which they were working through steadily from start to finish. When the prayers were over, and everyone had said their goodnights, Mrs Thornton gave him that long, steady, intense look of hers, the kind that held all the force of a blessing even if she never said a tender word, and he kept on pacing the empty dining room.

All his carefully laid business plans, the product of months of late nights and anxious thought, had been torn to shreds by this sudden, insane strike. The workers’ folly would hurt themselves far more than it would ever hurt him, but no one could calculate how much ruin it would spread across the whole town. Hamper had said only that day that if the strike ruined him, he’d start over again from nothing, comforted by the fact that the men who brought it on would be far worse off than him—he had a head for business as well as two hands to work, while the workers had nothing but their two hands, and if they chased their own market away, they could never follow it, never turn to any other trade. But that thought was no comfort to Thornton. He took no pleasure in the idea of revenge. He valued the position he had built with the sweat of his own brow far too much, and the thought of it being endangered

> “As angels in some brighter dreams Call to the soul when man doth sleep, So some strange thoughts transcend our wonted themes, And into glory peep.”

The thought of the Thornton dinner party had seized Mrs. Hale’s fancy with the wide-eyed curiosity of a small child, begging to hear every detail of the joy it has already begun to anticipate. For invalids confined to a narrow, unchanging routine soon come to resemble children in this: they lose all sense of proportion between events, and the curtains and four walls that make up their whole world feel far larger and more important than anything that lies hidden outside. Mrs. Hale had carried quiet girlhood vanities, too, long smothered and set aside after she married a poor country clergyman and endured the mortification of shrinking from the social standing she had once known—but they had never died, and now she delighted to dwell on the thought of Margaret dressed for a grand occasion, fretting over what she should wear with a tender, flustered anxiety that made Margaret smile. She had spent a full year in London’s Harley Street immersed in social life, far more practiced in such trivialities than her mother had been in five and twenty quiet years at Helstone.

“So you are set on your white silk? Are you certain it still fits? It’s nearly a year since Edith’s wedding, after all.”
“Oh yes, mamma! Mrs. Murray stitched it, and her work never fails. It might sit a hair’s breadth shorter or longer at the waist, depending on if I’ve gained or lost a little weight, but I’ve barely changed at all.”
“Shouldn’t you let Dixon check it over? It might have turned yellow tucked away in the press all this time.”
“I will, if you’d like. But even if the worst comes to pass, I’ve that lovely pink gauze Aunt Shaw gave me just months before Edith married—it can’t have yellowed.”
“No, but it might have faded.”
“Then there’s my green silk, too. It’s the very opposite of not having anything to wear, I’ve more than enough options.”
“I do wish I knew what would suit you best,” Mrs. Hale fretted, twisting her fingers in the edge of her shawl.
Margaret’s face softened at once. “Shall I go and put them on one after another, and you can pick the one you like most?”
Her mother nodded, and off Margaret ran. She was half tempted to play the silly pranks she’d loved as a girl: puff the full white silk skirt out like a balloon, retreat backwards across the room as if she were a queen making a royal procession, but she quickly saw these light tricks were interrupting her mother’s earnest pleasure, and grew grave and still, posing patiently for her inspection. She could not for the life of her understand why everyone around her fussed so over a simple gown—but that very afternoon, when she mentioned the dinner engagement to Bessy Higgins (speaking of the new housemaid Mrs. Thornton had promised to help them hire), Bessy sat bolt upright, her pale cheeks flushing with surprise.
“Dear heart, you’re going to dine with the Thorntons over at Marlborough Mills?”
“Yes, Bessy—why are you so startled?”
“Oh, I don’t rightly know. But they keep company with all the very first folk in Milton, don’t they?”
“And you don’t think we quite rank among Milton’s first folk, eh?” Margaret laughed gently.
Bessy’s face burned pink at being caught out. “Well, you see, this town values money above all else, and I reckon you haven’t got much of that.”
“That’s very true,” Margaret agreed. “But we are educated, and have always lived among educated people. Is it so strange that a man who came to my father for tutoring should invite us to dinner? I don’t blame Mr. Thornton, mind—very few former draper’s assistants could ever build themselves a life as fine as his.”
“But can you give them a dinner back, in that little house of yours? The Thorntons’ home is three times the size of yours.”
“I think we could manage to return the favour, never fear. It wouldn’t be in so grand a room, or with so many guests, but we’ve never measured company by the size of our dinner table here.”
“I never thought I’d live to see you dine with the Thorntons,” Bessy repeated, wonder in her voice. “Why, the mayor himself dines there, and Members of Parliament, and all the most important people in town.”
“I think I can rise to the honour of meeting Milton’s mayor,” Margaret said, still smiling.
“But those ladies there dress so grand!” Bessy fretted, glancing down at Margaret’s simple printed cotton gown, which her Milton eyes priced at no more than sevenpence a yard.
Margaret laughed, her dimples deepening. “Thank you, Bessy, for caring so much that I look nice among all the fine folks. But I have more than enough pretty gowns—only a week ago I would have said they were far too fancy for anything I’d ever need in this dull smoky town, but since I’m dining at Mr. Thornton’s and meeting the mayor, you can be sure I’ll wear my very best.”
“What will you wear?” Bessy asked, already a little relieved.
“White silk,” Margaret told her. “The gown I wore for a cousin’s wedding a year ago.”
“That’ll do,” Bessy said, sinking back against her cushions. “I’d hate to see you looked down on.”
“Oh, I’ll be fine enough to avoid that, never fear.”
“I wish I could see you all dressed up,” Bessy said softly. “I don’t reckon anyone would call you pretty, exactly—you haven’t the bright pink and white cheeks folks favour here. But do you know, I dreamt of you long, long before I ever laid eyes on you.”
“Nonsense, Bessy!”
“It’s true! Your very face, your clear steady eyes looking out of the dark, your hair blown back from your smooth straight forehead like rays of light—and you always came to give me strength, strength I drank right out of your kind, comforting eyes. And you were dressed all in shining white, just the gown you’re going to wear to dinner. So you see, it was you, all along.”
“Nay, Bessy,” Margaret said gently, “it was only a dream.”
“And why shouldn’t I dream a dream in my sickness, same as anyone else? Didn’t the good people in the Bible dream dreams and see visions? Even my father sets great store by dreams. I tell you I saw you as plain as day, coming swift towards me, your hair blown back by how fast you moved, just like the way it sticks up a little from your forehead now, all in that shining white dress. Let me come and see you in it. I want to see you, and touch you, so I know for certain the girl from my dream is really here.”
“Of course you can come, you are more than welcome. But don’t speak so—you make me feel quite unworthy.”
“Then I’ll hold my tongue, even if I bite it in two. It’s all true, anyway.”
Margaret fell silent. “We can talk of it whenever you like,” she said at last. “But not now. Tell me, has your father come home yet?”
“Aye,” Bessy said, her tone dropping suddenly heavy, far removed from the soft wonder of a minute before. “Him and hundreds of others—all of Hamper’s men, and many more besides. The women are as wild and bitter as the men this time. Food has got so dear, and they need to feed their bairns. If the Thorntons spent just the money they lay out on that fancy dinner on potatoes and meal, they could quiet a dozen crying babies, and ease a dozen mothers’ broken hearts for a little while.”
“Don’t speak so,” Margaret said, flushing. “You’ll make me feel wicked and guilty for going at all.”
“No,” Bessy said. “Some are pre-elected to sumptuous feasts, and purple and fine linen—maybe you’re one of them. Others toil and moil all their lives long, and not even the dogs pity them these days, like they pitied Lazarus in the old story. But if you ever needed me to cross that great gulf between us to cool your tongue with the tip of my finger, I’d come, for all the kindness you’ve shown me here.”
“Bessy, you’re feverish— I can feel it in your hand, and hear it in your words. On that final day, we won’t be judged by the accident of who was rich and who was poor. We’ll be judged by how faithfully we followed Christ.”
Margaret fetched cool water, soaked her handkerchief in it, and laid it on Bessy’s forehead, then chafed her cold, stiff feet until Bessy’s eyes fluttered shut, soothed.
“You’d be worn quite daft, same as me, if all hours of the day folks tramped in asking for father, and sat down to pour out all their troubles to me,” Bessy murmured. “Some spoke of deadly hatred, and told me such terrible things about the masters they made my blood run cold—but most of them, the women, just went on complaining, tears streaming down their cheeks unheeded, about the price of bread, and how their bairns wouldn’t sleep at night for hunger.”
“Do they truly think the strike will fix all this?” Margaret asked.
“They say so,” Bessy said. “Trade’s been good for years, and the masters have made heaps of money. Father doesn’t know how much, but the Union does. It’s only natural they want their fair share of the profits now that food’s so dear, and the Union says it’s doing its duty pushing the masters to give it. But the masters have got the upper hand somehow, and I’m afraid they’ll keep it forever. It’s like the great battle of Armageddon, the way they grin and fight each other, and even while they’re swinging their fists, they’re falling one by one into the pit.”
Just then Nicholas Higgins tramped in, and caught his daughter’s last words.
“Aye! And I’ll fight on too, and we’ll win this time. It won’t take long for them to cave. They’ve got a pile of orders all under contract, and they’ll soon learn it’s cheaper to give us our five percent raise than to lose all their profits, never mind the fines they’ll pay for breaking their contracts. Aha, my fine masters! I know who’s going to win.”
Margaret thought he had been drinking, not so much from his words as from the wild, over-excited edge to his voice, and Bessy’s obvious hurry to see her out the door confirmed the suspicion.
“The twenty-first—that’s Thursday week. I’ll come see you dressed for the Thorntons’ dinner, right? What time does it start?”
Before Margaret could answer, Higgins cut in.
“Thornton’s! You’re going to dine at Thornton’s? Ask him to drink a toast to the success of his orders. By the twenty-first, he’ll be scratching his head trying to figure out how he’s going to get them all filled on time. Tell him seven hundred of us will march into Marlborough Mills the morning after he gives us the five percent, and get all that work done for him in no time. You’ll meet all the top mill-owners at his dinner, no doubt. I’d love to corner them after the meal, when they’re full of food and slow to move, and tell them exactly what I think of the hard way they’re grinding us down.”
“Goodbye, Bessy!” Margaret said, hurrying for the door. “I’ll look for you on the twenty-first, if you’re well enough.”
Dr. Donaldson’s new treatment worked wonders on Mrs. Hale at first, so much so that Margaret, and even Mrs. Hale herself, began to dare hope the doctor had been wrong, and she might make a full permanent recovery. Mr. Hale, who had never fully grasped how grave her condition was, openly rejoiced at the change, his relief a clear sign of how deeply he had been frightened by the threat of losing her. Only Dixon grumbled endless dark warnings into Margaret’s ear, but Margaret defied the old raven, and clung tight to hope.
They badly needed that small glimmer of joy inside their home, for outside, even their untrained, country eyes could see the dark, brooding cloud of discontent hanging over all of Milton. Mr. Hale had made friends among the working men of the town, and came home heavy-hearted with their earnest, terrible tales of suffering and endless endurance. He brought all their stories and grievances to Mr. Thornton, to ask the master mill-owner to explain their root causes, and Thornton always did so clearly, laying out his cold, unflinching economic principles: that trade naturally rises and falls, and in the lean times, a certain number of masters and men alike will be crushed into ruin, wiped out of the ranks of the prosperous. He spoke as if this consequence was so perfectly logical no one had any right to complain if it befell them: the ruined master forced to drop out of the race, bitter at his own failure, trampled down by his rivals, begging for work he once handed out to others with a lordly air. Of course, if he could speak so coldly of the ruin that might one day be his own, he had no sympathy at all for the fate of the workmen, cast aside by the swift, merciless march of industrial progress—men who would gladly lie down and die if not for the clinging cries of the helpless families they would leave behind, who envied the wild birds that could feed their own young with their very heart’s blood.
Margaret’s whole soul rose up in rebellion against him as he spoke, as if commerce was everything and humanity nothing. She could barely thank him that very evening when he arrived at their house privately, with a quiet, delicate kindness that made him avoid drawing attention to the favour, to offer every medical comfort and supply his own well-staffed household held that Dr. Donaldson had warned him Mrs. Hale might soon need. The contrast was jarring: his generosity, his obvious pity written in his soft eyes, his tremulous gentle voice, all tangled up with that cold, hard, merciless logic of trade he had spoken of earlier, the very logic that threatened the future she was trying so desperately to pretend would not take her mother away. She knew he had guessed the dark, sacred secret she locked away in the deepest corner of her heart, that one day soon she would call for her mother and get no answer but silence from the empty room. She could not reconcile that hard, unfeeling reasoning man with this kind, pitying visitor, and the discord grated on her more than words could say.
Nicholas Higgins was appointed a strike committee man that same week, and he boasted he knew secrets no ordinary worker did. He was arguing the point with his neighbour John Boucher, whom Margaret had heard of often: a clumsy unskilled worker with a huge family to support, who alternately stirred Higgins’ pity, and roused his rage for lacking what Higgins called “spirit”. When Margaret walked in, it was clear Higgins was in a towering passion. Boucher stood leaning his weight on the high mantelpiece, swaying a little as he stared wild-eyed into the fire, a despair so raw it roused Higgins’ irritation even as it tore at his heart. Bessy rocked violently back and forth in her chair, as she always did when agitated, while her sister Mary tied on her bonnet with her clumsy, work-calloused fingers to go to her fustian cutting job, snivelling aloud, desperate to escape the tense, sad scene.
Margaret paused in the doorway, put a finger to her lips, and stole over to sit on the small sofa beside Bessy. Higgins saw her, and gave her a gruff, not unfriendly nod. Mary bolted for the door the second she could, crying loud the moment she was out of the house. Only Boucher noticed no one, his gaze fixed firmly on the flames.
“It’s no use, Higgins. My wife won’t live much longer like this. She’s wasting away, not for want of food herself, but because she can’t bear to see our bairns starving. Aye, starving! Five shillings a week might be enough for you, with only two mouths to feed and one of them a lass who earns her own keep. But for us, it’s starvation. I tell you plain—if she dies, before we ever get that five percent raise, I’ll fling your money back in the masters’ faces, and tell them to go to hell, the whole cruel lot of them, for robbing me of the best wife a man ever had. And look you, lad: I’ll hate you, and the whole cursed Union. Aye, and I’ll chase you through heaven with my hatred, I will, if you’ve led me astray in this. You said a fortnight ago that before two weeks were up, the masters would be begging us to come back to work on our own wages. Time’s almost up, and our little Jack lies in bed too weak to cry, only sobbing every now and then his little heart out for want of food. Our little Jack, that used to wake me every morning by pressing his soft little lips to my rough dirty face, hunting for a smooth spot to kiss—now he’s starving.” Deep, ragged sobs choked off his words, and Higgins lifted his own eyes full of tears to Margaret before he could find the courage to speak.
“Hold up, man. Your little Jack won’t starve. I’ve got money tucked away, and we’ll go buy the lad a sup of milk and a fresh four-pound loaf this very minute. What’s mine is yours, if you’re in need. Only don’t lose heart,” he said, fumbling in an old teapot for the savings he had stashed there. “I swear on my soul we’re going to win. It’s only one more week, and you’ll see the masters come crawling to us, begging us to come back to the mills. The Union—that’s to say, me—will make sure you’ve got enough to feed the bairns and your wife. Don’t you go running faint-hearted to the tyrants to beg for work.”
Boucher turned around, his face so white, gaunt, and streaked with tears that his very calmness made Margaret weep.
“You know well that a worse tyrant than any master ever was says starve to death, and let all of us starve, before you dare cross the Union. You know that, Nicholas, even if you are one of their leaders. You might all be kind men on your own, but once you’ve banded together, you’ve no more pity for a man than a wild, hunger-maddened wolf.”
Higgins

“Old and young, boy, let ’em all eat, I have it;
Let ’em have ten tire of teeth a-piece, I care not.”

Margaret walked home weighed down by all she had seen and heard at the Higgins cottage, her mind so full of the small, brutal miseries of the strike that she could barely rouse herself to meet the quiet duties waiting for her. Her mother, now too unwell to leave the house, always greeted even the shortest of Margaret’s walks as a chance to hear news from the wider world, and expected a steady stream of cheerful chat to pass the hours.

“And did your factory friend agree to come on Thursday, to see you all dressed up for the Thorntons’ dinner?” Mrs Hale asked.

Margaret’s face fell. “She was so unwell I never even thought to ask her.”

“Dear me, it seems everyone is ill these days,” Mrs Hale said, with the faint, petty jealousy one invalid feels for another who commands more sympathy. Her kind nature won out a moment later, though, and her old Helstone habits of thought crept back in. “It must be so sad, being sick in one of those narrow little back streets. It’s bad enough being poorly here. What did you do to help her, Margaret? Mr Thornton dropped off some of his old port wine while you were out. Would a bottle of that do her any good, do you think?”

“No, mamma. I don’t think they are desperately poor — at least, they don’t speak as if they are — and Bessy’s illness is consumption. She won’t want wine. I thought, maybe, I could bring her a little of that preserve we made from our dear Helstone orchard fruit. No, wait — there’s another family I’d rather give that to — oh mamma, mamma! How can I possibly dress up in my finery and go off to some fancy party, when I’ve seen such sorrow today?” Margaret burst, spilling all the details of the dead baby, the starving Boucher children, and Bessy’s ragged cough before she could check herself.

The story distressed Mrs Hale terribly. She grew restless, irritated until she could do something to fix the harm, and ordered Margaret to pack a hamper of food, warm blankets, and extra remedies right there in the drawing room, to be dispatched that very minute. She almost snapped at Margaret when her daughter pointed out there was no rush, that Higgins had already covered their immediate needs and Margaret had left money with Bessy, calling her unfeeling for suggesting the hamper could wait until morning. She did not rest easy until the basket was carried out the door.

Then she paused, and a shadow crossed her face. “Wait — after all, we might have done wrong. Only last time Mr Thornton was here, he said you can never be a true friend to the turn-outs if you give them help that lets them drag the strike out. This Boucher man is one of the strikers, isn’t he?”

Mr Hale, fresh from a tutoring session with Thornton that had drifted into their usual long, wide-ranging conversation, came upstairs just then, and his wife put the question to him. Margaret was too wound up and upset to care if their hamper prolonged the conflict, far too preoccupied with the immediate human cost to think through the longer consequences. Mr Hale listened carefully, trying to judge the matter as calmly as a magistrate, recalling all the clear, logical arguments Thornton had laid out for him barely half an hour before. He landed on a gentle, unsatisfactory compromise.

“For this family, at this moment, you two did exactly the right thing. I can’t imagine you could have done otherwise,” he said. “Still, as a general rule, what Thornton says holds true. If this strike drags on, the masters will just bring in labour from other towns — if the end result isn’t, as it so often has been before, some new machine that cuts the number of workers we need entirely. The kindest thing for the strikers in the long run is not to prop them up in their folly. But I will go see Boucher first thing tomorrow, and see what else can be done for him.”

Mr Hale set out early the next morning, as he promised. Boucher was not at home, but he had a long, gentle talk with his wife, promised to secure her an infirmary admission note, and saw that the children, left to run wild downstairs while their mother talked, were making quick, happy work of the hamper the Hales had sent. He came home with a far more cheerful, hopeful account than Margaret had dared to hope for; his imagination, primed by Margaret’s dramatic stories of the night before to expect far worse, softened the edges of what he had seen, making the little cottage seem far cosier and more stable than it really was.

“But I will go back, and speak to the man himself,” he said. “I can hardly get used to these Milton houses. They have furniture our Helstone labourers would never dream of buying, and eat food our villagers would consider a luxury — yet the moment their weekly wages stop, their only resource is the pawn shop. You have to learn an entirely new standard for what counts as hardship, up here in Milton.”

Bessy, too, was a little better that day. She was still so weak that the feverish wish she had once expressed to see Margaret dressed up for the dinner seemed to have slipped entirely from her mind, a half-delirious fancy rather than a real desire. Margaret could not help comparing the heavy-hearted, joyless task of getting ready for a party she did not care to attend to the giddy, silly girlish toilettes she and Edith had shared barely a year before, back at Helstone. The only small pleasure she took in decking herself out was the thought that her mother would enjoy seeing her look nice.

Dixon threw open the drawing room door a moment later, eager for admiration. “Miss Hale looks well, ma’am — doesn’t she? Mrs Shaw’s coral set could not have come at a better time. It gives just the right touch of colour, else Miss Margaret, you’d look far too pale.”

Margaret’s thick, glossy black hair would not hold a plait; she twisted it tight into heavy, smooth coils that wrapped around her head like a dark crown, gathered into a large spiral knot at the nape of her neck. She secured it with two long coral pins, small as arrows, looped the white silk of her sleeves with strings of matching coral, and wore a heavy string of the same red beads low on her milk-white throat, just below the curve of her jaw.

“Oh Margaret!” Mrs Hale sighed. “How I wish I could be going with you to one of the old Barrington assemblies, taking you the way Lady Beresford used to take me.”

Margaret kissed her mother for that little burst of maternal pride, but she could barely smile, her heart was so heavy. “I would far rather stay home with you, mamma.”

“Nonsense, darling! Be sure to pay close attention to the dinner, won’t you? I want to hear all about how they do things here in Milton. Especially the second course, dear — see what they serve instead of game.”

Mrs Hale would have been astonished past words if she could have seen the sumptuous spread on the Thorntons’ dining table. Margaret, whose London upbringing had taught her to prefer quiet, refined elegance, found the sheer quantity of delicacies almost oppressive; half the number of dishes would have been more than enough, and far more tasteful. But lavish hospitality was one of Mrs Thornton’s non-negotiable rules: every single dish had to have enough portions for every guest to try it, if they wished. Though she lived simply, almost abstemiously in her daily habits, she was proud to set a feast before guests who valued it, and her son shared that sentiment. He had never known any high society that did not revolve around the exchange of superb meals, and even now, when he was denying himself every unnecessary sixpence and had more than once regretted sending out the dinner invitations, he was glad to see the grand, careful spread laid out.

Margaret and Mr Hale were the first to arrive, for he was anxiously punctual to the minute they had been invited. The drawing room was empty save for Mrs Thornton and Fanny. Every cover had been taken off the furniture, and the room blazed with yellow silk damask and a carpet printed so thick with bright flowers it made your eyes tired. Every corner was stuffed with ornament, a jarring contrast to the ugly, wide mill-yard visible through the windows, its great folding gates propped open for carriages to drive through. The mill loomed tall on the left side of the panes, its many storeys casting a long shadow that darkened the bright summer evening long before the sun began to set.

“My son was tied up with business until the very last moment. He will be here directly, Mr Hale. Please, sit down,” Mrs Thornton said.

Mr Hale was standing at one of the windows, and he turned to her. “Don’t you find living so close to the mill rather unpleasant, at times? The smoke, the noise — the constant stream of workers coming and going?”

Mrs Thornton drew herself up stiffly. “Never. I am not so grand that I want to forget where my son’s wealth and power come from. Besides, there is no other factory in Milton half as fine. One single room is two hundred and twenty square yards.”

“I meant no insult — I was thinking of the steam and the oily fumes,” Mr Hale said.

“I quite agree with Mr Hale!” Fanny put in. “There’s a permanent smell of hot steam and machine oil, and the noise is positively deafening.”

“I have heard ‘music’ far more deafening than that,” Mrs Thornton said, her face hard. “The engine room is at the far street end of the factory. We barely hear it, save in summer, when all the windows are open. As for the murmur of the workers, it disturbs me no more than the hum of a beehive. If I think of it at all, I connect it all to my son — remember that every part of it belongs to him, that he is the head directing every piece. Just now, there is no sound from the mill at all. The hands have been ungrateful enough to turn out, as you have no doubt heard. The business I was attending to when you arrived? All about the steps he will take to make them learn their proper place.”

Her face, always stern, darkened to angry fury as she spoke. The expression did not soften even when John Thornton walked into the room, for she saw at once the weight of care and worry he could not hide — though he greeted his guests with a cheer that felt warm and genuine. He shook Margaret’s hand, the first time their hands had ever touched, though Margaret did not notice the fact at all. He asked after Mrs Hale, listened to Mr Hale’s bright, hopeful account of visiting the Boucher cottage, then glanced over at Margaret to see if she agreed with her father’s rosy assessment, and saw no shadow of disagreement on her face.

The sight of her stopped him short. He had never seen her dressed like this before, and it struck him that this elegant finery suited her noble bearing and lofty, serene face so perfectly that she ought to wear clothes like this always. She was talking to Fanny, but he could not hear what they said. He watched his sister fidgeting, adjusting her gown every few seconds, her eyes wandering aimlessly around the room with no purpose, and contrasted that restlessness uneasily with Margaret’s large, soft eyes, steady on whatever her companion was saying, as if a gentle hush radiated from them. The curve of her red lips, just parted as she listened; her head tilted slightly forward, making a long, graceful line from the crown of her glossy raven hair down to the smooth ivory tip of her shoulder; her round white arms and tapered hands, laid lightly across each other, perfectly still. Thornton sighed, taking all that in in one quick, comprehensive glance, then turned his back on the two young women and threw himself wholeheartedly into conversation with Mr Hale.

More guests began to pile in, one after another. Fanny left Margaret’s side to help her mother receive them. Thornton noticed that no one was speaking to Margaret, and grew restless at the apparent neglect — but he did not go near her, did not even look at her. He just knew exactly what she was doing, far better than he tracked the movements of anyone else in the room. Margaret, meanwhile, was so unselfconscious, and so amused watching all the new faces, that she never once wondered if she was being overlooked. Someone led her down to dinner, but she did not catch his name, and he made no effort to talk to her. The gentlemen were deep in very animated debate, while most of the ladies stayed quiet, making mental notes of the food and quietly criticising each other’s gowns. Margaret soon picked up the thread of the general conversation, and leaned in to listen carefully.

Mr Horsfall, the visiting stranger who had inspired the whole dinner party, was asking questions about Milton’s trade and manufacturing, and the rest of the gentlemen — all mill-owners — were giving him answers and explanations. A dispute broke out, heated and earnest, and the group referred the question to Thornton, who had barely spoken all evening. He laid out his opinion, and the logic underpinning it, so clearly that even his opponents yielded. Margaret watched him, struck by how straightforward, simple, modest and dignified he was in his own home, among his peers. She had never seen him look so fine. When he had come to their cottage, he had always seemed over-eager, or prickly with a quiet annoyance that assumed he was being judged unfairly, too proud to ever try and explain himself. But here, surrounded by equals, there was no uncertainty about his position. Everyone saw him as a man of great character, immense force. He did not have to fight for their respect; he already had it, and he knew it, and that quiet security lent his voice and his manner a grand, easy calm Margaret had never seen in him before.

He was not used to talking to ladies, and what little he said to them was slightly formal. He barely spoke to Margaret at all. She was surprised to find how much she enjoyed the dinner. She knew enough of Milton’s local interests now, even some of the technical jargon the mill-owners tossed around as they talked, and she found herself silently taking a very firm side in their debate. They talked with desperate, unfeigned earnestness, not the tired, rehearsed small talk that had made London parties so wearisome to her. She wondered that none of them mentioned the looming strike. She did not know yet how coolly the masters regarded it, as an inconvenience with only one possible end. The men were cutting their own throats, they agreed, just as they had done so many times before. If they were foolish enough to put themselves in the hands of rascally paid union delegates, they would have to take the consequences. One or two of them thought Thornton looked out of sorts, and of course he would lose money over the turn-out — but it was an accident that could happen to any of them, and Thornton was as capable of managing a strike as any man alive, as hard as iron. The workers had picked the wrong man to try and fool, and they all chuckled quietly at the thought of the strikers’ coming discomfiture.

The drawing room felt rather dull for Margaret after the men went off to drink. She was relieved when the gentlemen filed back in, not just so she could catch her father’s eye and chase away her sleepiness, but so she could listen to conversations far bigger than the petty gossip about dresses and dinner the ladies had been passing around. She liked the unapologetic sense of power these Milton men carried, the giddy intoxication that came from remembering what they had built already, and imagining what they would build in future. Their confidence could tip over into boasting, but they defied the old limits of what was thought possible, too caught up in their anticipated triumphs over the natural world to think of themselves, or even the present moment, half the time.

She started a little when Mr Thornton spoke right at her elbow. “I could see you were on our side in that discussion at dinner, weren’t you, Miss Hale?”

“Certainly. But I know so little about it. I was surprised, though, to hear from Mr Horsfall that there are people who think so completely the opposite way — that man Morison he was talking about. He cannot be a gentleman, can he?”

“I am hardly the right person to judge anyone else’s ‘gentlemanliness’, Miss Hale. I don’t quite follow how you’re using the word. But I will say that Morison is no true man. I don’t know him, I’m just judging from what Horsfall said of him.”

“I think my idea of ‘gentleman’ includes your ‘true man’,” Margaret said.

“And a great deal more, you mean to imply. I disagree. To me, a man is a far higher, far more complete being than a gentleman.”

“What do you mean?” Margaret asked, frowning. “We must be defining the word differently.”

“I take it ‘gentleman’ only describes how a person behaves in relation to other people. But when you call someone ‘a man’, you are thinking of him not just relative to his fellow men, but relative to himself — to life, to time, to eternity. A castaway as alone as Robinson Crusoe, a man locked in a dungeon for his whole life, even a saint exiled on Patmos: his endurance, his strength, his faith, all of that is better described as the mark of a man, than the mark of a gentleman. I am rather tired of the word ‘gentlemanly’ these days. It gets misused constantly, twisted out of all recognition, while the simple, honest weight of the words ‘man’ and ‘manly’ go unappreciated. I think it is just part of the empty cant of the age.”

Margaret paused, turning the thought over in her head, but before she could speak her slow, carefully considered opinion, Thornton was called away by a group of eager mill-owners, whose words she could not hear. She could guess what they were saying, though, from Thornton’s short, clear answers, steady and firm as the boom of a distant cannon. They were clearly talking about the strike, suggesting different courses of action. She heard Thornton say, “That has already been done.” Then came a hurried murmur as two of the men spoke at once. “All those arrangements are made already.”

Mr Slickson grabbed hold of Thornton’s arm, trying to emphasise the doubts and difficulties he was naming. Thornton moved slightly away, lifted his eyebrows just a little, and said: “I take the risk. None of you need join me in it unless you choose to.” More fears were voiced, but he shook his head. “I am not afraid of something as cowardly as arson. We are open enemies.

“On earth is known to none
The smile that is not sister to a tear.”

Margaret Hale walked home at her father’s side, the cool fine night air stinging her cheeks, her pretty white silk gown hitched up above her ankles just as the old ballad’s Leezie Lindsay kilted her green satin gown, light enough on her feet that she might have broken into a giddy dance down the clean, lamplit Milton pavements, dizzy with the sharp, fresh thrill of the evening.

“I rather suspect Mr. Thornton is far less easy in his mind over this strike than he lets on,” Mr. Hale said, breaking their quiet stroll. “He looked anxious, all through the dinner conversation tonight.”
“I should wonder if he were not,” Margaret replied. “Though he kept that usual unshakable cool of his when the other manufacturers threw out their wild suggestions, just before we left the dining room.”
“He did the same all through dinner. It would take an earthquake to rattle his measured tone, but I could not miss the tight line around his mouth, the furrow between his brows. He is frightened, for all his steely calm.”
“I would be, too, were I in his shoes. He must see how the anger of his workers swells, barely smothered under their polite faces—all of them think him the ‘hard man’ of the Bible, not so much cruel as utterly unfeeling. Clear-judging, rigidly clinging to his own ‘rights’ in a way no mortal ought, when you consider how tiny all our petty claims look in the sight of God. I am glad you see his anxiety, too. When I think of Boucher, half-mad with hunger and rage the other day, I can scarcely bear how lightly Mr. Thornton dismissed the workers’ grievances tonight.”
“Hold, child,” Mr. Hale soothed. “I do not deny the man is hard pressed, and Boucher is certainly suffering at this moment. But the Unions have a never-ending, mysterious supply of funds for their members, from all I hear. And you yourself said Boucher is a man of hot, passionate temperament, who makes every feeling he feels twice as loud as it ought to be.”
“Oh, papa!” Margaret protested, half-laughing.
“I only ask you to do Thornton justice,” he went on. “He is the exact opposite of Boucher—proud to a fault, far too proud to ever show a sliver of what he feels beneath that stony surface. The very sort of character I would have thought you would admire, Margaret.”
“I do admire him! I do,” she said quickly. “I just do not feel as certain as you are that there is any softness under that granite exterior. He is a man of immense strength of character, sharp, unusual intellect for all the lack of formal schooling he had as a boy.”
“His life has been no idle one,” her father corrected. “He has had to exercise judgment and self-control every single day since he was a child, and that builds a kind of intelligence no book can teach. To be sure, he lacks that grounding in the history of past ages that gives you a sure footing to guess what the future may bring—but he knows that gap in his own knowledge, and that is no small thing. You are quite prejudiced against him, Margaret, I think.”
“He is the first manufacturer, the first man who makes his living in trade, that I have ever gotten to know well,” she smiled. “He is my first olive, papa—let me make a face while I swallow the first bitter bite. I know he is good of his kind, and by and by I shall grow to like the whole kind. I think I am already halfway there. I was so absorbed in all their talk of labour and mills tonight, I hardly understood half of the jargon, and Miss Thornton had to come fetch me away to the other end of the drawing room, saying she was sure I would be uncomfortable being the only lady among so many gentlemen. I had not even noticed. And the other ladies there were so dull, papa—so dreadfully dull! Their talk reminded me of that old game we used to play at Helstone, where each of us had to weave a list of set nouns into a natural-sounding sentence.”
“What on earth do you mean, child?” Mr. Hale asked, bewildered.
“Why, they took turns dropping little words that marked wealth—their under-gardeners, the number of panes of glass in their greenhouses, the price of their lace shawls, their diamonds—each of them working every single one of those boastful little details into their conversation as if they had stumbled on it by accident, not planned it out for an hour beforehand.”
“You will be just as proud of our one little servant when we get her, if half of what Mrs. Thornton says about her is true,” he laughed.
“I am sure I shall. I felt like such a hypocrite tonight, sitting there in my fine white silk gown with my idle hands folded in my lap, thinking of all the hard, messy housework Dixon and our new girl will have done all day. I’ll warrant they all thought me a very grand lady indeed.”
“Even I was fool enough to think you looked every inch a lady, my dear,” Mr. Hale said, smiling gently.

The smile died on his lips, turning his face white and trembling, the second Dixon pulled open their front door.
“Oh master! Oh Miss Margaret! Thank the good Lord you are home!” the maid gasped, her face streaked with tears. “Dr. Donaldson is here. The neighbour next door fetched him, for the charwoman had gone home for the night. She’s easier now—but sir, I thought she’d die an hour ago.”
Mr. Hale’s hand clamped tight on Margaret’s arm to keep himself from falling. He looked at her, and saw not the wild terror that had seized his own unprepared heart, but quiet, awed sorrow, as if she had half suspected this blow was coming all along.
“Oh! Wicked, wicked daughter that I am, I should never have left her!” Margaret moaned, as she half-supported her trembling father, hurrying him up the stairs.

Dr. Donaldson met them on the landing, his face grave. “The opiate has taken effect,” he whispered. “The spasms were very severe—no wonder your maid was terrified. But she will rally this time.”
This time. The words hung sharp in the air. “Let me go to her,” Margaret pleaded.
Half an hour before, Mr. Hale had been a sturdy middle-aged man. Now his eyes were dim, his senses reeling, his steps tottering as if he had already aged to seventy. Dr. Donaldson guided him into the bedroom, Margaret at their heels. There lay Mrs. Hale, the faint, unmistakable stamp of coming death on her sleeping face. She might be easier for now, she might rest—but Death had already marked her for his own, and it was plain he would soon return to claim what was his.
Mr. Hale stared at her for a long, silent minute. Then he began to shake all over, pulling away from the doctor’s steadying hand to grope for the drawing room door—though a dozen candles, lit in the first wild panic of the crisis, flared bright all around him, he could not see a thing. He staggered into the front room, and fumbled blindly for a chair. Dr. Donaldson wheeled one over, sat him down, and felt his racing pulse.
“Speak to him, Miss Hale,” the doctor said. “We must rouse him.”
“Papa!” Margaret cried, her voice thick with sobs. “Papa, speak to me.”
The blank daze cleared a little from Mr. Hale’s eyes, and he forced himself to focus on her face. “Margaret—you knew this was coming, did you not? Oh, it was cruel of you to hide it from me.”
“No, sir, it was not cruel,” Dr. Donaldson cut in, sharp and decisive. “Miss Hale acted entirely under my orders. It may have been a mistake to keep it from you, but it was never cruel. Your wife will wake a much easier woman tomorrow, I promise you. She had those spasms exactly as I feared she might, though I did not speak of my apprehensions to Miss Hale for fear of distressing her. The opiate I brought with her will give her a long, deep, painless sleep, and that ghastly look on her face that has frightened you so will be gone by dawn.”
“But the disease will not be gone, will it?”
Dr. Donaldson glanced at Margaret. She lifted her head, no plea for soft half-truths in her eyes, and the seasoned doctor who read faces so well saw at once she would rather have the whole unvarnished truth out in the open. “No. The disease is still there. For all our boasting of our skill, we cannot touch it. We can only slow its progress, and ease the pain it brings. Be a man, sir. Be a Christian. Remember the immortality of the soul, which no pain, no mortal sickness, can ever harm or touch.”
All Mr. Hale could reply was choked, broken: “You have never been married, Dr. Donaldson. You do not know what this loss will mean,” and the deep, manly sobs that tore out of him rang through the still dark house like heavy pulses of raw agony.
Margaret knelt beside him, wrapping her arms around him, her tears falling on his hands. No one there could tell how many hours slipped by, lost in grief. It was Mr. Hale who roused himself first, to speak of the hard, practical necessities of the moment. “What must we do?” he asked. “Tell us both. Margaret is my staff—my right hand, now.”
Dr. Donaldson gave clear, sensible instructions. There was no danger for that night—indeed, no immediate danger for many days yet. But there was no hope of full recovery. He advised Mr. Hale to go to bed, to leave only one watcher by the sickbed to conserve their strength. He promised to return before sunrise, and left them with a warm, firm handshake.
They spoke very few words after he was gone, too worn thin by terror and sorrow to do more than lay out a simple plan. Mr. Hale swore he would sit up all night with his wife, and all Margaret could do was persuade him to rest on the drawing room sofa. Dixon bluntly refused to leave her mistress’s side for a second, and Margaret would not have dreamed of going to bed herself, no matter how many times the doctor spoke of husbanding their strength. So Dixon sat in her chair, nodding off, jerking herself awake every few minutes, until at last she surrendered entirely and fell snoring softly. Margaret had thrown off her fine white silk gown in a fit of impatient disgust, and pulled on her soft dressing gown. She felt as if she would never sleep again, all her senses sharpened to twice their normal keenness, tuned to every sound and stir from her mother’s bed.
For two hours, she heard her father restlessly pacing the next room, creeping to the sickbed door every few minutes to listen, too afraid to knock, until Margaret noticed his unseen presence and opened the door to reassure him all was well. At last he fell asleep, and the whole house went still. Margaret sat behind the curtain by the bed, lost in thought. All the interests that had filled her life only a day before felt like faint, distant memories from some other life—Bessy Higgins, poor starving Boucher, the fine dinner at the Thorntons’ house, the streets of Milton outside their door—none of it felt real, next to her mother’s sleeping face. Even their old life in Harley Street felt far away, the days she used to sit tracing her mother’s features in her Aunt Shaw’s face, pining for the quiet cottage at Helstone. The dull gray uneventful winter days back home, that she had once thought tedious beyond bearing, were now the most precious thing in the world. She would have given anything to turn back the clock, to return to those quiet days she had never appreciated half enough. Life felt like nothing but a vain, flickering show, all shadows, all slipping away. It was as if a bell in some high, empty belfry far above the noise of the world was tolling nonstop in her head: All are shadows. All are passing. All is past.
When the gray cool dawn broke, no different from any other happier dawn that had come before, Margaret looked at the three sleeping people in the house one by one. The terrible dark night felt as unreal as a dream itself, a shadow that was already slipping away into the past.
When Mrs. Hale woke, she had no idea how near she had come to death the night before. She was only surprised to see Dr. Donaldson arriving so early, and confused by the anxious, worn faces of her husband and daughter. She agreed to stay in bed that day, complaining only that she felt tired—but the next morning, she insisted on getting up, and the doctor gave his permission for her to sit in the drawing room.
