She was restless, no position feeling comfortable, and by nightfall she was burning with fever. Mr. Hale was utterly listless, unable to make even the smallest decision on his own.
“What can we do to spare mamma another such terrible night?” Margaret asked the doctor on the third day.
“It is the natural reaction to the strong opiates I was forced to give her,” Dr. Donaldson replied. “I think it is far more painful for you to watch her than it is for her to bear. But there is something that may help—if we can get a water-bed, that will take the pressure off her aching joints. I know Mrs. Thornton has one. I was going to call there this afternoon to ask, but I have a very long round of patients today, I may not have time to stop. You look pale from all your nights of watching, Margaret—a brisk two-mile walk to Marlborough Street will do you good. Go ask Mrs. Thornton if she can lend it to us.”
“Of course,” Margaret said at once. “I can go while mamma is napping this afternoon. I am sure she will be happy to lend it to us.”
The doctor’s prediction was right. Mrs. Hale rallied fast that afternoon, looking brighter and more like her old self than Margaret had dared hope. She left her sitting in her easy chair, her hand resting in Mr. Hale’s, who looked far more worn and haggard than his wife. He could even smile now, faint and slow though it was—something Margaret had never thought to see again, only a few nights before.
It was a two-mile walk from their house in Crampton Crescent to Marlborough Street. The August sun beat down hot and heavy on the pavements at three o’clock in the afternoon, too warm to hurry. Margaret walked the first mile and a half lost in her own sombre thoughts, so used to weaving through the crowds of Milton’s busy streets that she scarcely noticed anything unusual. But soon, she realized something was different. The mass of people clogging the road ahead were not moving along to their errands, they were clustered in tight little groups, talking in low, heated voices, buzzing with a kind of roiling, suppressed excitement that hung thick in the air. They made way for her as she passed, and she was too wrapped up in thoughts of her mother to pay much mind at first. It was only when she turned onto Marlborough Street that the full weight of the oppressive, angry atmosphere hit her. A low, distant roar drifted up from every narrow side lane, like the growl of ten thousand furious, indignant voices. The inhabitants of every squalid little cottage in the lanes were clustered on their doorsteps and windowsills, every single eye turned in the direction of the Thornton mill. Some faces were blazing with unbridled rage, others set with cold, relentless threat, others wide with fear or desperate plea. She reached the small side entrance set into the high blank mill wall, and rang the bell, waiting for the porter to answer. Glancing back, she saw the first slow, surging wave of the dark crowd roll down the far end of Marlborough Street, threatening and cresting, before it receded, leaving a dead, ominous quiet hanging over the whole road. None of it sank deep into her preoccupied mind, her thoughts still fixed on her sick mother at home, trying to brace herself for the inevitable loss ahead.
The porter pulled the door open just a crack, barely wide enough for her to slip through. “It’s you, is it, miss?” he breathed, relief flooding his face as he wrenched the door a little wider to let her in. He shot the heavy bolt home hard behind her. “I reckon that lot out there are all coming this way, soon enough.”
“I did notice something unusual,” Margaret said. “But the street looks empty now.”
She crossed the mill yard, and climbed the steps to the Thorntons’ front door. There was no sound of the steam engine’s steady thud and pant, no click of the looms, no clatter of workmen’s voices—only that far, deep, rising roar of the angry crowd, drawing closer every second.

“But work grew scarce, while bread grew dear,
And wages lessened, too;
For Irish hordes were bidders here,
Our half-paid work to do.”

Margaret Hale was ushered into the Thorntons’ drawing-room, the space fallen back to its usual state of dust sheets and furniture covers after the small party of days prior. The summer heat hung thick and heavy, so the windows were propped half open, Venetian blinds pulled down over the glass to block the direct glare—all that filtered through was a grim, gray reflected glow bouncing off the paved street below, twisting every shadow into unnatural shape, tinged green by the slats of the blinds. When Margaret caught her own reflection in the wall mirror, she looked ghastly and wan, her face sallow as unglazed porcelain. She sat waiting, no one coming to greet her. At intervals, a distant, low, multitudinous murmur seemed to drift closer, though there was no wind to carry it; it faded away again into a profound, eerie stillness that hummed in her ears.

At last Fanny Thornton bustled in, cheeks pink and her bonnet askew.
“Mamma will be with you directly, Miss Hale, she bade me apologise for the delay. I expect you’ve heard my brother brought over new labourers from Ireland, haven’t you? The whole of Milton’s turned savage over it, as if he hadn’t every right to hire labour where he can find it. Those stupid, greedy oafs around here refused to work for his offered rate, so now they’ve threatened these poor starving Irish folks so fiercely we daren’t let them step foot outside the mill grounds. You can see them huddled in that top back room of the mill, and they’re going to sleep there tonight to keep them safe from the brutes who won’t work themselves and won’t let any other soul work either. Mamma’s sorting out their food right now, and John’s in there speaking to them—some of the women are crying their eyes out, begging to go back home. Oh, here’s mamma now!”

Mrs. Thornton crossed the threshold with a face set in lines of black, unyielding sternness, and Margaret realised instantly she had arrived at the worst possible moment to press her request. Still, she had only come in answer to Mrs. Thornton’s own repeated invitation to call on them for any assistance she needed while her mother lay ill. She spoke gently, modestly, explaining how her mother had been restless and wracked with pain, and Dr. Donaldson had prescribed a water-bed to ease her symptoms.

She fell silent. Mrs. Thornton did not reply at once. Then she started to her feet with a sharp cry.
“They’re at the gates! Call John, Fanny—run fetch him out of the mill! They’re battering the gates down! Call him, I say!”

At the very same moment, the heavy, trampling tramp of hundreds of feet that Mrs. Thornton had been straining to listen to, ignoring Margaret’s every word, swelled loud just outside the mill wall. A din of angry, wordless voices raged behind the heavy wooden gates, which shook as if the maddened crowd were using their own bodies as battering rams, drawing back a few paces before throwing their full weight against the barrier again. The strong iron-bound gates quivered like thin reeds in a gale under the force of their blows.

Every woman in the house clustered at the windows, transfixed even as they were terrified: Mrs. Thornton, the two housemaids, Margaret all pressed close to the glass. Fanny had already fled, screaming up the stairs as if hounds were at her heels, and thrown herself onto the sofa in the upper parlour, heaving in hysterical sobs. Mrs. Thornton kept her gaze fixed on her son, who was still inside the mill yard. He stepped out of the factory door, looked up at their pale, frightened faces grouped at the window, and gave them a smile meant to steady their courage before he locked the factory door behind him. He called up, bracing his voice, for one of the women to come down and unlock the house’s front door, which Fanny had slammed and bolted behind her in her mad dash upstairs. Mrs. Thornton herself went to do it. The sound of his deep, commanding voice drifting out the door acted on the crowd like the taste of blood to a pack of wolves. Until that moment they had been silent, pouring all their breath into the work of breaking down the gates, but now they let loose a fierce, unearthly groan so raw with hatred that even Mrs. Thornton went white with fear as she stepped back into the parlour ahead of her son.

Thornton stepped inside, a faint flush on his cheeks, his eyes blazing bright as if the call of danger had woken something fierce and noble in him. He looked prouder, taller, a man made grander by defiance even if he was not conventionally handsome. Margaret had always feared she would prove a coward in any real emergency, but now, faced with very real, tangible terror, she forgot her own fear entirely, swept up in a sharp, painful sympathy for every soul trapped in the mess: Thornton, the Irish strangers, even the ragged, starving men raging outside.

Thornton stepped forward frankly.
“I am dreadfully sorry, Miss Hale, that you have walked right into this catastrophe. I fear you may be caught up in whatever risk we have to bear here. Mother—would it not be wiser to move everyone to the back rooms? I cannot be certain the crowd hasn’t already broken through from Pinner’s Lane into the stable yard. You will be far safer back there than at these front windows. Go, Jane!” he added, addressing the head housemaid, and she hurried away, the other servant trailing after her.

“I will not budge,” his mother said flatly. “Where you stand, I stand.” Retreat to the back rooms would have done no good anyway: the crowd had already circled the outbuildings at the rear, and their awful, threatening roar boomed from behind the house too. The two maids fled up to the garrets, screaming and wailing as they went. Thornton smiled a thin, scornful smile as he heard their cries. He glanced over at Margaret, who stood alone by the window nearest the mill. Her eyes were bright, a high flush had risen on her cheeks and lips. She felt his gaze, and turned to ask the question that had been turning over in her head for minutes.
“Where are those poor imported workpeople? Are they inside the factory?”

“Aye. I left them huddled up in a small closet at the top of a back staircase, told them to bolt themselves in there and run for the back exit if they heard the mob break through the mill doors. But it is not the Irish they want. It is me.”

“How long until the soldiers get here?” his mother asked, her voice low but steady.

Thornton pulled out his pocket watch with the same calm composure he brought to every part of his work, did a quick mental calculation.
“Assuming Williams ran straight for the barracks the moment I told him to, and did not have to weave and dodge through the crowd, it will be at least twenty minutes yet.”

“Twenty minutes!” his mother echoed, and for the first time, terror crept into her tone.

“Shut the windows at once, mother,” he exclaimed sharply. “Those gates will not survive another full blow from the crowd. Miss Hale, shut that window, please.”

Margaret pulled the sash down, then crossed to help Mrs. Thornton fumble with the fastenings, her fingers trembling.

For several long, endless minutes, the noise outside fell completely quiet. Mrs. Thornton stared wild-eyed at her son’s face, trying to read the meaning of that sudden stillness in his expression. His features were set into rigid lines of contemptuous defiance, no trace of hope or fear visible there.

Fanny lifted her head from the sofa where she had been sobbing.
“Have they gone?” she whispered.

“Gone?” Thornton said. “Listen.”

They all heard it then: one great, straining collective heave, the slow, groaning creak of wood giving way, the sharp wrench of iron bolts tearing free, and the great, thunderous crash of the massive gates falling flat to the ground. Fanny tottered to her feet, stumbled a step or two towards her mother, and fainted dead away in her arms. Mrs. Thornton lifted her up with a strength born as much of iron will as physical force, and carried her out of the room.

“Thank God for that,” Thornton muttered, watching her go. “Miss Hale, had you not better go upstairs with them?”

Margaret’s lips formed the word No, but he could not hear her speak over the thunder of hundreds of pairs of feet tramping right past the house wall, and the low, deep, ferocious murmur of angry voices that hummed with a horrible, satisfied greed far worse than their frustrated roars of minutes before.

“Do not be afraid,” he said, trying to reassure her. “I am very sorry you have been trapped in all this alarm, but it will not last much longer. A few more minutes, and the soldiers will be here.”

“Oh God!” Margaret cried out suddenly. “That is Boucher! I know his face, even though he is livid with rage. He is pushing and shoving his way to the very front of the crowd—look, look!”

“Who is Boucher?” Thornton asked coolly, stepping up to the window to peer at the man Margaret was pointing out. The moment the mob caught sight of Thornton’s face at the window, they let loose a yell so inhuman it sounded like the snarl of a wild beast denied the food it has been ravening for. Even Thornton drew back for a second, staggered by the sheer intensity of the hatred he had roused.

“Let them yell,” he said. “In five more minutes—. I only hope those poor Irish folk do not get scared out of their wits by that fiendish noise. Keep your courage for five more minutes, Miss Hale.”

“Do not worry about me,” she said hastily. “But can you do nothing at all to soothe these poor people? It is awful to watch them suffer like this.”

“The soldiers will be here directly, and that will bring them to their senses.”

“Bring them to their senses?” Margaret said quickly. “What kind of sense?”

“The only kind that works on men who have turned themselves into wild beasts. By heaven! They are turning for the mill doors!”

“Mr. Thornton,” Margaret said, shaking all over with the force of her passion, “go down this instant, if you are no coward. Go out and face them like a man. Save those poor strangers you invited here. Speak to your workmen as if they are human beings. Speak to them kindly. Do not let the soldiers come in and cut down men who are half driven mad by hunger. I see one of them right there who is. If you have any spark of courage or honour in you, go out to them, speak man to man!”

He turned and looked at her as she spoke, a dark, heavy shadow crossing his face, his teeth clenched tight.
“I will go. Perhaps I can ask you to follow me downstairs, and bar the front door behind me. My mother and sister will need that extra protection.”

“Oh, Mr. Thornton! I do not know—maybe I am wrong—only—”

But he was already gone, thudding down the stairs to the hall, unbolting and unbarring the heavy front door. All Margaret could do was rush after him, slam the door shut and bolt it behind him, then stumble back up the stairs with a sick, heavy heart and a dizzy head. She took her place again at the farthest window. There he was, standing on the front steps, hundreds of angry eyes fixed on him. She could not see his face, nor could she hear anything above the low, rolling murmur of the crowd’s savage satisfaction.

She wrenched the window wide open. Most of the men in the crowd were just boys: cruel, thoughtless boys, cruel precisely because they were too young to think through the consequences of their actions. The rest were men, gaunt as starving wolves, wild for relief. Margaret knew exactly what drove them. They were like Boucher, with starving children waiting at home, and they had counted on winning higher wages by holding out together—only to find Irish men brought in to steal the bread right out of their little ones’ mouths. She could read that desperation written all over Boucher’s face, forlorn and livid with rage. If only Thornton would speak to them, let them hear his voice, it would be better than this wild, useless beating against the stony silence that gave them not even a word of anger or reproach.

For a second the noise fell quiet, still and wordless as the hush before a pack of wolves charges. Margaret tore off her bonnet, leaned far out of the window to hear. She saw it all then: Thornton had tried to speak, but the momentary hush of attention had already passed, and the crowd was raging worse than ever. He stood with his arms folded across his chest, still as a marble statue, his face pale with repressed fury. The men were jeering at him, trying to make him flinch, each urging the next on to some sudden act of personal violence. Margaret knew instantly that the violence was only seconds away: the first touch would spark an explosion, and among hundreds of infuriated men and reckless boys, even Thornton’s life would not be safe. Even as she watched, lads at the back of the crowd stooped to yank off their heavy wooden clogs, the readiest missiles they could lay their hands on. That was the spark to the gunpowder. With a cry no one could hear, Margaret ran out of the room, down the stairs. She hefted the great iron bar off the front door with a strength that felt like it did not belong to her, wrenched the door wide open, and stepped out face to face with that roaring sea of angry men. Her eyes blazed at them, sharp with reproach. The clogs hung arrested in the lads’ hands. Faces that had looked fiendish a minute before now looked confused, uncertain, wondering what on earth a fine lady was doing stepping out into the middle of their riot.

She could not speak for breath at first, only held her arms out towards them until she could catch her voice.
“Oh do not use violence! He is one man, and you are hundreds,” she called out, but her voice was hoarse, no more than a whisper, her words barely carrying. Thornton had stepped a little to one side, moving out from behind her as if he resented anything standing between him and the danger he was meant to face.

“Go!” she cried again, this time her voice ringing out like a sob. “The soldiers have been sent for—they are on their way. Go peaceably. Leave now. You will get relief for whatever grievances you have, I promise you.”

“Will you send those Irish blackguards back home, then?” a man shouted from the crowd, his voice thick with threat.

“Never, at your bidding!” Thornton roared back. The storm broke at once. Hoots and jeers rose to fill the air, but Margaret did not hear them. Her eyes were fixed on the group of lads who had armed themselves with their clogs. She saw their arm drawn back, she knew what they meant to do. If that clog hit Thornton, he would be struck down dead. She did not think of herself at all, only of saving him. She threw her arms around him, using her own body as a shield between him and the crowd. He shook her off roughly, his arms still folded.

“Go away,” he rumbled, his voice deep and hard. “This is no place for a woman.”

“It is,” she said. “You did not see what I saw.” She had thought her presence, her sex, would stop the crowd, make them pause, think, slink away ashamed. She was wrong. Their reckless passion had carried them too far to turn back, the cruel, excitable boys at the front leading the charge, reckless of what blood they spilled. A clog whizzed through the air. Margaret watched it fly, her eyes fixed. It missed its mark, and she turned sick with fright, but did not move an inch, only buried her face against Thornton’s arm.

“For God’s sake!” she called out to the crowd. “Do not ruin your own cause with this violence. You do not know what you are doing.” She was forcing her voice to be clear and steady, when a sharp pebble flew through the air, grazing her forehead and her cheek. A blinding sheet of white light burst before her eyes, and she slumped like a dead weight against Thornton’s shoulder.

He unfolded his arms then, holding her for a single second against his chest.
“You do well!” he roared at the crowd, his voice shaking with rage. “You come to turn out innocent, starving strangers. You hundreds, against one lone man, and when a woman steps out to beg you for your own sakes to act like reasonable human beings, your cowardly wrath falls on her! You do well!”

The crowd went dead silent. They stared, open-mouthed and wide-eyed, at the thin thread of dark red blood trickling down her face, the sight jolting them straight out of their maddened trance. The men nearest the gate slipped away, ashamed. A ripple of retreat moved through the whole mob. Only one voice called out as they fled:
“That stone was meant for thee! Thou wert only hiding behind a woman!”

Thornton quivered with rage. The flow of blood had roused Margaret to faint, dim consciousness. He laid her gentle down on the front door step, her head resting against the frame.
“Can you rest there for a moment?” he asked. Without waiting for an answer, he stepped slowly down the steps, right into the middle of the last lingering knots of the crowd. “Now kill me, if that is your brutal will. There is no woman to shield me here. You may beat me to death—you will never, ever make me change my mind on this matter. Not you!” He stood amongst them, his arms still folded across his chest, exactly the same defiant pose he had held on the steps.

The retreat turned into a full, panicky flight. Maybe it was the sound of the soldiers’ boots clanking in the distance, maybe it was the sight of Margaret’s pale upturned face, still and still as marble, tears seeping out through her tangled eyelashes, and the slower, heavier drip of blood falling down from her temple. Even Boucher, the most desperate of all of them, drew back, faltered away, scowled, and vanished around the corner, muttering curses under his breath. The moment the last of the rioters were out

“Which when his mother saw, she in her mind Was troubled sore, he wist well what to ween.” — SPENSER

Margaret Hale had not been gone five minutes when Mr. Thornton burst through his own front door, face glowing hot from exertion and the raw, lingering thrum of the riot. “I could not come sooner — the superintendent would not release me— Where is she?” He scanned the empty dining room in two quick strides, then fixed his mother with a near-fierce gaze, as she slowly straightened the furniture the mob had knocked askew, and did not answer at once. “Where is Miss Hale?” he repeated, sharper this time.

“Gone home,” she said shortly.

“Gone home?” The shock sent him pacing back and forth across the rug, his boots thudding hard against the floorboards. “She could not possibly have been fit to walk that far.”

“She had a cab,” Mrs. Thornton said. “Mr. Lowe signed off that she was well enough to travel. I fetched him myself, you know. Her injury was never half so bad as it looked — some people faint dead away at the smallest knock, that is all.”

“Thank you for that, mother.” He paused, half extending his hand to shake hers in gratitude, but she did not notice the gesture, her eyes fixed straight ahead.

“What have you done for the Irish labourers caught up in the crowd?” she asked.

“Sent them all to the Dragon Inn for a hot square meal, poor wretches. By some luck I ran into Father Grady on the street right after, and persuaded him to come speak to them, to talk them out of clearing out of Milton as a group. Margaret could not have walked home, surely?”

“Everything was paid for and seen to properly, never you fear,” she said, dismissive. “Let us talk of something else. That girl has caused more than enough disturbance this afternoon.”

“I do not know what would have become of me but for her,” Thornton said quietly.

“Are you so helpless now that you must be defended by a slip of a girl?” his mother scoffed, her voice sharp with scorn.

He flushed bright red. “Not one girl in a hundred would have thrown herself between the blows meant for me, blows that were struck with full, vicious intent.”

“A girl in love will do a great many foolish things,” Mrs. Thornton replied, brief and unyielding.

“Mother!” He stepped forward, then froze, his whole frame heaving with suppressed passion. She started a little at the obvious, enormous effort he was expending just to hold his temper in check. She could not parse the tangle of emotion on his face — joy, anger, pride, wild surprise, breathless doubt all warring beneath his glowing eyes, his chest rising and falling fast — only its raw, unnameable intensity. It made her uneasy, as any strong feeling whose root you do not fully understand always does. She crossed to the sideboard, pulled open a drawer, and took out a duster stowed there for odd jobs, pretending to wipe a stray drop of eau de Cologne off the polished sofa arm. She kept her back turned to him far longer than the task required, and when she spoke her voice was tight, constrained, nothing like her usual crisp, decisive tone.

“I suppose you have taken steps about the rioters? You do not expect more violence tonight, surely? Where were the police, anyway? Never around when you actually need them!”

“On the contrary, I saw three or four of them struggling to hold the crowd back the second the yard gates gave way, and a dozen more came running just as the mob scattered. I could have arrested half a dozen of the ringleaders right then if I had been thinking straight. There will be no trouble identifying them later, plenty of witnesses saw their faces.”

“Will they not come back after dark?”

“I am off to arrange for a full, permanent guard on the mill premises. I am meeting Captain Hanbury at the station in half an hour.”

“Sit and have some tea first,” she said. “You cannot run about on an empty stomach.”

“Tea — yes, I suppose I must. It is half past six now, and I may be out for hours. Do not sit up waiting for me, mother.”

“You would have me go to bed before I know you are safe?” she retorted.

He hesitated. “Well, perhaps not. But if I have time after sorting the police arrangements and meeting Hamper and Clarkson, I was going to swing round by Crampton.” Their eyes locked, and they stared at each other intently for a long, quiet minute.

“Why would you go to Crampton?” she asked.

“To ask after Miss Hale.”

“I will send Williams. He can take that water-bed Margaret came to beg for, and ask after her state while he is there.”

“I must go myself.”

“Not merely to ask how she fares?”

“No. Not merely that. I have to thank her for the way she stood between me and that mob.”

“What on earth possessed you to go down to the yard in the first place? You might as well have stuck your head straight in the lion’s mouth!”

He glanced at her sharply, and saw that she had no idea of the quiet, charged exchange he and Margaret had shared in his drawing room before the riot turned violent. He deflected the question smoothly. “Will you be afraid to stay here alone without me until the police patrol arrives? Or ought I send Williams for them now, so they can be here by the time we finish tea? There is no time to waste, I have to be out the door in fifteen minutes.”

Mrs. Thornton left the room, her directions to the servants muddled and uncertain — a stark contrast to her usual sharp, unwavering orders. Left alone, Thornton stared at the dining table, trying to force his mind to focus on the business he had to conduct at the station, but every other thought slipped away back to Margaret: the weight of her arms around his neck, the soft, desperate pressure of her clinging to him, the way the colour rose and fell in his own cheeks every time he replayed the moment. The tea meal would have been utterly silent if not for Fanny chattering nonstop about her own terror that afternoon, how she had been certain they would all be murdered, then thought the mob was gone, then grew so faint and sick her whole body shook.

“That is quite enough,” her brother said, pushing back his chair to stand. “I saw the real thing with my own eyes, I have no need to hear your dramatic retelling of it.” He was half way to the door when his mother laid a hand firm on his arm to stop him.

“Come back here to this house first before you go to the Hales’,” she said, low and anxious. Fanny, hovering in the doorway, smiled to herself, certain she had guessed every secret.

“Why? Will it be too late to disturb them?”

“John, come back to me this one evening. It will be late for Mrs. Hale, that is true, but that is not why I ask. Tomorrow you will — come home first tonight, John!” She almost never pleaded with her son, too proud to beg for anything, but she had never once asked him for something he refused.

“I will come straight back here as soon as I finish my business. You will make sure to ask after them — after her, won’t you?”

When he returned later that night, Mrs. Thornton was unusually attentive, her eyes and ears fixed on every detail he gave of the steps he had taken to secure the mill, to punish the rioters who had dared threaten his property and his life. To him it was simple: punishment was the natural, just consequence for anyone who had joined the riot, and property must be protected, the will of the factory owner cutting clean and sharp as a sword to its end.

“Mother,” he said suddenly, mid-step as he paced the room, “you know what I am going to say to Miss Hale tomorrow?”

The question caught her off guard; for a moment she had forgotten Margaret entirely, lost in her own thoughts. She looked up at him. “Yes. I do. You can hardly do anything else.”

“Do anything else? I do not follow you.”

“I mean that, after letting your feelings for her get so far, after the way she rushed to your defense, you are bound in honour to —”

“Bound in honour?” he laughed, scornful. “I am afraid honour has little to do with it. ‘Her feelings overcame her?’ What feelings are you talking about?”

“Now John, do not be angry. Did she not fling herself at you, clinging to your neck to save you from harm?”

“She did,” he said, his voice softening. “But mother — I dare not let myself hope. I was never faint-hearted about anything before, but I cannot bring myself to believe a creature like that could care for me.”

“Do not be a fool, John. To hear you talk, she might as well be a duke’s daughter! What more proof could you possibly need that she cares for you? I imagine she fought that aristocratic, distant pride of hers tooth and nail before she acted. I respect her all the more for seeing past those silly scruples at last.” She smiled slowly, tears glistening in her eyes. “It is no easy thing for me to say this. After tonight, I know I stand second in your heart. I begged you not to go to her straight away because I wanted you all to myself for just a few more hours.”

“Dearest mother!” But his love was selfish, and in the next breath he was back to his own swirling hopes and fears, casting a cold, quiet shadow over Mrs. Thornton’s heart. “But I know she does not care for me. I will lay myself at her feet, I have to. If there was only one chance in a thousand — in a million — that she might return my feelings, I still have to try.”

“Do not be afraid,” his mother said, crushing down her own sharp, stung mortification at how little notice he had taken of her rare, vulnerable display of maternal love, the bitter pang of jealousy that betrayed how fiercely she hated to lose him. “Do not be afraid. As far as love goes, she is worthy of you. It must have taken an enormous force to break through that stiff pride of hers. Goodnight, John.” She kissed his forehead, then turned and left the room slowly, with her usual stately grace. But once she had locked the door to her own bedchamber, she sank down onto her bed and wept tears she had not let fall in decades.

***

Margaret slipped into the Hales’ parlour, where her father and mother sat talking quietly together, her face as pale as fresh linen. She walked all the way up to them before she could trust her voice to speak. “Mrs. Thornton will send the water-bed for you, mamma.”

“Dear child, you look so worn out!” Mrs. Hale fussed. “Is the street air that hot today?”

“Very hot. And the roads are rough, what with the strike going on.” A bright, vivid flush rose in Margaret’s cheeks, then faded away just as fast, leaving her paler than before.

“Bessy Higgins sent round a message not long ago, asking if you could come visit her,” her mother said. “But you look far too tired to go out again tonight.”

“Yes,” Margaret said. “I am tired. I cannot go.” She was quiet, trembling faintly as she made tea, and she was grateful to see her father so occupied tending to her mother that he did not notice how unsteady she was. Even after Mrs. Hale went up to bed, Mr. Hale would not leave Margaret alone, insisting on sitting by her to read aloud until she fell asleep.

At last he too retired, and Margaret was left by herself.

“Now I will think about it — now I will remember all of it,” she told herself. She sat perfectly still in her armchair, hands clasped tight on her knees, lips pressed together, eyes fixed on the wall as if she was staring at a vision only she could see. She drew a long, shaky breath. “I, who hate all public scenes — I, who have always despised people for showing unseemly emotion, who thought anyone who lost their self-control was weak — ran down and threw myself into the middle of that crowd like a silly, romantic fool. Did I even do any good? They would have left anyway, I dare say.” But her sharp, well-honed judgment corrected her instantly. No, they might not have. She had done some good. “But what possessed me to defend that man as if he was a helpless child! No wonder all those rioters thought I was madly in love with him! Me, in love — with him, of all people!” Her pale cheeks burst into flame, and she covered her face with her hands. When she pulled them away, her palms were wet with scalding tears.

“Oh how shameful, that they would all think that of me! I never would have been that brave for anyone else — except that he was so utterly indifferent to the suffering of the men on strike, so blind to how desperate they were, that I could not stand by and let unfairness happen on either side. It was not fair that he should stand there sheltered, waiting for the soldiers to trap those poor, maddened rioters like animals, without even trying to reason with them. And it was worse than unfair for the mob to turn on him and beat him as they threatened. I would do it all again, no matter what anyone says of me. If I stopped even one cruel blow, one needless act of violence, I did a woman’s work. Let them insult my maiden pride all they like. I walk pure before God.” A noble, quiet peace settled over her face, stiller and calmer than chiselled marble.

Dixon knocked and stepped in. “If you please, Miss Margaret, the water-bed has arrived from Mrs. Thornton’s. It is too late to set it up for your mother tonight, I am afraid, she is nearly fast asleep, but it will do her a world of good tomorrow.”

“Thank you. Send our best thanks back to them,” Margaret said. Dixon stepped out for a moment, then returned. “The man who brought it says he is under orders to ask particularly how you are, Miss Margaret. I think he must have meant Mrs. Thornton, but he kept repeating he had to ask after you, specifically.”

“Me?” Margaret drew herself up straight, her voice cold and distant. “I am quite well. Tell him I am perfectly well.” But her face was as white as the linen handkerchief tucked in her pocket, and her head throbbed so fiercely she could barely see straight.

Mr. Hale came in then, fresh from checking on his sleeping wife, eager for Margaret to tell him some light, pleasant story to pass the evening. She bore her pain with gentle patience, not a single word of complaint, digging up dozens of tiny, harmless little topics to chat about — never once mentioning the riot, just thinking of it made her feel sick to her stomach.

“Goodnight, Margaret,” her father said at last, rising to head to his own room. “I shall sleep very well tonight, and you look so pale and worn out from all your watching over us. I will call Dixon if your mother needs anything in the night. Go to bed now and sleep like a top, you poor child, you have earned the rest.”

“Goodnight, papa,” she whispered. She let the false, pleasant smile slip off her face, let her eyes go dull with heavy, throbbing exhaustion. She released the iron grip of her will, which had held her together all evening. Until morning, she did not have to fight the weariness. She lay down on her bed and never stirred. To lift a hand, or a foot, or even a single finger was beyond her power, of will or of muscle. She was so tired, so stunned by the day’s events, that she thought she never slept at all. Her feverish thoughts drifted back and forth across the line between waking and sleeping, replaying the same miserable scenes over and over. She could not escape the feeling that hundreds of eyes were fixed on her, every person from the mob staring at her, judging her, and the shame of it was so acute she half wanted to burrow straight down into the earth to hide — but she could not move, could not slip out from under that unblinking, universal glare.

> “Your beauty was the first that won the place
> And scal’d the walls of my undaunted heart,
> Which, captive now, pines in a caitive case,
> Unkindly met with rigour for desert:—
> Yet not the less your servant shall abide,
> In spite of rude repulse or silent pride.”

Margaret dragged herself out of bed the next morning, breathless with relief that the long, haunted night was over. She had not slept deeply, not refreshed, but the mere passing of the riot’s chaos had granted her a kind of quiet rest. All in the Hale household had stayed peaceful through the dark hours: her mother had woken only once, and the doctor said her fever had dipped at last. A faint, welcome breeze stirred in Milton’s usual heavy, smoke-thick air. No leafy trees lined the town’s cobblestone streets to show the wind’s playful toss, but Margaret could picture it clear as day, far beyond the factory walls, in the hedgerow lanes, the little bramble copses, the thick green woods south of the town—could hear that soft, murmuring rustle, that rushing, dancing sound that sent a little echo of unlooked-for gladness thrumming through her tired chest.

She settled to her sewing in her mother’s bedroom, resolving that once Mrs. Hale’s usual forenoon nap ended, she would help her dress, and after dinner she would walk to the Higgins’ cottage to see Bessy. She would banish every thought of the Thornton family, she told herself. There was no need to waste a moment’s consideration on them until they stood before her in the flesh. But of course, the effort of trying not to think of them only dragged every memory sharper to the front of her mind. Hot, unbidden flushes swept over her pale face, staining her cheeks bright as a sudden sunbeam skittering across a grey, choppy sea.

Dixon opened the door so softly the hinges did not creak, and tiptoed across the carpet to Margaret’s side by the shaded window.
“Mr. Thornton, Miss Margaret. He’s waiting in the drawing-room.”

Margaret’s sewing slipped from her fingers and fell to her lap. “Did he ask for me? Isn’t papa come back from his call on Mr. Bell?”
“He asked for you, miss. Master’s out still.”

“Very well. I will come,” Margaret said, steadying her voice. But she lingered strangely, smoothing down her gown, tucking the stray strands of dark hair that covered the faint bruise on her temple tighter against her skin.

Downstairs, Mr. Thornton stood stiff at the drawing-room window, his back turned to the door, apparently fixed on watching some commotion in the street. In truth, he was fighting a war against himself. His heart thudded heavy and thick at the thought of her stepping into the room. He could not forget the press of her arms around his neck, the day before—at the time he had been too furious, too caught up in the threat of the rioters to feel anything but impatient to get her out of harm’s way, but now the memory of her throwing herself between him and the stones sent a thrill shooting through every limb, melting every iron resolve he had built up over years of running his mill, all his hard-won self-control, like wax held to a flame. He dreaded the moment he would turn and see her, afraid he would stride forward and hold his arms out, wordlessly begging her to nestle there the way she had clung to him unthinking the day before—afraid that this time, he would never let her go. His pulse hammered loud and fast. Strong, steady man that he was, he trembled at the thought of the speech he had rehearsed all night, at the way she might receive it. Half his heart burned with a wild, impatient hope that she would soften, that she would flush and falter and fall into his arms like she was coming home. The other half flinched from the thought of cold, angry rejection, a fate that would wither every bright prospect of his future so thoroughly he refused to even name it to himself.

He only startled to awareness when he felt her presence in the room, quiet as a shadow. He turned, and realized he had not heard her step at all: the clatter of the street carts and factory whistles had drowned out the soft rustle of her muslin gown.

She stood by the table, making no move to sit. Her eyelids were half-drooped over her eyes, her teeth set firm but not clenched, her lips just barely parted to show a faint white line between their curves. Slow, deep breaths only moved her chin and the fine curve of her nostrils; every other line of her face was still and marble-pale. The fine grain of her skin, the soft oval of her cheeks, the rich full outline of her mouth with its corners tucked into deep dimples—all were drained of their usual warm, healthy colour, made paler still by the dark fall of her hair pulled low over her temples to hide the bruise the rioter’s stone had left. Her head tilted just a little back, in that old proud, unyielding stance he had admired from the first day she stepped into his Milton drawing-room. Her long arms hung perfectly still at her sides. She looked exactly like some noble prisoner, falsely accused of a crime she loathed and despised, too indignant to even waste breath justifying herself.

Mr. Thornton took one hasty step forward, then reined himself in, walked quiet and firm to the open door, and shut it. He came back to stand opposite her, pausing for a long moment just to drink in the sight of her, beautiful and fierce, before he dared to break the spell with the words he had to say.

“Miss Hale. I was very ungrateful yesterday—”
“You had nothing to be grateful for,” she cut in, lifting her eyes to look him full and straight in the face. “You mean, I suppose, that you think you owe me thanks for what I did.” Against every effort of her will, a deep hot blush spread all over her face, burning so bright it stung the corners of her eyes—but her gaze never wavered from his, steady and grave. “It was only a natural instinct. Any woman would have done exactly the same. We all hold the sanctity of our sex as a high privilege, to stand between danger and a man when the crowd runs mad. I ought rather to apologise to you,” she added hastily, “for speaking those thoughtless words that sent you down into the thick of the danger in the first place.”
“It was not your words that did it—it was the truth they carried, sharp as it was,” he said. “But you will not turn me aside from this, and let me skip over saying how deeply grateful I am—how—” He was teetering on the edge of the confession he had held back for months. He forced himself to slow, to weigh every word, to master his hot, rushing passion. For a second his will held, and he stopped mid-sentence.
“I do not try to escape anything,” she said. “I only tell you, you owe me no gratitude. And I will add that any expression of it will be painful to me, because I do not feel I deserve it. Still, if speaking will relieve you of even a fancied obligation, you may go on.”
“I do not want to be relieved of that obligation,” he said, stung sharp by her calm. “Fancied or real, I do not stop to question it. I choose to believe I owe you my very life—ay, smile if you will, and call it exaggeration. I believe it, because it makes that life worth more to think that every bit of joy I ever feel, every honest pride I take in my work at the mill, every sharp, bright moment of being alive from this day forward, I owe it all to you.” His voice dropped so low, thick with tender, burning passion, that Margaret shivered where she stood. “To owe all of that to you—you must hear me out,” he said, stepping forward, his tone stern and determined, his hand closing tight around hers. “To the one woman I love, as I do not believe any man ever loved a woman before.”

He panted, waiting to hear her answer. The first icy, faltering words that fell from her lips made him fling her hand away as if it burned him.
“Your way of speaking shocks me. It is blasphemous. I cannot help that being my first feeling. I dare say it might not be, if I understood the kind of feeling you describe. I do not want to vex you, and we must speak softly, for mamma is still asleep—but your whole manner offends me—”
“Offends you!” he exclaimed, raw with hurt. “I am the most unfortunate man alive.”
“Yes,” she said, regaining all her quiet dignity. “I do feel offended, and I think justly. You seem to fancy my conduct yesterday”—the deep carnation blush flooded her cheeks again, but this time her eyes flashed with indignation, not shame—“was some personal act between you and me. You come to thank me for it, instead of seeing, as any gentleman would—yes, a gentleman,” she repeated, echoing their old argument on the very meaning of the word, “that any woman worthy of the name would step forward to shield a man in danger from a violent crowd, with all the revered helplessness of her sex.”
“And the man you rescued is forbidden the small relief of saying thank you!” he broke in, his tone sharp with contempt. “I am a man. I claim the right to speak my feelings plainly.”
“And I yield you that right,” she replied, proud and steady. “I only tell you, insisting on it causes me pain. But you seem to have imagined I was not just following a natural womanly instinct, but”—the tears she had held down for hours, fighting against them fiercely, rose thick in her eyes and choked her voice—“that I was prompted by some particular feeling for you—you! Why, there was not a man in that crowd, not a poor desperate rioter driven to rage by hunger, for whom I did not have more sympathy—for whom I would not have done every small thing in my power far more heartily.”
“Speak on, Miss Hale. I am well aware of these misplaced sympathies of yours,” he said, his voice hard. “I know now that what made you act so nobly was only your innate hatred of oppression—yes, even I, a mill master, may be oppressed, too. I know you despise me. Let me say it is only because you do not understand me.”
“I do not care to understand you,” she said, catching hold of the edge of the table to steady herself. She thought him cruel then, and she was weak from all the day before’s shocks, indignant to the bone.

Margaret pressed her lips tight together. She would not deign to answer such unfair accusations. But for all his savage, sharp words, he longed to throw himself at her feet, to press a kiss to the hem of her gown. She said nothing, did not move. Hot tears of wounded pride spilled fast down her cheeks. He waited, for what felt like an hour, hoping she would say something—even a taunt, any line he could reply to. But she was silent. He picked up his hat.
“One word more. You look as if you thought being loved by me somehow taints you. You cannot avoid it. Nay, I, even if I wished, could not take that feeling back, and cleanse you from it. And I would not if I could. I have never loved any woman before. My life has been too busy, my thoughts too swallowed up by work at the mill to leave any room for it. Now I love, and I will love. But do not fear that I will ever embarrass you with any more unasked-for displays of feeling.”
“I am not afraid,” she said, lifting herself straight, tall and proud. “No one has ever dared to be impertinent to me, and no one ever shall. But Mr. Thornton, you have been so very kind to my father,” she said, her whole tone softening into something gentle and womanly. “Do not let us go on making each other angry. Pray, do not.”

He paid no mind to her plea. He stood smoothing the nap of his hat with his coat sleeve for half a minute, his face set hard, then deliberately ignored the hand she held out to him, turned away before he could let her see the look of raw, breaking grief on his face, and stormed out of the room.

Margaret caught one last glance of his face as he left. When the door clicked shut behind him, she realized she had seen the bright, glistening track of unshed tears in his eyes. In a second, all her hot, proud dislike melted into something softer, and far more painful: sharp, biting self-reproach, for having caused such deep, public mortification to any living soul.
“But how could I help it?” she whispered to herself, leaning against the table. “I never liked him. I was always civil, but I never made any effort to hide my indifference. I never even thought of how he might take my manners, so of course they showed the truth of it. That act yesterday was just an act of decency, nothing more. He mistook it. But that is his fault, not mine. I would do it all again, if there was need—even if it leads me to all this shame and trouble.”

*Revenge may have her own; Roused discipline aloud proclaims their cause, And injured navies urge their broken laws.* — BYRON

Margaret Hale found herself wondering if every marriage proposal landed with the same unforeseen, gutting weight as the two she had received. Unbidden, a comparison rose unspooled in her mind between Henry Lennox and John Thornton. With Lennox, she had felt only gentle regret: circumstances had lured a confession of love out of him that strayed over the line of their easy friendship, and he had seemed almost as embarrassed by the slip as she was, their awkwardness shared.

But this second proposal left her reeling, the deep, rough timbre of Thornton’s voice still ringing in the corners of the room long after he had left. There had been no soft, gradual stretch of friendship between her and the master of Marlborough Mills. Their every interaction had been a series of headstrong clashes, their views on labor, on justice, on the very rhythm of life in Milton forever at odds. She had never once suspected he cared for her opinions, let alone for her as a person; he had brushed off her protests against his harshness as if they were no more than gnat stings, until she grew tired of expending breath on arguments that never landed. And now he had burst in on her, wild and passionate, to lay his love bare.

At first she had assumed his declaration was no more than pity, spurred by the riot the day before, when he had seen her throw herself between him and the rioters, and mistaken that act of kindness for something softer. But before he had even crossed the threshold to leave, certainly no more than five minutes after he had gone, the truth settled over her clear and bright: he loved her. He had loved her for months, he would go on loving her no matter how she pushed him away.

She shuddered as if some great, unyielding force had wrapped itself around her will, alien to the quiet, gentle life she had known back in Helstone. She fled to the far end of the room to hide from the thought of him, but it would not leave her. To twist a line from Fairfax’s translation of Tasso: his strong, stubborn idea wandered through every corner of her thought. She resented him all the more for having forced his way past the guards of her inner life. How dare he claim he would love her still even if she spurned him with all the contempt she could muster? Too late, sharp, cutting replies crowded into her head, things she should have said to drive him off for good. The memory of their interview clung to her like the horror from a half-waking nightmare: no matter how hard she rubbed her eyes, no matter how she forced a stiff, bright smile to her lips, the ghastly thing lurked in the corner of the room, waiting for her to dare speak of it to anyone else.

She could not bear the weight of the air around her a moment longer, and flew to throw open the sash of the window, then wrenched the door open too, desperate to outrun the memory in company or work. But the house lay wrapped in the heavy, midday stillness that settled over Milton homes when invalids caught the shallow, unrefreshing nap that eluded them through the night hours. She would not be alone. Of course, she would go see Bessy Higgins — the message the poor girl had sent the night before came rushing back to her. She set off at once.

When she arrived at the Higgins cottage, Bessy lay stretched flat on the settle, dragged as close to the fire as she could get even though the day was sweltering and close, as if she could not shake a deep, inner chill after a fit of coughing pain. Margaret said nothing, just lifted her gently and adjusted the pillows so she could sit half-upright, easier on her straining lungs.

“I thought I’d never see you again,” Bessy whispered at last, gazing up at her face with heavy, wistful eyes.

“I’m so sorry you’re worse. I couldn’t come yesterday — my mother was very ill, for so many reasons,” Margaret said, her cheeks flushing as she thought of the riot.

“Might’ve thought I was out of my place sending Mary round to fetch you. But the shouting and the wrangling last night tore me all to bits, and when father left, I thought if I could just hear your voice, reading me some words of peace and promise, I could drift off into God’s quiet rest just like a baby lulled to sleep by its mother’s lullaby.”

“Shall I read you a chapter now?”

“Ay, do. I might not take in the sense at first — it’ll seem far away. But when you get to the texts I know and love, the comforting ones, it’ll feel close, like it’s sinking right through me.”

Margaret began to read, but Bessy tossed and turned restlessly on the settle, every effort to pay attention for a second leaving her more convulsed with agitation the next. At last she burst out: “Stop. It’s no use. I’m blaspheming in my own head the whole time, thinking angry thoughts about all the things that can’t be mended. You heard about the riot yesterday down at Marlborough Mills, Thornton’s factory, didn’t you?”

“Your father wasn’t there, was he?” Margaret asked, her face going scarlet.

“Not he. He’d’ve given his right hand if it never happened. That’s what’s eating at him, right now. He’s broken-hearted over it. You never saw a man so down.”

“But why?” Margaret frowned, confused.

“Because he’s on the strike committee. The Union elected him, and between you and me, they were right — he’s sharp, and loyal to the bone. Him and the rest of the committee drew up all the plans. They agreed every man would stand together, no splitting off, no matter how hungry they got. And above all things, no breaking the law. People would stand with them if they saw them starving quiet and patient, they knew — but the second there was any noise, any fighting, even sticks thrown, all their public support would vanish, same as it had a dozen times before. They planned to go out and talk to the troublemakers, coax them, reason with them, warn them off. The committee ordered every Union man to lie down and die before he struck a blow. They thought they had the whole public on their side, sure they were in the right with their wage demands, no risk of all their good cause tangled up with wrong, no one could mix the two up no more than I could pick the bitter physic powder out of the sweet jelly you gave me to mix it in. But I’ve rambled on too long, and I’m worn out. You think for yourself what it’s like for father, all his work undone, by a fool like Boucher who went right against committee orders and ruined the whole strike, just like he was a Judas on purpose. Father gave it to him good last night. He even said he’d go tell the police where to find the riot ringleader, hand Boucher over to the mill owners to do with what they would. He wanted to show the world the real strike leaders weren’t thugs like Boucher, but steady, thoughtful men — good workers, good citizens, who respected law and order, who only wanted their fair wage, wouldn’t go back to work even if they starved until they got it, but who’d never hurt a soul or break a single window.” She dropped her voice to a hoarse whisper. “They say Boucher threw a stone at Thornton’s sister that nearly killed her.”

“That’s not true,” Margaret said, first flushing red, then draining white. “It wasn’t Boucher who threw that stone.”

“Were you there, then?” Bessy asked, languid, speech coming slow and hard between gasps.

“Yes. Never mind that. Go on — what did Boucher say to your father?”

“Hardly a word. He was shaking all over, spent from passion and hunger, I could scarce bear to look at him. His breath was coming quick, and at one point I thought he was sobbing. When father said he’d hand him over to the police, he let out a great cry, punched father right in the face with his fist, and ran off like lightning. Father was stunned at first, even though Boucher was weak with rage and starvation. He sat down a minute, covered his eyes, then made for the door. I don’t know where I got the strength, but I threw myself across the settle and clung to his legs. ‘Father, father!’ I said. ‘You’ll never peach on that poor starving man. I’ll never let go of you till you promise you won’t.’ ‘Don’t be a fool,’ he said. ‘Words come easier than deeds to most men. I never really meant to tell the police on him — though God knows he deserves it, and I wouldn’t have minded if someone else had done the dirty work and clapped him in jail. But now he’s struck me, I could never do it, not now — it’d be getting other men to fight my quarrel for me. But once he’s past this hunger, and well again, him and I will have it out face to face, and I’ll make him see sense.’ Then he shook me off, and I was so faint I nearly fell. His face was as white as clay, where it wasn’t bloody, and it turned my stomach to look at it. I don’t know if I slept or fainted dead away until Mary came in, and I told her to go fetch you. Now don’t talk to me. Just read me a chapter. I feel easier now I’ve spat it all out, but I want thoughts of that far-off world to take the bitter taste out of my mouth. Don’t read a sermon. Read the story of the New Heavens and the New Earth, the ones with pictures I can see behind my eyelids when I shut my eyes.”

Margaret read in her soft, low voice. Bessy’s eyes stayed shut, but she listened long enough for heavy tears to gather on her lashes, until at last she drifted off to a fitful sleep, full of small starts and muttered, pleading half-words. Margaret covered her gently, and left, her chest tight with a guilty feeling that she might be needed at home, though it felt cruel to leave the dying girl alone.

Her mother was in the drawing room when she got back, having one of her rare good days, full of nothing but praise for their new water bed. It was more like the feather beds they had had at Sir John Beresford’s estate than anything she had slept on in years. She could not understand why no one made beds like that anymore — the feathers were the same, surely, but she had not had a full, restful night’s sleep in decades until the night before. Mr Hale suggested maybe the better rest back in their youth was down to the energy of youth, but his wife brushed the idea off sharply.

“No, Mr. Hale, it was all the beds at Sir John’s. Margaret, you’re young, you move about all day — are your beds comfortable? Do you wake feeling perfectly rested, or do you toss and turn trying to find a soft spot, and wake up just as tired as you were when you laid down?”

Margaret laughed. “To tell the truth, mamma, I’ve never even thought about my bed. I’m so tired by nightfall I drop off the second my head hits the pillow. I’m no good as a witness. And besides, I never got to try the beds at Sir John’s — I never went to Oxenham, remember?”

“Didn’t you? Oh, no! That’s right. I took poor dear little Fred there once, after we married, for your Aunt Shaw’s wedding. Fred was just a baby then. I remember Dixon didn’t want to step down from being my lady’s maid to being a nurse, and I was afraid if I took her near her old home she’d want to leave me. But baby Fred got sick teething while we were there, and I was busy so much with Anna in the days before her wedding, and I was still weak myself, that Dixon ended up caring for him almost full time. She got so fond of him, so proud when he’d cling to her and turn away from everyone else, that I don’t think she ever thought of leaving me again. Everyone loved Fred. He was born with the gift of winning hearts. It makes me think very ill of Captain Reid, to hear he disliked my sweet boy. It proves for certain he had a bad heart.” She paused, and sighed. “Oh, your poor father, Margaret — he left the room. He can’t bear to hear Fred spoken of.”

“I love hearing stories about him, mamma. Tell me all you want, you can never tell me too much. What was he like as a baby?”

“Margaret, you won’t be hurt if I say he was far prettier than you were. I remember the first time I saw you in Dixon’s arms, I said ‘Dear, what an ugly little thing!’ and she said ‘Not every child’s like Master Fred, bless him!’ I could have held Fred every minute of the day, his cot was right next to my bed. And now, Margaret — I don’t know where my boy is. Sometimes I think I’ll never see him again.”

Margaret sank down on a small stool by her mother’s sofa, took her hand gently and kissed it, to soothe her. Mrs Hale cried freely, no restraint. At last she sat up straight, her face wet with tears, and looked at her daughter with solemn, earnest eyes.

“Margaret, if I get better — if God lets me recover — it will only be from seeing Frederick once more. It will wake up all the little bits of health left in me.” Her voice shook, quavering as if she was staring at some half-formed, urgent vision right in front of her. “And Margaret — if I am meant to die, if God calls me home in the next few weeks — I must see my child first. I cannot die without blessing him. Only five minutes, Margaret. There can’t be any danger in five minutes. Oh, Margaret, bring him to me before I die.”

Margaret did not stop to question the reasonableness of the request. No one looks for logic in the desperate pleas of a person sick unto death. The thought of all the small, trivial wishes she had denied her mother over the years pricked at her, and she would have laid down her own future happiness at her mother’s feet without a second thought to grant this one. It was so natural, so right — for her mother’s sake, for Frederick’s too, she would overlook every risk. The great, pleading eyes of Mrs Hale were fixed on her, steady, her pale lips trembling like a small child’s. Margaret stood up, and faced her, her own face calm and steady, so her mother could read her promise there.

“Mamma, I will write tonight, tell Frederick exactly what you said. I know as sure as I’m alive that he will come home to us at once. Be easy. You shall see him, as far as any earthly thing can be promised.”

“Write tonight? The post goes out at five! You’ll write by that post, won’t you? I have so little time left — sometimes your father talks me into hoping I’ll get better, but I know I won’t. Don’t miss a single post — I could miss him entirely, just by one day’s delay.”

“But mamma, papa is out —”

“Papa is out? What of that? You don’t mean he would deny me my last wish, Margaret? I wouldn’t be sick, dying, if he hadn’t dragged us away from Helstone to this smoky, sunless, unhealthy Milton!”

“Oh mamma!” Margaret protested, hurt.

“It’s true! He knows it himself, he’s said so a dozen times. He’d do anything for me. You don’t mean he’d refuse me this last prayer? And Margaret, this longing to see Frederick stands between me and God. I can’t pray, not until I have this. Don’t waste time, dear. Write right now, by the five o’clock post. Then he can be here in twenty-two days! No chains, no law will stop him. In twenty-two days I’ll hold my boy again.”

She fell back against the cushions, and was quiet for a minute, until she noticed Margaret sitting motionless, one hand shading her eyes. “You’re not writing! Bring me pen and paper, I’ll write it myself.” She sat up, trembling all over with feverish eagerness.

Margaret laid a gentle hand on hers. “Only wait till papa comes in. Let us ask him the best way to do it?”

“You promised me, Margaret, not fifteen minutes ago, you said he would come.”

“And he will, mamma. Don’t cry, my dearest mother. I’ll write it right now, here, you can watch me. It will go by the five o’clock post. And if papa wants to, he can write a follow-up letter when he gets home — that’s only a day’s delay, nothing to Frederick. Don’t cry so pitifully, it breaks my heart.”

Mrs Hale could not stop her hysterical tears, she even called up images of her own corpse laid out in the drawing room while Frederick arrived too late to see her alive, until she dissolved into exhausted sobs that ached Margaret’s heart. At last she calmed, and watched greedily as Margaret wrote the letter, swift and full of urgent pleading, sealed it up, and then ran herself to the post office to make sure it went out on time, per her mother’s orders.

She was on her way home when her father overtook her on the lane. “And where have you run off to, my pretty maid?” he asked.

“To the post office, with a letter — to Frederick. Oh papa, I think I might have done wrong! But mamma was seized with this terrible, desperate longing to see him. She said it would make her well again, and then she said she had to see him before she died. I can’t tell you how urgent she was. Did I do wrong?”

Mr Hale was quiet for a long moment. “You should have waited for me to come back, Margaret.”

“I tried to make her —” Margaret’s voice trailed off.

“I don’t know,” Mr Hale said, after another long pause. “If she wants to see him that badly, she should see him. I truly believe it would do her more good than all the doctor’s medicine, it might even set her up entirely. But the danger to Frederick is very great.”

“All these years since the mutiny, papa?”

“The Admiralty never lets those offences go. They have to enforce strict discipline in the navy, that their officers feel all the power of the home government behind them. They don’t care how much tyrannizing drove men to madness in the first place. They send

“I have found that holy place of rest Still changeless.”

When John Thornton stepped out his front door that morning, the sting of his rejected confession had left him so dazed he could barely see. The blow Margaret Hale had dealt him felt more solid than if a brawny fishwife had knocked him sideways with a fist, and the throbbing in his temples, the shooting, intermittent pulse under his skin, was a physical ache so heavy he could barely draw a full breath. The roar of Milton’s traffic, the harsh glint of gaslight off damp cobblestones, the endless rumble of mill carts trundling past grated on his raw nerves like a file. He called himself a fool over and over for suffering so sharply, but for the life of him he could not untangle the root of the pain, nor tell if it was half bad enough to warrant the havoc it was wreaking on his whole body.

It would have been a relief, he thought wildly, to sink down on a doorstep next to some screaming, tear-soaked toddler in the throes of a rage over a stolen toy, and cry right alongside him. The moment he framed the words *I hate Margaret* in his head, a searing, sharp flash of love cut through his thunderous, dull fog of feeling like a streak of lightning, bright and unshakable. He clung to his torment, even, for the certainty it gave him: no matter how she despised him, looked down on him, treated him with that cool, proud indifference he had seen on her face that morning, he would not change. She could not make him stop. He loved her, and he would go on loving her, in defiance of her will, in defiance of this throbbing, miserable pain in his bones.

He paused still on the pavement to firm that resolution into something unbreakable. An omnibus bound for the countryside rumbled past, and the conductor, mistaking his motion for a hail, pulled up to the curb. Too worn out to stop and explain, Thornton clambered aboard, and let the cart carry him away: past the endless terraces of smoke-stained Milton townhouses, past the scattered suburban villas with their tiny, neat clipped gardens, until the streets fell away to wild, flowering hedgerows, and before he knew it, they had pulled into a small quiet market town far from the clatter of the mills.

All the other passengers got off, and Thornton followed, striding fast across the open fields beyond the town, the sharp, brisk movement clearing the fog from his mind at last. He could see every foolish choice he had made now, the ridiculous figure he must have cut when he blundered into declaring his love, after years of telling himself that blurting out a proposal in the heat of the moment was the stupidest thing a man could ever do. Had he been bewitched by those soft, luminous eyes, that half-open, sighing mouth that had rested so close to his shoulder the day before, when she had thrown herself between him and the rage of the rioters? He could not shake the memory of her arms around him, warm and steady, even for a second. He replayed every single encounter he had ever had with her, trying to etch her out of his mind for good, only to find he could not settle on which of her moods he admired most: the sharp, brave fire she showed when standing up for the mill workers, the soft shyness that came over her when they spoke of her home in the south, the haughty, regal pride that flashed that morning when he dared to assume she cared for him just because she had shared his danger.

His sixpenny omnibus ride left him no wiser in the end, only more certain: there was no one else in the world like Margaret Hale. She did not love him, and she never would, but neither she nor anyone else would ever make him stop loving her. By late afternoon, he was back at the Milton market square, climbing the return omnibus that dropped him steps away from his warehouse.

The familiar sights of his work pulled him back to his usual routines, and he remembered the pile of tasks waiting for him: meetings with his fellow magistrates to review the aftermath of the riot, half-finished arrangements to house his newly arrived Irish migrant workers safely, far away from the discontented striking Milton hands who would surely turn on them for crossing the picket line. Last of all, he would have to go home and face his mother.

Mrs. Thornton had sat in the dining room all day, every muscle tensed, waiting for the sound of her son’s footstep to bring news that Miss Hale had accepted him. At every small, unexpected noise in the house, she would jerk upright, catch up her half-finished needlework, and ply her thread fast even as her spectacles misted over and her hands shook. A dozen times the door swung open, only to admit a maid or the baker’s boy on some trivial errand, and her rigid, frost-locked face would soften back into a slack, rare look of dejection, almost unrecognizable on a woman so stern and unyielding. She had forced her mind away from the dark, dreary visions of the new life her son’s marriage would bring her, back to the familiar grooves of household business: she had stacks and stacks of linen chests hauled out, full of tablecloths and napkins, to count over in preparation for the new couple’s household. She sorted through the damask, separating the pieces marked G. H. T. – stitched for her own name, Hannah, and her late husband George – from the newer pieces bought with her son’s money, marked only with his initials. Some of the old damask was fine Dutch weave, the kind no weaver made anymore, the same pieces she had been so proud of when she was first a bride. She stood staring at them for a long time, then knit her brows, pinched her lips tight, and unpicked the G and H from the old marking thread, until only the T was left. She went as far as to reach for her spool of turkey-red thread to stitch his new wife’s initials in their place, only to find she had used up every last scrap of it, and could not bear to send the maid out for more. She sat staring at empty air then, all her visions circling around her son, her pride, her whole life’s work, convinced he was lingering somewhere with Margaret Hale, that the new girl was already stealing the place she had held in his heart since he was a small boy. A sharp, terrible pang of useless jealousy shot through her, so strong she could not tell if it was a pain in her bones or in her soul, and forced her to sit down gasping. A moment later she was standing straight again, her face set in the grim, unyielding smile she had prepared to greet him with, ready to hide every last trace of her grief at his marriage, so he would never guess how much it cost her to see him give his heart away. She barely thought of Margaret Hale as a person, at all, only as John’s wife: a girl chosen by him would be raised above every other woman in the world, and Margaret was not even that bad, with her sharp wit, her taste and spirit. It was only her southern upbringing that left her so sadly prejudiced and ignorant, Mrs. Thornton told herself, and she even snapped at her own dull, soft daughter Fanny mid-afternoon for no reason, before picking up Henry’s Commentaries to punish herself, forcing her attention onto the dry legal text instead of staring at the pile of wedding linen any longer.

At last, his step. She heard it even before she had finished the sentence she was pretending to read. He paused by the hat stand in the hall, then came to the dining room door, and stopped just on the other side. She kept her head bowed over her book, refusing to look up, until he stood right at her elbow, waiting for her to finish her page. She dragged her eyes up to his face.

“Well, John?”

He knew exactly what that quiet line meant. He had steeled himself to keep his face neutral, and for a wild second he wanted to make a bitter joke to ease the blow, but his mother deserved more than that. He circled around behind her so she could not see the raw pain in his eyes, bent down, and kissed her cold, stony gray cheek, murmuring, “No one loves me – no one cares for me, but you, mother.”

He turned away and leaned his head against the mantelpiece, big, manly tears forcing their way out despite every effort he made to hold them back. Mrs. Thornton stood up, and tottered. For the first time in her whole life, that iron-strong woman swayed on her feet. She set her hands on his shoulders – she was tall enough to look him dead in the eye – and forced him to meet her gaze.

“Mother’s love is given by God, John. It holds fast for ever and ever. A girl’s love is a puff of smoke – shifts with every wind. She turned you down, my own lad, didn’t she?” Her teeth were bared, sharp as a dog’s, when she spoke. He shook his head.

“I’m not fit for her, mother. I knew I wasn’t.”

She ground her words out between clenched teeth, too quiet for him to make out, but the fury in her eyes spoke of a curse as bitter as any ever uttered. Even so, her heart leapt light inside her: he was hers again.

“Mother!” he said, hurrying to speak, his voice raw and unsteady. “I won’t hear a word against her. Spare me, please. My heart’s sore, and I’m weak right now – I still love her. More than ever.”

“Then I hate her,” Mrs. Thornton said, low and fierce. “I tried not to hate her, when she stood between you and me. I told myself she’d make you happy, and I’d give every drop of my heart’s blood to see that happen. But now? I hate her for the misery she’s put you in. I’m the one that bore you. Your pain is my pain, and if you won’t hate her, I will.”

“Then you make me love her more,” he said. “You’re treating her unfairly, and I have to even the balance. But what’s the use of talking of love and hate? She doesn’t care for me. That’s enough, more than enough. Let’s never name the subject again. It’s the only thing you can do for me now. Let’s never speak her name.”

“With all my heart,” she said. “I only wish the girl and every last one of her people were swept back to the south they came from.”

He stood staring into the fire for a long minute after that. Her dry, usually untearful eyes filled with rare tears as she watched him, but when he spoke next, her face was as grim and calm as it had ever been.

“Warrants are out for three men, for conspiracy, mother. The riot yesterday all but broke the strike.”

Margaret Hale’s name never passed their lips again that day. They fell back into their old, familiar pattern of speech, talking only of facts, never of opinions, and certainly never of their raw, tangled feelings. A stranger passing through might have thought there was no colder, more frigid pair of near relations in all of Milton, and never guessed at the storm of grief and loyalty that hung thick between them, unspoken.

“For never any thing can be amiss
When simpleness and duty tender it.”
— *A Midsummer Night’s Dream*

John Thornton stepped into the next day with the sharp, unswerving focus of a man who has buried personal pain deep under layers of practical purpose. There was a small but steady uptick in demand for finished cotton goods that morning, and he moved fast to seize the advantage, striking shrewd, unyielding bargains that left his trade counterparts nodding in reluctant respect. He arrived exactly on the hour for the magistrates’ meeting, and his quick, unclouded judgement—his gift for spotting the long trail of consequences any choice would leave in its wake before anyone else in the room had finished sorting their notes—made him the de facto leader of the group. Older, wealthier men whose fortunes were locked up in land and property, while his own capital was all tied to the mills, turned to him for direction. He was the one deputized to coordinate with the police, to lay out every necessary step to keep the peace in the wake of the riot.

He noticed none of their quiet deference, no more than he noticed the soft west wind that barely ruffled the straight plume of smoke rising from his mill’s tallest chimney. The only goal he cared about was pushing all necessary business to its swift end. It was his mother, greedy for every scrap of regard paid to her son, who drank in the gossip from the magistrates’ wives and the town’s wealthy households: that if Thornton had not been there, the riot aftermath would have unfolded far worse, a disaster for the whole of Milton.

All day he swept through task after task, the raw mortification of Margaret Hale’s rejection the day before, and the dazed, purposeless hours that followed it, having burned away every last fog of confusion from his mind. He revelled in the sharp, unbreakable power he felt in his work, almost convinced he could lock his heart away entirely, like the old miller by the Dee who sang that he cared for nobody, and nobody cared for him.

The evidence against Boucher and the other riot ringleaders was laid before him that afternoon; charges of conspiracy against three other men fell apart for lack of proof, but he charged the police sternly to keep constant watch, so the long arm of the law would be ready to strike the second they had solid, unassailable proof of wrongdoing. Stepping out of the hot, reeking borough court into the sultry, close air of the street, all that iron resolve seemed to slip away at once.
