He went languid, unable to rein in his wandering thoughts, that kept drifting back not to his public work, not even to his rejection the day prior—but to that half-hour in the riot, when Margaret had clung to him for safety, her heart beating fast against his chest. He wound his way through the crowded, busy streets mechanically, not seeing a single face around him, sick with a sharp, unnameable longing to relive that one short, bright moment again.

“Why, Mr. Thornton! You’re cutting me dead, aren’t you?” a voice laughed. “How is Mrs. Thornton? Terribly close weather this, and us doctors hate it, I can tell you.”

He snapped out of his trance. “Beg your pardon, Dr. Donaldson—truly didn’t see you. My mother’s quite well, thank you. It’s fine weather for the harvest, at least. If the wheat comes in well, trade’ll bounce next year, no matter how your patients fare.”

“Aye, every man for himself,” the doctor chuckled. “Bad times for trade mean good times for my practice, you mark my words. You Milton millfolk work yourselves thin, undermine your health, and you don’t even notice it till it’s too late.”

“Not me,” Thornton said, half-smiling. “I’m made of iron. The news of the worst bad debt I ever owned never made my pulse skip a beat, and this strike that hits me harder than Hamper, harder than any other man in town? It’s never once spoiled my appetite. You’ll have to look elsewhere for a patient, Doctor.”

“Speaking of patients you recommended, that poor lady in Crampton—Mrs. Hale, you know her? I don’t mind saying, she hasn’t many weeks left. I never held out hope for a cure, as I told you before, but I saw her today, and her fever’s far worse than I thought.”

Thornton went still. That vaunted steady pulse of his stuttered for a full second. “Is there anything I can do?” he asked, his voice rougher than usual. “Money’s no object for anything she might need—any comfort, any dainty at all.”

The doctor shook his head. “She’s got a constant fever, and all she craves is fruit. Jargonelle pears are the best we’ve got in the market right now, but there’s plenty of them around.”

“Send word to me the second she needs anything else, will you?” Thornton said. “I rely on you for that.”

“Oh, never fear, I won’t spare your deep purse,” Donaldson laughed. “I only wish every one of my patients had a patron as generous as you.”

Thornton was no broad philanthropist, no lover of all mankind—few would have guessed he was capable of such fierce, private affection for anyone. But he made straight for the finest fruit shop in Milton, picked out bunches of purple grapes still dusted with their delicate silvery bloom, the richest, sun-ripened peaches, and the freshest green vine leaves to tuck between them. The shopman packed them neatly into a wicker basket, then paused to ask, “Where shall we send this, sir? Back to Marlborough Mills, I suppose?”

“No,” Thornton said, and he hefted the heavy basket into his own two hands. He carried it all the way through the busiest shopping thoroughfare in Milton, where every young lady of his acquaintance turned to stare, shocked to see the stern, formidable mill owner trundling a fruit basket like a common errand boy.

He told himself he was not daunted by the thought of Margaret Hale, not at all. He was doing a kindness for Mr. Hale, his old friend, and he would not be cowed by some haughty girl’s rejection into shirking a simple good turn. This was nothing short of defiance, he told himself, defying her to make him feel small for doing what was right.

He walked fast, reaching Crampton in no time, taking the stairs two at a time, and burst into the Hales’ drawing room before Dixon could even announce him. His face was flushed, his eyes bright with earnest, unselfconscious kindness. Mrs. Hale lay prostrate on the sofa, hot with her fever. Mr. Hale sat beside her reading aloud, and Margaret was perched on a low stool at her mother’s side, stitching quietly. Her heart fluttered fast when she saw him—though he gave no sign he had noticed her at all, barely glancing at Mr. Hale as he walked straight up to the fevered woman on the sofa.

“I ran into Dr. Donaldson, ma’am,” he said, in that soft, gentle tone that feels so striking coming from a big, robust, strong man, addressing a fragile invalid. “He said fruit would do you good, so I took the great liberty of bringing some that looked particularly fine to me.”

Mrs. Hale was almost trembling with delight, so pleased at the thought. Mr. Hale’s gratitude was quieter, far deeper. “Margaret, fetch a plate, a basket, anything to set these out.”

Margaret stood up, half afraid to make a sound, half convinced he had not seen her at all, that her spot on the low stool had hidden her from his notice. She thought a sudden collision between the two of them would be unbearably awkward for both. She had no way of knowing he had been acutely, painfully aware of every inch of her presence from the second he stepped through the door.

“I can’t stay,” he said, already turning for the door. “Forgive my abrupt, rough ways. I’ll try to be gentler next time. If I see any fine, tempting fruit as I go about town, I hope you’ll let me bring you some more. Good afternoon, Mr. Hale. Goodbye, ma’am.”

He was gone. Not one word, not one single glance spared for Margaret. She truly believed he had not seen her at all. She went to fetch a plate silently, lifting the fruit out one by one with her slim, delicate fingers, her heart softening. It was so good of him, she thought—especially after what had happened between them only the day before.

“Oh, these are so delicious!” Mrs. Hale murmured weakly. “How kind of him to think of me! Margaret, love, taste these grapes! Was that not the kindest thing?”

“Yes,” Margaret said, quietly.

“Margaret,” Mrs. Hale said, a little petulant, “you will not admire a single thing Mr. Thornton does. I never saw anyone so stubbornly prejudiced.”

Mr. Hale was peeling a peach for his wife, and he cut off a small sliver for himself to taste, smiling softly. “If I held any prejudices against him, this fruit would melt every last one away. I have not tasted fruit this fine—no, not even in Hampshire—since I was a boy. Back then, I thought all fruit was good, even sour sloes and crabapples I ate out in the hedgerows. Do you remember the matted currant bushes at the corner of the west wall in our old garden, Margaret?”

Of course she remembered. She remembered every weather stain on that old stone wall, the grey and yellow lichen streaked across it like a map, the tiny crane’s-bill weeds growing in every crack. The strain of the last two days, the terror of the riot, the weight of her mother’s worsening illness, all came crashing down on her at the soft, careless mention of their old sunny Hampshire home. She jumped to her feet, dropped her sewing on the floor, and fled to her own small bedroom before anyone could see her tears.

She was just holding back her first choking sob when she spotted Dixon rummaging through her dresser drawers. “Bless me, miss, you startled me half to death!” Dixon said, jumping. “Your mother’s not worse, is she? Nothing’s gone wrong?”

“No, nothing at all,” Margaret said, wiping at her eyes. “I’m just being silly, that’s all. I wanted a glass of water. What are you looking for? I keep my muslin things in that drawer.”

Dixon kept rummaging, the sharp, sweet scent of lavender sachets wafting out of the open drawer. “I didn’t want to tell you,” Dixon said at last, turning to face her, “not when you’ve already got so much to fret over. I was going to keep it from you till nightfall, mayhap. That Higgins girl—Bessy, the one you go visit? She died this morning. Her sister Mary’s downstairs, asking a very strange favour. Bessy took a real fancy to you, and she said she wanted to be buried in something that belonged to you. I was looking for an old nightcap, nothing too fine, that we could give her.”

“Oh, let me find one,” Margaret said, even as her tears started falling again. “Poor Bessy. I never thought I would not see her again.”

“Her sister’s been begging me to ask if you’d go see her laid out,” Dixon said. “You’ve never seen a dead body, miss, you said once. I told her you wouldn’t want to go.”

“I will go down and speak to her,” Margaret said, afraid Dixon’s sharp, gruff manner might hurt the poor grieving girl. She took the soft nightcap in her hand and headed for the kitchen, where Mary sat huddled by the fire, her face puffed and red from crying. She burst into fresh sobs the second she saw Margaret.

“Oh, ma’am, she loved you so, she did! She talked of nothing but you, all the hours before she died. Said you were the prettiest, kindest thing she ever clapped eyes on. Her last words were ‘Give her my affectionate respects, and keep father from drink.’ You’ll come see her, ma’am? She’d have thought it the greatest compliment ever paid her.”

Margaret shrank back for a second, she had never seen a dead person, the thought made her skin prickle. But before Dixon could interject and offer to go in her place, she set her jaw.

“Yes,” she said firmly. “I will come to see you before tea, Mary.” And she turned and left the kitchen fast, before her own cowardice could make her change her mind.

*"Through cross to crown!—And though thy spirit’s life Trials untold assail with giant strength, Good cheer! good cheer! Soon ends the bitter strife, And thou shalt reign in peace with Christ at length." — Kosegarten*
*"Ay sooth, we feel too strong in weal, to need Thee on that road; But woe being come, the soul is dumb, that crieth not on ‘God.’" — Mrs. Browning*

That afternoon, Margaret walked swift as the wind down the narrow Milton lanes to the Higgins cottage. Mary was waiting at the door, her face half pinched with wary doubt, and Margaret smiled soft and steady into her eyes to reassure her. They slipped past the cluttered house-place and up the steep, rickety stairs in silence, stepping into the small upper room where Bessy lay at last still. The sight made Margaret’s throat tighten, and she thanked God she had come. The face that had so often been drawn tight with coughing fits, restless with the ache of unspoken hopes, now wore a faint, soft smile of perfect, unbroken rest. Slow, warm tears welled in her eyes, but a deep, quiet calm settled deep into her bones. This was death, she thought, and it looked far gentler than the harsh, weary life Bessy had led. Verses she had not thought of in years rose unbidden to her mind: *They rest from their labours. The weary are at rest. He giveth his beloved sleep.*

Slowly, Margaret turned away from the bed. Mary was huddled in the corner, sobbing quiet and humble behind her apron. They trundled back down the stairs without a single word passing between them.

Nicholas Higgins stood planted in the middle of the kitchen floor, one calloused hand braced hard against the wooden table, his great dark eyes blown wide. He had only just stepped through the door, the news of his daughter’s death shouted at him by a dozen neighbours out in the court, and his eyes were dry and sharp, fixed hard on making the impossible fact real, forcing himself to accept Bessy’s place would know her no more. For she had lingered sick and half-dying for so many months, he had talked himself into believing she would beat the illness, that she would “pull through” one more time.

Margaret felt like an intruder, a stranger who had no business standing witness to this raw, private household grief that had only just crashed over the father. She paused for a heartbeat on the crooked stairs, then tried to slip past his unseeing, absorbed gaze to leave him alone in the hush of his loss.

Mary dropped onto the first chair she reached, threw her apron over her head, and broke into loud, shuddering sobs.

The noise jolted Higgins out of his trance. He caught Margaret’s arm in a bruising grip, holding fast while he fumbled for words, his throat thick and dry, his voice coming out hoarse and choked.
“Were yo’ with her? Did yo’ see her die?”
“No,” Margaret answered, standing perfectly still, quiet and patient now that he had registered her presence. He did not let go of her arm for a long stretch, his gaze still distant.
“All men must die,” he said at last, with a heavy, strange gravity that made Margaret wonder if he had stopped for a quick drink on his way home — not enough to make him drunk, but enough to muddle his thoughts. “But she were younger than me.” He fell quiet again, turning the fact over and over in his head, before he looked up at her with a wild, searching stare. “Yo’re sure and certain she’s dead? Not in a dwam, a faint? She’s slipped off that way many a time before.”
“She is dead,” Margaret said. She felt no fear of him, even when his grip dug deep enough to leave purple marks on her arm, and wild, unhinged glints flickered across his dulled, grief-struck eyes. “She is dead.”

The searching look faded slowly out of his face. Suddenly he wrenched his hand away from her, threw his full weight half across the table, and great, heaving sobs burst out of him that shook the whole table and every pot and pan on it, every wobbly chair in the room. Mary stumbled towards him, trembling.
“Get thee gone! — get thee gone!” he roared, lashing out blindly at her. “What do I care for thee?” Margaret pulled Mary close, tucking the girl’s cold hand into her own. Higgins tore at his hair, beat his head against the hard wooden table edge until he collapsed back in the chair, spent and vacant. Neither Margaret nor Mary moved. Mary shook so hard her teeth chattered.

After what might have been fifteen minutes, what might have been an hour, Higgins lifted himself heavily to his feet. His eyes were swollen and bloodshot, and he seemed to have forgotten anyone else was in the room until he spotted them, and scowled dark at their presence. He shook himself like a shaggy dog fresh from a rainstorm, gave them one last sullen glare, and said not a word as he made straight for the front door.
“Oh father, father!” Mary cried, throwing herself against his arm. “Not to-night! Any night but to-night. Oh, help me — he’s going out to drink again! Father, I’ll not leave yo’. Yo’ may strike me all yo’ want, but I’ll not stir. She told me last of all to keep yo’ fro’ drink!”

But Margaret was planted firm in the doorway, silent, unyielding. He looked up at her, defying her with every line of his hunched frame.
“It’s my own house. Stand out o’ the way, wench, or I’ll make yo’!” He had shoved Mary away so hard she fell, her face cutting against the sharp edge of a chair. He looked ready to strike Margaret, but she did not flinch, did not shift a muscle, never took her steady, serious eyes off his face. He stared back, fierce and gloomy, daunted little by little by her unshaking calm.
“What are yo’ looking at me in that way for?” he demanded at last. “If yo’ think yo’ can keep me from going where I choose, just because she loved yo’ — and in my own house too, where I never asked yo’ to step foot — yo’re mista’en. It’s cruel hard on a man that he can’t go to the only comfort left him after all this.”

Margaret knew he had acknowledged her power, however grudging. What was there left she could do? He dropped back onto a chair right next to the door, half beaten, half still angry, fully intent on slipping out the second she moved from the doorway, but too ashamed to lash out at her like he had at Mary. She laid a soft hand on his arm.
“Come with me,” she said, her voice low and solemn, no trace of fear in it. “Come and see her one more time.”

He rose sullenly, his face set in dogged, stubborn resistance. She waited for him there, quiet and patient, until he finally tramped slowly towards the stairs. The two of them stood side by side by Bessy’s bed.
“Her last words to Mary were, ‘Keep my father fro’ drink,’” Margaret told him.
“It canna hurt her now,” he muttered. “Nought can hurt her now.” Then his voice broke into a wail. “We may quarrel and fall out — we may make peace and be friends — we may clem to skin and bone — and nought o’ all our griefs will ever touch her more. Hoo’s had her full portion of ’em. What wi’ back-breaking work first, and wasting sickness at the last, hoo led the life of a half-starved dog. And to die without ever knowing one good piece o’ rejoicing in all her days! Nay, wench, whatever hoo said, hoo can know nought about it now, and I mun ha’ a sup o’ drink just to steady me against this sorrow.”
“No,” Margaret said, softening as his grief spilled over. “You shall not. If her life was as hard as you say, at least she never feared death like so many do. Oh, you should have heard her speak of the life to come — the life hidden with God, that she has gone to join now.”
He shook his head, glancing up sideways at her, his face haggard and pale enough to make her chest ache. “You are sorely tried, are you not? Where have you been all day? You have not been at work, have you?”
“Not at work, sure enough,” he said, with a short, bitter laugh. “Not at what you call work. I were at the Union Committee all morning, till I were sickened clean through wi’ trying to make fools hear reason. I were fetched to Boucher’s wife afore seven this morning. She’s bed-fast, but she were raving and raging to know where her dunner-headed brute of a husband had got to, as if I could keep him on a leash, as if he were ever fit to be ruled by me. The damned fool, who’s put paid to all our strike plans! And I’ve walked my feet raw going round to see men who wouldn’t answer their doors, now the law’s turned against all of us. And I were sore-hearted too, which is worse than sore-footed. And if I did see a friend who offered to stand me a drink, I never knew hoo lay a-dying here. Bess, lass, thou’d believe me, thou wouldst — wouldstn’t thou?” he added, turning to the still, quiet form on the bed in a wild, desperate appeal.
“I am sure,” Margaret said. “I am sure you did not know. It was all so sudden. But now — now you do know. You do see her lying there. You hear what she said with her last breath. You will not go to the gin-shop?”

He did not answer. Where else was a man to turn for comfort, after he had lost the one person who had always understood him?
“Come home with me,” Margaret said at last, taking a bold, nervous risk, half trembling at her own proposal. “At the very least, you shall have a plate of warm, proper food, which I can see you need badly.”
“Yo’r father’s a parson?” he asked, the thought striking him out of nowhere.
“He was,” Margaret said shortly.
“I’ll come and take a dish o’ tea with him, since yo’ve asked me. I’ve many a thing I’ve always wanted to say to a parson, and I don’t care much whether he’s preaching now or not.”

Margaret’s stomach twisted. Nicholas turning up unannounced for tea, when her mother was so unwell, her father totally unprepared for the visitor, seemed like madness — but she knew if she drew back now, he would head straight for the nearest pub, and all her work to hold him back would be for nothing. She decided that getting him across her own threshold was a victory big enough to trust to whatever might happen next.
“Good-bye, ou’d wench! We’ve parted company at last, we have! But thou’st been a blessing to thy father ever sin’ thou wert born. Bless thy white lips, lass — they’ve a smile on ’em now! And I’m glad to see it once again, even if I’m lone and forlorn for evermore.”
He stooped down and kissed Bessy’s forehead tenderly, pulled the sheet up over her face, and turned to follow Margaret. She hurried downstairs first to explain the plan to Mary, tell her it was the only way she could think of to keep her father from the gin-palace, and urge her to come with them too — but Mary said neighbours were already knocking at the door to sit with her, that she would be fine. Nicholas followed close behind, he had shaken off his tears and was half ashamed of breaking down, putting on a harsh, bitter kind of mirth that sounded like dry twigs crackling under a pot.
“I’m going to take my tea wi’ her father, I am.”
He pulled his cap low over his brow as they stepped out into the street, staring straight ahead, not looking left or right, as he tramped along beside Margaret. He was terrified that a sympathetic neighbour’s word, or even a glance, would break the fragile hold he had on his self-control. They walked all the way to the Hales’ house in total silence.
When they neared the street where Margaret lived, he glanced down at his ragged clothes, his grime-streaked hands, his caked clogs. “I should m’appen ha’ cleaned mysel’ first.”
It certainly would have been for the best, and Margaret reassured him he could use the pump in the yard, that there was soap and a clean towel waiting for him, and she would not let him slip away from her now. She sent the servant to lead him down the back passage to the kitchen, and he stepped so carefully over every dark pattern on the oil-cloth floor trying to hide his dirty footprints he looked almost comical.

Margaret ran straight upstairs, and met Dixon on the landing.
“How is mamma? Where is papa?”
Missus was worn out, Dixon told her, and had gone to lie down on the sofa in her room, not bed — Dixon had persuaded her that taking tea there would be better than fretting over being cooped up in bed too long. Good so far. Mr Hale was in the drawing room. Margaret hurried in, breathless, spilling the whole messy, hurried story out in a rush. Mr Hale was very much taken aback at the idea of a rough mill worker who had been drinking a little waiting for him in his quiet study, expecting to share tea, the man Margaret was pleading for so anxiously. The gentle, kind-hearted Mr Hale would have been happy to comfort any grieving soul, but Margaret had dwelt so hard on the fact that he had been drinking, that she had brought him home as a last resort to stop him from going to the gin-shop, that she noticed a flicker of repugnance cross her father’s face.
“Oh papa! He is a man you will not dislike at all, if you don’t get shocked at the very start.”
“But Margaret, to bring a drunken stranger home — when your mother is so ill!”
Margaret’s face fell. “I am sorry, papa. He is very quiet — he is not tipsy at all. He was only a little strange at first, and that’s just the shock of poor Bessy’s death.” Her eyes filled with tears. Mr Hale cupped her sweet, pleading face in both his hands, and kissed her forehead.
“It is all right, dear. I will go and make him as comfortable as I can. You go attend to your mother. Only, if she falls asleep, do come join us in the study. I would be glad of you there.”
“Oh yes — thank you.” But as Mr Hale turned to leave the room, Margaret ran after him. “Papa — you must not wonder at whatever he says. He — he does not believe much in the kind of faith we follow, you see.”
“Oh dear! A drunken infidel weaver!” Mr Hale thought to himself, dismayed. But to Margaret he only said, “If your mother falls asleep, come right down to us.”

Margaret went into her mother’s bedroom. Mrs Hale lifted herself out of a doze.
“When did you write to Frederick, Margaret? Yesterday, or the day before?”
“Yesterday, mamma.”
“Yesterday, and the letter went off?”
“Yes. I took it to the post myself.”
“Oh Margaret, I’m so afraid of his coming! If he should be recognised! If he should be taken! If he should be executed, after all these years that he has kept away and lived in safety!” she burst out. “I keep falling asleep and dreaming he is caught, standing trial right in front of me.”
“Oh mamma, don’t be afraid,” Margaret soothed her. “There is some risk, of course — but we will make it as small as possible. And it is so little risk! If we were at Helstone, it would be a hundred times worse. Everyone there remembers him. If a stranger was known to be staying in the house, they would all guess it was Frederick at once. But here in Milton, no one knows or cares enough about us to notice what we do. Dixon will guard the front door like a dragon, won’t you, Dixon, while he is here?”
“They’ll be clever if they get past me!” Dixon said, baring her teeth at the very idea.
“And he need never go out, except after dark, poor fellow.”
“Poor fellow!” Mrs Hale echoed, her voice softening. “But I almost wish you had never sent that letter. Would it be too late to stop him if you wrote again, Margaret?”
“I’m afraid it would, mamma,” Margaret said quietly, remembering how urgent she had been in her note, begging Frederick to come at once if he wanted to see his mother alive.
“I never like rushing into things like this,” Mrs Hale fretted.
Margaret kept quiet.
“Come now, ma’am,” Dixon said, with cheerful, no-nonsense authority, “you know seeing Master Frederick is the very thing you’ve been pining for most of all. I’m glad Miss Margaret sent that letter straight off, no shilly-shallying. I’d half a mind to do it myself. And we’ll keep him snug, depend on it. There’s only Martha in the house that wouldn’t do all she could to protect him in a pinch — and she’s been saying for weeks she’d love to go visit her mother, who had a stroke last month, only she didn’t like to ask for time off. I’ll see she’s got a ticket to go stay with her the very day Frederick arrives, God bless him. No one will suspect a thing. Now drink your tea, ma’am, and rest easy, leave all the worrying to me.”
Mrs Hale trusted Dixon far more than she trusted Margaret, and the housekeeper’s words quieted her for the time being. Margaret poured out her mother’s tea in silence, trying to think of something cheerful to say — but her own imagination kept circling back to all the terrible fates that could befall Frederick. She was glad when her filial duties were done, and she could slip downstairs to the study, wondering how on earth her gentle father and the rough grieving weaver had got on.

She found the two of them deep in earnest, gentle conversation. Mr Hale, who treated every living soul with the same unfailing courtesy, had unconsciously drawn out every spark of latent politeness in Higgins. He had pulled a chair out for him, stood waiting until he sat, and called him “Mr. Higgins” the whole time — not

“Some wishes crossed my mind and dimly cheered it,
And one or two poor melancholy pleasures,
Each in the pale unwarming light of hope,
Silvering its flimsy wing, flew silent by—
Moths in the moonbeam!”

The next morning, a thin, smog-dimmed slant of sun slipped through the Hale’s drawing-room window, and Martha trundled in with the post, one plump, scrawled envelope sticking out above the rest, addressed to Margaret in Edith’s unruly, looping hand. It was as affectionate and disjointed as the cousin who had written it: the warmth of it wrapped around Margaret’s chest the moment she broke the seal, for she had grown up with Edith’s whims and meanderings, and never thought to find them foolish.

The letter ran thus:
“Oh Margaret, it is worth crossing the whole ocean from England to see my boy! He is the most superb little creature alive, especially in his caps, and most especially in that pretty stitched one you sent him, you good, deft-fingered, patient dear! I’ve made every other mother on this base envious of him, and now I’m dying to show him off to someone new, to hear a fresh set of exclamations about how perfect he is—maybe that’s all the reason I’m writing, but no, nay, there’s a little bit of cousinly love mixed in too, I swear, and I want you here so badly I can barely sit still to write it. It would do Aunt Hale’s health a world of good, I know it! Everyone here is young and bright, our skies are always blue, the sun shines from dawn till dusk, the military band plays the loveliest tunes all day long, and to circle back to my favourite subject, my baby never stops smiling.
I keep wishing you were here to draw him for me, Margaret. No matter what mischief he’s up to at any given moment, that very thing is the prettiest, gracefullest, best pose he could possibly be in. I think I love him more than I love Cosmo, truth be told—he’s gotten stout and grumpy lately, or what he calls ‘too busy for nonsense.’ No, no, that’s not fair! He just walked in with news of a perfect picnic the officers of the *Hazard* (the warship anchored in the bay below) are throwing for all of us, and I take back every cross word I just said. Didn’t some old story say a man burned his hand to atone for a careless word? I can’t burn mine, it would hurt and leave an ugly scar, so I’ll take it all back as fast as my pen can move. Cosmo is every bit as much a darling as the baby, not a bit stout, and the most ungrumpy husband alive—only sometimes he is very, very busy. That’s not unkind to say, is it? It’s wifely duty, almost.
Where was I? I know I had something very important to tell you. Oh! Dearest Margaret, you absolutely must come visit us! It will cure Aunt Hale, I promise. Tell her doctor to order it, say the thick smoke of Milton is what’s been making her poorly. I’m sure it is! Three months of this soft, sunny air, where grapes grow wild as blackberries, and she’ll be as strong as she was when we were little girls.
I won’t even ask Uncle Hale to come, the letter grew a little stiffer, more carefully worded here, as if Edith was tiptoeing around the fact that Mr. Hale had left his old parish for the sake of his conscience, because I expect he disapproves of war, and soldiers, and bands playing loud, merry tunes. I know lots of Dissenters are in the Peace Society, and I was afraid he wouldn’t care for it—but if he does want to come, tell him Cosmo and I will do everything we can to make him happy. I’ll hide Cosmo’s red uniform coat and his sword, and I’ll make the band play all the most solemn, slow hymns they know, no loud ‘pomps and vanities’ allowed anywhere he can hear them. Dear Margaret, he’d be so welcome, even if I am a little bit awed of anyone who gives up everything for their beliefs. You never did anything that drastic, did you?
Tell Aunt Hale not to bother bringing any warm clothes, even if it’s late in the year before you can get here. You can’t imagine how hot it is! I tried to wear my finest Indian shawl to a picnic last week, and I kept repeating proverbs to myself like ‘Pride must abide’ to justify it, but it was no use. I felt like Mamma’s tiny dog Tiny all tricked out in elephant harnesses—smothered, hidden, nearly killed by my own finery—so I spread it out on the grass and made it a carpet for all of us to sit on.
Now, Margaret, if you don’t pack your bags the second you finish this letter and come straight over to see my baby, I’m going to assume you’re descended from King Herod!”

Margaret ached for even one single day of that life Edith described—no weight of care, no smog hanging thick over the streets, no constant quiet anxiety about her mother’s fading strength. She was not yet twenty, but the harsh pressures of their life in Milton had made her feel old and weary, and the thought of stepping into that bright, sunlit world even for a few hours made her chest tight with longing. She laughed merrily at the silliest lines of Edith’s letter, so caught up in the joke that she did not hear her mother enter the room, leaning heavy on Dixon’s arm.
“Whatever are you laughing at, Margaret?” Mrs. Hale asked once she had been settled onto the sofa, the effort of walking leaving her breathless.
Margaret handed her the letter, then read it aloud, and for a little while Mrs. Hale was fully distracted, guessing at every possible name Edith might have given her new baby, listing a dozen reasons each name might have been chosen.

Right in the middle of that gentle chatter, Mr. Thornton stepped through the door, carrying a wicker basket of sun-ripened peaches he had grown himself. He could not bring himself to deny the small, sharp pleasure of seeing Margaret, even for a moment: it was the stubborn, unruly impulse of a man who was usually so steady, so self-controlled, no one else would have guessed he was capable of such wilful spontaneity. His eyes took in Margaret’s presence in a single quick glance, he gave her one cold, distant bow, and after that he seemed determined never to let his gaze rest on her again. He stayed just long enough to set the fruit on the table, speak a few soft, kind words about hoping the fresh peaches would do Mrs. Hale good, and then he met Margaret’s eyes for one grave, distant farewell before he turned to leave.
Margaret sat back, pale and silent for a long minute.
“Do you know, Margaret? I’m really starting to like Mr. Thornton,” Mrs. Hale said softly.
Margaret forced herself to reply, her voice icy at first, then steadying. “He is very kind and attentive. There is no doubt of that.”
“I wonder his mother never calls on me,” Mrs. Hale went on. “She must know I’m ill, everyone in town hears I’ve got a water-bed set up in my room. I should so like to meet her. You have so very few friends here, Margaret.”
Margaret knew what her mother was really thinking: a tender, quiet craving to leave her daughter in the care of another steady, kind woman, when she was no longer there to look after her. She could not bring herself to speak, the thought of her mother’s coming loss sticking in her throat.
“Do you think you could go and call on Mrs. Thornton, and ask her to come see me?” Mrs. Hale asked after a pause. “Only once. I don’t want to be any trouble at all.”
“I will do anything you wish, Mamma,” Margaret said. “Only—when Frederick comes—”
“Ah, of course. We must lock the doors, we must let no strangers in. I hardly know if I dare wish him to come at all, some days. I have such terrible dreams about him.”
“Oh Mamma, we will take such good care of him,” Margaret promised fiercely. “I will stand guard before any harm can touch him. I will watch over him like a lioness over her cubs.”
“It will be a week at least before we hear from him, won’t it?”
“Longer, very likely.”
“We must send Martha away well before he arrives, so we don’t have to rush her off at the last minute and rouse her suspicions.”
“Dixon will remind us of that, I am sure,” Margaret said. “I was thinking, if we need extra help around the house while he’s here, we can hire Mary Higgins. She’s slack of work right now, and she’s such a good, steady girl, she’d do her very best. She’d sleep at home, and never need to come upstairs, so she would never even guess we have a guest staying.”
“Whatever you think best, whatever Dixon thinks best,” Mrs. Hale said, frowning a little. “But Margaret, you must stop using these horrid Milton provincialisms. ‘Slack of work’! What will Aunt Shaw say when she hears you talking that way when she comes home?”
“Oh Mamma, don’t make a bugbear out of Aunt Shaw,” Margaret laughed. “Edith picked up all sorts of military slang from Captain Lennox, and Aunt Shaw never said a single word about it.”
“But this is factory slang.”
“I live in a factory town,” Margaret shrugged. “Why shouldn’t I speak the language of it when I need to? I could tell you a dozen words you have never heard in your life, that would astonish you. I’ll bet you don’t even know what a knobstick is.”
“No, and I don’t want to know,” Mrs. Hale said. “It sounds dreadfully vulgar. I don’t want you using it.”
“Very well, dearest. I won’t. I’ll just have to use a whole long explanatory sentence instead.”
“I hate this Milton,” Mrs. Hale sighed. “Edith is perfectly right. It’s the thick smoke that has made me so ill.”
Margaret jumped to her feet, worried: she had seen a faint little flicker of doubt in her father’s mind lately, that his choice to move to Milton might be making his wife sick, and she did not want that quiet guilt to sink in deeper. She did not know if Mr. Hale had just stepped in the door and heard those words, so she began talking quickly to redirect the conversation, unaware that Mr. Thornton was just a step behind her father.
“Mamma’s teasing me for picking up all sorts of vulgar Milton habits since we moved here,” Margaret said. She had only been referring to the local factory slang, but she saw Mr. Thornton’s brow darken immediately, as if he thought she was calling the whole town and its people vulgar. Eager to avoid hurting his feelings, she turned to him directly, forcing a small greeting she had not planned to give.
“Mr. Thornton, don’t you think ‘knobstick’ is quite expressive, even if it doesn’t sound very pretty? Could I ever find a longer phrase to carry that same exact meaning, when I’m talking about the thing it refers to? If using local words is vulgar, I was dreadfully vulgar back in the New Forest too, wasn’t I, Mamma?”
Margaret almost never drew attention to herself in conversation, and by the time she finished speaking she was bright red, flustered, especially when Thornton only gave her a cold, distant, ceremonious little nod, as if he barely understood what she was saying at all, and stepped past her to speak to Mrs. Hale.
That was when Mrs. Hale made her soft request, that Mrs. Thornton come to call on her as soon as possible, even tomorrow if she could, so she could make her quiet plea for the woman to look after Margaret when she was gone. Mr. Thornton promised his mother would come, chatted gently for a few more minutes, then took his leave. Only when the door clicked shut behind him did Margaret feel some invisible, tight weight lift from her shoulders, her voice and her movements no longer stiff with awkward tension.
He never once looked directly at her while he was in the room, but his careful avoidance of her gaze told her he knew exactly where she stood in the room, exactly every movement she made. If she spoke, he gave no outward sign he was listening, but the very next thing he said to someone else was always adjusted to account for her words, sometimes even a direct answer to a comment she had made, offered to her father or mother as if it had never come from her at all. It was not rudeness born of ignorance: it was wilful, deliberate rudeness, born of the deep, sharp offence she had dealt him the day of the riot, when she had called him a coward to his face. He regretted the cruelty of it later, the second he was out of her sight, but he thought the cold distance served him well.
Margaret found herself thinking of him more than she ever had before. Not with any spark of romance, no—only with sharp, quiet regret that she had wounded him so deeply. She missed the old, comfortable antagonistic friendship they had shared, before the riot, and she found herself behaving with a gentle, quiet humility around him, as if she was making a silent, unspoken apology for the harsh words she had spat out in the heat of that terrible afternoon.
But Thornton still smarted from those words, they echoed in his ears every time he saw her, and he was proud of the sense of justice that made him go out of his way to do small, thoughtful kindnesses for her parents. He revelled in the quiet power he wielded, forcing himself to face her, to be in the same room as her, whenever he could find an excuse to do something that would give her father or mother pleasure. He told himself he hated seeing the woman who had humiliated him so publicly—but he was wrong. There was a sharp, stinging, bright pleasure in being near her, feeling her presence in the same room. He was never one to analyse his own inner feelings too closely, and he never guessed how much he longed for that quiet, painful closeness, even when he thought he was punishing her for the hurt she had done him.

“The saddest birds a season find to sing.” — SOUTHWELL.

“Never to fold the robe o’er secret pain, Never, weighed down by memory’s clouds again, To bow thy head! Thou art gone home!” — MRS. HEMANS.

Mrs. Thornton called on the Hales the very next morning, every line of her face set in sharp, unyielding resentment. Her son John had begged the visit as a personal favour, but for weeks she had nursed a bitter, proud anger against the whole Hale household, and Margaret in particular. She had railed at her son before leaving, complaining that the Hales ought never to have crossed their paths at all, that no useless old dead languages like Latin and Greek should ever have been invented to bring such trouble down on them. John had borne her ill temper in silence, only repeating his quiet, firm wish that she would call exactly at the hour he thought would be easiest for the ailing woman. She had submitted to his request with as ill a grace as she could muster, even while her heart warmed a little to him for his stubborn, steady kindness, which she saw as a soft virtue verging on weakness.

All her harsh, dismissive thoughts vanished the moment she stepped over the threshold of Mrs. Hale’s bedroom. Overnight, the dying woman had taken one of those terrible, sudden strides towards the grave, and even her own family had been startled by the gaunt, sunken gray of her features. Mrs. Hale, a mother just like her, far younger than herself, lay still in the dim, curtained bed, no hope left that she would ever rise from it. Tears glistened heavy on her eyelashes before she even lifted her eyes to her visitor, and her thin, groping hand searched across the bedclothes for the firm, strong fingers Mrs. Thornton held out. She spoke so faintly the other woman had to stoop nearly double to hear her.

“Margaret — you have a daughter. My sister is in Italy. My child will be left motherless, in this strange Milton place — if I die, will you —”

Her eyes, dim and filmy, fixed on Mrs. Thornton’s face with a longing so sharp it was almost tangible. For a long moment the older woman’s features stayed stern and unmoved, but a dark, unbidden cloud crossed her cold expression — stirred not by any thought of her son, or her own living daughter Fanny, but by a sudden memory of the tiny infant daughter she had buried decades before, long forgotten until that quiet, dim bedroom pulled the memory to the surface like a sunbeam melting thick river ice.

“You wish me to be a friend to Miss Hale,” Mrs. Thornton said, her voice still measured and clear, no trace of softness warming it even as her heart ached.

Mrs. Hale pressed the hand that lay under hers on the coverlet, too weak to speak.

“I will be a true friend, if circumstances require it,” Mrs. Thornton went on, sighing heavily. “Not a tender friend. That I cannot be — it is not my nature to show affection even where I feel it, nor do I volunteer unasked advice. But at your request, to give you comfort, I will promise.” She spoke with grave, unyielding severity, the kind of rock-solid reliability that gave the dying woman far more faith than any soft, wavering, empty vow could have done. “I promise that in any difficulty where Margaret comes to me for help, I will aid her with every power I have, as if she were my own daughter. I also promise that if ever I see her doing what I believe to be wrong — wrong that does not touch me or mine, so no one can say I act out of self-interest — I will tell her of it faithfully and plainly, as I would wish my own child to be told.”

Mrs. Hale felt the promise carried quiet reservations she could not quite untangle, but it was far more than she had dared hope for, weak and dizzy as she was on the edge of death. “I thank you. I pray God to bless you. I shall never see you again in this world, but my last words are gratitude for your promise of kindness to my child.”

“Not kindness,” Mrs. Thornton corrected, ungraciously truthful to the last. She was half glad the dying woman was too far gone to hear her. She pressed Mrs. Hale’s soft, languid hand, and slipped out of the house without stopping to speak to anyone else.

While that interview played out upstairs, Margaret and Dixon were hatching their plan to keep Frederick’s homecoming a strict secret from every soul outside the household. They could expect a letter from him any day now, and he was sure to follow hard on its heels. Martha, the young housemaid, would be sent off to visit her mother that very afternoon; Dixon would stand strict guard at the front door, turning away any casual callers to Mr. Hale’s downstairs study, using Mrs. Hale’s extreme illness as a perfect excuse to keep strangers away from the sick rooms. If they needed extra help in the kitchen, they would call on slow, incurious Mary Higgins from next door, who could be trusted to ask no questions, see nothing she was not meant to see — and if they had to, they would introduce Frederick to her as “Mr. Dickinson.” Her sluggish, uninterested nature was the very best safeguard they could ask for.

Margaret fretted that sending Martha off on an unexpected holiday, at a time when her mistress was so ill, would look strange to anyone watching the house, but there was no help for it. She spent the rest of the afternoon tending to her father, who could not bear to be alone in his study, who swung wildly between foolish, buoyant hope that his wife would rally and make a full recovery, and gut-wrenching despair every time a new, sharper paroxysm of pain wracked her frame. He sat hunched over the drawing-room table, his head buried in his folded arms, too broken to do any work, too raw for Margaret to dare offer empty words of comfort. The house grew dark, no one had thought to light the candles, and Margaret sat by the window watching the gas lamps glow on the Milton streets, seeing none of it, only listening to her father’s heavy, ragged sighs.

She was just about to slip down to tend the kitchen fire, when the front door bell jangled — pulled so hard the wires rattled all through the house, even through the muffling of the curtained door. She started to her feet, kissed her unheeding father tenderly on the top of his head, and crept down the dark stairs before he even stirred. Dixon would have shot the safety chain across the door before she unlatched it, but Margaret’s mind was so full of worry she never thought of fear. A tall, broad figure stood silhouetted against the bright street light. He turned the moment the latch clicked.

“Is this Mr. Hale’s house?” he asked, in a clear, full, familiar voice.

Margaret trembled so hard she could not speak for a second. Then she gasped out, “Frederick!” and stretched out both hands to pull him in over the threshold.

“Oh, Margaret,” he said, holding her off by the shoulders the moment they had kissed, his bright sea-blue eyes searching her face even in the dimness. “My mother — is she alive?”

“She is alive, dearest brother. She is as ill as ill can be, but she is still with us.”

“Thank God,” he breathed.

No letter had come ahead of him. He had sailed home faster than any of them had expected, and made the journey straight to Milton without stopping to post warning. Margaret led him to her father’s study, lit a single tallow taper, and for a long moment they stared at each other across the tiny flame, the years of separation falling away in one quiet glance. Margaret ran back upstairs to get her father, who could not at first bring himself to believe the news, sobbing that he was too weak to bear such a joy if it turned out to be nothing but a cruel dream. But Margaret coaxed him to his feet, leaning his weight on her arm, and led him to the study door. She could not bear to watch their first meeting, and ran back upstairs to cry, the tight, terrible strain that had gripped her for days breaking at last. For the first time in weeks, she was not the only one carrying the full weight of care for her parents. She slipped down later to check on them, heard the low, warm hum of their voices through the door, and went to the kitchen to light the fire and prepare refreshment for her long-lost brother.

When the tea was brewed and the buttered toast laid out, Margaret carried a heavy tray into the study like a serving maid, proud to wait on the brother she had not seen in eight years. Frederick sprang up immediately to take the weight from her arms, laughing that Dixon always said lighting a fire was a gift, not a skill to be learned, teasing Margaret that her hands were all thumbs at tending the hearth, and taking the poker from her to get the flames leaping bright in the grate. They moved in and out of the small room, little touches of their hands, little shared glances, no words needed between people of the same blood. Margaret bustled restlessly, happy to run any errand Frederick could think of, giddy with the small, stolen joy of being all together again in the house of mourning.

They heard Dixon’s measured step on the stairs halfway through their tea, and for one sharp, cold second they all froze, remembering the risk they ran if anyone discovered Frederick was here, the warrant hanging over his head for the mutiny on board the *Orion*. But it was only Dixon, coming to check on them, and they decided together they would wait until Dr. Donaldson called later that evening before waking Mrs. Hale to tell her her son was home, to make sure the news would not overexert her.

Margaret wandered past the study door again and again, catching glimpses of Frederick laughing over old Helstone stories, recalling long afternoons they had spent running wild in the New Forest, memories he had carried with him all through his years sailing the Mediterranean and the South Atlantic. He was not the rough, strange stranger Margaret had feared he might turn into, not a man unrecognisable from the bright young midshipman she had waved goodbye to all those years before. He was quick to laugh, quick to flare with earnest passion, but gentle as ever with his father, thoughtful to wait for the right moment to mention their long lost home. When he fumed that they ought to bring a London specialist down to see their mother, that the family had made a blunder leaving Helstone, Margaret gently reminded him how their father tormented himself with endless self-reproach over that very choice, and they could never say such a thing in his hearing.

Frederick hugged her at once, the fire of his impatience melting. “My little Margaret, dry those tears. We’ll hope as long as we possibly can, I don’t care what a thousand doctors say. Bear up. And remember my own maxim: do something, my sister. Do good if you can, but at any rate, do something.”

Margaret smiled, faint and wobbly, through her tears. The whole Hale family was home, at last, and for a little while, they could all cling together, no matter what hard days lay ahead.

The first chill, shivery dawn of October broke over Milton, bearing none of the soft silvery mists that would have draped the old Hampshire countryside of Helstone. Up here, the “silvery mist” was a thick, smut-heavy fog that clung to throat and clothing for hours, and when the sun did finally claw its way through the haze, it lit nothing but endless dusky, soot-streaked terraced streets. Margaret moved languidly about the house, helping Dixon tidy rooms and lay out linens, her eyes perpetually pricked blind by unshed tears. She had no time to give over to full, unreserved weeping. Her father and brother were both swallowed whole by their grief for Mrs. Hale, and someone had to hold the household steady, to plan, to negotiate, to make the necessary, heavy arrangements for the funeral that had fallen entirely to her.

When the fire was crackling bright in the hearth, the tea kettle singing on its hob, and the breakfast table set as pleasantly as she could manage, Margaret paused for a last look around the parlour before going to summon Mr. Hale and Frederick. She had striven to make the room feel as warm and welcoming as possible, but the brightness of the fire and the neat set of cups only sharpened the cruel contrast between the living present and the empty place her mother should occupy, and she sank down on her knees by the sofa, pressing her face deep into the cushions to muffle the sudden ragged sob tearing out of her throat.

A soft hand tapped her shoulder. “Come now, Miss Hale, my dear,” Dixon said, her usually sharp, prim voice softened by an uncharacteristic tenderness. “You cannot give way like this, or where will the rest of us be? There is no other soul in this house fit to give a single direction, and so much to settle—who will manage the funeral, who will come, where it will be held. Master Frederick is half crazed with weeping, and your poor master wanders about the house like a lost ghost. I know it’s bitter hard, my dear. But death comes for us all, and you have not had to bear the loss of anyone you loved before, thank God for that.”

Margaret drew no small comfort from the old housekeeper’s words, but the quiet kindness in her touch and tone struck her far deeper. She brushed the tears from her cheeks, forced a wavering smile for Dixon’s anxious face, and went to call her father and brother to breakfast.

Mr. Hale drifted in as if in a dream, moving with the unseeing gait of a sleepwalker whose eyes and mind are still fixed on the world they have left behind. Frederick came in fast, with a forced, bright cheerfulness that fell apart the second he grasped Margaret’s hand, looked into her red-rimmed eyes, and burst into great heaving sobs. Through the whole meal, Margaret chattered of small, trivial things, desperate to keep her companions from circling back to the last breakfast they had shared with their mother, when the three of them had sat straining their ears for any faint sound from her sickroom.

After breakfast, Margaret approached her father to discuss the funeral arrangements. He shook his head numbly, assenting to every proposal she made, even the ones that directly contradicted each other, too lost in grief to make a single firm decision. She turned to leave the room to confer with Dixon, when he motioned her back to his side.

“Write to Mr. Bell,” he said in a hollow, distant voice.

“Mr. Bell? The one from Oxford?” Margaret asked, surprised.

“Mr. Bell,” he repeated. “He was my groomsman, long ago.”

Margaret understood the quiet association to old, happier days, and promised she would write that very day. He sank back into his listless daze immediately, and Margaret spent the rest of the morning toiling through a whirl of melancholy small tasks, longing for a rest that would not come.

Towards evening, Dixon drew her aside. “I did it, miss. I was so afraid your poor father would drop down dead of a stroke, he’s spent every hour of this day in Mrs. Hale’s old room, and I’ve stood outside the door listening to him talk to her, just as if she was sitting there answering back. I thought a sharp shock would rouse him from that half-dead stupor, even if it stung at first. So I told him Master Frederick can’t stay here, it’s not safe. And it’s the truth. Just last Tuesday, when I was out running errands, I ran into a Southampton man—the first I’ve seen since we came to Milton. It was that young George Leonards, old Leonards the draper’s son, the biggest scamp that ever walked the earth, who plagued his father half to death before he ran off to sea. I know he was serving on the *Orion* at the same time as Master Frederick, though I can’t say for certain if he was there the day of the mutiny.”

“Did he recognise you?” Margaret asked, her blood running cold.

“That’s the worst of it. I never would have called out his name, but I was caught off guard, seeing a familiar face in such a strange place. He came right up to me grinning, calling me Miss Dixon, asking after you all. The more we talked, the more he got sharp, and started needling me about Master Frederick—saying he’d be strung up for mutiny the second anyone caught him, that there was a hundred pound reward out for his arrest, going on about what a disgrace he was to his family, all because I’d helped his old father give him a sound thrashing once for stealing from the shop till. He hopped on the bus at last, but turned back at the last second to leer at me and say, ‘If you want to partner up for that reward to trap Lieutenant Hale, Miss Dixon, I’ll cut you a fair share. Don’t be shy, say yes.’ Then he was gone, cackling.”

Margaret sank down into the nearest chair, suddenly very uneasy. “You haven’t told Frederick yet?”

“Not yet, miss. But when I saw your father wandering the rooms so glassy-eyed and lost, I thought the worry for his son’s safety would rouse him better than anything. Master Frederick will have to leave before Mr. Bell arrives, that’s certain.”

“Oh, I am not afraid of Mr. Bell, but this Leonards—he’s a vicious, spiteful man. I must tell Frederick at once.”

It was settled then. Frederick had to go, just when he had slotted so easily back into the Hale household, when his shared grief for their mother had made him feel more like a beloved, necessary part of the family than he had ever been before. Margaret was lost in these sad thoughts by the drawing room fire, her father restless and fidgeting with the new, unspoken fear hanging over the house, when Frederick wandered in. His wild, violent grief had softened, but his face was still wan and pale. He crossed the room to kiss her forehead.

“How thin and tired you look, Margaret,” he said softly. “You have tended to every single one of us, and no one has tended to you. Lie down on this sofa, there is nothing left for you to do right now.”

Margaret obeyed, and he tucked a shawl over her feet, then sat cross-legged on the floor beside her, their voices dropping to soft, low tones as she told him the full story of Dixon’s run-in with Leonards. Frederick let out a long, low whistle of dismay.

“That scoundrel. I never met a worse sailor, nor a worse man, in all my years at sea. He was the sort that curried favour with that brutal captain that caused the mutiny, tattling on the rest of us to save his own skin. To think he’s here in Milton! If he had even a hint I was within twenty miles of him, he’d hunt me down for that hundred pound out of pure old spite.”

Mr. Hale had drifted close to them, and overheard the last of their words. He reached out to take Frederick’s hand in both of his own, trembling. “My boy, you must go. It is cruel, but there is no way around it. You have done all you can for your mother, for us.”

“Oh papa, must he really go?” Margaret pleaded, even as her own heart told her it was the only safe choice.

Frederick laughed a little, bitter. “I’ve half a mind to stay and face it, stand my trial. I can’t bear the thought of being at the mercy of that blackguard Leonards. Though I’ll admit, this stolen visit home has had all that strange, sharp sweetness the French talk about, the charm of forbidden pleasures.”

“Do you remember when you were seven?” Margaret said, a faint, wobbly smile tugging at her mouth. “You snuck over to the neighbour’s orchard to steal apples, even though our own trees were heavy with far better fruit. Someone told you stolen apples tasted sweeter, and you took it completely literally. You haven’t changed an ounce since then.”

“You must go,” Mr. Hale repeated firmly, his mind fixed on the only clear, urgent thought in his head. “No delays.”

Frederick shook off his glum mood, and chuckled. “You know, I almost gave myself and Dixon a terrible fright this afternoon. I heard a ring at the front door, and assumed whoever it was had left ages ago, so I went to step out of my bedroom into the passage. Then Dixon came rushing up the stairs, frowning so hard she looked fit to burst, and kicked me back into hiding before I could be seen. I left the door cracked, and I heard a big, burly man giving a message to father, then leaving. I thought he was some shopman come to collect an order.”

“It was Mr. Thornton,” Mr. Hale said, from his chair.

Margaret blinked, surprised. “Mr. Thornton? I thought—”

“Thought what, little one?” Frederick prompted, when she trailed off.

“Oh nothing,” Margaret said, flushing. “I just assumed you meant a workman, not a gentleman.”

“Sure looked like a working man, to me,” Frederick said carelessly. “Took him for a mill hand at first. Turns out he’s the manufacturer, then.”

Margaret said nothing. She remembered thinking the very same thing when she first met Mr. Thornton, before she learned how kind, thoughtful, honourable he was, and a tiny flicker of annoyance pricked at her, though she could not find the words to defend him without sounding odd.

“He came by to offer any help he could give us, I think,” Mr. Hale went on, absently. “I was too lost to see him. I told Dixon to send him to you, Margaret, but I can’t rightly remember what else I said.”

“He has been a very kind friend to us through all of this,” Margaret said, when Frederick tossed out a light question about their acquaintance.

Frederick was quiet for a long minute, then he spoke, a little sheepish. “Margaret, I wish you and father would come away to Spain with me. I have a good post there, and a chance at an even better one. There’s a girl, Dolores Barbour—she’s not quite eighteen yet, but if she feels the same a year from now, we’re to be married. Her father won’t let us call it a formal engagement, but I’ve never cared for anyone half so much. I only wish you could meet her. Father, you’d love her, Margaret—everyone would. We could make a whole new life there.”

“No more moves for me,” Mr. Hale said, shaking his head. “This last one cost me my wife. I will not move again. She is buried here, and I will stay here for the rest of my days.”

Margaret leaned over, and squeezed Frederick’s hand. “Tell us more about her. I am so happy for you. You will have someone to love and care for you, far from here. But Fred—for her sake, shouldn’t you try to clear your name? You disobeyed a brutal, cruel captain to protect the rest of the crew, not out of malice. The world only knows you as a mutineer, and that will hang over her for the rest of her life, unless you prove otherwise. You should at least talk to a good, honest lawyer, see what your chances are of clearing your name.”

Frederick scoffed a little, but Margaret persisted, until at last he agreed to take her suggestion. She proposed Henry Lennox, Edith’s brother-in-law, the brilliant young barrister who had once been so fond of her, a man whose honour was beyond reproach. They planned for him to take the night train to London, write out a list of all the surviving crew members from the *Orion*, meet Lennox at his chambers in the Temple, and then sail back to Spain from a London port, no need to go near Liverpool.

Margaret wrote the note to Mr. Lennox quickly, before she could overthink every word, and Frederick snatched it from her hand before she could proofread it, tucking it into his pocket-book. As he did, a long lock of glossy black hair slipped out onto the table, and his face lit up.

“You’d like to see that, wouldn’t you?” he grinned, tucking it carefully back into the pocket-book. “But no. You will wait to meet Dolores herself. She is far too perfect to be summed up in little fragments. No mean brick can ever be a proper sample of the whole palace I intend to bring you to visit, one day soon.”

“What! remain to be Denounced—dragged, it may be, in chains.” — WERNER

The three Hales sat huddled together in the parlour, the whole long day slipping by in a heavy, hush-thick fog of grief. Mr. Hale barely spoke unless his children pressed him, drawing him back from his haunted ruminations to the present moment, almost as if he had to be forced into interacting with the world around him. Frederick’s raw, screaming paroxysm of grief the day before had burned itself out; he was almost ashamed now of how thoroughly emotion had unravelled him, though the deep, unshakable ache of losing his mother settled permanent and heavy in his bones, a weight he knew he would carry for the rest of his life, never to speak of aloud again. Margaret, who had held herself stiff and resolute at first, was unravelling now instead. She cried in quiet, uncontrollable bursts, and even her speech on trivial, everyday topics carried a soft, mournful tenderness that deepened every time her eyes fell on Frederick, counting down the hours until his inevitable, dangerous departure.

For her father’s sake, she was glad to see him go, no matter how much she grieved the separation for her own part. The constant, frayed terror Mr. Hale lived in — that his mutinous, long-absent son would be recognized, dragged off in chains to face the charges hanging over his head — far outweighed the fragile, fleeting joy he drew from having Frederick home. Since Mrs. Hale’s death, that nervousness had only grown sharper. He jumped at every unusual creak of the floorboard, every distant unfamiliar voice, and never felt at ease unless Frederick was tucked entirely out of the line of sight of anyone who might step through the parlour door.

Towards dusk, he spoke up, his voice thin and trembling: “You will go with Frederick to the station, Margaret? I need to know he is safely away. Promise me you will come back and tell me he has cleared Milton, at the very least.”

“Of course,” Margaret said. “I will be glad to go, as long as you will not be lonely here by yourself, Papa.”

“No, no! I will sit here imagining every stranger on the road has found him out and dragged him off, unless you come home and tell me you saw him off safe. And go to Outwood Station, not the main Milton one. It is just as close, and far less crowded. Take a cab there, too. Fewer chances of anyone spotting him. What time is your train, Fred?”

“Ten minutes past six. It will be nearly dark by then. What will you do on the way back, Margaret?” Frederick asked.

“Oh, I will manage,” she said, forcing a bright, steady tone. “I have gotten very brave lately, have I not? The whole road home is well-lit even after dark, and I was out much later than that just last week.”

Margaret breathed a quiet sigh of relief when the lingering, painful goodbye to their mother’s body and their father was over. She herded Frederick into the waiting cab as fast as she could, to cut short the scene that was tearing Mr. Hale apart, who had followed his son to the threshold of the room where his wife lay, to stare at her face one last time. Between that rush to leave, and the careless, all-too-common error in the railway guide’s listed arrival times for small country stations, they pulled up to Outwood to find they had nearly twenty minutes to kill before the train was due. The booking office was still locked, so they could not even buy their tickets ahead of time. They wandered down the flight of stone steps that led below the railway embankment, to a wide cinder path that cut diagonally through a field beside the main carriage road, walking back and forth to pass the minutes.

Margaret’s hand rested light on Frederick’s arm, and he curled his own hand over hers, warm and tight. “Margaret, once I am safely away I am going to write to Mr. Lennox to see if there is any way I can clear my name, make it safe to come back to England whenever I want — more for your sake than anyone else’s. I cannot bear the thought of you all alone here if anything happens to Papa. He looks so worn, so terribly shaken, I barely recognize him. I wish you would get him to agree to that plan of moving to Cadiz with me, for a dozen reasons. What would you do if he was gone? You have no close friends, no family nearby. We are strangely bare of relations to fall back on, are we not?”

Margaret had to fight hard not to burst into tears, at the tender, worried way he laid out a future she already feared was all too possible, every care of the last months having worn Mr. Hale down to a shadow of his old self. But she rallied her voice, soft as ever: “The last two years have been so full of strange, unexpected turns, that I have learned not to waste time trying to plan for every possible bad thing that might happen. I try only to live in the present, now.” She paused, and they stilled for a moment, right beside the stile that led from the field out to the road, the last slants of the setting sun gilding their faces. Frederick looked down at her, his face tight with unspoken worry, reading all the quiet grief and weariness she tried not to put into words.

“We will write to each other all the time,” she went on, “and I promise you — I can see it will put your mind at ease — I will tell you every little worry that crops up. Papa is —” She cut off, a tiny, almost invisible flinch running through her, but Frederick felt the jolt of her hand in his, and turned his full face to the road. A man on horseback was riding slow past the stile right where they stood. Margaret bowed stiffly, and the man returned the gesture with a sharp, cold nod.

“Who was that?” Frederick asked, before the man was even out of earshot. Margaret was a little flushed, her shoulders drooping a little as she answered: “Mr. Thornton. You saw him once before, remember, from behind.”

“He is an unprepossessing-looking fellow, is he not?” Frederick said. “What a scowl he had on his face.”

“Something must have happened to vex him,” Margaret said, quick to defend him. “You would not think he looked harsh if you had seen him sitting with my mother, the days before she died.”

“I think it must be time to head back for the station to get my ticket,” Frederick said, shifting the subject. “If I had known how fast the dark was going to fall, Margaret, I would not have paid off the cab to send it back.”

“Do not fuss over me,” she said. “I can always hail another cab right here if I want, or I can take the stopping train back to Milton, where there are shops and people and lamps all along the road from the station to our house. Do not think about me, just watch yourself. I am sick at the thought that Leonards might be on the very same train as you. Look every carriage over carefully before you step on board.”

They walked back up to the platform. Margaret insisted on stepping right up to the flaring bright gas light inside the station office to buy her brother’s ticket. A handful of idle young men were loitering by the station master, and Margaret swore she recognized one of their faces; she met his impudent, openly staring gaze with a proud, sharp look of offended dignity, before hurrying back out to Frederick where he waited on the platform, and looping her arm through his.

“Do you have your bag? Let us walk up and down the platform a little,” she said, a little flustered, her old brave resolve oozing away faster than she cared to admit, even to herself. She heard footsteps following them along the flagstones, steps that stopped when they stopped, both of them leaning out over the edge of the platform to listen for the distant whine of the coming train. Neither spoke; their hearts were too full, too tight with unsaid things. In one more minute the train would be there, and a minute after that, he would be gone. Margaret almost regretted pushing him so hard to leave for London first; if he had sailed straight for Spain out of Liverpool, he could have been out of the country in two or three hours, no risk of being caught at all.

Frederick turned sharply, right towards the bright platform lamp where the gas flared high in anticipation of the arriving train. A man dressed as a railway porter lunged forward: a rough, brutish-looking man, whose face was bloated with drink even as his senses were still sharp enough to act. “Begging your pardon, miss!” he grunted, shoving Margaret roughly out of the way, and seizing Frederick hard by the collar of his coat. “Your name is Hale, I believe?”

Everything swam before Margaret’s eyes, she never saw exactly how it happened — but with a quick, sharp twist of a wrestling move he had learned years before at sea, Frederick tripped the man hard, and he toppled off the three-foot high platform, falling hard onto the soft dirt and gravel beside the railway tracks. He lay there, motionless for a second.

“Run! Run!” Margaret gasped out, shoving Frederick towards the train that was now pulling to a stop. “That was Leonards, was it not? Go, quickly! I will carry your bag!” She pushed him with all the strength she had left. A carriage door swung open right in front of them; Frederick leaped inside, and as he leaned out the window to yell “God bless you, Margaret!” the train rumbled forwards, carrying him away, and left her standing all alone on the empty platform.

She felt so sick, so faint with shock, that she stumbled gratefully into the ladies’ waiting room and sank down onto a bench for a minute. At first she could do nothing but gasp for air. The rush, the terror, the near miss had been too much. If the train had not pulled up exactly at that moment, Leonards would have scrambled back up and called for help to arrest him. She wondered if the man had gotten up; she tried to remember if she had seen him move, wondered if he had been badly hurt in the fall. She forced herself back out onto the platform, which was lit bright by gas lamps but still completely empty, and walked to the far end to peer down over the edge, heart thudding. No one was there. She was glad she had made herself look, otherwise terrible half-imagined fears would have haunted her all night, in her sleep. Even so, she was trembling so hard, so shaken, that she knew she could not walk the long dark lonely road home, which stretched out black and empty as far as she could see beyond the station lights. She would wait for the next down train, and ride it back to Milton. But what if Leonards recognized her as the woman who had helped Frederick escape?

She peered around carefully before she dared step into the booking office to buy her ticket. Only a handful of railway officials stood there, talking loudly to each other.

“So Leonards has been on the drink again, eh?” said one man, who sounded like he was in charge. “He will need all that boasted influence of his to keep his job after this.”

“Where is he, then?” another asked. Margaret stood with her back to them, fumbling with her change with shaking fingers, not daring to turn around until she heard the answer.

“Damned if I know. He stumbled in here not five minutes back, rambling some nonsense about falling off the platform, swearing fit to make your ears burn, begging me for money to get on the next up train to London. He was making all sorts of daft promises to pay me back, but I had better things to do than listen to a drunk. Told him to get lost, and he went staggering out the front door.”

“He is down the nearest pub vault, I would wager,” the first man laughed. “Your money would have gone straight there too, if you had been fool enough to lend it to him.”

“Catch me doing that! I know what his ‘London trip’ nonsense means. He still owes me five shillings from last month —” And their voices drifted on, no longer of any concern to her.

All Margaret could do now was wait, on tenterhooks, for her train to arrive. She slipped back into the ladies’ waiting room, and every footstep that passed the door, every loud, rough laugh, sounded like Leonards to her. But no one disturbed her until the train pulled into the station, when a porter helped her politely up into an empty carriage. She did not dare look up into his face until the train had started moving, and then she saw with a flood of relief that it was not Leonards at all.

“SLEEP on, my love, in thy cold bed,
Never to be disquieted!
My last Good Night—thou wilt not wake
Till I thy fate shall overtake.”

The Hale house hung unnaturally, eerily quiet after the roar of the mob, the scuffling terror, and the breathless commotion of the night before. Mr. Hale had overseen every small preparation for Margaret’s return, seeing that the fire was stoked high, that warm tea and bread waited on the table, that every last trace of the riot’s intrusion had been swept away—then he sank back into his accustomed armchair and drifted off into one of his heavy, sad waking dreams, his thoughts turned entirely to the wife he had lost.

Dixon had dragged Mary Higgins into the kitchen to chivvy her along with household tasks, and her scolding was no less sharp for being delivered in a fierce, hushed whisper: to speak above her breath, while a dead woman lay still in the upstairs parlour, would have struck her as irreverent, unforgivable.

Margaret had long since resolved never to speak of that final, shattering fright on the platform to her father. There was no use. It had ended well, and Frederick was safely away—save for the small, gnawing fear that Leonards might scrape together enough borrowed money to follow him to London, to hunt him down there. But the odds were stacked heavily against such a plan succeeding. She would not torment herself over a threat she was powerless to stop. Frederick would be as wary as she could warn him to be, and in a day or two at most he would be safely across the Channel, out of England for good.

“I expect we shall hear from Mr. Bell tomorrow,” she said softly.
“Yes,” her father replied, hollow-voiced. “I suppose so.”
“If he can travel, he will be here tomorrow evening, I should think.”
“If he cannot come, I shall ask Mr. Thornton to walk with me to the funeral. I cannot face it alone. I should break down entirely.”
“Don’t ask Mr. Thornton, papa. Let me go with you,” Margaret burst out, impetuous.
“You? My dear, women do not usually attend funerals.”
“No—because they cannot master their own hearts. Women of our class stay away, for they have no control over their emotions, and yet are ashamed to show them. Poor working women go, and do not care if they are seen bowed and overwhelmed by grief. But I promise you, papa, if you will let me come, I shall be no trouble. Do not bring a stranger, and shut me out. Dear papa—if Mr. Bell cannot come, I shall go. I will not press the matter against your will if he arrives, though.”

Mr. Bell never came. A letter arrived soon after, warm with affection, full of genuine, deep regret: a severe gout attack had confined him to his home, and he could not travel an inch. He wrote that he planned to visit Milton soon regardless—his Milton estate required tending, his agent had written that his presence was unavoidable, and the only thing that reconciled him to the tedious journey he had long avoided was the thought of seeing his old friend, and doing what he could to comfort him in his loss.

Margaret fought to the last to persuade her father not to send for Mr. Thornton. There was an indescribable, sharp repugnance in her chest at the very thought. The night before the funeral, a stiff, formal note arrived from Mrs. Thornton, addressed to Margaret: at her son’s request, their carriage would be sent to attend the funeral, if the arrangement would not be disagreeable to the Hales.

Margaret tossed the note to her father. “Oh, do not let us have these empty formalities,” she said. “Let us go alone—you and me, papa. They do not care for us, or else he would have offered to come himself, instead of sending a useless empty carriage as a performative show of respect.”
“I thought you were so very averse to his attending at all, Margaret,” Mr. Hale said, bewildered.
“And so I am. I do not want him anywhere near us. I especially hated the thought that we might beg him to come. But this—this feels like a mockery of mourning. I never expected that from him.”

She startled her father by bursting into sudden, heavy tears. For days she had been so quiet and subdued in her grief, so thoughtful of everyone else, so gentle and patient, that he could not account for her sudden restlessness and agitation that night. The more he fussed over her, soft with his own sorrowful tenderness, the harder she cried.

She slept so poorly that night she was scarcely prepared for the new spike of anxiety that came with the post: a letter from Frederick. Mr. Lennox was out of town, his clerk wrote, and would not return until the latest Tuesday, perhaps not until Monday. After weighing his options, Frederick had decided to stay in London two days longer.
