The temptation to sneak back to Milton had been almost overwhelming, but the thought of Mr. Bell installed in his father’s home, and the final fright he had received at the railway platform, had made him resolve to stay put. He swore to Margaret he would take every possible precaution to avoid being tracked by Leonards.

Margaret thanked her lucky stars she received the letter while her father was upstairs tending to his wife’s things. If he had been there, he would have expected her to read it aloud, and the news of Frederick’s extended stay in London—coupled with references to the close call at the station, the very real risk of pursuit—would have plunged him into a state of nervous panic Margaret could never have soothed. More than once she found herself wincing at the memory of suggesting they consult Mr. Lennox. At the time it had seemed such a small, harmless step, barely adding to the tiny risks of detection—but every event since had proven it to be a terrible mistake. She fought that useless, bitter self-reproach hard, for there was no undoing what was done. Her father’s mind and body were far too weak to bear the weight of that secret, that fear; he would crumple entirely under it. She summoned every scrap of her fortitude to the surface, to stand strong for both of them.

Her father had forgotten all expectation of a letter from Frederick that morning. His whole world had narrowed to one single, aching thought: that the last living trace of his wife was about to be carried away from him, hidden from his sight forever. He trembled pitifully as the undertaker’s assistant arranged the crape draperies over his shoulders. He looked at Margaret with a lost, wistful gaze, and when the man was done, he tottered over to her, murmuring, “Pray for me, Margaret. I have no strength left. I cannot find the words to pray. I give her up because I must. I try to bear it, truly I do. I know it is God’s will. But I cannot understand why she had to die. Pray for me, Margaret, that I might find the faith to pray. It is such a hard strait, my child.”

Margaret sat beside him in the closed coach, almost holding him up in her arms, reciting every verse of holy comfort, every text of faithful resignation she could call to mind. Her voice never faltered, and in speaking the words, she drew strength into herself. Her father’s lips moved after hers, repeating the familiar texts as she spoke them. It was unbearable, watching him struggle so hard to grasp the resignation he was too broken to hold in his heart.

Margaret’s own fortitude nearly snapped when Dixon gave a tiny, quiet nod of her head, drawing her attention to Nicholas Higgins and his youngest daughter standing a little apart from the other mourners, watching the ceremony with grave, respectful attention. Nicholas wore his usual rough fustian work clothes, but a scrap of black cloth was stitched around the brim of his hat—a mark of mourning he had never worn even for his own lost daughter Bessy.

Mr. Hale saw none of it. He went on mechanically repeating the words of the funeral service as the clergyman read them. He sighed two or three times when the final words were spoken, then laid a hand on Margaret’s arm, mutely begging her to lead him away, as if he had gone blind, and she was his only faithful guide.

Dixon was sobbing openly, her face buried in her handkerchief, so lost in her own grief that she never noticed the crowd of onlookers dispersing until a man spoke close beside her. It was Mr. Thornton. He had been standing there the whole time, head bowed, hidden behind a group of workmen, so that no one had spotted him.
“I beg your pardon—but can you tell me how Mr. Hale is? And Miss Hale, too? I should like to know how they fare.”
“Of course, sir. They are as well as can be expected. Master is terribly broken down. Miss Hale bears up far better than anyone would dare hope.”

Mr. Thornton would rather have heard that she was consumed by raw, natural sorrow. There was a selfish, hungry part of him that had clung to the fantasy that his great love could step in to comfort her, to console her— the same fierce, tender pleasure that stings a mother’s heart when her sick, drooping infant nestles close, dependent on her for everything. But that soft, glowing vision of what might have been, that he would have indulged in only days before, shattered completely at the memory of what he had seen near the Outwood railway station. “Shattered” was not half strong enough. The memory of the handsome young man standing so close to her, so familiar, so trusted, shot through him like a physical agony. He clenched his fists tight at his sides to subdue the pain.

It took a tremendous effort of will to try to resuscitate the perfect, unshakable trust he had once held in Margaret’s pure, delicate maidenliness. The second that effort slackened, the trust died again, powerless, and all kinds of wild, jealous fancies chased each other through his mind like half-formed dreams. Dixon’s words were a sharp, gnawing confirmation: she bore up too well under this grief. That meant she had some other bright hope to cling to, bright enough to cut through the dark of losing her mother. He knew full well how fiercely Margaret could love. He had loved her long enough to know the full depth of passion in her soul. Her whole world would glow in sunlight if a man worthy of that love won her heart, and she would rest peacefully, trustingly, on his sympathy. His sympathy? No. That other man’s.

The thought of that other man made Mr. Thornton’s already pale, grave face go twice as wan and stern.
“I may call, I suppose,” he said coldly. “On Mr. Hale, that is. He might admit me in a day or two, once he is more rested.” He spoke as if the answer meant nothing to him, but it was a lie. For all the pain gnawing at his chest, he ached to see the woman who had caused it. Even when he hated her, at the thought of that familiar, gentle pose by the railway, he felt a restless, unquenchable urge to see her again, to breathe the same air she breathed, to re-memorize every line of her face. He was caught in the Charybdis of his own passion, forced to circle closer and closer to the fatal centre he could not escape.
“I daresay master will see you, sir,” Dixon replied. “He was very sorry he had to turn you away the other day, but things were very unsettled then, very unpleasant.”

For some unaccountable reason, Dixon never mentioned this conversation, or the fact that Mr. Thornton had stood hidden at Mrs. Hale’s funeral, to Margaret. It was a small secret, held quiet between the two of them, that Margaret would never learn.

“Truth will fail thee never, never!
Though thy bark be tempest-driven,
Though each plank be rent and riven,
Truth will bear thee on for ever!”

Everyone had praised Margaret for bearing her mother’s loss far better than anyone could have expected, but that constant, rigid self-restraint was tearing her to pieces from the inside. Mid-laugh while making soft, gentle conversation with her father, a sudden sharp memory would cut through her: her mother was gone, forever, and it was all she could do not to cry out from the raw, unnameable pain of it.

Worse still was the gnawing, unspoken anxiety over Frederick. The Sunday post had delayed their expected letters from London, and when Tuesday rolled around, Margaret opened the post bag to find no word from him at all. She had no idea what plans he had made, no way of knowing if he was safe, and the uncertainty was eating her father alive. He could no longer stick to his new, quiet habit of sitting still in his favorite easy chair for half a day at a time. He paced restlessly from room to room, wandered out onto the landing to open and shut empty bedroom doors for no clear purpose, and could not even follow along when Margaret tried to read aloud to soothe him. She thanked God every minute that she had never told him of their run-in with the disreputable Leonards at the railway station— that extra weight of fear was hers, and hers alone to carry.

Her relief was immense when the maid announced Mr. Thornton. His visit would distract her father, pull his jumbled, anxious thoughts onto something steady. He walked straight across the room to Mr. Hale, clasped both his hands in silence, and held them for a long minute. The grief and quiet sympathy on his face spoke volumes more than any formal condolence could have managed. Then he turned to Margaret. Her usual stately, luminous beauty was dimmed from weeks of late nights watching over her ailing mother and all the hidden tears she had cried when no one was looking. Her face bore the soft mark of patient, unhidden suffering, sharp with quiet current pain.

He had fully intended to keep up the cold, distant demeanor he had practiced around her since the misunderstanding in Outwood Lane, but he could not stop himself from stepping toward her where she stood half-hidden at the edge of the room, and speaking the expected polite greetings in a voice so soft and tender that her eyes filled with tears at once, and she turned quickly away to hide her emotion. She picked up her sewing and sat very still, silent in the corner. Mr. Thornton’s heart thudded fast in his chest, and for a little while he forgot all about that bitter quarrel entirely. He talked with Mr. Hale, and his steady, decisive presence was such a comforting, safe anchor for the frazzled, unsettled older man that Margaret could see the lines of tension in her father’s face softening just a little.

Then Dixon appeared at the drawing room door, her face flushed and flurried. “Miss Hale, might I have a word?” Margaret’s stomach dropped like a stone. She knew at once something had gone wrong with Frederick. She was grateful her father and Mr. Thornton were so caught up in their conversation that neither noticed her pale face as she stepped out into the hall.

“What is it, Dixon?” she whispered the second the drawing room door clicked shut behind her. “Come this way, miss,” Dixon said, leading her to the bedchamber that had once belonged to Mrs. Hale— now Margaret’s, for Mr. Hale could not bear to step foot in it after his wife’s death. “It’s nothing, probably nothing at all, miss— just a police inspector asking to see you. I tried speaking to him first, but he says he will only speak to you. Martha showed him into your father’s study.”

Margaret did not speak again until her hand was on the study door lock. She turned back to Dixon, her voice tight. “Make sure my father does not come down here. Mr. Thornton is with him, he can keep him occupied.”

The inspector was almost taken aback by the haughty, unyielding air she carried into the room. There was sharp, carefully contained indignation on her face, no surprise, no fidgety fear, no eagerness to explain herself. She simply stood, perfectly still, waiting for him to state his business.

“Begging your pardon, ma’am, but duty compels me to ask a few plain questions. A man died this morning at the town Infirmary, from injuries sustained in a fall at Outwood railway station last Thursday, between five and six in the evening. Doctors say the fall itself would not have killed him, but a long-standing internal ailment and his heavy drinking turned the injury fatal. We are expecting to hold an inquest. A witness on the platform saw a scuffle break out, when a half-intoxicated man made impertinent advances to a young lady walking with a gentleman, and that gentleman pushed the deceased back over the edge of the platform. The witness thought nothing of it at the time, as the fall looked trivial. We have reason to believe that young lady is you, Miss Hale.”

Margaret’s large dark eyes dilated just a fraction. Otherwise, she did not flinch, did not tremble, did not look away. Her lips tensed into a firm, unyielding curve, but the inspector, though sharp, did not know her face well enough to spot the defiance in the set of her jaw. “Well?” she said, steady as a rock. “Go on.”

“I was not there,” Margaret said, her voice even, unwavering, her gaze fixed on his face like that of a sleepwalker, glassy and unblinking.

The inspector ran over the facts he knew in his head as he studied her: the porter who saw the scuffle had only caught a glimpse of the pair from the far end of the platform, the station master remembered a remarkably handsome young lady and gentleman on the platform at that exact hour, and a local grocer’s assistant who was waiting for a train had recognized the lady as Miss Hale of Crampton. The dead man, Leonards, had stumbled away from the station drunk and enraged, raving that a “Cornish trick” had cost him a hundred pounds, before collapsing unconscious in the road. He had never woken up enough to give a clear statement, only rambling about being at sea and shouting names of naval lieutenants and captains alongside those of his fellow porters.

“So, madam, you deny that you were the lady accompanying the gentleman who gave the push that caused this poor man’s death?” he asked directly.

A searing, sharp pain lanced through Margaret’s brain. *Oh God—if only I knew Frederick was safely across the water.* A master observer of human emotion might have caught that flash of raw, cornered agony shoot across her features, but the inspector missed it. Her reply was slow, heavy, almost a dull echo of her first denial: “I was not there.”

His suspicions pricked. It sounded as if she had forced herself to speak one untruth so hard, she had become incapable of varying it. He deliberately closed his notebook. “It is very unlikely, ma’am, but I may be forced to call on you again, and summon you to appear at the inquest to provide an alibi, if our witness persists in identifying you as the lady present.” He had expected to see her wince, to see a flicker of guilt cross her proud face. She did not move a muscle, as still and unyielding as a carved Egyptian statue. Abashed a little by her total composure, he apologized for the intrusion, noting he had only been doing his duty, and turned to leave.

Margaret walked before him, eyes still fixed on his back in that glazed, unblinking stare, until she had seen him out the front door. She shut the door behind him, half-stepped into the study, then turned back on a sudden fierce impulse to lock the front bolt. She stumbled a few more steps forward, swayed once where she stood, and fell prone to the floor in a dead faint, all the iron self-control she had held for weeks finally snapping clean in two.

“There’s nought so finely spun But it cometh to the sun.”

Mr. Thornton lingered long past the hour he had meant to take his leave of the Hales’ cottage. He could see that his company soothed the widowed Mr. Hale, who every few minutes would murmur a plaintive, half-childish plea: “Don’t go yet.” He had no lingering hope of seeing Margaret on this visit, nor did he seek her out; in the hush of that house, where the very air hummed with the quiet shock of recent grief, he was calm and self-contained, drawn to the soft-spoken scholar whose mind turned endlessly to the mysteries of death and the dim, foggy edges of faith.

It was a curious thing, how Thornton’s steady, unassuming presence unlocked thoughts in Mr. Hale that he had never dared share even with his own daughter. He feared Margaret’s sympathy would burn too bright, that her sharp distress at his unsteady doubts of the afterlife would shake what little peace he still held. But Thornton—hardened man of action, leader of thousands of mill workers, tangled in the daily strife of Midland industry—met every half-formed fear, every wandering, tear-blinded uncertainty, not with shock or rebuke, but with a quiet insight that proved he had trod that very dark path of doubt himself. No grand piety, no empty platitudes, only a deep, stubborn faith rooted in the mistakes and struggles of his own life, far deeper than Mr. Hale had ever guessed lay beneath the rough exterior of the mill owner. They would never speak of these matters again, but that one quiet conversation wove a bond between them no casual, loose talk of sacred things could ever match: for when all is laid bare, there is no Holy of Holies left to hold between two souls.

All this while, Margaret lay white and still as death on the floor of the next room, where she had sunk under the weight of the terrible secret she had carried for hours. She had been meek, patient, bearing the strain of her brother’s dangerous return to England unflinching, until her faith had snapped, and she had fallen in a dead faint. Her beautiful brows were tight with invisible pain, the sullen, defiant jut of her lips from moments earlier softened to a pale, livid slackness.

“E par che de la sua labbia si mova Uno spirto soave e pien d’amore, Chi va dicendo a l’anima: sospira!”

The first flicker of returning life was a faint, wordless quiver of her lips, a silent attempt to speak, before she summoned the last of her strength to push herself upright. Her hair comb had fallen to the floor, and she fumbled for it, pausing again and again to sit down and catch her breath, desperate to smooth her hair, wipe the traces of her weakness away before anyone found her. She could not bring herself to recall the exact details of the terror that had felled her, only two facts: her brother Frederick was hunted, at risk of being dragged back to face charges of mutiny and manslaughter, and she had lied, boldly, to the inspector to protect him. That lie had bought him time, that was the small comfort. If the inspector returned before she got word that Frederick had set sail for safety, she would tell that lie again, no matter how bitter the penance later, no matter if she had to stand in a crowded courtroom and confess her falsehood to all the world after.

Dixon bustled in to tell her she had just shown Mr. Thornton out.

He had barely walked ten steps down the lane when a passing omnibus pulled up beside him, and a man in police uniform climbed down, touching his hat to him. It was George Watson, the inspector Thornton had gotten his first job in the force years prior, a protégé he had not seen in many months. Watson explained he was working the case of the man Leonards, who had died the night before in the town infirmary, whose deposition Thornton had taken as a local magistrate. The man’s death, Watson said, was tangled up with the Hales’ household: he had a witness who swore he saw a gentleman walking with Miss Margaret Hale on the Outwood Station platform the evening Leonards had fallen, minutes before he was fatally injured. But Miss Hale had sworn, clear as day, that she had never been anywhere near the station that night.

“Miss Hale denies she was there?” Thornton repeated, his voice dropping low, sharp with sudden dread. He asked for the date, and when he heard it was the Thursday of Frederick’s visit, his blood ran cold. He told Watson not to take a single further step in the case, to come meet him at his private warehouse at four o’clock, and he would give him his instructions then.

Thornton hurried to his warehouse, locked himself in his private office, and gave himself over to the torture of replaying every moment. He remembered the sight of her that night, walking arm in arm with a strange man, and the savage, jealous distrust that had flared up in him then, and how he had pitied her that very afternoon, thinking her soft, tearful grief for her mother had left her vulnerable. How could the Margaret he had admired for her upright, uncompromising truthfulness have stooped to a bold, deliberate lie? He realized then what she must be hiding: that strange man was her disgraced mutineer brother, back in England illegally, and she would do anything to keep him from being taken. There was no reason for her to hide the truth, after all—Leonards had been a brute, drunk and disorderly, no public shame would have attached to her for testifying to the scuffle. But she would lie, she would perjure herself, to save that brother. And Thornton resolved he would not let her be dragged through that public shame. He knew the medical testimony was shaky: the surgeons had found Leonards was already dying of an advanced internal disease, that the fall might only have accelerated his death, or the hours he spent drinking and laying out in the cold after. He left the warehouse, went to the coroner, and used all his influence to cancel the inquest entirely. He wrote a short note to Watson: *There will be no inquest. Medical evidence not sufficient to justify it. Take no further steps. I have not seen the coroner; but I will take the responsibility.*

Watson was immensely relieved. All his witnesses were wobbly as jelly: the station porter who thought he saw a scuffle now swore it was only some larking, the shop assistant who thought he recognized Margaret now said he would not dare swear to it. The whole case was a mess, and he was glad to be rid of it.

That evening, he returned to the Hales’ cottage. Margaret had refused to go to bed for hours, fighting exhaustion, sure he would come back before word of Frederick’s safe arrival on the packet ship reached her. When the doorbell rang, she ran down the stairs faster than her weak, shaken frame seemed capable of, waving Dixon aside to face him alone.

He told her there would be no inquest, no further questions, no more investigation. He fumbled in his pocket for Thornton’s note, and Margaret could barely see the words swimming before her eyes. She made Watson read the note aloud to her, her chest tight with a strange, mingled flood of relief and dread. He apologized for the trouble he had caused her, tipped his hat, and left.

Margaret flew up the stairs, bolted her bedchamber door, and threw herself fully clothed on her bed. The first great rush of relief was that Frederick was safe, the strain of the last days falling off her shoulders at last. But then the full weight of the truth settled over her: Mr. Thornton knew she had lied. He had heard her denial, he had guessed her secret, and he had used all his power to stop the inquest, just to spare her the shame of repeating that lie in public. The proud, high-minded Margaret, who had always looked down on Thornton as a rough, unrefined tradesman, now stood exposed before him, a deliberate liar. She could not even feel grateful at first—her humiliation was too sharp, the realization that she valued his good opinion far more than she had ever dared admit cutting deeper than any fear she had felt for her brother. She tossed through the night, dreaming of monstrous, exaggerated versions of her sin, and woke to soft morning light seeping through her curtains.

Dixon opened the door cautiously, holding a letter in her hand. “A letter from Master Frederick, miss, just came post. It’ll do you a world of good.”

Margaret reached for it slowly. It was dated two days prior, written right before he stepped on board the packet ship bound for safety. He had met Henry Lennox, the lawyer, who was reviewing his case and thought he might have a chance of acquittal if he returned at a later date to stand trial. He had sent a cask of rare old sherry for his father, said he would send word as soon as he landed, and begged her to tell no living soul of his visit, not even the Shaws.

Margaret laid the letter down on the coverlet. The danger was past. But the long, quiet work of her expiation, the quiet weight of the knowledge that John Thornton had seen the worst of her, had only just begun.

At the time they had agreed the day before, Margaret Hale and her father set out to walk to Nicholas Higgins’ cottage. The stiff, unyielding feel of their new mourning clothes reminded both of them of the sharp, fresh loss of Mrs Hale; it was the first time in weeks they had deliberately gone out together, and they drew close without a word, bound by quiet, unspoken sympathy.

Nicholas was sat in his usual spot by the fire, but his pipe was nowhere in sight, his head propped on his hand, his elbow resting heavy on his knee. He did not rise when he saw them, though Margaret could read the faint, tired welcome in his eyes.
“Sit ye down, sit ye down. Fire’s welly out,” he said, giving the embers a vicious poke as if to draw attention away from his own red, puffy eyes. He looked unkempt, his dark beard unshaven for three days making his pale, worn face look even more gaunt, his frayed jacket in sore need of patching.
“We thought we would be sure to find you just after dinner time,” Margaret said softly.
“We have had our own sorrows, too, since last we saw you,” Mr Hale added.
“Ay, ay. Sorrows is far more plentiful than dinners just now. I reckon my dinner hour stretches all o’er the day — yo’re pretty much guaranteed to find me home,” Higgins said.
“Are you out of work?” Margaret asked.
He nodded shortly. After a beat of silence, he looked up for the first time. “I’m not wanting brass, mind. Dunnot you think that. Poor Bess left a little sum stashed under her pillow, ready to slip into my hand at the last, and Mary’s got steady work cutting fustian. But I’m out of work all the same.”
“We owe Mary some money for all her kind help with my wife,” Mr Hale began, before Margaret could dig her elbow into his arm to stop him.
“If she takes it, I’ll turn her out of doors this minute. I’ll bide inside these four walls, and she’ll bide outside. That’s all there is to it,” Higgins snapped.
“But we owe her so much gratitude for her care,” Mr Hale protested.
“I ne’er thanked your daughter there for all she did for my poor lass Bess. I could never find the right words. I’d have to start trying now, if yo’ keep making a fuss over the little work Mary did for your lady.”
“Is it because of the strike you cannot find work?” Margaret asked gently.
“The strike’s ended. O’er for this time. I’m out o’ work because I will not beg for it. And I will not beg, because good words are scarce in this world, and bad words are everywhere.” He was in a surly, half-defiant mood, half-pleased to speak in riddles, but Margaret could see he was eager to be pressed for an explanation.
“Good words, you mean?”
“Asking for work. That’s the best words a man can say. ‘Gi’ me work’ means ‘I’ll do it like a man.’ Those are good words.”
“And the bad words are bosses turning you away when you ask,” Margaret said.
“Ay. Bad words are them saying ‘Aha, my fine chap! You’ve stood true to your union men, and I’ll stand true to mine. You did all you could for the folks who needed help — that’s your way of being faithful to your kind. I’ll be faithful to mine. You’re a poor fool who didn’t know better than to stand by your mates. So go to hell. There’s no work for you here.’ Those are the bad words. I’m no fool — and if I was, folk ought to have taught me how to be wise by their rules. I might have learnt, if anyone had ever bothered to try.”
“Would it not be worth the attempt to ask your old master to take you back?” Mr Hale suggested. “It may be a slim chance, but it is a chance all the same.”
Higgins glanced up at him sharply, then let out a low, bitter laugh.
“Measter! No offence, but let me ask yo’ a few questions in turn.”
“Ask away, you are perfectly welcome,” Mr Hale smiled.
“I reckon you have some way to earn your bread. Folk don’t live in Milton for pleasure, if they can live anywhere else.”
“You are quite right. I have a small independent income, but I settled here to work as a private tutor.”
“Teaching folk. Well, they pay you for that work, don’t they?”
“Yes,” Mr Hale nodded. “I teach to be paid.”
“And the folks who pay you — do they tell you what you can or cannot do with the money they give you as fair payment for your pains? Do they say, ‘You might have a friend as dear as a brother who needs that money for a purpose you both think right, but you must promise never to give it to him. We do not think that use of your money is good, so if you spend it that way, we will stop giving you work entirely.’ Do they say that?”
“Of course not.”
“Would you stand for it if they did?”
“It would take the harshest possible pressure to make me even consider submitting to such dictation,” Mr Hale said.
“There is no pressure on all this earth that would make me,” Higgins declared. “Now you see it. Hampers Mill, that’s where I worked before — they make every man sign a pledge promising he will never give a penny to the union, never feed a starving locked-out turn-out. They can make men sign all the pledges they like,” he scoffed, “all it makes is liars and hypocrites. And that is a smaller sin, in my mind, than making men’s hearts so hard they will not do a kindness to a neighbour in need, or help a cause they know is just, even if it defies the bosses. I will never forswear myself for any work the king himself could offer me. I am a union man. I believe it is the only thing that will ever do working people any good. I have been a turn-out, I have known what it is to starve. If any union man asks me for a shilling, sixpence of it is his. Consequence is, I cannot get a single shilling’s work anywhere in Milton.”
“Is that anti-union pledge rule in force at all the mills?” Margaret asked.
“I cannot say. It’s new at our mill, and I reckon they will find soon enough they cannot keep enforcing it. But it’s law right now. Tyrants always end up making liars of everyone around them, sooner or later.”
There was a short silence. Margaret hesitated, reluctant to irritate a man who was already worn thin with grief and hopelessness, but the thought would not stay buried. She spoke softly, slowly, so he would not take offence.
“Do you remember poor John Boucher saying the union was a tyrant? That it was the worst tyrant of all? I agreed with him, when he said it.”
It was a long time before Higgins replied. He rested his head between his two hands, staring down into the fire, so she could not read his face.
“I will not deny that the union sometimes has to force a man to act for his own good. I will speak the truth. A man leads a dreary, lonely life if he is not in the union. But once he is in, his interests are cared for far better than he could ever look after them by himself. It is the only way working men can get their rights, all joining together as one. The more members we have, the more chance every single separate man has of getting justice. The government takes care of fools and madmen, and if a man is likely to hurt himself or his neighbour, it puts a check on him, whether he likes it or no. That is all we do in the union. We cannot throw people in prison — but we can make a man’s life so heavy to bear that he has to join, and be wise and helpful in spite of himself. Boucher was a fool from the start, and never more a fool than at the end.”
“Did he do you harm?” Margaret asked.
“Aye, that he did. We had public opinion on our side, right up until he and his lot started rioting and breaking windows. The strike was lost, then and there.”
“Then would it not have been far better to leave him alone, not force him to join the union against his will? He did you no good, and you drove him mad.”
“Margaret!” Mr Hale said, a low warning tone, for he saw the dark, angry cloud rise on Higgins’ face.
“I like her,” Higgins said, suddenly looking up. “She speaks plain what is in her mind. She does not understand the union, though. It is a great power — our only power. I once read a bit of poetry about a plough driving over a daisy, that made tears come to my eyes before I had any other reason to cry. But the man never stopped driving that plough, I will warrant, no matter how sorry he felt for the daisy. He had too much common sense for that. The union is that plough, turning over the land to get it ready for the harvest. Men like Boucher — it would be too much of a compliment to call him a daisy, he is more a weed sprawling over the ground — they just have to be torn up and moved out of the way. I am still so angry with him I may not be speaking fair about him. I could drive that plough over him myself right now, and take pleasure in it.”
“Why? What has he done now? Anything new?”
“Aye, of course. He is never out of mischief, that man. First he goes raging like a mad fool and kicks up that riot that lost us the strike. Then he runs off and hides, where he would still be if Thornton had followed through and prosecuted him like I hoped he would. But Thornton got what he wanted out of the strike, so he did not bother chasing Boucher down. So he slunk back home. He did not show his face in the street for a day or two, at least he had that much sense. And then — where do you think he went? Straight to Hamper’s mill. Damn him! He turned up there with his mealy-mouthed face that makes me sick to look at, begging for work, even though he knew full well about the new pledge rule, that you cannot give a penny to starving turn-outs. He would have starved to death months back if the union had not helped him in his pinch. And there he was, offering to promise anything, pledge anything — tell every last secret he knew about our union meetings — the good-for-nothing Judas! But I will say this for Hamper, and thank him for it on my dying day — he drove Boucher away, would not listen to a single word of it. Folk standing by say the traitor cried like a baby when he was turned out.”
“Oh! How shocking! How pitiful!” Margaret exclaimed. “Higgins, I do not recognise you today. Do you not see you made Boucher what he is, by forcing him into the union against his will, without his whole heart going with it? You made him what he is!”

Made him what he is? What was he?
A low, slow, measured tramp of feet drifted down the narrow street, growing louder, forcing itself into their awareness. Many voices were hushed, soft; many footsteps shuffled not forward fast, but circling, clustering around one single spot. There was one distinct, heavy rhythm, the laboured walk of men carrying a great weight.
They were all drawn to the cottage door by some unshakable, solemn impulse, not by idle curiosity, but as if a cold gale of sorrow had blown through the street and pulled them all outside.
Six men walked down the middle of the road, three of them uniformed policemen. They carried a door prized off its hinges across their shoulders, and a dead man lay stretched out on it. Water dripped steadily from the edges of the wood, leaving dark wet splotches on the cobblestones. The whole street poured out to see, and once they saw, they joined the procession, every person questioning the bearers, who repeated their tale over and over until they answered almost reluctantly.
“We found him in the brook in the field past the end of the lane.”
“The brook? There is not enough water there to drown a man!”
“He was a determined chap. Laid face down in the shallow water. He was sick enough of living, no matter what made him that way.”
Higgins crept up to Margaret’s side, his voice thin and reedy.
“It is not John Boucher? He had not the spunk for that. Sure, it is not John Boucher! Why are they all walking this way? There is a singing noise in my head, I cannot hear.”
They set the door down gently on the cobblestones, and everyone could see the poor drowned man. His glassy eyes, one half-open, stared straight up at the sky. His face was swollen and discoloured from being face down in the water, his skin stained dark by the dye waste the mills poured into that brook. The front of his head was bald, his thin long hair at the back soaked through, every separate lock dripping dark water.
Through all that disfigurement, Margaret recognised John Boucher. It felt sacrilegious to stand gaping at that twisted, agonised dead face, and on instinct she stepped forward, pulled her linen handkerchief from her pocket, and gently covered his countenance. The crowd followed her as she stepped back, and their gazes landed on Nicholas Higgins, rooted to the spot where he stood.
One of the men stepped up to him. “Higgins, you knew him. You have to go tell the wife. Do it gentle, man, but do it quick — we cannot leave him here out in the street much longer.”
“I cannot go,” Higgins said, shrinking back towards his door. “Dunnot ask me. I cannot face her.”
“You know her best,” the man said. “We did the hard part bringing him all this way. It is your turn to do your share.”
“I cannot do it,” Higgins repeated, his voice breaking. “I am welly felled just looking at him. We was not friends. And now he is dead.”
“Well, if you will not, you will not. But someone has to. Every minute that passes is a chance for her to hear the news rough and cruel, from some stranger, instead of someone who can break it to her soft.”
“Papa, you go,” Margaret said, turning to her father, but she saw him trembling from head to foot, his face as white as a sheet. He could not do it, not all at once. “I will go,” she said.
“Bless you, miss, that is a kind act. She has been a sickly, fragile thing these past months, and few folks around here know her well,” one of the bystanders said.
Margaret knocked at the Boucher’s closed door, but the noise of half a dozen small, unsupervised children drowned out her knock. She doubted she had been heard, and the longer she waited, the more her courage faltered, so she turned the latch and stepped inside, shutting the door behind her and bolting it unseen, to keep the crowd of gawkers out.
Mrs Boucher sat in her rocking chair on the far side of the cold, messy fireplace; the house looked as if no one had cleaned or tidied it for days. Margaret’s throat was so dry she could barely speak, and the children’s chatter drowned out her first attempt at greeting.
“How are you, Mrs Boucher? I am afraid you are very poorly,” she tried again.
“I have every right to be poorly,” the woman snapped peevishly. “I am left all alone to look after these children, and there is not a scrap of food in the house to keep them quiet. John should never have left me, not when I am this sick.”
“How long has he been gone?” Margaret asked.
“Four days. No one would give him work here, so he set off walking towards Greenfield to look for it. He ought to have been back by now, or sent word he found work. He ought to —”
“Oh, do not blame him,” Margaret cut her off. “He suffered very deeply, I am sure —”
“Will you hush your noise and let the lady speak!” she snapped at a tiny toddler at her feet, then turned back to Margaret apologetically. “He is always whinging for daddy and bread, and I have no bread to give him, and daddy is away and forgotten all of us, I think. He is his father’s darling,” she added, her mood softening all at once, as she dragged the little boy up onto her knee and kissed his hair.
Margaret laid her hand on the woman’s arm to get her attention. Their eyes met.
“Poor little fellow,” Margaret said slowly. “He was his father’s darling.”
“He is his father’s darling,” Mrs Boucher said, standing up so fast her chair scraped across the floor. They stared at each other in silence for a long moment. Then the woman’s voice rose in a low, wild growl, shaking Margaret’s arm as she demanded: “He is his father’s darling, I say. Poor folk can love their children as well as rich folks. Why will you not speak? Why do you stare at me with your great pitying eyes? Where is John?”
A second later the meaning of Margaret’s tearful look crashed over her. She cried out, “Oh my God,” and sank back into the rocking chair. Margaret picked up the toddler and set him in her arms.
“He loved him,” Margaret said.
“Ay,” the woman nodded, tears spilling down her face at last. “He loved all of us. We had someone to love us once. It feels like a long time ago now, but when he was home with us, he loved us. He loved this baby the best, I think. I was calling him a useless fool not five minutes ago. Are you sure he is dead?” She tried to stagger to her feet. “If he is only ill, not drowned for good, they can still bring him round. I am ailing too, I have been for months —”
“But he is dead. He is drowned.”
“Folks get brought back to life after drowning all the time! What am I doing sitting still? Where has all my strength gone? John — my husband —”
Margaret caught her as she fainted, easing her down into the rocking chair, holding her limp form against her shoulder

“A spade! a rake! a hoe!
A pickaxe or a bill!
A hook to reap, or a scythe to mow,
A flail or what ye will—
And here’s a ready hand
To ply the needful tool,
And skill’d enough, by lessons rough,
In Labour’s rugged school.”

When the Hales set out to call on the widow Boucher the next day, they found Nicholas Higgins’ cottage door locked fast. A garrulous, over-involved neighbour hurried to set their old assumption straight: this time, Higgins was truly out and about, not hiding away from well-meaning callers. He had popped in to see the grieving widow before dawn that morning, before vanishing off to whatever errand held him for the rest of the day.

Their visit to Mrs. Boucher was little more than disheartening. She nursed a bitter, half-justified conviction that the world had treated her poor husband cruelly, a grievance stoked by enough real hardship to be impossible to reason away. Worse still, every thought she held circled back entirely to her own suffering: she saw her children not as a comfort, but as a heavy burden, even in the midst of her dull, animal love for them. While Mr. Hale strove to lift her muddled, sullen thoughts above the fog of helpless self-pity, Margaret knelt to speak to the children one by one, and found them far truer, simpler mourners than their mother. Their father had been soft and gentle with them, each stammering out eager little stories of the treats he had brought home, the games he had played with them after long shifts at the mill.

“Is that thing upstairs really him?” the oldest boy whispered, eyes wide with dread. “It doesn’t look a bit like daddy. I’m feared of it, and I never was feared of daddy before.”

Margaret’s heart ached to the core. In her selfish, desperate hunger for sympathy, Mrs. Boucher had dragged all the children up to see their father’s disfigured, water-swollen corpse, weaving raw, harsh horror into the natural softness of their grief. She turned their attention gently to small, useful tasks: what they could do to ease their mother’s burden, what kind daddy would have wanted them to do for her now. The children, eager to act, soon fell to tidying the slovenly, soot-streaked rooms, but Mr. Hale’s lofty, abstract appeals fell entirely flat on the widow. Torpid, bitter, wrapped in her own hurt, she could not summon a single thought for her husband’s final hours of despair: she could only see his suicide as a slight against her, not a tragedy born of unbearable suffering. She raged against everyone she could name as the cause of her ruin: the mill masters, Mr. Thornton most of all (even though he had withdrawn the arrest warrant for Boucher after the riot), the Union that Higgins represented, her swarm of hungry, noisy children — all of them a vast army of personal enemies who had conspired to leave her a helpless widow.

Margaret left the cottage heavy-hearted, and could not rouse her father out of his quiet dejection as they walked home. “It is the town life, you see,” she said. “Their nerves are frayed thin by the rush and bustle, the unceasing speed of everything around them, not to mention the cramped, airless little houses they are pent up in, enough on their own to breed constant depression and worry. In the country, people spend so much more of their time outdoors, even the children, even in the depth of winter.”

“But stagnant country life breeds its own fatalism, too,” Mr. Hale sighed. “People there get stuck in ruts just as deep as any mill worker’s.”

“I do not deny that,” Margaret replied. “Every way of living carries its own trials. Town dwellers struggle to be patient, the way country folk struggle to adapt to sudden, unaccustomed change. Both find it hard to imagine a future far beyond the present: the townsman, because the rush of the world around him crowds every other thought out, and the countryman, because he is lulled into nothing more than quiet, animal contentment, no urge to strive or plan or hope for anything more.”

And so they moved on, turning their steps home, neither able to shake the weight of how little they could do for the broken Boucher household. Soon their conversation drifted to brighter memories: Frederick’s recent, joyfully secret visit, the snatched hours they had all shared together. But the thought of her brother’s name pulled Margaret up short. She had lied to protect him, lied straight to Mr. Thornton’s face, and he had seen through her, clear as day. She had always despised cowardice and petty deceit more than any other flaw, and now she was guilty of the very thing she had once scorned.

The shame of it burned hot in her cheeks. She could bear almost anyone knowing of her lie: her aunt Shaw, Edith, even Frederick, the very brother she had lied to save. But the thought of facing Mr. Thornton again, of looking him in the eye and knowing he knew she had stooped to falsehood, made her shrink back. She remembered, all at once, the early days of their acquaintance, when she had foolishly dismissed trade as a profession riddled with deceit, where merchants passed off shoddy goods as fine and pretended to wealth they did not have. She remembered his quiet, calm disdain as he told her that every dishonest act in commerce always unraveled in the long run, that even measured by the low standard of mere success, deceit was folly, not cleverness. Back then, she had been so proud of her own untested, unbroken honesty, she had lectured him that buying cheap and selling dear lacked the chivalric virtue that true justice required, and her father had corrected her, gently, to say it was Christian virtue they ought to strive for. No more pride for her. No more sneering at the trade he had built his life around. Now she could only feel humiliated, disgraced in his sight.

Yet she found herself longing for him to come, to get the meeting over, to know exactly how low she had fallen in his esteem. Her heart leapt at every single ring of the doorbell, only to sink back into dull, disappointed sadness when it was not him. Mr. Hale, who had been expecting Mr. Thornton to call for days to continue their unfinished conversation about mill work and labour, was equally confused by his absence. He had not yet resumed tutoring his pupils, a duty he had set aside during his wife’s final illness, and he was restless, eager to pick up their discussion where they had left off.

At last, a heavy step sounded on the stairs. It was not Mr. Thornton: it was Dixon, announcing that Nicholas Higgins was at the door, acting very strange, asking to see first Margaret, then Mr. Hale. They bade him come up, and Dixon huffed off, complaining that his muddy work boots would dirty the carpet. But when Higgins stepped into the hall, he paused, sat down on the lowest stair, and pulled his scuffed, mud-caked shoes off in silence, walking up the steps in his stocking feet.

“Your servant, sir,” he said, slicking his matted hair down when he stepped into the parlour, nodding at Margaret in apology for his bare feet. “Tramping all day, and Milton’s streets are none too clean.” He was unusually quiet, subdued, almost shy, as if he did not know how to say the thing he had come all this way to tell them. Mr. Hale pressed him to stay for tea, and Margaret slipped out to hurry the meal along, much to Dixon’s irritated surprise — the housekeeper had never gotten used to the Hales inviting working men up into their parlour, no matter how much Margaret softened her irritation with gentle, kind words.

Once the door was shut behind Margaret, Higgins leaned in close to Mr. Hale. “Master,” he said, “you’d never guess what I’ve been tramping after all day, not after all the angry speeches I gave you yesterday. I’ve been looking for work. I swore I’d bite my own tongue clean through before I spoke a hasty, angry word to any master who’d give me a job. I’m doing it for him, you see.” He jerked his thumb vaguely towards the direction of the Boucher cottage.

“For Boucher,” he clarified, when Mr. Hale looked confused. “Him that drowned himself. I never thought he’d have it in him to lie still and let the water creep over him. I know I led him astray, set him off the right path, so it’s my job to look after his wife and his little ones.”

Mr. Hale grabbed his hand and shook it hard, too moved to speak. Higgins shifted awkwardly, abashed. “Aye, well, there’s not a man among us that wouldn’t do the same. But not a single master will take me on. I told Hamper I’d be the hardest worker in his whole mill, that I wouldn’t even ask for union benefits — but I couldn’t sign that no-union pledge, not even for them. He turned me away, same as all the rest. I’m a useless, blacklisted sheep. The children’ll starve if I can’t find work. Parson, can you help me?”

Margaret stepped back into the room then, and he turned to her. “You’ve talked many a time about the South, the countryside down there. I don’t know how far off it is, but I thought if we could all move down there, where food’s cheap and wages are steady, where master and man get along friendly like, I could find work. I’m not even forty-five, I’ve got plenty of strength left in me.”

Margaret stepped forward, gentle but firm, to disabuse him of the dream. “Higgins, the hard farm labour you’d do down south would pay you at most ten shillings a week. Food costs nearly as much as it does here, and you’d be out in the rain and the frost and the blistering sun all day long. It would cripple you with rheumatism before the year was out. The life there is dull — stagnant, like standing water. The farm workers, they labour day after day in the steaming fields, too tired to speak to each other after their shifts, too worn down to argue or debate or laugh the way Milton’s men do. The peace that feels like rest to them would eat at you, fret at you, drive you half mad. You’d miss the company, the talk, too much. And besides, you could never afford to move the widow and all those children hundreds of miles south, even if you wanted to.”

Higgins sighed, the wild hope he had clung to all day draining out of him. “Aye, you’re right. North and South, both of them have their own miseries. Work’s steady down south but pays starvation wages. Here, we’ve got a flood of money one quarter, not a farthing the next. The whole world’s all mixed up, and no man alive knows how to set it right.”

He ate in silence, barely noticing the food on his plate, lost in gloom, until Margaret spoke up, the words sticking in her throat for a long while before she could say them. “Higgins — have you gone to Marlborough Mills to ask for work? To Mr. Thornton’s mill?”

“I went there,” he grunted. “The overlooker told me to go to hell. That’s all the welcome I got.”

“Would you go back tomorrow?” Margaret asked, her soft, grave eyes fixed on his, impossible to resist. “I know it’s a great deal to ask, but would you go there and try to speak directly to Mr. Thornton?”

Higgins heaved a great, heavy sigh. “Pride would chafe me raw to do it. I’d sooner knock him senseless than beg a favour from him. But you’re no common girl, Miss Hale. You’ve never treated any of us like we’re beneath you. I’ll do it. I’ll stand outside his lodge gate at six in the morning and wait till he comes out. But don’t you hold out much hope. That man’s stubborn as flint. I’ll never get a job out of him. But I’ll try, for your sake. No woman’s ever got me to back down from anything before. Not even my wife, not even Bessy.”

Mr. Hale offered to write him a note of introduction, but Higgins refused. “I don’t want no one meddling between master and man. It’s like meddling between a husband and wife — takes more wisdom than most folks have to do any good. I’ll stand on my own two feet.” He said his good nights, and Margaret pointed him to the kitchen fire, where she had set his wet shoes out to dry. He paused at the door, looked at her steadily for a long moment, then brushed a calloused, lean hand across his eyes and tramped off down the path.

“Pride is running strong in that man,” Mr. Hale said, a little amused at Higgins’ refusal of his help.

“There’s granite in all these northern folk, isn’t there?” Margaret replied, softly. “Even poor Higgins, with all his stubbornness, he’s a fine, strong man at the core.”

Her father pinched her ear, smiling. “You’re finally doing Mr. Thornton justice at last, my dear.”

Margaret’s throat tightened, and she could not speak. All she could think, in the quiet hush of the parlour, was how she wished he would come. She wished she was brave enough to face him, to tell him openly that she knew she had been wicked and cowardly, that she deserved all his disapprobation. It felt cruel to lose him as a friend, just when she had finally come to value how good and true he was. She thought of how gentle he had been with her mother, in her final days, and she ached to see him, just to know exactly how far she had fallen in his eyes.

> “Then proudly, proudly up she rose, Tho’ the tear was in her e’e, Whate’er ye say, think what ye may, Ye’s get na word frae me!”

Mr. Thornton did not merely believe Margaret had spoken a deliberate falsehood, though she imagined that single misdeed was the sum of his low opinion of her. That lie, to his mind, was inextricably tangled with the memory of the tender, earnest glance he had caught passing between her and a strange man, the air of familiar, unguarded confidence he had witnessed between them, if not of outright endearment. The image gnawed at him perpetually, burn itself behind his eyes no matter where he walked, no matter what work he turned his hand to. Worse still—he ground his teeth at the recollection—was the setting: that dusky twilight hour, that lane far removed from the bustle of Milton’s central streets, so quiet, so rarely trod by any passerby.

At first his nobler self had argued the whole thing might be innocent, accidental, entirely justifiable. But he could not long cling to that soft thought. Once he allowed that Margaret had every right to love and be loved—had she not cast his own offer of love away from him with sharp, unyielding clarity?—it was all too easy to imagine she had been beguiled into extending her walk, lingering later than she had ever intended, for the sake of that other man. But that lie! It betrayed a fatal, guilty consciousness of something to hide, something uncharacteristic of the frank, truthful girl he had thought he knew. Even so, he never could fully convince himself she was utterly unworthy of his esteem. That was the source of his misery: he loved her so passionately that even with all these perceived flaws, she outshone every other woman he had ever met. And yet he was convinced she was so wrapped up in her affection for this unknown man, that slight, elegant, handsome stranger, that she had violated her own nature to lie for him. The very falsehood that stained her reputation in his eyes was proof of how blindly she loved another—while he, rough, stern, broad-shouldered, could never hope to compete. He lashed himself into a fever of fierce, consuming jealousy, thinking of that glance, that soft, unresisting way she had leaned toward the other man. He would have laid down his life at her feet to earn such tender looks, such fond, lingering attention. He mocked himself for once being proud that she had stepped between him and the fury of the rioting mill-workers: now he understood how soft and bewitching she could be, when she was with a man she truly loved. He repeated her words to himself, sharp as shards of glass: *“There was not a man in all that crowd for whom she would not have done as much, far more readily than for him.”* He had shared that small act of protection with a whole mob of strangers. But this hidden lover? He had all her secret glances, all her quiet words, the pressure of her hand, all her lies and concealments, all to himself.

Thornton had never in his life been so irritable as he was these days. Every question asked of him earned a sharp, abrupt reply, closer to a bark than civil speech. He was ashamed of this loss of the self-control he had always prided himself on, and forced himself to master it: his manner softened into a quiet, deliberate calm, but his decisions grew harsher, sterner than ever. At home he was uncommonly silent, pacing back and forth across the parlour floor evening after evening, a habit that would have roused his mother’s ire against any other person in the house, and which made her far less inclined to extend even him any forbearance.

“Can you stop that everlasting walk, walk, walk, and sit down for a moment?” she demanded at last. “I have something to say to you.”

He sat instantly, hard against the wall on a wooden chair.

“It’s about Betsy. She says she must leave our service. Her lover’s death has so broken her spirits, she can’t bring herself to give her full heart to her cooking work.”

“Very well. We can find another cook easily enough.”

“That’s exactly like a man. It’s not just the cooking. She knows every quirk of this house, every way we like things done. And besides, she told me something about your friend Miss Hale.”

“Miss Hale is no friend of mine. Her father is my acquaintance, that is all.”

“I’m glad to hear you say that. If she had been your friend, what Betsy says would have annoyed you deeply.”

“Tell me,” he said, his tone the same forced, steady quiet he had maintained for days.

“Betsy says that the night her lover—I forget his name, she only ever calls him ‘he’—”

“Leonards.”

“The night Leonards was last seen on duty at the station, Miss Hale was there, walking back and forth with a young man whom Bessy believes struck or pushed him to his death.”

“Leonards was not killed by any blow or push.”

“How do you know?”

“I asked the Infirmary surgeon directly. He told me Leonards had long suffered from an internal disease caused by his habitual heavy drinking, and that the fact he took a turn for the worse while he was drunk proved the fatal attack was caused by excess alcohol, not any fall.”

“The fall! What fall?”

“The fall that came from the blow or push Betsy spoke of.”

“Then there *was* a blow or push?”

“I believe so.”

“Who did it?”

“There was no inquest, the doctor’s testimony made it unnecessary. I cannot say for certain.”

“But Miss Hale was there?”

He made no answer.

“And with a young man?”

Still silence. At last he spoke. “Mother, there was no formal judicial inquiry into the matter. Nothing has been proven.”

“Betsy says Woolmer—her acquaintance who works at the grocer’s shop out in Crampton—swears he saw Miss Hale at the station at that exact hour, walking back and forth with that young man.”

“I do not see what that has to do with us. Miss Hale may do as she pleases.”

“I am glad you take that view,” Mrs. Thornton said eagerly. “It means nothing to us, of course, not after what passed between you! But I— I made a promise to her mother, that I would not let her daughter go astray without giving her good advice and remonstrance. I shall certainly make my opinion of such conduct clear to her.”

“I do not see any harm in what she did that evening,” Thornton said, standing abruptly and moving to stand beside the chimney-piece, his face turned away from the room.

“You would never have approved of Fanny being seen out after dark, in such a lonely place, wandering about with an unchaperoned young man. I will not even speak of the shocking taste that would choose the very hours when her own mother lay unburied for such a promenade. Would you have liked a grocer’s assistant gossiping about Fanny for doing such a thing?”

“First, I myself was a draper’s assistant not many years ago. The fact that a grocer’s assistant notices an act does not change its moral character for me. Second, there is a world of difference between Miss Hale and Fanny. I can imagine Miss Hale has weighty, urgent reasons that make any seeming impropriety in her conduct entirely justified. I never knew Fanny to have a weighty reason for anything. Other people must guard her. I believe Miss Hale is perfectly capable of guarding herself.”

“That is a fine character to give your own sister!” Mrs. Thornton scoffed. “Really, John, you would think Miss Hale had done enough to make you see clearly. She drew you on to make an offer, with a bold, false display of regard for you, all to play you off against this very same young man, I have no doubt. Her whole conduct is plain as day to me now. You believe he is her lover, I suppose? You agree to that much?”

Thornton turned to face his mother. His face was gray, his expression grim. “Yes, mother. I do believe he is her lover.” The words hung in the air for a heartbeat, and then he twisted in his chair as if in the grip of physical agony, pressing his face into one hand. Before she could speak, he spun around sharply again. “Mother. He is her lover, whoever he is. But she may need help, gentle womanly counsel. There may be difficulties, temptations, I know nothing of. I fear there are. I do not want to know the details. But as you have always been a good, and a tender mother to me, go to her. Gain her confidence. Tell her what is best to be done. I know there is something wrong, some terrible dread that must be torturing her.”

“For God’s sake, John!” Mrs. Thornton was truly shocked now. “What do you mean? What do you know?”

He would not answer.

“John! I shall think the worst if you do not speak. You have no right to cast these half-insinuations against her character.”

“Not against her, mother. I could never speak against her.”

“Well, you have no right to say what you just did, unless you explain more. These half-spoken accusations are the very thing that ruin a woman’s reputation.”

“Her character! Mother, you do not dare—” He stepped forward, his eyes blazing, then forced himself back into a stance of deliberate, calm dignity. “I will only say this, and it is the simple, unvarnished truth I know you will believe: I have good reason to think Miss Hale is in some strait and difficulty connected to an attachment that, from all I know of her character, is perfectly innocent and right. I will not tell you what that reason is. But never let me hear anyone say a word against her implying anything worse than that she now needs the counsel of some kind, gentle woman. You promised her dying mother you would be that woman!”

“No,” Mrs. Thornton corrected him, drawing herself up. “I am happy to say I never promised her kindness or gentleness. I knew at the time I could not offer that sort of soft treatment to a girl of Miss Hale’s character and disposition. I promised her counsel, the same advice I would give to my own daughter. I shall speak to her exactly as I would speak to Fanny if she had been gallivanting around with a young man at dusk. I will not be swayed one way or another by these ‘strong reasons’ you refuse to share. Then I shall have kept my promise, and done my duty.”

“She will never bear it,” Thornton said passionately.

“She will bear it, if I speak in her dead mother’s name.”

“Very well!” He broke away toward the door. “Do not tell me any more about it. I cannot bear to think of it. It is better you speak to her than no one do at all. Oh, that look of love!” he muttered between his teeth as he slammed his private study door behind him. “And that cursed lie, that showed some terrible shame in the background, that kept her from the bright, honest life I thought she lived perpetually! Oh, Margaret, Margaret! Mother, how you have tortured me! Oh, Margaret, could you not have loved me? I am only uncouth and hard, but I would never have led you to tell a single falsehood for my sake.”

The more Mrs. Thornton turned her son’s pleas for leniency over in her mind, the more bitter her resentment of Margaret grew. She took a savage, grim pleasure in the thought of “speaking her mind” to her, all under the guise of fulfilling a sacred duty. She delighted at the thought of proving herself immune to the “glamour” she knew Margaret was capable of casting over soft-headed people. She snorted in scorn thinking of her victim’s famed beauty: her jet black hair, her clear smooth skin, her luminous eyes would not save her a single word of the just, stern reproach she spent half the night rehearsing in her head.

“Is Miss Hale within?” She already knew Margaret was home, she had seen her standing at the front window, and she had her foot over the threshold of the small cottage hall before the servant Martha could finish half her answer.

Margaret was sitting alone, writing to Edith, detailing the last quiet days of her mother’s life. The soft, sad work had left her eyes damp with unbidden tears when Mrs. Thornton was announced.

Her greeting was so gentle, so gracious and ladylike that Mrs. Thornton was momentarily daunted. The sharp speech she had rehearsed so easily in her parlour would not form on her tongue. Margaret’s low, rich voice was softer than usual, her manner warmer than ever, for she felt deeply grateful that Mrs. Thornton had thought to call on her in her bereavement. She exerted herself to find pleasant topics for conversation, praised the cook Martha that Mrs. Thornton had recommended to them, mentioned she had written to Edith to send over the little Greek air that she had discussed with Fanny once. Mrs. Thornton was completely discomfited. Her sharp, Damascus steel-edged rebuke felt entirely out of place, useless, scattered on rose leaves. She stayed silent, trying to summon her resolve to perform her “duty”, until a foolish, unlikely suspicion stung her into action: all this sweetness was a act, put on to propitiate Mr. Thornton. The other secret attachment must have fallen through, and Margaret meant to win back her rejected suitor.

Poor Margaret! There was just enough truth to the suspicion: she knew Mrs. Thornton was the mother of the man whose good opinion she still ached to retain, unconsciously adding extra warmth to her usual graciousness. Mrs. Thornton stood up to leave, but it was obvious she still had something to say. She cleared her throat, and began.

“Miss Hale, I have a duty to perform. I promised your poor mother that, as far as my judgment went, I would not let you act wrongly, or even inadvertently, without remonstrating with you, at the very least offering you advice whether you chose to take it or not.”

Margaret stood before her, blushing like a caught criminal, her eyes wide as she stared at Mrs. Thornton. She was certain the older woman had come to speak to her about the lie she had told Mr. Thornton about not being at the station—she thought he had sent her to warn her that her lie might be exposed in the coroner’s inquest! Her heart sank a little that he had not chosen to come himself, to upbraid her, to take her penitence, and restore her to his good opinion, but she was so humbled that she would have borne any rebuke on that meekly, patiently.

Mrs. Thornton went on.

“At first, when one of my servants told me you had been seen walking about with a gentleman all the way out at the Outwood station, at such a late hour of the evening, I could barely believe it. But my son, I am sorry to say, confirmed the story. It was indiscreet, to put it mildly. Many a young woman has lost her good name for far less—”

Margaret’s eyes flashed fire. This was a new insult, far worse than the one she had braced herself for. If Mrs. Thornton had spoken to her about her lie, she would have owned it, and bowed her head in humiliation. But to interfere in her conduct, to question her character—Mrs. Thornton, almost a stranger to her! It was intolerable. She would not answer. Not one single word.

Mrs. Thornton caught the battle-spark in Margaret’s eyes, and her own combativeness rose to match it.

“For your mother’s sake, I thought it right to warn you against these improprieties. They will degrade you in the eyes of the world in time, even if they do not lead you into outright harm.”

“For my mother’s sake,” Margaret said, her voice thick with tears, “I will bear much. But I cannot bear everything. She never would have wished me to be exposed to insult, I am sure.”

“Insult, Miss Hale!”

“Yes, madam,” Margaret said, more steadily now. “It is insult. What do you know of me that should make you suspect—Oh!” She broke off, covering her face with her hands. “I know now. Mr. Thornton told you—”

“No, Miss Hale,” Mrs. Thornton’s innate truthfulness made her stop the confession Margaret was on the brink of making, even as her curiosity burned to hear it. “Stop. Mr. Thornton told me nothing. You do not know my son. You are not worthy to know him. Listen, young lady, so that you may understand what sort of man you rejected. This Milton manufacturer, his great tender heart scorned as it was scorned, said to me only last night: ‘Go to her. I have good reason to know she is in some strait arising out of some attachment, and she needs womanly counsel.’ Those were his exact words. Beyond admitting you were at the Outwood station with a gentleman on the evening of the twenty-sixth, he said nothing against you. Not a single word. If he knows anything that would make you sob so, he keeps it entirely to himself.”

Margaret’s face was still hidden in her hands, the fingers wet with tears. Mrs. Thornton was a little mollified.

“Come, Miss Hale. I will allow there may be circumstances, that if explained, would clear all seeming impropriety away.”

Still no answer. Margaret was struggling over what to say. She wanted to remain in Mrs. Thornton’s good graces, but she could not, dared not, explain. Mrs. Thornton grew impatient.

“I shall be sorry to cut off our acquaintance entirely, but for Fanny’s sake—as I told my son, if Fanny had done such a thing we would have considered it a great disgrace, and Fanny is easily led astray—”

“I can give you no explanation,” Margaret said in a low, trembling voice. “I have done wrong, but not in the way you think, not in the way you accuse me of. I believe Mr. Thornton judges me more mercifully than you—” She had to fight to keep from choking on her tears. “But I believe, madam, you mean to do rightly.”

“Thank you,” Mrs. Thornton said, drawing herself up stiffly. “I was not aware my intentions were being doubted. This will be the last time I ever interfere. I did not want to make that promise to your mother, in the first place. Even before I knew of my son’s attachment to you, I did not think you were worthy of him. But when you compromised yourself during the riot, exposing yourself to the gossip of servants and workpeople, I thought it

“Nay, I have done; you get no more of me;
And I am glad, yea glad with all my heart,
That thus so clearly I myself am free.”

Margaret slipped away to her bedchamber the moment she left Mrs. Thornton, and began pacing back and forth in the old, restless way that only surfaced when her emotions were frayed raw. Halfway across the rug, she remembered how thin the walls of the Milton house were—every footfall, every sharp breath, carried clear from one room to the next. She sank onto a chair, forcing herself to sit perfectly still until she heard the front door click shut behind Mrs. Thornton, who had set out on her own errands. Then she reviewed every word of their recent conversation, line by line, dragging her memory through each bitter, accusing phrase until she could make no more of it. At last she stood, her voice soft and heavy with melancholy.
“At least none of her accusations stick. They fall right off me, for I am innocent of every selfish motive she lays at my door. But it cuts deep, all the same, to think any woman could believe such cruel things of me so easily. And the wrong I *did* do—she never names it, for she does not even know. He never told her. I might have guessed he never would.”
She lifted her chin, a quiet spark of pride lighting in her chest at the thought of Thornton’s quiet, delicate discretion over the secret of Frederick’s visit. Then a new thought struck her, and she clutched her hands tight together. “He must think Frederick is some lover of mine.” She blushed hotly at the word. “I see it all now. It is not just that he knows I lied to the constable. He believes there is another man who cares for me, that I— Oh, dear. Oh, dear. What do I care? Why do I care so much what he thinks, past the simple loss of his good opinion over that single falsehood? I cannot say. I am so utterly miserable. This last year has worn me right through. I have skipped right from childhood to old age. No youth, no soft, quiet womanhood for me. All the bright hopes a girl holds for her future are closed off— I will never marry. I carry all the weight of old age’s cares and sorrows, and all its ready tears besides. I am so tired of being called on to be strong, every hour of every day. I could bear up for Papa, that is a natural, pious duty. I could even summon the anger to stand against Mrs. Thornton’s impertinent, unfair suspicions. But it is unbearable, to know how completely he must misunderstand me. I do not know what has made me so morbid today. I only know I cannot fight it any longer. No,” she added, springing to her feet sharp, “no, I will not give way. I will not sit here picking apart my own feelings, not now. It is useless. Maybe if I live to be a very old woman, I will sit by my fire, stare into the embers, and see all the life that might have been.”

All the while she spoke, she was hastily pulling on her outdoor bonnet and shawl, pausing only to swipe impatiently at the tears that kept spilling down her cheeks, angry that they would not stop despite all her brave resolve. “I dare say thousands of women make the same stupid, sad mistake I did, and only realize it far too late. And how proud, how insolent I was to him that day he came to the house to speak to me! I had no idea then. It crept up on me little by little, and I cannot even name the day it began. But I will not give way now. It will be so hard to meet him normally, with this wretched secret weighing between us, but I will be calm, I will be quiet, I will say very little. Though for all I know, I may never see him again. He has been keeping out of our way for weeks, after all. That would be worse than anything. But no wonder he avoids me, when he believes all he does about me.”

She stepped out and walked as fast as her legs could carry her towards the open country, trying to drown out every intrusive thought by the sheer speed of her motion. When she returned home to her doorstep at mid-afternoon, her father came walking up the lane beside her.
“Good girl!” he said, smiling. “I knew you would go check on Mrs. Boucher. I was just about to head over there myself, if I had time before dinner.”
Margaret flushed bright. “No, Papa, I did not. I never thought of her, I am ashamed to say. But I will go right after dinner, while you take your nap.”

True to her word, she set off for the Boucher cottage once he had drifted off to sleep. Mrs. Boucher was very ill—dangerously so, not just a passing malaise. A kind, sensible neighbor had taken charge of the whole household the moment she’d heard the news, sending most of the children to stay with nearby families, and Mary Higgins had collected the three youngest at dinner time. Nicholas Higgins had gone for the doctor hours before, but he had not yet returned. Mrs. Boucher was slipping away, and there was nothing for anyone to do but wait. Margaret decided to walk the short distance to Higgins’ cottage, to see if he had had any luck with his request for work from Mr. Thornton.

She found Higgins kneeling on the floor by the dresser, spinning a copper penny over and over for the three little Boucher children, who clustered around him unafraid, shrieking with glee every time the coin spun a full ten seconds without falling flat. Higgins was smiling too, and Margaret thought that soft, happy look on his face was a good sign. When the penny finally toppled, the littlest boy, lile Johnnie, burst into tears.
“Come here,” Margaret said, lifting him off the dresser and tucking him against her chest. She held her watch to his ear to distract him, then asked Nicholas if he had spoken to Mr. Thornton that morning.
The smile dropped right off his face. “Aye, I saw him, and heard more than enough out of him.”
“Did he refuse you, then?” Margaret asked, her heart sinking.
“Of course he did. I knew he would, all along. No point expecting mercy from masters like that. You’re new to Milton, a stranger here, so you don’t know their ways, but I’ve seen it all my life.”
“I am so sorry I ever asked you to go. Was he angry? He did not speak to you as cruelly as Mr. Hamper did, did he?”
“He weren’t over-civil, that’s for certain,” Higgins grumbled, spinning the penny again, this time more for his own distraction than the children’s. “Don’t you fret, I’m no worse off than I was before. I’ll set out tramping the roads tomorrow. I gave him as good as I got, too. Told him I never thought well enough of him to come begging work on my own, but that some woman who knew no better told me to try, and I owed her for the suggestion.”
“Did you tell him it was me?”
“I don’t rightly know if I said your name. Told him some woman who didn’t know how things work here had sent me, said there might be a soft spot in his heart for folks in trouble.”
“And he said what?” Margaret pressed.
“Said I was to tell you to mind your own business. That’s the longest spin we’ve had yet, lads!” Higgins called out to the children. “And those were the mildest words he threw at me. But never mind. We’re no worse off than we were. I’ll break stones on the side of the road before I let these little ones starve.”

Margaret set the squirming Johnnie back down on the dresser. “I am so sorry I pushed you to go to him. I am deeply disappointed in Mr. Thornton.”
There was a soft scrape of a footstep behind her. Both Margaret and Higgins turned at the same moment, and there stood Mr. Thornton, his face tight with displeased surprise. Acting on nothing but instinct, Margaret slipped past him out of the door without a word, bowing low to hide the sudden deathly pale that washed over her face. He bowed equally low in return, and shut the door firmly behind her. The clang of the latch sounded to Margaret like the final note of her utter mortification as she hurried back to the Boucher cottage. He was annoyed to see her there, she thought. Of course he was.

Thornton watched her go, frowning. He did have that soft spot in his heart Higgins had spoken of, but he guarded it like something sacred, hated to think anyone might guess it existed. But if he was fiercely private about his tenderness, he was even more determined that every person around him recognize his commitment to justice. He realized suddenly he had been unfair, to turn Higgins away with nothing but scorn after the man had waited five whole patient hours to speak to him that morning. The saucy words Higgins had thrown at him later did not bother Thornton in the least—he rather admired the man for speaking his mind. He knew his own temper had been frayed that morning, that they had been even in their sharpness. But those five hours of waiting stuck in his head. He gave up two full hours of his packed work day to go from one mill hand to the next, collecting every bit of information he could about Higgins’ character, his life, his history. By the end of his rounds, he was thoroughly convinced every word Higgins had told him was true. The quiet generosity of the man, taking in a dead drunkard’s children no one else would care for, the five hours he had waited without complaint, melted the last of Thornton’s cold reserve.
