He had come all the way to the cottage to offer Higgins work, and he was far more annoyed to find Margaret there than he was at her overheard words calling him cruel—for he realized she was the woman who had urged Higgins to come to him, and he hated the thought that anyone might think he was doing this small kindness because of her, not because it was the right thing to do.
“So that was the lady you called a silly woman who gave you bad advice?” he said to Higgins, half indignant. “You might have told me who she was.”
“And then you’d have spoken far more civil of her than you did just now, wouldn’t you? You’ve a mother of your own, who I’d wager would have clipped your tongue short before you went spouting nonsense about all women being the root of every bit of trouble.”
“Of course you told all that to Miss Hale?”
“Course I did. Told her she’d best not meddle in anything that concerns you ever again.”
“Whose children are those? Are they yours?” Thornton asked, awkwardly shifting the subject away from the tense topic of Margaret. He already knew perfectly well they were Boucher’s kids, from what his workers had told him.
“They’re not mine, and they are mine, all at once.”
“They’re the children you told me about this morning?”
“When you said my story might be true or might not, but it sounded far too unlikely to be believed,” Higgins snapped, turning around, his fierce temper barely held back. “Master, I haven’t forgotten that.”
Thornton went quiet for a long moment. “Neither have I. I remember exactly what I said. I spoke to you about those children in a way I had no right to. I did not believe you. I knew I could never have taken care of another man’s children if that man had treated me the way I heard Boucher treated you. But I know now you spoke nothing but truth. I beg your pardon.”
Higgins did not turn around, did not reply right away. When he did speak, his gruff tone was softened, however. “You’ve no business prying into what went on between Boucher and me. He’s dead now, and I’m sorry for it. That’s all there is to say.”
“That’s all there is to say, indeed. Will you come take work with me? That is what I came here to ask you.”
Higgins’ obstinacy wavered for a second, then stiffened again. He would not speak. Thornton would not ask twice. Then Higgins’ eyes fell on the three little children, clustered quiet by the dresser.
“You called me impudent, a liar, a mischief-maker,” he said, “and you might well have added that I was too fond of drink now and then. And I called you a tyrant, an old bulldog, a hard cruel master. That’s where we stand. But for the children’s sake, master—do you really think we can ever get on together?”
“Well,” Thornton said, half laughing, “it was not my idea that we should work side by side. But you are right about one thing: neither of us can possibly think worse of the other than we already do. That’s a very comfortable place to start.”
“That’s true enough,” Higgins said, nodding slowly. “I’ve been thinking ever since I saw you this morning, what a mercy it was you refused me, because I never met a man I could abide less. But that was a hasty judgment. Work is work for folks like me. So, master— I’ll come. And what’s more, thank you. That’s a great deal more than I usually say to any employer.” He turned fully around to face Thornton, holding out his hand.
“And that’s a great deal more than I usually get,” Thornton said, gripping his hand hard enough to make his knuckles white. “Now, mind you, you show up sharp on time tomorrow. I will not have laggards at my mill. We keep our fines strictly, and the first time I catch you stirring up trouble among the other men, you are gone. You know where you stand.”
“You talked of my wisdom this morning. Am I to bring that with me, or would you rather have me leave my wits at home?”
“Bring your wits if you use them to meddle in my business. Leave them at home if you intend to stick to your own work.”
“I’ll need a whole lot of wits to figure out where my work ends and yours begins.”
“Your work has not even started yet, and mine waits for no man. Good afternoon.”

Just before Thornton reached the Boucher cottage, Margaret stepped out of the door. She did not see him at first, and he followed her several paces down the lane, admiring her light, easy step, her tall, graceful figure. Then a rush of bitter jealousy poisoned that soft, simple pleasure. He wanted to overtake her, speak to her, see how she would react now that she must know he believed that unnamed man from the station was her suitor. He also, a little ashamed of the urge, wanted her to know he had repented of his unfair judgment that morning, that he had done the right thing by Higgins. He hurried to catch up, and Margaret started when he spoke.
“Allow me to say, Miss Hale, that you were rather premature when you expressed your disappointment in me just now. I have taken Higgins on.”
“I am very glad to hear it,” Margaret said, her voice cool and distant.
“He told me he repeated the words I sent him back with this morning, about women not meddling in matters that do not concern them,” Thornton said, hesitating.
Margaret spoke up quickly. “You had every right to express that opinion, which I have no doubt was perfectly correct. But Higgins did not tell you the exact truth of the situation,” she added, growing a little more eager—until the word *truth* hit her, and she clammed up, suddenly overwhelmed with discomfort, remembering her own lie to the constable.
Thornton stared at her, confused for a moment, then the memory of that lie, the memory of the constable’s testimony, flooded back. “The exact truth. Very few people speak the exact truth. I have given up hoping for it. Miss Hale—have you no explanation to give me? You must know exactly what I have been forced to think.”
Margaret was silent, turning over in her head whether any explanation could ever be given without betraying Frederick’s safety.
“No,” Thornton said, “I will not press you. I might only be putting temptation in your way. But I give you my word, your secret is perfectly safe with me. Though I must warn you, you run very grave risks by being so indiscreet. I speak only as a friend to your father. Any other hope I may have once held for you is entirely gone. It is all over. I am completely disinterested.”
“I am well aware of that,” Margaret said, forcing her voice to sound indifferent, careless. “I know exactly what I must look like to you, but that secret belongs to another person. I cannot explain it without doing him great harm.”
“I have not the slightest wish to pry into that gentleman’s secrets,” Thornton said, his temper rising. “My only interest in you now is that of a friend. You may not believe me, but it is true. All that foolish passion I once threatened you with is entirely gone, long past. You do believe me, Miss Hale?”
“Yes,” Margaret said, quiet and sad.
“Then I see no reason for us to continue walking here together. I thought perhaps you might have something to say to me, but it is clear we are nothing to one another. If you are quite convinced that any feeling I once had for you is entirely over, then I wish you good afternoon.” He turned and walked away very quickly, leaving Margaret standing there on the lane, staring after him.

What can he mean? Margaret thought. Why does he speak as if I am still hanging on to the idea that he cares for me? He does not, he cannot. His mother must have filled his head with all those cruel stories about me. But I will not care for him. I am strong enough to master this wild, miserable, foolish feeling, that almost tempted me to betray my own dear Frederick just to win back his good opinion—the good opinion of a man who goes out of his way to tell me I mean nothing to him. Come now, poor little heart. Be brave, be cheerful. We will be far more to each other, the two of us, now that the whole world has turned its back on us.

Her father was startled by the almost wild merriment Margaret showed when she got home that evening. She talked incessantly, forcing her natural good humor to a pitch she had not reached for months, and if there was a faint bitter edge to her words, if her stories of their old life in Harley Street were a little sharper than usual, Mr. Hale did not have the heart to check her. He was just glad to see her seeming to shake off her cares. Halfway through the evening, she was called downstairs to speak to Mary Higgins. When she came back up

“I have no wrong, where I can claim no right, Naught ta’en me fro, where I have nothing had, Yet of my woe I cannot so be quite; Namely, since that another may be glad With that, that thus in sorrow makes me sad.” — WYATT

Margaret had never expected to find much joy for herself in Mr. Bell’s upcoming visit; she had only looked forward to it for her father’s sake. But the moment her sharp, warm-hearted godfather stepped across their threshold, the two of them settled into the easiest, most natural friendship as if they had known each other their whole lives. He teased her that there was no merit to her being exactly the kind of girl he adored: her affection was her birthright, an inheritance of character that let her walk straight into the hold of his regard without so much as asking permission. She laughed in turn, and told him he was far more fresh and young at heart than half the lads decades his junior, hidden though that warmth was under his stuffy Oxford Fellow’s cap and gown.

“Fresh and young in kindness, you mean,” she corrected him. “I’m afraid I must admit that your opinions are the oldest, mustiest things I have run across in months.”

“Hey, Hale! Listen to this daughter of yours! Her time in Milton has quite corrupted her. She’s a democrat, a red republican, a member of the Peace Society, a socialist—”
“Papa, it’s all only because I’m standing up for the progress of commerce,” Margaret protested. “Mr. Bell would have us stuck forever bartering wild beast skins for acorns, no progress at all.”
“Nonsense, missy. I’d dig the soil and grow potatoes. I’d shave those wild hides down and spin their wool into broadcloth. Don’t exaggerate. But I am tired of all this bustle. Everyone rushing over everyone else, all scrambling to get rich as fast as they can.”
“Not everyone is fortunate enough to sit comfortably in a set of college rooms and watch their fortunes grow without lifting a finger,” Mr. Hale put in gently. “I’ve no doubt many a man in Milton would give a great deal to have property increase as yours does, without the slightest exertion on his part.”
“I don’t believe they would. It’s the scramble and the struggle they love. As for sitting still, learning from the past, or shaping the future through slow, faithful work done with a thoughtful, prophetic spirit—pooh! I don’t think there’s a single man in Milton who knows how to sit still. And that is a very great art.”
“I suspect the people of Milton think Oxford men do not know how to move at all. It would do both sides a great deal of good to mix a little more often.”
“It might do the Miltoners good, certainly. Many things would do them good that would be perfectly disagreeable to everyone else.”
“Are you not a Milton man yourself, though?” Margaret asked. “I should think you would be proud of your hometown.”
“I confess I do not see anything there to be proud of. But if you will come to Oxford with me, Margaret, I will show you a place worth glorying in.”
“Well,” Mr. Hale said, “Mr. Thornton is coming to take tea with us tonight, and he is as proud of Milton as you are of Oxford. You two shall have to try and make each other a little more liberal-minded.”
“I do not want to be more liberal-minded, thank you,” Mr. Bell huffed.

“Is Mr. Thornton coming to tea, papa?” Margaret asked, her voice soft and low.
“Either for tea or soon after. He said he could not tell for certain, and told us not to wait for him.”

Mr. Thornton had made up his mind not to pester his mother for details of the conversation she had planned to have with Margaret, over that impropriety she was so convinced Margaret had committed. He knew full well if he heard his mother’s account of any such meeting, every word would be twisted and coloured through her disapproval, leaving him chafing and angry, even as he recognised how biased her perspective was. He shrank from even hearing Margaret’s name mentioned in his own home. He blamed her, he was jealous of her, he told himself he had renounced her entirely — and still he loved her, sorely, in spite of every effort to stop. He dreamed of her: dancing towards him, arms outstretched, light and giddy, a version of her so unlike the Margaret he knew that it made him loathe her even as the vision drew him in. The false, untrue figure of her was stamped so deep into his imagination that when he woke, he could barely untangle the real, noble Margaret from that evil, giddy doppelgänger, and his dislike for the fake one seemed to taint and disfigure the memory of the real.

But he was too proud to admit his own weakness by avoiding her. He would not seek out her company, and he would not run from it, just to prove he had full control over himself. That afternoon, he dragged out every piece of business on his desk, forcing every movement to unnaturally slow, deliberate pace, so that it was past eight o’clock before he finally reached the Hales’ door. He was then kept trapped in the study with Mr. Bell, sorting through old business accounts, and Bell sat on by the fire rambling on wearily long after all the work was done, both of them perfectly free to head upstairs. Thornton said nothing to suggest moving, chafing in silence all the while, convinced Bell was the most tedious, prosy companion he had ever met. Bell returned the favour in secret, deciding Thornton was as brusque and short-tempered a fellow as he had ever encountered, and had lost half the intelligence and good manners he used to have. At last, a faint sound of movement from the room above reminded them they ought to join the others.

They found Margaret seated at the table, an open letter spread out before her, deep in excited discussion of its contents with her father. The gentlemen’s entrance made her fold the letter away at once, but Thornton’s sharp, eager ears caught the last few words Mr. Hale said to Bell. “A letter from Henry Lennox. It makes Margaret very hopeful.”

Bell nodded. Margaret’s cheeks flooded bright as a rose the second she met Thornton’s gaze. He was halfway out of his chair, ready to walk straight out the door and never set foot in this house again.

“We were thinking,” Mr. Hale said, “that you and Mr. Thornton must have taken Margaret’s advice this morning, and set about trying to convert each other, you were so long shut away in the study.”
“And no doubt you thought there would be nothing left of us but our respective opinions, like the famous Kilkenny cat that ate its own tail. Tell me, which of our opinions did you think would prove the most stubborn and hard to kill?”

Thornton had no idea what they were talking about, and disdained to ask. Mr. Hale politely filled him in. “Mr. Thornton, we were teasing Mr. Bell earlier today for that old Oxford mediaeval bigotry of his against his own native Milton. We — Margaret, I believe — suggested it would do him a great deal of good to spend more time in the company of Milton’s manufacturers.”
“Pardon me,” Margaret said, smiling. “I said I thought it would do the Milton manufacturers good to spend more time with Oxford men. Is that not right?”
“No,” Bell countered. “You argued that both sides would benefit from a little more familiarity. But never mind that. Now, Mr. Thornton, I wonder when you Milton men intend to actually live. All your lives seem to be spent gathering all the materials for life, and never getting round to living it at all.”
“By ‘live’ I suppose you mean seeking enjoyment?” Thornton said.
“Yes, enjoyment — not empty, idle pleasure, of course, I am sure we both agree mere trivial amusement is very poor fare for a full life.”
“I would rather you defined exactly what kind of enjoyment you are referring to.”
“Very well — leisure, the quiet enjoyment of the power and influence money brings. All of you in Milton are striving for wealth. What do you want it for?”

Thornton went silent for a long moment. Then he said, “I truly do not know. But money is not what I strive for.”
“What, then?”
“That is a home question. I do not think I am prepared to lay myself open to that kind of catechism this evening.”
“No,” Mr. Hale intervened quickly. “Let us not make our questions personal. Neither of you is a perfect representative of your respective worlds — you are both far too individual for that.”
“I am not sure if that is a compliment or not,” Bell laughed. “I would love to be the representative of Oxford, with all its beauty and learning, and its proud, ancient history. What do you say, Margaret? Ought I to be flattered?”
“I do not know Oxford well enough to say,” she replied. “But there is a very great difference between being the representative of a city, and being the representative man of all its inhabitants.”
“Very true, Miss Margaret. Now I remember, you were against me this morning, all Miltonian and manufacturing in your loyalties.”

Margaret caught the quick, surprised glance Thornton shot her, and grew annoyed at the wrong, unkind meaning he might read into Bell’s offhand remark. Bell went on, oblivious. “Ah, I wish I could show you our High Street, our Radcliffe Square. I am leaving out the colleges just as I give Mr. Thornton leave to omit all his factories when he tries to sell Milton’s charms. I have every right to make fun of my birthplace, remember — I was born and raised there.”

Thornton was far more annoyed by all this talk than he ought to have been. He was in no mood for light joking. On any other day, he would have enjoyed Bell’s half-testy condemnation of a town whose busy, brutal life ran so counter to the slow, leisurely Oxford rhythms he loved — but tonight, he was raw and stinging, and found himself trying to defend a town that had never been meant as a serious target for mockery. “I do not hold Milton up as a perfect model of a town.”
“Not in the matter of architecture, then?” Bell asked slyly.
“No! We have all been far too busy to waste time on mere outward appearances.”
“Do not call them mere outward appearances,” Mr. Hale said gently. “They impress upon every one of us, from the day we are born until the day we die, every single hour of our lives.”
“Wait a few years,” Thornton said. “Remember we are a different race from the ancient Greeks, for whom beauty was everything, and to whom you might speak of a life of leisure and serene enjoyment taken in through the senses. I do not despise them, any more than I would try to copy them. We Teutons, in this part of Darkshire, do not see life as something to be enjoyed — we see it as a time for action and exertion. Our glory, our beauty, comes from the strength of our work, that lets us overcome material resistance, and far greater obstacles still. We are Teutonic in another way here, too: we hate having laws made for us from far away in London. We wish people would let us right our own wrongs, instead of constantly meddling with their half-baked legislation. We stand for self-government, and oppose centralisation.”
“In short, you want to bring back the old Saxon Heptarchy. Well, at any rate, I take back what I said this morning about you Milton folk not respecting the past. You are full-on worshippers of Thor.”
“If we do not revere the past the way you do at Oxford, it is because we want lessons that apply directly to the present,” Thornton went on, heated. “It is a fine thing when study of the past leads to a vision of a better future. But for men groping their way through entirely new circumstances, it would be far finer if old wisdom could tell us exactly how to act in the matters that touch us most closely, full of hard difficulties to meet and conquer — not just push aside for a while — because the way we face them defines our entire future. But no! People can prattle on about Utopia far easier than they can tell you what your duty is tomorrow. And when that duty has already been done by other people, they are the first to cry out ‘Fie, for shame!’”

“And all this time I have no idea what you are talking about,” Bell laughed. “Would you Milton men ever condescend to send your latest troubles up to Oxford to solve? You have not even tried us yet.”
Thornton laughed outright at that. “I suppose I was thinking of all the trouble we have had lately, the strikes we have been through — troublesome, costly things, as I am finding out to my own detriment. Even the most recent strike, the one I am still stinging from, was a respectable one.”
“A respectable strike?” Bell echoed, amused. “That sounds like you are far gone in that worship of Thor I mentioned.”

Margaret could sense, more than see, how deeply Thornton was galled by every light joke made about the subject that was so serious, so personal to him. She forced herself to speak, to turn the conversation away before he grew any more upset. “Edith says the printed calicoes they sell in Corfu are both better quality and cheaper than anything you can get in London.”
“Does she?” her father said. “I think that must be one of Edith’s famous exaggerations. Are you sure that is true, Margaret?”
“I am only sure that is what she says, papa.”
“Then I am sure it is fact,” Bell put in. “I have so much faith in your truthfulness, Margaret, that it extends to cover even your cousin’s character. I will not believe any cousin of yours is capable of exaggeration.”

“Is Miss Hale so remarkable for her truth?” Thornton said, sharply and bitterly. The words were no sooner out of his mouth than he could have bitten his tongue out. What was he doing? Why was he stabbing her with the secret shame she had carried for her brother, right in front of her father? He was possessed by ill humour tonight: cross at being delayed so long before coming to see her, infuriated by the mention of Henry Lennox’s name, which he was sure belonged to a rival more fortunate than him. He could not meet the gentle, light-hearted Bell on his own ground of easy banter, and now he had lashed out at Margaret.

She did not get up and leave the room, as she had done in the old days when his brusque temper had annoyed her. She sat perfectly still, for a moment staring at him with the bewildered hurt of a child struck for no reason, her eyes wide and soft with mournful, reproachful sadness, before they dropped. She bent her head over her sewing, and did not speak again. Thornton could not help but stare at her, and saw a faint sigh tremble over her whole frame, as if she had been caught in an unexpected cold chill. He gave short, sharp answers to every question, restless and cross, unable to tell jest from earnest, desperate only for one look, one single word from her to let him throw himself at her feet in penitence. But she never looked up, never spoke. Her long, taper fingers moved steadily in and out of her stitching, as fast and steady as if sewing was the whole purpose of her life. She cannot care for me, he thought. If she did, the fierceness of my regret would have forced her to raise her eyes, if only for an instant, to see how sorry I am. He almost wanted to do something deliberately rude before he left, to earn the right to tell her the remorse eating him alive. It was a mercy that the long, cold walk home under the open sky sobered him, leaving him with a grave resolution: he would see as little of her as possible from now on. The very sight of her face, the very sound of her voice, had far too much power to unbalance him. He knew what this love was: a sharp pang, a fierce, burning experience he was struggling through in the flames. But he would fight his way through the furnace, out to the calm serenity of middle age, richer, more human, for having felt such a great passion.

As soon as he had abruptly left the house, Margaret rose from her seat and slowly folded up her sewing. The heavy seams felt far too weighty for her languid, tired arms. The soft round lines of her face had gone long and sharp, and she looked as if she had been labouring through a day of unendurable fatigue. As the three of them prepared for bed, Bell muttered a little condemnation of Thornton. “I never saw a man so spoiled by success. He cannot bear a single word of joke. Everything seems to touch some raw, sore spot in his overinflated dignity. Once he was as simple and noble as open daylight. You could never offend him, because he had no vanity at all.”
“He is not vain now,” Margaret said, turning slowly from the table, her voice clear and quiet. “Tonight he was not himself at all. Something must have upset him terribly before he even arrived here.”

Bell shot her one of his sharp glances over the top of his spectacles. She met it calmly, without flinching. After she had left the room, he leaned over to Hale and said, “Hale! Did it ever strike you that Thornton and your daughter have what the French call a tendresse for each other?”
“Never!” Mr. Hale said, first startled, then flustered by the suggestion. “No, I am sure you are wrong. I am almost certain you are mistaken. If there is any feeling at all, it is all on Mr. Thornton’s side. Poor fellow! I hope and pray he is not thinking of marrying her, because I am sure she would never accept him.”
“Well, I am a bachelor, and have steered clear of all love affairs my whole life,” Bell said, yawning. “So perhaps my opinion is worthless. Otherwise I would swear there are very pretty symptoms of feeling on her side too.”
“Then you are definitely wrong,” Hale insisted. “He may care for her, even though she has been almost rude to him at times. But Margaret! Margaret would never think of him, I am sure. The idea has never once entered her head.”
“Entering her heart would do, of course,” Bell said. “I merely threw out the suggestion. No doubt I was wrong. And whether I am or not, I am very sleepy. I have disturbed your night’s rest with my silly, untimely fancies, so I will go off to bed with an easy mind.”

Mr. Hale resolved not to let the nonsensical idea bother him — but he lay awake for a long time, unable to stop turning it over in his head.

Mr. Bell left

*I see my way as birds their trackless way— I shall arrive! what time, what circuit first, I ask not: but unless God sends his hail Or blinding fire-balls, sleet, or stifling snow, In some time—his good time—I shall arrive; He guides me and the bird. In His good time! — BROWNING’S PARACELSUS*

Winter dragged thin, the days stretching longer with February’s pale sun, but bringing none of the bright, stirring hope that usually comes with the first lengthening rays of the year. Mrs. Thornton had long ceased calling at the Hales’ little Milton home. Mr. Thornton still stopped by occasionally, but his visits were confined strictly to the study, shared only with Mr. Hale. The rareness of their meetings seemed only to deepen the old clergyman’s high regard for his friend; no lingering irritation or umbrage hung over Thornton’s absences, Margaret gathered. The aftermath of the strike had tangled his business affairs far more than he’d anticipated the winter prior, demanding almost his full attention. He spoke of her sometimes, too, she learned—casually, calmly, no awkward avoidance, no overeager dwelling, only quiet, steady goodwill.

Margaret had no strength left to lift her father’s heavy mood. Months of gnawing anxiety, even the open storms of the strike, had worn the elasticity out of her mind, leaving her life bleak and wind-scoured. She poured herself into teaching the two youngest Boucher children, striving hard for goodness even when her heart felt numb to any warmth or joy, going through every duty punctually but painfully, still far from any light cheer. The only thing she did without effort, out of an unspoken, unconscious piety, was tending to her father: no passing mood of his went without her sympathy, no small, shy wish of his unanticipated and granted, the gentle meekness of her obedience wrapping all his small, hesitant hopes in softness.

March brought news of Frederick’s wedding. The couple’s letters arrived hand in hand: Dolores wrote in a charming lilt of mixed Spanish and English, while Frederick’s own prose was dotted with Spanish turns of phrase that showed how fully he’d settled into his new life across the sea. Henry Lennox had written some weeks prior to warn them there was no hope of clearing Frederick’s name at court martial, the critical missing witnesses vanished without a trace. Frederick had sent back a fiery letter swearing he would never return to England, would renounce the country entirely rather than live there even if he was granted a full pardon. Margaret had cried sorely at the angry passion of it at first, before she realized it was only the raw sting of his disappointment speaking. His next letter was bright with plans for the future, no shadow left of his old grievance against the land of his birth, and Margaret found she needed that same patience to carry her own days forward. Dolores’ sweet, girlish letters began to win over both Margaret and her father, every little erased line showing the young Spaniard had fussed over every word to make a good impression on the English family her new husband had spoken of so fondly. Along with the letters came a stunning black lace mantilla, Dolores’ own gift to the “paragon of beauty, sense and virtue” Frederick had described his sister to be. The marriage had lifted Frederick’s worldly prospects higher than the Hales had ever dared hope: he had been made junior partner at Barbour & Co., one of the largest trading houses in all of Spain. Margaret smiled, then sighed, remembering her old fiery tirades against trade—her gallant, knightly brother turned a merchant now! She shook off the silly, unfair instinct to lump a proud Spanish trading partner in with the Milton mill owners she’d once scorned. Trade or no trade, Frederick was happy, and Dolores must be every bit as charming as her letters suggested.

That spring, Mr. Hale began to suffer from occasional spells of breathlessness that distressed him severely for as long as they lasted. Margaret was not overly alarmed, as they passed completely in the intervals, but she was determined to help him shake the habit entirely, so she pressed him hard to accept Mr. Bell’s invitation to spend April with him at Oxford. Bell had invited Margaret too, even writing a separate, playful special note commanding her to come, but she found the thought of staying home alone far more restful: no responsibilities, no one depending on her for comfort, no one to tend through illness. She had not known such unburdened quiet for more than two years.

When her father’s carriage pulled away for the railway station, the sheer lightness of being without anyone to care for nearly stunned her. She could be idle, silent, even miserable if she wished—all the private griefs and mistakes she had stuffed away in a dark corner of her mind for months now had room to be brought out, examined, and laid gently to rest. She sat almost motionless for hours in the drawing room, turning over every bitter memory without flinching, only crying aloud once when the sting of the lie she had told to protect Frederick pricked her again. She saw now how foolish, even useless that temptation had been; the lie had gained nothing, and faith in the power of truth would have been wiser by far.

Nervous and raw, her hand fell open a book of her father’s resting on the table, and old French words of gentle consolation leapt straight out at her, written for her exact state of self-reproach: I would not tear my heart to pieces in harsh self-loathing, it read. My poor heart, we have fallen into the pit we swore so hard to escape. Rise up, leave it behind forever, beg for God’s mercy, and trust that it will hold us up so we may be stronger now, and set our feet back on the path of humility. Courage, little heart. Margaret breathed the words to herself. That was what she had missed.

Rising, she determined to find some small task to pull her out of her own dark thoughts, and called Martha in as the maid passed the drawing room on her way upstairs. After gentle prodding, Martha’s stiff, mechanical servant’s mask cracked, and she told Margaret the full story of how Mrs. Thornton had saved her and her sister when they were orphaned and destitute: how Thornton’s mother had sought them out, nursed Martha through a dangerous contagious fever in her own home, sent her to the seaside to recover, never fearing for her own health. “Miss Fanny was the only one afraid,” Martha added. “She ran off to visit the family of the gentleman she’s going to marry.”

Margaret was stunned to hear Fanny Thornton was engaged, to a wealthy older mill owner named Watson whose property lay far beyond Hayleigh, a very advantageous match. She fell quiet, catching herself about to wonder how Mr. Thornton felt about his sister’s marriage—an old silly habit she had slipped into lately, of measuring every bit of news related to him by how it might land, and she shook it off quickly.

The next day she taught the Boucher children their lessons, took a long walk, and ended by calling on Mary Higgins. To her surprise, Nicholas was already home from work, the lengthening spring twilight fooling her into thinking it was far earlier in the evening than it was. He too seemed to have stepped onto that path of humility Margaret had been thinking of: quieter, less brash, no trace of his old insolence. He teased her about her father travelling off to Oxford, and assured her he never shirked a minute of his work at Thornton’s mill. “Your Thornton’s good enough to fight with, but too good a man to cheat,” he told her. He called his youngest little boy out, who proceeded to recite one loud, rumbustious Methodist hymn after another, his cadence as dramatic as a Member of Parliament making a speech. Nicholas, who had once scoffed at all religion out of hand, looked on with quiet, unmistakeable pleasure.

Margaret got home past her usual tea time, but no one was waiting impatiently for her, no one’s plans were ruined. She sat sorting through a large packet of old letters that evening, picking out which ones to keep and which to destroy, and came across the stack of Henry Lennox’s letters all related to Frederick’s case. She reread them carefully at first to confirm exactly how slim the last chance of clearing her brother’s name had been, then noticed the stiffness and cold cleverness of the wording: no trace of the old personal regard he had once held for her, every line strictly professional, no warmth to be found anywhere. She set them carefully aside to keep, valuable legal documents, and fell into a reverie. Two days of quiet had already refreshed her, the morbid fog falling away from her eyes. The future no longer looked impossibly bleak; if she could only win back Mr. Thornton’s friendship, if he would only keep calling on her father as he once had, even if she never spoke a word to him again, she thought her life could stretch out calm and even ahead of her. She sighed as she rose for bed, but a small, lingering ache of sorrow still lay heavy at her heart.

That same April evening, miles away in Oxford, Mr. Hale was thinking of Margaret just as persistently. He had wandered all his old familiar haunts at his small old college, far more warmly welcomed by his old friends than he had dared hope. Though some might have been shocked by his choice to leave the church, the sight of the quiet, sensitive man they had known in their youth washed all their abstract disapproval away, and they took him into their hearts with the tender protective kindness one might show a gentle, beloved sister. Bell teased him gently for being worn thin by Milton’s sharp air, but Mr. Hale shook his head. “Even if I had foreseen every bit of suffering my choice would bring my family,” he told Bell earnestly, “I would never have denied my conscience. I would never take that step back.” His mind turned then to Margaret, and he fretted aloud over what would become of her if he died, imagining her going to live with Edith and the Lennoxes in London. Bell swore on the spot he would take care of her. “Your daughter is my goddaughter, and she has nearly all my heart,” he said. “I will be her preux chevalier, sixty and gouty though I am. All I have is hers, whenever she needs it.”

That night, Mr. Hale laid his head down on his pillow, and his still, tender heart ceased beating in his sleep. There was no struggle, no pain, and the servant who came to wake him the next morning found his face calm, white, and beautiful under the unmistakeable seal of death.

Bell was stunned past speech for a long while, before he roused himself, snapped at his man for daring to suggest a coroner’s inquest, and ordered a carpet bag packed in five minutes flat. He would take the very next train to Milton, to break the terrible news to Margaret. He stumbled onto the train, collapsed into his seat, and had scarcely closed his eyes to try and wrap his head around the fact that his old friend was gone, when he spotted a man sitting across from him, half hidden behind a spread copy of *The Times*. It was Mr. Thornton. Bell clambered over to his side, shook his hand hard enough to squeeze tears out from under his grizzled eyelashes, and told him the news, that Hale was dead, that he was on his way to Milton to tell his daughter.

For more than a quarter of an hour, Thornton could not find his voice at all. At last he managed to speak, thick and unsteady. “And her?”

“Margaret? I am going to her,” Bell said. “Poor old Hale could speak of no one else last night. I told him I would be her protector, and I will. I would take a live dragon into my home as a chaperon if it meant Margaret could live with me as my own daughter. But there are the Lennoxes, you see. Smart London folk, who will no doubt think they have the first claim on her. And that young barrister Henry Lennox, who has been chasing Margaret for five years, held back only by lack of fortune. Now he has made his way in the world, you may be sure he will press his suit.”

Thornton leaned forward, too earnestly curious to notice the seeming impertinence of his question. “How?”

“When some beloved voice that was to you
Both sound and sweetness, faileth suddenly,
And silence, against which you dare not cry,
Aches round you like a strong disease and new,—
What hope? what help? what music will undo
That silence to your sense?”

The blow of her father’s sudden death had been so sharp Margaret sank into a total, numb prostration, no wracking sobs, no word of grief to offer relief. She lay motionless on the drawing-room sofa, eyes half-shut, never speaking save when directly addressed, and then only in the faintest of whispers.

Mr. Bell was at his wits’ end. He dared not leave her side, nor could he persuade her to travel back to Oxford with him as he had originally planned on his journey north to Milton; her exhaustion was so complete even the shortest carriage ride would have been agony, before he even considered the grim, unspoken funeral arrangements awaiting them. He pulled a chair close to the fire, sat staring at the flames, and turned over every possible plan in his head. Margaret lay still beside him, so quiet he could barely catch the soft catch of her breath. He would not even step downstairs for the dinner Dixon had prepared for him, the housemaid red-eyed and fussing over him in her sorrowful hospitality, so he sent a plate up to the drawing-room. Normally a man careful and particular over every subtle shade of flavour in his food, the devilled chicken now tasted like dry sawdust to him. He minced a portion of the fowl, seasoned it heavily with pepper and salt, and bade Dixon try to feed it to Margaret—but the faint, languid shake of her head made it plain any attempt to swallow food now would only choke her, not nourish her.

Mr. Bell heaved a great, heavy sigh, hauled his stout, travel-stiff limbs out of his chair, and followed Dixon out into the hall. “I cannot leave her. I shall write back to Oxford tonight, tell them to make all the necessary arrangements for the funeral there, they can proceed without me. Is there no woman who can come be with her? Not Mrs. Lennox? I’ll write at once and insist she come—this girl needs a friend of her own sex, if only to coax her into a good, healing cry.”

Dixon was already crying enough for two people, but she wiped her eyes steady enough to tell him Mrs. Lennox was months deep in her confinement, and could not possibly bear the strain of a journey. “Then we must send for Mrs. Shaw, her aunt, who came back to England not long ago, has she not?”

“Indeed she has, sir,” Dixon replied, “but I doubt she’ll wish to leave Mrs. Lennox at such an interesting time.” She had no great desire for a stranger to enter her household and steal any part of the tender, sovereign care she lavished on Margaret.

“Interesting time be—” Mr. Bell cut himself off with a loud cough before he swore outright. “She was perfectly happy gallivanting off in Venice and Naples, those Popish places, for Mrs. Hale’s last interesting time, if I recollect correctly, in Corfu? And what does that silly little prosperous woman’s lying-in signify, compared to that poor, helpless, homeless, friendless child lying as still on that sofa as if it were an altar tomb and she the stone statue atop it? I tell you, Mrs. Shaw will come. Get a room and all she could possibly want ready for her by tomorrow night. I shall see to it she comes.”

He wrote that very night a letter so sharp and unapologetically commanding that Mrs. Shaw later declared it was exactly the sort of note her late husband the General used to send when he was gearing up for a severe gout attack, and she would treasure it all her days. If he had softened his request, framed it as a favour she might refuse, she might well have delayed even with all her sincere sympathy for Margaret; it was that unceremonious, sharp order that spurred her to shake off her natural inertia and let her maid pack all her trunks immediately.

Edith, swaddled in cap and shawl and teary, stood at the top of the stairs as Captain Lennox led her mother down to the waiting carriage. “Don’t forget, mamma—Margaret must come back to live with us. Sholto will go to Oxford on Wednesday, and you must send word by Mr. Bell telling him when we are to expect the two of you. And if you need Sholto at all, he can travel straight on from Oxford down to Milton. Don’t forget, mamma—you are to bring Margaret home with you.” She wandered back into the drawing-room, where Henry Lennox sat cutting the pages of a new review. Without lifting his head he said, “If you would rather not have Sholto gone so long while you are unwell, Edith, I can go down to Milton in his place and offer whatever help I can.”

“Oh thank you, dear,” Edith replied, wiping her eyes, “but I am sure good old Mr. Bell is doing all that can be done already, and a resident Oxford fellow has not the slightest bit of savoir-faire for these sorts of troubles, more help might only get in the way. Won’t it be lovely to have Margaret with us again? You two were such great allies, years ago.”

“Were we?” he asked, indifferent, feigning deep interest in a passage he had just reached in the review.

“Well, perhaps not—I have forgotten, I was so wrapped up in Sholto then. But does it not fall perfectly that poor Uncle Hale died just now, when we have only just come home and settled into the old Harley Street house, perfectly ready to receive her? What a relief it will be for her to escape that grim Milton smoke! I shall buy new bright chintz for her bedroom, make it all fresh and cheerful to raise her spirits a little.”

Mrs. Shaw travelled north in that same warm, fussy spirit of kindness, dreading the moment she first met Margaret, but spending far more time planning how quickly she could whisk her niece away from “that horrid smoky place” back to all the gentle comforts of London. “Oh dear!” she said to her maid as their carriage rumbled past mile after mile of factory chimneys belching black smoke. “Look at those! My poor sister Hale! I do not think I could ever have rested easy in Naples if I had known what a dreadful place she was living in! I would have come fetched her and Margaret away years ago.” Privately, she had always thought her brother-in-law a little soft-headed, but never half so foolish as she thought him now, to have traded the sunlit green lanes of Helstone for this grimy, industrial wilderness.

Margaret had not moved from her state of pale, motionless, tearless quiet. They told her her aunt Shaw was coming, but she showed no surprise, no pleasure, not a flicker of dislike. Mr. Bell, whose appetite had returned now that he had a plan in motion, happily ate all the sweetbreads stewed with oysters Dixon had prepared for Margaret after she refused to take a single bite, just as stubbornly quiet as she had been the day before.

Margaret was the first to hear the hackney carriage pull up outside the house. Her eyelids fluttered, her pale lips flushed pink and trembled. Mr. Bell went downstairs to meet Mrs. Shaw, and by the time they climbed back up to the drawing room Margaret was on her feet, swaying a little, trying to steady her dizzy, grief-heavy body. The moment she saw her aunt, she stumbled forward into her open arms, and at last the long pent-up tears burst out, hot and healing, on her aunt’s shoulder. All the quiet years of shared family tenderness, the familiar, inexplicable likeness in tone, gesture, and the set of her aunt’s face that reminded her so sharply of her dead mother, melted her numbed heart completely, and the silence that had ached around her for days broke at last in sobs.

Mr. Bell slipped quietly out of the room, down into the late Mr. Hale’s little study, ordered a fire lit, and pulled books off the shelves to distract himself. But every volume brought back some memory of his dead old friend, a change of scene if not a change of thought, and he was glad when he heard Mr. Thornton’s voice at the front door asking after Miss Hale. Dixon was dismissing him with no little curt sharpness; with the arrival of Mrs. Shaw’s lady’s maid, she had begun to dwell fondly on visions of the old Beresford family grandeur, the noble “station” Margaret had been ousted from and was now, please God, returning to. She had been gossiping with the maid, pumping her for every story of the grand Harley Street household to tell to the other maids, and felt far too grand now to be deferential to any common Milton mill-owner. Even though she had always stood in some awe of Mr. Thornton, she was as sharp as she dared be telling him no one could see anyone in the house that night—until Mr. Bell opened the study door and called out, “Thornton! Is that you? Come in a minute, I want to speak to you.”

Dixon had no choice but to retreat back to the kitchen, where she restored her own ruffled dignity by telling the other maids a wild, grand story of Sir John Beresford driving a coach and six when he was high sheriff of the county.

“I do not know what I wanted to say to you, after all,” Mr. Bell said, as Thornton stepped inside. “It’s dismal enough sitting in a room where every single object speaks of a dead friend. But Margaret and her aunt need the drawing room all to themselves. I cannot very well stay here overnight, I was about to book a room at the Clarendon inn.”

“Nonsense, you will not go to the Clarendon. We have five or six spare bedrooms at our house, all well aired, you can trust my mother to see to that.”

“Then I shall just pop back upstairs to say good night to that poor wan girl, pay my respects to her aunt, and come straight off with you.”

Mr. Bell stayed upstairs longer than Thornton expected. Thornton had barely spared the time to run over from the mill to check on Margaret, all his work still piling up waiting for him. As they walked the dark Milton streets back to the Thornton house, Bell said, “Mrs. Shaw is desperate to get back to London, for her daughter’s sake, and wants to drag Margaret off with her at once. But the girl is no more fit to travel than I am to fly. And then she says, very fairly, that there are people here in Milton she must see, people she has to say goodbye to, and her aunt keeps badgering her about old friends, asking if she’s forgotten all the people who loved her. And she burst out crying just now, saying she would be glad to leave the place where she had suffered so much. I have to go back to Oxford tomorrow, and I do not know which side to throw my vote in on.”

He paused, waiting for an answer, but Thornton said nothing at all. His own mind was echoing those words over and over: *Where she had suffered so much*. All those eighteen months in Milton—so unspeakably precious to him, every bit of its bitterness sweeter than any other part of his life—would be remembered by her as nothing but pain. The death of his own mother, the loss of any person dear to him, could never poison the memory of those days when he could walk two pleasant miles every evening to her door, every step bringing him closer to her sweet presence, every moment with her revealing some new soft grace or sharp, bright, delightful quirk of her character. No matter what other misfortunes had befallen him, he would never call those days suffering. They had been a time of pure, unrivalled luxury, even with all their stings and humiliations, compared to the empty, grey poverty of a future with no hope, no chance of seeing her again every day.

Back at the Thornton house, Fanny was in the dining room, fluttering with quiet little glee as her maid held up one glossy silk after another to the candlelight, testing the colours for her upcoming wedding gowns. Her mother tried hard to sympathise, but could not care less about fabrics or frills, and heartily wished Fanny had taken her brother’s offer to have all her wedding clothes made by a top London dressmaker, instead of fussing endlessly over every little detail herself. Mr. Thornton was only too happy to show his approval of the man who had married Fanny, a sensible fellow who appreciated her little airs and graces, so he gave her unlimited funds to buy every bit of finery her heart desired, finery that rivalled even her love for her fiancé in her estimation.

When her brother and Mr. Bell walked in, Fanny blushed and simpered, fussed over hiding the silk scraps strewn across the table, though Mr. Bell barely noticed her at all. If he spared her a single thought, it was only to compare her, in all her trivial, bright happiness, to the pale, quiet grief he had left back at the Hales’ house, Margaret sitting bent-headed with folded hands in a room so still you could almost hear the faint rustle of the dead man’s spirit lingering close beside his grieving daughter.

Mrs. Thornton gave Mr. Bell her formal, stately, hospitable welcome; she was never more gracious than when she received her son’s friends in her own home, and the more unexpected the guest, the more it proved her housekeeping was always perfectly prepared for any emergency. “How is Miss Hale?” she asked.

“Broke almost completely by this last blow.”

“It is a very great mercy for her that she has such a devoted friend as you.”

“I wish I were her only friend, madam,” Bell replied gruffly. “Doubtless it sounds brutal, but here I have been displaced from my post as her comforter by a fine lady aunt, and there are cousins and relations in London all laying claim to her as if she were a lapdog left to them in a will. The poor girl is too weak and miserable to have a will of her own right now.”

“She must indeed be very weak,” Mrs. Thornton said, with a quiet, pointed implication her son understood perfectly. “But where have all these relations been this past year and more, when Miss Hale was so nearly friendless, bearing so much sorrow and anxiety?” She did not care enough for the answer to wait for it, and left the room to arrange the guest’s supper.

“They have been living abroad,” Bell said to Thornton. “The aunt did bring her up, she and Edith have been like sisters, so their claim is real. The thing that vexes me is that I wanted to take her as my own ward, and I am jealous of these people who do not seem to half value the precious privilege of loving her. It would be different if Frederick came back to claim her, of course.”

“Frederick!” Thornton exclaimed, startled. “Who is that? What right—” He cut himself off mid-sentence, checking his outburst.

“Frederick Hale, Margaret’s brother! Surely you know about him? I thought I told you long ago, when the Hales first came to Milton, that their eldest son was mixed up in a naval mutiny, and cannot set foot on English soil without being arrested.”

“I have never heard his name before. Where does he live?”

“Spain, as far as I know. The poor fellow will be heartbroken he cannot come home for his father’s funeral. We shall have to make do with Captain Lennox as the closest male relation.”

“I hope I may be allowed to attend the service, to pay my respects.”

“Of course you may! Hale liked you, you know, he spoke of you just a few days ago when I met him in Oxford. He regretted he had not seen more of you lately. I am very grateful you wish to honour him.”

“But about Frederick,” Thornton pressed. “He never came back to England secretly, did he?”

“Never. Good heavens, no.”

“Was he not here, around the time Mrs. Hale died? I saw a young man walking with Miss Hale one day, around that very time.”

“Oh that would be young Henry Lennox, the Captain’s brother, the lawyer. They corresponded with him regularly, and Mr. Hale told me once he was expecting him down for a visit.” Bell turned, squinting one eye shut to scrutinise Thornton’s face closely. “Do you know, I once fancied you had a little soft spot for Margaret?”

Thornton said nothing, did not change his expression.

“And poor Hale thought so too, though it never occurred to him till I put the idea in his head.”

“I admire Miss Hale,” Thornton said, steady as a rock, pushed to defence by Bell’s prodding. “Everyone must admire her. She is a beautiful creature.”

“Is that all? You speak of her in that cold, measured way, as if she is nothing but a pretty thing to catch the eye! I had hoped you had enough nobleness in you to give her the homage of a true heart. Even if she would have rejected you, I know she would have—loving her without return would have lifted you higher than all those people who ever knew her to love back. ‘Beautiful creature’ indeed! You would not speak of a horse or a dog that way, would you?”

Thornton’s eyes glowed like hot red embers. “Mr. Bell, before you speak so, remember not all men are free to say every single thing they feel out loud the way you are. Let us speak of something else.” His heart had leaped at every word Bell said, he knew he would value this old Oxford fellow’s understanding for the rest of his life, but he would not be badgered into gushing praise of Margaret like some cheap parrot reciting verses. He turned the conversation to the business they had between them, as landlord and tenant. “What is that heap of bricks and mortar I saw in the mill yard just now? Repairs needed?”

“No, no repairs. I am building a new dining hall for the mill hands.”

“I thought as a bachelor you would have no use for a second dining room big enough for a dozen families.”

“I got to know a very strange chap, Higgins the weaver, some time back. I put a couple of children he was fond of into school, and one day I stopped by his cottage to pay him a small sum of money he was owed, and saw his family eating the most miserable, cinder-greasy, burnt dinner I have ever seen. That got me thinking. Then this winter provisions got so expensive, and it struck me that if we bought food wholesale, and cooked large quantities together in a common kitchen, the men could save a fortune, and eat far better hot meals every day. I told Higgins my plan at first

“The meanest thing to which we bid adieu,
Loses its meanness in the parting hour.”

Mrs. Shaw conceived a vehement dislike of Milton as fierce as her gentle, sheltered nature was capable of feeling. The air hummed unceasingly with the clank of looms, reeked of coal smoke that stained fine lace brown by midday; the poor folk in the streets were grimed with mill dust, the wealthy ladies over-dressed in stiff silk that looked absurdly out of place amid the grime, and not a single man she saw, high or low, wore a coat that hung straight and true across his shoulders. She was certain Margaret would never recover her faded, post-illness strength while trapped in that smoky chaos, and she herself could feel the old nervous, fluttering weakness creeping up on her, the kind that only quiet Harley Street drawing rooms could soothe. Margaret must return to London with her at once, no delays. Worn thin by grief and exhaustion, Margaret at last yielded, promising that as soon as the Wednesday of her father’s funeral was past, she would prepare to leave, leaving Dixon behind to settle all outstanding bills, sell the bulk of the household furniture, and lock up the Hale house for good.

That mournful Wednesday saw Mr. Hale laid to rest far from the sun-dappled Helstone parsonage he had loved, and far from Mrs. Hale’s lonely grave in the southern county where she had died — a loss that gnawed at Margaret most of all, for she blamed the heavy, numb stupor that had swallowed her whole in the first days after his death for robbing her of the chance to arrange to bury him beside his wife. Before that Wednesday even dawned, a letter arrived from Mr. Bell.
> I had meant to return to Milton on Thursday, but unluckily our little band of Plymouth Fellows has been called to one of our rare mandatory duties, and I cannot absent myself from my post. Captain Lennox and Mr. Thornton are staying with me here. The Captain seems a sharp, well-meaning sort, and has volunteered to travel to Milton to help you search for your father’s will — though of course there is no such will, or you would have found it long ago if you followed my directions. He swears he will escort you and his mother-in-law back to town, and given Edith’s delicate condition, I cannot see how you can reasonably expect him to stay away later than Friday. Your Dixon is a reliable woman; she can hold down the house, and keep Mrs. Shaw calm, until I arrive. I will hand all legal matters over to my Milton solicitor if no will turns up, for I doubt that fine, moustachioed Captain is much of a hand at business, splendid as his facial hair may be.
> There will have to be a sale of most of the furniture, so mark out what pieces you wish reserved, or send me a list later. Two last things, and I am done. You know (or if you did not, your poor father did) that all my money and goods will be yours when I die. Not that I mean to die any time soon, mind — I only say this to explain what follows. The Lennoxes seem very fond of you now, but that fondness may wear thin, and it is best to have a formal agreement in place: you will pay them two hundred and fifty pounds a year for your board and lodging, which includes Dixon’s wages too — do not let anyone cajole you into paying more for her service. That way, if the Captain ever decides he wants his house to himself, you will not be cast adrift penniless: you can take your annuity and Dixon, and set up a little home of your own, or come to live with me and keep my house, whichever you prefer.
> I know you must be fuming right now, wondering what right this old man has to meddle in your affairs so cavalierly. But I have every right. I loved your father for thirty-five years; I stood beside him on his wedding day; I closed his eyes in death. I am your godfather, and I know full well I cannot help you much in spiritual matters, for you are far more devout than I will ever be. So I will do what little I can to look after your material comfort. I have not a single living relation on earth; “who is there to mourn for Adam Bell?” The whole of my heart is set on this, and Margaret Hale is not the girl to say me nay. Write me two lines by return post to confirm your answer. No thanks, mind.

Margaret picked up her pen, her hand trembling, and scrawled across the paper: “Margaret Hale is not the girl to say him nay.” She was vexed at repeating his exact phrasing, but she was too weak to frame any other words, so exhausted by the tiny exertion that she had to lie back down on the sofa the second she set down the pen, too tired to think. Her aunt fussed over her, convinced that the moment they escaped Milton’s foul air, Margaret would be her old self again. Margaret would only reply that she had no choice but to stay, for she could not leave her parents behind, and that Milton could be very pretty in summer, before she closed her eyes to end the conversation.

Mrs. Shaw filled the house with small comforts: fires lit in every bedroom at dusk, soft jellies and sweetmeats brought from the finest confectioner in town, thick wool blankets draped over every sofa. Margaret barely noticed any of it, only feeling quiet gratitude for the trouble her aunt went to on her behalf. Restless even in her weakness, she wandered from room to room all day, sorting through her belongings to pick what she would take with her. Dixon followed at her heels, per Mrs. Shaw’s orders, prattling nonstop about trivial things to distract her from her grief. Margaret set aside all her father’s books to send to Mr. Bell, for she knew he would value them both for their own sake and for Mr. Hale’s. Then she wrapped up a small, well-loved volume, scribbled a short note to Mr. Thornton asking him to keep it in memory of her father, and bade Dixon deliver it after she was gone. She wandered on, touching every worn, shabby relic from Helstone with soft, lingering fingers, loath to leave them behind, until by evening she was so tired she slept more deeply than she had in the days since her father’s death.

The next morning at breakfast, Margaret said she wished to go say goodbye to a few friends before they left. Mrs. Shaw objected at first, but finally agreed to accompany her, bringing her own maid along to carry shawls and air cushions. Margaret’s first stop was the narrow, dingy court where Nicholas Higgins lived; she was half relieved when Mrs. Shaw, fussing about lurking fevers and damp washing strung across the walkway, decided to wait in the carriage rather than get out. Margaret stepped inside to find Nicholas out at work, only Mary Boucher and the younger children at home. Mary burst into loud, unreserved sobs the second she realised Margaret was leaving Milton forever, and Margaret could do little but pat her shoulder, stammer vague promises of meeting again one day, and ask her to tell Higgins to come visit her at the house before she left. When she asked for a small memento of Bessy, she picked out the chipped fourpenny mug that had sat by Bessy’s bedside throughout her illness, to Mary’s confusion, who tried to press a finer, more expensive keepsake on her. Margaret would not be moved, and left the girl glowing with the quiet joy of having given her something she truly wanted.

Next they called at the Thorntons’ house. Mrs. Shaw got out of the carriage, huddling deep in her shawl in the newly lit drawing room, and complained that the room was icy cold. Mrs. Thornton entered a few minutes later, her usual sharp expression softened at the sight of Margaret’s pale, tear-swollen face and the tremor in her voice as she tried to speak steadily. Margaret apologized for her rudeness the last time they had met, and said she knew Mrs. Thornton had acted out of good intentions, no matter how much they had misunderstood each other. For once, Mrs. Thornton’s hardened reserve cracked, and she agreed she would believe Margaret’s word that she had never acted improperly, even without an explanation. Mrs. Shaw then gushed that if the Thorntons ever visited London, they would be more than welcome in her Harley Street home, never realizing that the “Mr. Thornton” she referenced as a separate stranger was the tall, dark man who just stepped through the doorway, still wearing his fresh mourning suit fresh from Oxford.

Mr. Thornton froze. He did not sit down, pretending to study papers scattered on the table, so lost in his own tangled thoughts he barely registered that the two guests were preparing to take their leave. He jumped up at the last moment to escort Mrs. Shaw back to the carriage, and for a moment he and Margaret stood alone on the doorstep, both thinking of the day of the riot, of the passionate words she had spoken the next day that had haunted him ever since. His heart swelled with longing, but he steeled himself: he had made his declaration once, and lost his chance. He said goodbye in a steady, unemotional tone, took her outstretched hand for a second as if it was a dry, withered flower, and turned away before she could speak another word. No one in his household saw him for the rest of the day.

Margaret was so utterly exhausted when they returned that her aunt and Dixon considered delaying the journey for a few days, but Margaret twisted in what looked like acute pain when the suggestion was made, begging them to leave as soon as possible: she could not bear to stay in Milton one unnecessary hour, she said, she wanted to forget. Captain Lennox arrived that evening, bringing cheerful, unpressured tales of Edith and the new baby, and his easy, unanxious company did Margaret far more good than all her aunt’s fussy fretting. Soon after, Higgins came by. He greeted her gruffly, saying he had been gutted when he heard Mr. Hale had died, that every mill worker in Milton knew the late parson was the kindest man who had ever set foot in the town. He laughed and said Mr. Thornton had told him Margaret was off to London to be a grand lady, but he had sworn he would walk all the way to the capital next Whitsuntide if he had to, just to say goodbye. Margaret handed him her father’s well-worn Bible, bidding him keep it and read it for Mr. Hale’s sake, then pressed a handful of coins into his hand for the Boucher children. Higgins protested he would not take charity, but Margaret laughed and said none of it was for him, it was all for the little ones, and he had no right to refuse it for their sakes. He bowed his head, muttering a dozen quiet blessings on her, and that was the end of Margaret’s life in Milton.

“A dull rotation, never at a stay,
Yesterday’s face twin image of to-day.”

“Of what each one should be, he sees the form and rule,
And till he reach to that, his joy can ne’er be full.”

The deep, unbroken quiet of the Harley Street house, while Edith recovered from her confinement, was a gentle blessing to Margaret Hale. It gave her the slow, unhurried rest her overstrained nerves had craved, and space to wrap her head around the staggering shift in her circumstances that had unfolded over the past two months. She found herself lodged in a home of such unsparing luxury that the very existence of hardship or small, grinding cares seemed never to have crossed its threshold. The cogs of daily life were so perfectly oiled that every routine turned with soft, unjarring smoothness. Mrs. Shaw and Edith fussed over her endlessly, insisting that this house was her true home now, and Margaret could not shake the faint, guilty secret that it did not feel like home at all: her idea of belonging was still tied to the sun-dappled Helstone vicarage, even to the cramped, drafty little cottage in Milton, with her anxious father, her ailing mother, and all the tiny, messy household troubles of their modest means.

Edith could barely wait to regain her strength before she set about refurnishing Margaret’s bedroom with the same heap of silk cushions, laced trinkets, and tiny porcelain curios that crowded every surface of her own chambers. Mrs. Shaw and her lady’s maid spent days restyling Margaret’s wardrobe, restoring it to an elegant, cheerful variety that suited the London season. Captain Lennox, meanwhile, was easy, kind, and unfailingly gentlemanly: he sat with Edith in her dressing room for an hour or two every day, played with his infant son Sholto for another, and lounged away the rest of his hours at his club or at dinner engagements with his fellow officers.

Just before Margaret had begun to chafe at the lack of purpose in her days, Edith came downstairs to resume her place at the head of the household, and Margaret fell back into the old familiar habit of tending to her cousin. She happily took all the trivial tedious duties off Edith’s hands: answered every social note, reminded her of forgotten calls, and sat by her side to soothe her when no gala was on the calendar and she felt languid enough to fancy herself ill. But the rest of the family was swept up entirely in the whirl of the London Season, and Margaret was often left entirely alone.

Her thoughts drifted back to Milton, and she was struck by the stark, unbridgeable contrast between that life and this one. She was growing sick of this eventless, effortless ease, where no struggle, no small act of meaningful work, was ever required. She feared she would grow sleepy and dull, forgetting every part of the world beyond the soft luxury that wrapped around her. There were toilers and drudges in London, she knew, but she never saw them: the servants lived in an invisible, underground world of their own, whose hopes and fears she would never learn, and they only seemed to spring into existence when a master or mistress rang a bell to satisfy some small want or whim. A strange, unsatisfied hollow yawned in Margaret’s chest, and once, when she hinted at this vague emptiness to Edith, the latter, still languid from dancing until three the night before, stroked Margaret’s cheek idly as she perched on a footstool beside the sofa where Edith lay.

“Poor child!” Edith said. “It’s so sad for you to be left home night after night just when all the world is at its gayest. But we’ll be hosting our dinner parties soon—just as soon as Henry comes back from circuit—and then you’ll have all sorts of nice little variety. No wonder you’re moped, poor darling.”

Margaret did not believe a string of dinner parties could fix that hollow feeling. But Edith prided herself on her dinner spreads, insisting they were nothing like the old stuffy, formal dowager dinners her mother used to host, and Mrs. Shaw herself took just as much delight in the Lennoxes’ bright, lively social circle as she had once taken in her own heavy, old-fashioned entertainments. Captain Lennox was unfailingly kind and brotherly to Margaret, and she was very fond of him—save for those rare moments when he fussed over Edith’s dress and appearance, determined that her beauty should dazzle every guest in the room. Then all the fiery, independent core of Margaret’s nature rose up, and she bit her tongue to keep from speaking her mind.

Her days unfolded in a lazy, unchanging rhythm: an hour or two of quiet before a late breakfast that no one bothered to serve punctually, eaten slowly by half-asleep, groggy people, where she was expected to sit through every dragged-out minute even though none of the family’s upcoming plans concerned her, expected to offer sympathy even if she could not offer advice. After that, there were endless stacks of notes Edith left for her to write, heaping soft compliments on Margaret’s elegant, charming letter-writing. She would play with Sholto after his morning walk, mind him while the servants ate their own dinner, go for a drive or receive callers, and then her aunt and cousins would head off to some engagement, leaving her free—yet tired, listless, her already delicate health worsened by days of no real work or purpose.

She waited, unspoken but longingly, for Dixon to return from Milton, where the old servant had been busy wrapping up every last one of the Hale family’s affairs. The total lack of news from the place she had called home for two years had felt like a famine to her. Dixon’s formal, businesslike letters only dropped in the stray mention of Mr. Thornton’s advice, about what to do with the Hale’s old furniture, or how to negotiate with the landlord of their Crampton Terrace cottage. Hardly any other Milton names ever crossed the page. One evening, she sat alone in the Lennox drawing room, holding Dixon’s half-read letters in her hand, staring into space as she thought back over old days, picturing Milton’s noisy, busy streets, wondering if that whole whirl went on just the same as if her and her father had never existed at all, wondering if anyone in that whole crowd missed her (not Higgins—she was not thinking of him at all) when the servant announced Mr. Bell. Margaret stuffed the letters into her workbasket in a hurry, and stood up blushing as if she had been caught doing something guilty.

“Oh, Mr. Bell! I never thought I would see you here!”
“I hope that surprised little start is paired with a proper welcome,” he laughed. “Have you dined? How did you get here? Let me order you something to eat at once,” Margaret fussed.
“Only if you’re planning to dine too. Otherwise, you know no one cares less about eating than I do. But where are all the rest? Gone out to dinner? Left you all alone?”
“Oh yes, and it’s such a quiet, lovely rest. I was just thinking—would you chance an impromptu dinner? I’ve no idea what’s in the larder.”
“To tell you the truth, I already dined at my club. Their cooks aren’t half as good as they used to be, though, so I thought if you were going to eat I might make a second go of it. Never mind, never mind! There aren’t ten cooks in all of England you can trust with an unexpected meal. Their skills and their fires might hold, but their tempers certainly won’t. You can make me tea instead, Margaret. Now—what were you thinking about, just now? You were going to tell me. Whose letters were those you hid away so fast, my god-daughter?”
“Only Dixon’s,” Margaret said, flushing bright.
“Whew! That’s all? Do you know who rode up from Milton in the same train as me today?”
“I’ve no idea,” Margaret said, refusing to guess.
“Your what-d’ye-call-him? The brother of your cousin-in-law? Henry Lennox?”
“Mr. Henry Lennox?” Margaret asked.
“That’s him,” Mr. Bell said. “You knew him long ago, didn’t you? What do you make of him, Margaret?”
“I liked him a very long time ago,” Margaret said, glancing down for a moment, then lifting her eyes to speak in her usual steady tone. “We’ve been corresponding about Frederick for years after that, but I haven’t seen him for nearly three. He might be quite changed now. What did you think of him?”
“I’m not sure. He was so busy trying to work out who I was, first, then what sort of status I held, that he never let a single thing slip about himself—unless all that veiled curiosity about the man he was talking to is a fair marker of his character. Do you think he’s good-looking, Margaret?”
“Certainly not,” she said. “Do you?”
“Not a bit. I thought you might, though. Is he often down here in town?”
“I think he is, when he’s not out on circuit. He’s been away on circuit ever since I arrived. But—Mr. Bell—did you come straight from Oxford, or from Milton?”
“From Milton. Can’t you see I’m still smoke-dried from the mills?”
“I did wonder if it might be all that ancient Oxford dust clinging to you,” Margaret teased.
“Come now, be sensible! In Oxford I could cajole every landlord in the city to give me my way with half the trouble your Milton landlord has given me—beaten me in the end, too. He won’t release our lease on the Crampton Terrace house until next June twelvemonth. Luckily, Mr. Thornton tracked down a new tenant for it for us. Why don’t you ask after Mr. Thornton, Margaret? He’s been a wonderfully active friend to you, you know. Took more than half the whole mess of work off my shoulders.”
“How is he? How is Mrs. Thornton?” Margaret asked, breathless, voice so quiet it was almost a murmur before she forced herself to speak louder.
“They’re well enough, as far as I can tell. I was staying at their house until I was driven half mad by nonstop chatter about the Thornton girl’s wedding. It was too much for Thornton himself, even though she’s his own sister. He kept escaping to lock himself in his study, the whole time. He’s far too old to care for all that frippery nonsense, either as the guest of honor or a bystander. I was shocked to see old Mrs. Thornton get swept up in the whole fuss, all her daughter’s glee over orange blossoms and lace carrying her away. I thought she was made of far sterner stuff.”
“She would put on any show of soft sentiment to hide her daughter’s silliness,” Margaret said softly. “You’re right, she doesn’t much care for me.”
“I know that for a fact,” Mr. Bell nodded.
“Oh, thank goodness, tea is here at last!” Margaret exclaimed, grateful for the interruption.

And with the tea came Henry Lennox himself, who had walked up to Harley Street after a late dinner, evidently expecting to find his brother and Edith at home. Margaret suspected he was just as relieved as she was to have a third party in the room: this was their first meeting since the fateful afternoon on the Helstone garden terrace, when he had proposed and she had turned him down. She did not know what to say at first, and was glad for all the little tea-table tasks to keep her hands busy and her mouth still, giving him time to collect himself too. He had forced himself to walk over tonight, after all, just to get this awkward first meeting over with—awkward even if the rest of the family had been home, and twice as awkward now that Margaret was the only other person in the room, the one he would naturally have to address most of his conversation to.

Margaret was the first to regain her composure. Once the initial flush of awkward shyness faded, she spoke of the first thing that came to her mind. “Mr. Lennox, I cannot thank you enough for all the work you’ve done on Frederick’s behalf.”
“I only wish it had been more successful,” he replied, glancing quickly over at Mr. Bell, as if gauging how much he could safely say in front of a stranger. Margaret read his thought instantly, and turned to Mr. Bell, drawing him clearly into the conversation, making it plain he knew every detail of the efforts to clear Frederick’s name.
“That last witness, Horrocks—the very last one we held out hope for—proved to be no help at all. Mr. Lennox found out he sailed for Australia last August, just two months before Frederick came back to England, and gave us all the names of—”
“Frederick was in England? No one ever told me that!” Mr. Bell exclaimed, shocked.
“I thought you knew. I never doubted someone must have mentioned it. Of course it was a great secret—perhaps I ought not to have said it now,” Margaret said, a little dismayed.
“I never named it to my brother, or your cousin, for that matter,” Mr. Lennox said, with the cool, dry tone of a professional who guarded his clients’ secrets.
“Never you mind, Margaret,” Mr. Bell said. “I don’t live in a gossiping, prying world, and none of my acquaintances go digging for hidden facts. You don’t have to look so frightened spilling the secret to an old hermit like me. I’ll never tell a soul he was here—no one will even ask me. Wait a minute!” he interrupted himself, suddenly sharp. “Was he here for your mother’s funeral?”
“He was with her when she died,” Margaret said softly.
“Of course! Of course! Someone asked me not many weeks ago if he hadn’t come over then, and I denied it stoutly—who was that?
