Oh, I remember!” He did not say the name, and Margaret ached to know if her suspicion was right, if it had been Mr. Thornton who had asked after Frederick—but she could not bring herself to pose the question.

There was a short, quiet pause. Then Mr. Lennox turned back to Margaret. “Since Mr. Bell now knows every circumstance of your brother’s unfortunate predicament, there is no better way forward than to walk him through exactly what remains of the evidence we once hoped to produce in his favor. If he would do me the honor of breakfasting with me tomorrow, we can go over every name of those missing witnesses.”
“I want to hear every last detail too, if I may. Can’t you both come here instead? I don’t quite dare formally invite you both to breakfast, but I know you would be more than welcome. I need to know everything we still can about Frederick, even if there is no hope of clearing him right now.”
“I have an appointment at half past eleven. But I will certainly come if you wish it,” Mr. Lennox replied, with a faint, unmissable eagerness that made Margaret draw back shyly, almost wishing she had not made the natural request.

Mr. Bell got to his feet, hunting for his hat that the servant had moved to make room for the tea tray. “Well, I don’t know what Mr. Lennox intends to do, but I’m for heading home. I’ve traveled all day, and long journeys start to wear on a man of sixty odd years.”
“I think I shall stay and wait for my brother and Edith to get back,” Mr. Lennox said, making no move to leave. A sudden, shy panic seized Margaret at the thought of being left alone with him. The memory of that afternoon on the Helstone terrace was so vivid to her, she could only assume it was just as vivid for him.
“Don’t go yet, please Mr. Bell,” she said quickly, laying a light, firm hand on his arm. “I want you to meet Edith. Please stay.” He looked at her, saw the flustered confusion stirring in her face, and sat down again at once, as if that small touch had held a strength he could not resist.
“You see how she overpowers me, Mr. Lennox,” he laughed. “And did you notice how cleverly she phrased it? First she said she wanted me to come ‘see’ her cousin Edith, the great beauty everyone talks about, but then she corrected herself to say Edith should ‘know’ me. I suppose I’m not much worth looking at, eh Margaret?” He joked to give her time to settle her frayed nerves, and she caught his light tone and bantered back easily. Mr. Lennox found himself wondering how his brother the Captain could ever have claimed Margaret had lost all her good looks. Dressed in her quiet black mourning gown, she was the perfect contrast to Edith, who drifted in a little later in her white crape mourning dress with loose golden curls floating around her shoulders, all softness and glitter. Edith dimpled and blushed prettily when she was introduced to Mr. Bell, determined to live up to her reputation as a great beauty and win over even this gruff old Oxford don. Mrs. Shaw and Captain Lennox both welcomed Mr. Bell warmly, and he found himself liking them almost against his will, especially when he saw how naturally Margaret slipped into her place as a beloved sister and daughter of the house.

“It is such a shame we were not home to greet you,” Edith said. “You too, Henry! Though I don’t know if we would have skipped our dinner party for you. But for Mr. Bell! For Margaret’s Mr. Bell—”
“There is no limit to the sacrifices you would have made,” her brother-in-law cut in, dry. “You would have skipped the whole gala, missed the chance to wear this very lovely dress, no doubt.” Edith did not know whether to frown or smile, and Henry Lennox was quick to turn the joke light before he irritated her. “Would you be willing to make a small sacrifice tomorrow morning, then? First, agree to host the breakfast to meet Mr. Bell, and second, make it half past nine instead of ten o’clock? I have a stack of letters and papers I want to show Miss Hale and Mr. Bell.”
“Please make our house your own for the whole duration of your stay in London,” Captain Lennox said to Mr. Bell. “I am only sorry we cannot offer you a bedroom for the night.”
“Thank you, I am much obliged. And I would be a churl if you did, because I should have to turn you down, even for the pleasure of such agreeable company,” Mr. Bell replied, bowing politely all around. Privately he congratulated himself on the well-turned phrase, when the plain truth of it was: I could not stand the stuffy, over-polite restraints of this household. It would be like eating meat without salt. I am thankful they have no spare bed.

He was still smiling to

“Where are the sounds that swam along
The buoyant air when I was young;
The last vibration now is o’er,
And they who listened are no more;
Ah! let me close my eyes and dream.”

The memory of Helstone had risen unbidden to Mr. Bell’s waking mind mid-conversation with Mr. Lennox the day before, and it had run wild through every corner of his dreams all night long. He was young again, not a respected college Fellow but a lowly tutor, lingering through the long vacation as the guest of his newly married, giddy friend: the proud, overjoyed new Vicar of Helstone, Richard Hale. The two of them bounded across babbling little brooks in wild, impossible leaps, that hung suspended mid-air for what felt like whole sunlit days. Time and space melted away, even as every other detail felt sharp and true. No event followed the rules of real life, measured not by clock or calendar but by the soft swell of shared emotion, for none of it was real — and yet it all felt more vivid than his waking hours. The beech trees blazed in rich autumn crimson, the warm scents of wild herb and late blooming rose drifted thick and sweet on the breeze, and the young Mrs. Hale moved through the little parsonage with that exact familiar mixture of faint, playful irritation at their narrow household means, and unashamed pride in her handsome, adoring husband, that Bell had observed in real life a full quarter of a century prior.

The dream was so lifelike that when he jolted awake, his whole present life felt like the false, hollow fantasy. Where was he? Stuck in the close, over-furnished, stuffy rooms of a central London hotel! Where were the friends who had been laughing, speaking, brushing his sleeve not a heartbeat before? Dead. Buried. Lost forever, as far as any mortal “forever” could stretch. He was an old man now, his joints aching, even though only moments ago in the dream he had been brimming with the untested strength of youth. The utter, crushing loneliness of his life was too much to bear for a single second longer sitting still, so he stumbled out of bed and dressed at a rushed, clumsy pace, desperate to outrun the ghosts of the past and make it to the breakfast appointment in Harley Street.

He could barely focus on the fine details of Lennox’s legal presentation, even as he watched Margaret’s eyes grow wide and bright with tension, her lips blanching one by one, as every last scrap of evidence that might have exonerated Frederick Hale crumbled out from under them, one after another, as if fate itself had decreed no such proof could remain. Even Mr. Lennox, whose professional tone was always so measured and unshakable, softened into a gentle, tender cadence as he drew close to laying the last of their hopes to rest. It was not that Margaret had not known this outcome was likely all along: it was the relentless, sharp minuteness of every successive disappointment, laid bare one after another, that finally wore down her resolve, and she gave way to quiet, shaking tears.

Mr. Lennox set his papers down at once. “I ought not to go on with this,” he said, his voice thick with concern. “This was a foolish, ill-advised proposal of mine. Lieutenant Hale —” he used the naval title he had been so harshly stripped of, a small, soft gesture of respect that soothed Margaret more than any other comfort could — “is happy now. His fortune and his future across the sea are far more secure than anything he ever could have hoped for in the Royal Navy, and no doubt he has long since adopted his wife’s homeland as his own.”

“That is all true,” Margaret managed, trying to force a wobbly smile through her tears. “I feel so selfish for grieving as I do. But he is lost to me, and I am so very lonely.”

Mr. Lennox shuffled his papers into a neat stack, and silently wished with all his might that he was already as wealthy and successful as he was certain he would be in the years to come, so he could wave away every last one of her troubles. Mr. Bell blew his nose loudly into his handkerchief, and otherwise kept quiet, giving her space to collect herself. In a minute or two, Margaret had regained her usual quiet composure, and thanked Mr. Lennox with a courtesy and grace that was almost exaggerated, for she was all too aware that her tears might have led him to believe he had inflicted unnecessary pain. And yet, for all the sharp ache it carried, she would not have missed that full, honest reckoning with her loss for anything.

Mr. Bell shuffled over to her to say his goodbyes, his fumbling gout-ridden fingers tangling in his thick leather gloves. “Margaret,” he said, “I’m heading down to Helstone tomorrow, to go look over the old place. Would you like to come with me? Or would it be far too painful? Speak plain, don’t mince words on my account.”

“Oh, Mr. Bell —” Margaret could not get any more words past her tight throat. She only lifted his gnarled, swollen old hand and pressed a soft kiss to it.

“Come now, that’s quite enough of that,” he grumbled, his face flushing bright pink with awkward pleasure. “I’m sure your aunt Shaw will trust you in my care. We’ll leave first thing tomorrow morning, get there around two o’clock, I reckon. We’ll grab a light snack, order dinner at the little inn — the Lennard Arms, that’s what it used to be called — and then go wander the forest to work up an appetite. Can you bear it, Margaret? It’ll be a trial for both of us, I know, but it’ll be a pleasure to me, at the very least. We’ll dine there on roast venison, if the innkeeper still has access to the forest deer, and then I’ll take my usual afternoon nap while you go out and call on any remaining old friends from the parsonage days. I’ll bring you back safe and sound, barring railway accidents, and I’ll insure your life for a thousand pounds before we start out, that ought to be some comfort to your relations. I’ll have you back with Mrs. Shaw by lunchtime on Friday, no later. So if you say yes, I’ll go straight upstairs and put the plan to her.”

“There’s no way I can possibly tell you how much I want to go,” Margaret said, the tears still dripping down her cheeks.

“Alright then, prove your gratitude by drying those tear ducts up for the next two days,” Bell ordered gruffly. “If you don’t, I’ll get all wobbly lipped myself, and I hate that more than anything.”

“I won’t shed a single more drop,” Margaret promised, winking hard to shake the leftover tears off her eyelashes and forcing a bright, steady smile.

“That’s my good girl. Now let’s go upstairs and sort all of this out.”

Margaret was trembling with quiet, eager hope as Mr. Bell laid out his plan to Aunt Shaw, who went through every predictable stage of reaction: first startled, then doubtful, then hopelessly perplexed over whether the excursion was at all proper for a young unmarried woman. In the end she yielded far more to Mr. Bell’s gruff, earnest good sense than to her own uncertain misgivings, for right up until the day Margaret returned safe and sound, she could not make up her own mind if the trip was right or wrong, appropriate or unseemly. By the time the pair stepped back through the Harley Street door, the project had proved such a glowing success that Aunt Shaw declared without hesitation that it had been the kindest, most thoughtful idea Mr. Bell could possibly have had, and exactly the rest Margaret needed after all the long, anxious months of trouble she had weathered.

“So on those happy days of yore
Oft as I dare to dwell once more,
Still must I miss the friends so tried,
Whom Death has severed from my side.
But ever when true friendship binds,
Spirit it is that spirit finds;
In spirit then our bliss we found,
In spirit yet to them I’m bound.”

Margaret stood fully dressed and bonneted long before their arranged departure time, all her bags packed and waiting by the door. When no one was watching, she let quiet, unrushed tears slip down her cheeks; the moment any eye turned her way, she lifted her face with a bright, determined smile to hide how tight her chest felt. Her last lingering panic was that they would miss their train, but the day moved gentle as honey, and they reached the station with minutes to spare. She sank back against the carriage seat opposite Mr. Bell, breathing slow, happy, relieved breaths as the train whirled past familiar stations, bearing her south towards the land she had missed so fiercely for so long.

The old southern towns and hamlets lay drowsing in warm, clear sun, their terracotta tiled roofs glowing a deep russet far softer and warmer than the cold grey slate that roofed every building in Milton up north. Broods of plump pigeons circled the gables, drifting down to perch and ruffle their soft, iridescent feathers as if pressing every inch of themselves into the delicious golden warmth. The station platforms were nearly empty: everyone seemed too lazily, gently content to bother with travel, none of the sharp bustle and hurry Margaret had learned to expect on the busy northern railway lines. Later in the year, this route would be crowded with wealthy holiday-makers, but the constant throng of harried tradespeople rushing to and fro for work would never come here. A few idle men leaned against nearly every station wall, hands in their pockets, so absorbed in the simple act of watching the train that Margaret found herself wondering what on earth they would do for entertainment once the cars vanished, leaving nothing but empty railway track, a few sheds, and distant rolling fields behind. The hot air shimmered over the golden stillness of the countryside, each rolling farm passing by reminding her of German idylls—of Hermann and Dorothea, of Evangeline, quiet stories of home and hearth far from clanging factory mills.

She was jolted out of the waking dream when the conductor called out their stop: it was time to leave the train, and take the old horse-drawn fly the rest of the way to Helstone. Sharper, tangled feelings shot through her chest then, half pain, half joy, she could barely untangle the two. Every single mile of the road was thick with old associations she would not have traded for the world, and every memory made her ache for the days that were gone, that she could never get back. The last time she had driven this way was the day they left Helstone for Milton, her father and mother both beside her, the sky cold and grey, her own future hopeless and unknowable. Now she was alone, an orphan, and they had vanished from the face of the earth, never to walk these roads with her again. It hurt to see the Helstone road flooded with bright sunlight, every turn, every familiar oak and hawthorn tree standing in exactly the same summer glory they had shone in years before. Nature felt no grief, no change: it stayed forever young.

Mr. Bell guessed every line of what was passing through her head, and wisely, kindly, held his tongue. They drew up to the Lennard Arms, the old half-farmhouse half-inn that stood a little back from the road, as if to declare its owner did not need to fawn over travelers for custom—they would come seeking him out, if they knew what was good for them. The house faced the wide village green, and right out front stood an ancient lime tree, with benches nailed all the way around its trunk, and the grim, carved escutcheon of the long-gone Lennard family half hidden deep in its thick leafy boughs. The inn’s door stood propped wide open, but no one came rushing out to greet them. It was only after they had been standing in the dark parlour a few minutes that the landlady appeared, apologizing for her delay: haymaking season was in full swing, she had been busy packing baskets of provisions for the men working out in the fields, and had not heard their wheels rolling over the soft turf lane leading off the main road.

“Bless me!” she exclaimed, as a stray slant of sun fell full on Margaret’s face, half hidden before by shadow. “It’s Miss Hale! Jenny—Jenny come here at once, it’s Miss Hale!” She ran to the door to call her daughter, then hurried back to Margaret and shook her hands with the warm, motherly fondness of someone who had watched her grow up. “And how are you all? How’s the Vicar? How’s Dixon? We’ve never once stopped being sorry you all left us.”

Margaret tried to speak, to tell her of her father’s death. It was clear Mrs. Purkis already knew of her mother’s passing, for she did not speak of her. But the words stuck in her throat, and all she could do was brush a hand over the deep black of her mourning dress, and whisper one broken word: “Papa.”

“Surely, sir, it can’t be so,” Mrs. Purkis said, turning to Mr. Bell for confirmation, her face falling. “There was a gentleman came by last spring, even before that last winter, who told us Mrs. Hale was gone, poor lady—but he never said a word of the Vicar being unwell!”

“It is true, I’m afraid,” Mr. Bell said gently. “He died very suddenly, while he was staying with me in Oxford. He was a very good man, Mrs. Purkis, and any of us would be lucky to have as calm and gentle an end as he did. Margaret here is my goddaughter—her father was my oldest, dearest friend. We decided we would come down together to see the old places, and I knew well you’d give us comfortable rooms and a dinner worth eating. You don’t remember me, do you? My name’s Bell. A time or two when the parsonage was full to bursting, I slept right here, and tasted your famous home-brewed ale.”

“Of course! Forgive me, I was so taken aback seeing Miss Hale again,” Mrs. Purkis fussed. “Let me show you up to your room, Miss Margaret, you can take off your bonnet and wash your face. Only this very morning I stuck a handful of fresh musk roses head-down in the water jug, just thinking someone might come by, and there’s nothing sweeter than spring water scented with roses. To think the Vicar’s gone! Well, we all must go sometime—but that man who came by said he was picking right up, after the trouble of Mrs. Hale’s death.”

“Come down and find me after you’ve seen Margaret settled,” Mr. Bell said. “I want to talk to you about our dinner.”

The small casement window in Margaret’s bedroom was almost entirely framed by rose and vine branches. She pushed the foliage aside and leaned out, and could just see the tops of the old parsonage chimneys peeking above the treetops, every line of the roof and walls peeking familiar through the leaves.

“Times are changed, miss,” Mrs. Purkis chattered as she smoothed the linens on the bed, and sent Jenny off to fetch a stack of towels scented with lavender. “The new Vicar’s got seven children, and he’s building a new nursery right where your father’s old arbour and tool shed used to be. He’s put new grates in every room, and a big plate-glass window in the old drawing room. Him and his wife are busy, stirring folks, do a lot of what they call good, though if you ask me it’s turning the whole place upside down for no great purpose. The new Vicar’s a teetotaller, miss, and a magistrate. His wife’s got a hundred recipes for economical cooking, swears by making bread without yeast, and they both talk a mile a minute, all at the same time, so you can’t get a word in edgewise. He’ll go poking his nose in the haymakers’ cans to make sure no one’s drinking ale, and kick up a fuss if it’s not ginger beer—but I won’t have it. My mother and grandmother sent good malt liquor out to the hayfields before me, and I’ll go on their way, no matter what Mrs. Hepworth says about comfits being better than salts and senna for a cold. It’s nonsense, if you ask me. But I must run off, I’ll come back and hear all your news later.”

When Margaret came downstairs, Mr. Bell had a spread of fresh strawberries, thick clotted cream, a loaf of crusty brown bread and a jug of cold milk waiting for her, with a Stilton cheese and a bottle of port tucked off to the side for his own refreshment. After their light rustic lunch, they stepped outside for a walk, standing still for a moment uncertain which way to turn—every direction offered a dozen old, familiar paths to explore.

“Shall we head past the old vicarage first?” Mr. Bell asked.

“Not yet,” Margaret said. “We’ll go this way, take the long roundabout, and come back to it later.”

She spotted changes everywhere: old trees that had stood for decades cut down the previous autumn, rough little squatter cottages long rotting away now torn down entirely. She grieved for each one of them, as if they were old friends long gone. They passed the very spot where she and Henry Lennox had sat and sketched years before. The venerable lightning-scarred beech tree whose roots they had rested against was gone, the old man who lived in the ruinous cottage nearby was dead, the cottage itself pulled down and replaced with a spick-and-span new little house, with a neat vegetable patch where the old beech’s roots had once spread.

“I never realized how old I was,” Margaret said, after a long, quiet pause, and she turned away, sighing.

“It’s always the first changes to the familiar that make time feel like a strange, bewildering mystery to the young,” Mr. Bell said. “After a while, you learn to take all shifts as a matter of course. I know well that nothing human stays the same forever—but for you, it’s new, and heavy on the heart.”

“Let’s go see little Susan,” Margaret said, pulling him along a grassy lane that ran under the dappled shadow of a forest glade.

“I’d be delighted,” he laughed, “though I haven’t the faintest idea who little Susan is. But I’ve got a soft spot for all Susans, ever since I knew simple old Susan as a boy.”

“My little Susan was heartbroken when I left Helstone without stopping to say goodbye. It’s been weighing on my conscience ever since, that I hurt her when all it would have taken was a few extra minutes of my time. It’s a fair walk, though—are you sure you won’t tire?”

“Quite sure, so long as you don’t rush me. There’s no grand views here to give you an excuse to stop and catch your breath, after all. You’d think walking with a man ‘fat and scant o’ breath’ was terribly romantic if I were Hamlet, Prince of Denmark, wouldn’t you? Have compassion on my old legs for his sake.”

“I’ll walk slower for your own sake. I like you twenty times better than Hamlet, anyway.”

“On the old principle that a living ass is better than a dead lion?”

“Perhaps. I don’t bother analysing my feelings much these days.”

“Fair enough, I’ll take your liking for me without prying apart what it’s made of. Though we don’t need to crawl along at a snail’s pace, for heaven’s sake.”

The banter lightened Margaret’s mood for a while, even as they wandered further down the green lanes. Mr. Bell kept up the light, pointless chatter on purpose, to keep her from sinking too deep into sad old memories. She was grateful, though she almost would have preferred to walk these beloved paths in total silence, if not for the guilt of wishing she could make the whole journey alone.

They reached the cottage where Susan’s widowed mother lived, only to find Susan was not at home. She had gone off to the new parish school, and Margaret’s face fell.

“Oh, it’s quite all right,” Margaret said quickly, when the poor woman started apologizing. “I’m glad to hear it. I ought to have thought of it—she used to stay home with you, before.”

“Aye, she did, and I miss her something terrible. I used to teach her what little reading I knew at night, sitting by the fire. It wasn’t much, but she was getting such a handy little girl. Now she’s learned far more than I ever knew, all from the school.” The mother sighed.

“Hang it all,” Mr. Bell grumbled under his breath, “I’m a hundred years behind the times. I’d always thought a child got a far simpler, better education staying at home, helping her mother, and learning to read a chapter of the New Testament each night, than all the fancy schooling in the world.”

Margaret did not reply, not wanting to start an argument in front of the woman. Instead she asked, “How is old Betty Barnes doing these days?”

“I don’t know,” the woman said shortly. “We’re not speaking.”

“Why ever not?” Margaret asked—she had been the village’s little peacemaker, once upon a time, sorting out petty squabbles between neighbours.

“She stole my cat.”

“Did she know it was yours, do you think?”

“I reckon she didn’t.”

“Then why didn’t you ask her to give it back, once you told her it belonged to you?”

“Couldn’t. She’d burnt it.”

“Burnt it!” Margaret and Mr. Bell exclaimed in unison.

“Roasted it,” the woman clarified.

Margaret pressed her gently, bit by bit, until she drew out the full, horrible story. Betty Barnes had lent a gipsy fortune-teller her husband’s best Sunday suit, on the promise it would be returned to her on Saturday night before he noticed it was gone. When the gipsy never came back, Betty grew terrified of her husband’s rage. She’d heard an old savage country superstition that if you tortured a cat to death over the fire, its cries would force the powers of darkness to grant whatever wish you wanted. So she had grabbed the neighbour’s stray cat, and thrown it on the fire, to make the charm work. The woman telling the story had no sympathy for the suffering animal, only anger that her own cat had been chosen for the cruel sacrifice. She admitted it was wicked, but insisted everyone in the village knew the charm worked, and Betty had gotten her husband’s suit back the very next day. Margaret argued with her for ten minutes, trying to break her of the old pagan belief, but gave up in the end, sick at heart.

“You’re a good girl not to gloat over me,” Mr. Bell said, as they walked away.

“What do you mean?”

“I was dead wrong about schooling. Anything, anything at all, to stop little Susan growing up believing that kind of cruel, ignorant rubbish.”

“Oh! I remember,” Margaret said. “Poor little Susan. Would you mind stopping by the school to see her?”

“Not at all. I’m curious to see what kind of lessons they’re teaching the children, these days.”

They did not speak much more as they tramped through the shady, green dells. Even the soft sweet light of the southern woods could not erase the shock of the story they had just heard. Soon, the low murmur of dozens of voices, like the hum of a busy beehive, drifted up to them: they had stepped out of the trees onto the open village green, where the new schoolhouse stood. The door was propped wide open, and they walked in.

A brisk, sharp-eyed lady in black darted over to greet them, the very same air Margaret remembered her own mother putting on, soft and languid, when rare visitors dropped in to inspect the old Helstone school. This was Mrs. Hepworth, the new vicar’s wife. She instantly recognized Margaret, as did dozens of little faces in the classroom, half-muffled whispers of “That’s Miss Hale” rippling through the rows of desks.

“Your father, I presume, Miss Hale?” Mrs. Hepworth said, holding out a hand to Mr. Bell. “I can see the family resemblance. I’m so glad to meet you both!”

Margaret stammered out that Mr. Bell was not her father, that her own father had died suddenly, and Mrs. Hepworth’s manner turned immediately kind, though just a little patronizing, as if Margaret was a poor orphan to be pitied. She did not give Margaret time to recover, before gesturing to the front of the room.

“Ah, I see you miss taking a class, Miss Hale! First class, stand up—you’re doing a parsing lesson with Miss Hale today!”

Margaret was taken completely aback. Her visit was sentimental, not some formal inspection—but as she sat down at the front, she found herself looking at all the little familiar faces, dozens of children her own father had baptised, and all her awkwardness melted away. She slipped her hand over little Susan’s for a quiet minute unobserved, while Mrs. Hepworth dragged Mr. Bell over to a corner to explain the new phonetic reading system, chattering nonstop about her most recent conversation with the school inspector.

Margaret leaned over the children’s books, half lost in old memories of her father, until a little girl got stuck on the simplest word in the text, stammering over “a”.

“It’s an indefinite article,” Margaret said gently, by instinct.

“Oh, I beg your pardon!” Mrs. Hepworth said, popping over instantly. “Mr. Milsome, our inspector, teaches us to call ‘a’ an—who can tell?”

“An adjective absolute!” half a dozen children piped up at once. Margaret sat back, flustered and red-faced. The children knew more than she did. Mr. Bell turned away, snorting with quiet laughter.

She did not speak again for the rest of the lesson. When it was over, she wandered around the room to say hello to all her old little favourites, though they were fast growing up into young women, outgrowing the girlhood faces she remembered. When the school day ended, the summer afternoon was still young, and Mrs. Hepworth invited them back to the parsonage to see all the “alterations” the new vicar had made—she nearly said “improvements”, but caught herself in time. Margaret

“Experience, like a pale musician, holds
A dulcimer of patience in his hand;
Whence harmonies we cannot understand,
Of God’s will in His worlds, the strain unfolds
In sad, perplexed minors.”

About this time, Dixon returned from Milton to take up her post as Margaret’s personal maid once more, and came laden with an endless stream of the town’s latest gossip. She chattered on about how Martha had gone into service with Miss Thornton after her recent wedding, walking Margaret through every detail of the ceremony: the bridesmaids’ gowns, the wedding breakfast spread, every small spectacle the Milton crowds had crowded to glimpse. The town had whispered that Mr. Thornton had put on far too grand a show for a man who had lost a fortune to the recent strike, and been forced to pay out ruinous damages for all the broken contracts the work stoppage had rendered impossible to fulfill. She fumed over the sale of the Hales’ old Milton household goods, how every small, cherished piece she had tended for years had fetched next to nothing at auction, a scandal given how flush Milton’s mill-owners were at that very moment. She described how Mrs. Thornton had turned up one morning and snapped up half a dozen fine pieces for a fraction of their worth, then Mr. Thornton had arrived the very next day, so determined to secure a handful of small mementos of the Hale home that he had accidentally bid against himself, much to the uproarious amusement of all the onlookers. “It balances out perfectly, if you ask me,” Dixon huffed. “What the mistress underpaid, the son overpaid fair and square.”

Mr. Bell, too, had sent off a stream of fussy, contradictory orders for the Hale library left behind, so particular and hard to parse in his letters that no one could make head nor tail of his requests. Dixon swore letters were always more trouble than they were worth, and if he’d only come to Milton in person, the whole mess would have been sorted in an hour.

She had barely any news to share of the Higginses, however. Her memory bore a stubborn aristocratic bias, and grew treacherous the second she tried to recall small details of folk she deemed beneath her station. Nicholas, she believed, was well enough: he’d turned up at the old Hale house half a dozen times, asking only for news of Miss Margaret — the only person in the whole of Milton who had ever done that, save for Mr. Thornton once. Mary? Oh, of course she was well, a great strapping, slatternly lass, she said. She thought she’d heard (though it might well have been a dream, for who dreams of the likes of the Higgins family?) that Mary was now working at Mr. Thornton’s mill, and that her father was forcing her to learn cooking alongside her factory work. A notion so nonsensical, Dixon declared, it could be nothing more than a silly dream. Margaret laughed, agreeing the tale was far too disjointed to be true, but she was still glad to at last have someone in the household she could talk to of Milton and all its people, even if Dixon would far rather bury that whole chapter of their lives in shadow than revisit it.

Dixon far preferred to circle back to hints she’d picked up about Mr. Bell, dropping insinuations that he clearly intended to name Margaret his heiress. But Margaret gave her no encouragement, shutting down every one of her veiled enquiries at once, no matter how Dixon disguised them as casual suspicions or idle assertions.

All the while, Margaret carried a strange, unnameable quiet longing that Mr. Bell would soon take one of his usual business trips north to Milton. Back at Helstone, they had agreed that the explanation she owed Mr. Thornton — to untangle the terrible misunderstanding of the riot night, when he had seen Frederick — must be given word of mouth, gently, never forced on him against his will. But Mr. Bell was no devoted correspondent, sending off letters short or long as his mood took him, and though Margaret never admitted to holding out any definite hope when she opened them, every uneventful note left her with a faint, dull pang of disappointment. He never once mentioned travelling to Milton, never hinted he planned to head north at all. She told herself to be patient, that sooner or later all the mists would clear. His letters were nothing like his usual jovial self these days: they were short, full of vague complaints, stippled with rare touches of bitterness that she had never associated with him before. He seemed not to look forward to the future at all, but to dwell on regrets for the past, and to drag through the days of the present as if they tired him. Margaret feared he must be unwell, and when she wrote to ask after his health, he sent back a short, playful note saying he was suffering from that old, much-mocked ailment called the spleen, and left her to decide for herself if it was mental or physical. He begged leave to grumble to his heart’s content, without being made to send a formal health bulletin every other week. After that, Margaret asked no more questions about his wellbeing.

One day, Edith let slip an offhand fragment of a conversation she’d had with Mr. Bell during his last visit to London, and the news seized Margaret whole: he had half a mind to take her abroad that autumn, to visit Frederick and his new Spanish bride in Cadiz. Margaret badgered and cross-examined Edith until the latter grew cross and weary, swearing there was no more to remember. All he had said was that he thought he would go to hear Frederick’s own firsthand account of the mutiny, that it would be the perfect chance for Margaret to get to know her new sister-in-law, and that he always travelled over the long university vacation, so there was no reason Spain could not be his destination as easily as any other place. Edith pouted, then burst into tears, complaining that she could tell Margaret cared far more about this silly foreign trip than she did about staying with her own sister, that Margaret must think their quiet household life was nothing worth. Margaret soothed her as best she could, but she could not explain how the mere idea of Spain, even if it was nothing but a castle in the air, lit a bright, glowing charm in her chest. Edith was in the sort of mood that deemed any pleasure Margaret enjoyed away from her a quiet, unspoken insult, so Margaret kept the full extent of her glee to herself, only letting it spill out when she was dressing for dinner and asked Dixon if she would not love to see dear Master Frederick and his new wife.

“She’s a Papist, then, Miss, isn’t she?” Dixon asked immediately, her face falling.
“I believe — oh yes, certainly,” Margaret said, momentarily damped by the realization.
“And they live in a Papist country?”
“Yes.”
“Then I’m afraid I must say my soul is far dearer to me than even Master Frederick, dear as he is. I should be in perpetual terror, Miss, lest I get myself converted.”

Margaret laughed, saying she was not even sure the trip would come to pass, and if it did, she was far too unpretending a traveller to demand Dixon’s constant attendance: she could have a full, long holiday if she preferred not to come. Dixon did not care for that speech at all. For one thing, she hated Margaret’s little habit of calling her “dear old Dixon” whenever she was feeling particularly demonstrative. She knew Miss Hale used the word “old” for everyone she liked as a term of endearment, but Dixon winced every time it was applied to her — she was barely past fifty, and considered herself to be firmly in the prime of life. For another, for all her professed terror of popery, she harboured a fierce, lurking curiosity about Spain, the Inquisition, and all its strange mysteries. She cleared her throat loudly, as if to signal her willingness to set aside all difficulties, and asked Margaret if she thought there would really be any danger of conversion if she strictly avoided speaking to priests or stepping foot inside a Catholic church. Master Frederick, to be sure, had converted out of nowhere, she added.

“I fancy it was love that first predisposed him to that,” Margaret said, sighing softly.
“Indeed, Miss!” Dixon exclaimed. “Well, I can keep clear of priests and churches easy enough — but love steals in unawares, doesn’t it? I think it’s far wiser if I don’t go at all.”

Margaret tried hard not to dwell too much on the Spanish plan, lest she be disappointed, but it was a welcome distraction from the restless, impatient urge she felt to have the whole misunderstanding with Mr. Thornton resolved once and for all. Mr. Bell showed no sign of leaving Oxford, made no mention of any imminent trip to Milton, and a strange, quiet restraint hung over Margaret that kept her from even bringing up the possibility of such a visit to him.

Her greatest small joy in those days of quiet monotony was Edith’s little boy. He was the spoiled pride and plaything of both his parents, as long as he was good; but he had a fierce, unyielding will of his own, and the second he burst into one of his stormy, ungovernable passions, Edith would throw herself back on the sofa in despair and fatigue, sighing that she had no idea what to do with him, and begging Margaret to ring for Hanley the nurse to take him away. But Margaret liked him far better in those wild, untamed moments than in his docile, blue-sashed good moods. She would carry him off to a quiet room, where only the two of them remained, and battle his rage out with him: with gentle, unshakable firmness she would subdue him into calm, drawing on every soft charm she possessed to lead him back to kindness, until he would rub his hot, tear-streaked face all over hers, kissing and clinging to her until he often fell asleep heavy in her arms, or slumped over her shoulder. Those were the sweetest moments Margaret had known in months, giving her a small taste of that motherly affection she had long believed would be forever denied to her.

Mr. Henry Lennox began to visit the house very often at this time, adding a new, far from disagreeable sharpness to the rhythm of their household life. Margaret thought him colder now, even as he grew far more brilliant than she remembered him being in their old days of acquaintance. His deep, wide-ranging intellectual tastes and stores of varied, extensive knowledge gave a welcome tang to the otherwise rather insipid, frivolous family conversations. Margaret caught glimmers of a faint, quiet contempt he held for his brother and Edith, and for their empty, purposeless life of idle luxury. Once or twice she heard him speak to the Captain, right in front of her, in a sharp, pointed tone, asking if he truly intended to abandon his profession entirely. When Captain Lennox shrugged and replied that he had more than enough money to live comfortably, she saw Mr. Lennox curl his lip and say dryly, “And is that all you live for?” For all that, the brothers were deeply attached, as two people are when one is far cleverer and always leads the other, and the second is perfectly content to be led. Mr. Lennox was climbing fast in his legal career, carefully and calculatedly cultivating every connection that might one day be of use to him: keen-sighted, far-seeing, intelligent, sarcastic, and proud. Ever since that one long conversation about Frederick’s affairs that she had shared with him their very first evening in the house, with Mr. Bell present, they had not enjoyed any close private intercourse beyond what naturally arose from living in the same household. That casual constant contact was enough, though, to wear away the last of Margaret’s shyness around him, and the last traces of mortified pride and vanity that had lingered in him after she had turned down his proposal. They met constantly, of course, and Margaret thought he rather deliberately avoided being left alone with her. Both of them, she fancied, had noticed that they had drifted strangely far away from the old shared anchor of aligned opinions and overlapping tastes that had bound them together years before. And yet, whenever he spoke with unusual wit, or landed some sharp, perfectly phrased epigram, Margaret would catch his eye dart to her face first of all, for the briefest instant, to see if she had appreciated the turn of phrase. In all their casual family conversations, her opinion was the one he listened to with a quiet, unacknowledged deference — one all the more noticeable for how hard he tried to hide it.

“My own, my father’s friend! I cannot part with thee!
I ne’er have shown, thou ne’er hast known,
How dear thou art to me.”

The famous dinner parties Mrs. Lennox hosted ran on a gentle, well-blended chemistry: her wide circle of connections supplied all the soft, bright charm of their beauty, Captain Lennox contributed easy, unforced knowledge of every topic of the day from parliamentary debates to the new opera, while Mr. Henry Lennox and his coterie of sharp, rising young professional men brought the wit, the clever insight, the broad and precise learning they wielded so deftly no one ever thought to call them pedantic, no one ever felt the flow of conversation drag under the weight of a lecture. Those dinners were universally counted delightful, but even in that warm, glittering candlelight, Margaret’s quiet discontent found a home. Every talent, every tender feeling, every hard-earned accomplishment—even every flicker of impulse towards goodness—was treated as nothing more than fuel for fireworks. The hidden, sacred fire of real conviction burned out fast in a shower of sparks and empty crackle. They discussed art only in the shallowest, most sensuous terms, lingering over brushstrokes and composition as surface effects, never pausing to let the work teach them anything deeper. They whipped themselves up into gushing, performative enthusiasm about grand, noble causes in company, then never spared those subjects a single thought when they were home alone. They squandered every capacity for genuine appreciation into nothing but a stream of perfectly turned, appropriate words.

One evening, after the gentlemen had finished their port and joined the ladies in the drawing room, Henry Lennox made his way straight to Margaret, speaking to her almost of his own accord for the first time since she had come back to live in Harley Street.
“You did not look at all pleased at what Shirley was saying at dinner.”
“Did I not? My face must be far too easy to read,” Margaret replied.
“It always was. It never lost that trick of being eloquent, even when you tried to hold your tongue.”
“I did not like,” Margaret said hurriedly, “his way of arguing in favour of something he knew full well to be wrong—glaringly wrong—even when it was all just a jest.”
“But it was wonderfully clever. Every single word hit its mark. Did you take note of all those perfect, sharp little epithets he used?”
“Yes.”
“And you despise them, you want to add. Pray do not hold back on my account, even if he is my friend.”
“There!” Margaret began, then cut herself short, “that is exactly the tone in you that—” She stopped, flushing bright, and turned away. He waited a moment for her to finish her thought, but before she could move out of earshot, he spoke in a low, clear voice only she could hear.
“If my tones, or my habits of thought, are what you dislike, will you do me the justice to tell me so? Give me the chance to learn to please you.”

Weeks drifted by with no word that Mr. Bell had made the journey to Milton he had spoken of at Helstone, a trip he had said he would make almost immediately after they left. Margaret soon assumed he had settled all his business there entirely by post. She knew well he hated Milton, a smoky, loud manufacturing town entirely alien to his quiet Oxford life, and she guessed he did not fully grasp the quiet, urgent weight Margaret had placed on that in-person explanation between her and Mr. Thornton that could never be sorted out through letters. He knew it needed to be done, eventually, but to him it hardly mattered if it was done in summer, autumn, or the dead of winter.

It was now August, and there had been no mention of the Spanish trip he had once teased Edith about. Margaret had slowly, reluctantly begun to reconcile herself to that bright little illusion fading to nothing. But one morning a letter arrived: he would be coming up to London the following week, he wrote, wanted to speak to her about a plan he had been turning over in his head, and had finally agreed to “indulge in a little doctoring” now that he had come round to her way of thinking, that it was far pleasanter to blame his constant irritable, cross moods on poor health than on his own ill temper. There was a faint, forced cheer running through every line of the letter Margaret would notice later, but at the time her attention was completely taken up by Edith’s exclamations.
“Coming up to town! Oh dear, I am so worn through by this sweltering August heat I do not believe I have a single scrap of strength left for another dinner party. Besides, every single person of our acquaintance has left London for the country—we are the only silly souls left stuck here, still arguing over where we ought to go for our holiday. There will be no one at all to meet him.”
“I am certain he would far rather dine with just our small family than with the most entertaining strangers you could hunt up. And besides, if he is unwell, he will not want a crowd of invitations anyway. I am glad he has finally admitted it. I knew he was ill from the tone of all his recent letters, but he would never answer my questions when I wrote to ask after him, and I had no other person I could turn to for news of him.”
“Oh, he cannot be very ill, or he would never be thinking of Spain.”
“He has not mentioned Spain at all.”
“No, but this new plan he says he has is obviously tied to that. But would you really be willing to travel all the way there in this dreadful heat?”
“Oh, the weather will cool day by day. Yes! Think of it. I am only afraid I have wanted this far too fiercely, in that greedy, all-consuming wilful way that always ends in disappointment—or else the wish is granted exactly to the letter, but the spirit of it gives no joy at all.”
“That is just superstition, Margaret, I am sure of it.”
“No, I do not think it is. It is only a warning to me, to stop giving way to these passionate, all-or-nothing wishes. It is the same cry as the old biblical one: ‘Give me children, or else I die.’ I am afraid my own cry is, ‘Let me go to Cadiz, or else I die.’”
“My dear Margaret! You will be so charmed by the place you will want to stay there forever, and what on earth will I do then? Oh, I wish I could find you someone to marry here in London, someone I know and trust, so I could be sure of keeping you near.”
“I shall never marry.”
“Nonsense, and double nonsense! Why, as Sholto says, you are such an attraction to this house that he knows dozens of men who are already making plans to come visit us next year, just for the chance to meet you.”
Margaret drew herself up haughtily. “Do you know, Edith, I sometimes think all that time you spent living in Corfu has taught you—”
“Well?”
“Just a shade or two of coarseness.”

Edith immediately burst into bitter, heaving sobs, wailing vehemently that Margaret no longer loved her, no longer considered her a friend, until Margaret decided she had spoken far too harshly, out of nothing more than her own wounded pride. She ended up waiting on Edith hand and foot for the rest of the day, while the little lady, overcome by her hurt feelings, lay prostrate on the sofa like a wronged martyr, occasionally letting out a deep, mournful sigh, until at last she cried herself to sleep.

Mr. Bell never appeared even on the second date he had deferred his visit to. The next morning, a letter arrived from his servant Wallis, saying his master had been quietly unwell for weeks, that had been the real reason he kept putting off his journey to London. That very morning he had been due to leave, Wallis wrote, when he had been struck down by an apoplectic fit. The doctors all agreed he would never survive the night, and it was more than likely that by the time Miss Hale received this letter, her poor old master would already be gone.

Margaret turned deathly pale as she read the note at the breakfast table. Silently she handed it to Edith, then slipped out of the room. Edith was terribly shocked as she read it, and broke down into childish, frightened, gasping sobs, much to her husband’s distress. Mrs. Shaw was breakfasting in her own room, so it fell to Captain Lennox to calm his wife, to help her wrap her head around the fact that the man who was supposed to sit at their dinner table that very night was either dead or dying—her very first close brush with death in her entire sheltered life. It was some time before Edith even thought of Margaret, then she jumped to her feet and ran upstairs after her, to find Dixon packing a small case of toiletries, while Margaret hastily tied the ribbons of her bonnet, tears streaming down her face so fast her hands shook too badly to tie the strings properly.
“Oh, dear Margaret! How dreadful! What are you doing? Are you going out? Sholto will send a telegram, he will do anything you ask him.”
“I am going to Oxford. There is a train leaving in half an hour. Dixon offered to come with me, but I would go even if I had no one. I must see him one more time. Besides, he might still rally, and need someone to care for him. He has been like a father to me. Do not stop me, Edith.”
“But I must. Mamma will never approve of this. Come and ask her permission first, Margaret. You do not know what you are doing, traveling alone. I would not mind if he had his own private house, but he lives in a Fellow’s rooms in college! Come to Mamma, it will only take a minute.”

Margaret yielded to her pleading, and missed her train. In the sudden chaos of the news, Mrs. Shaw grew flustered and hysterical, and all their precious time slipped away. But there was another train due in a couple of hours, and after endless debates over what was proper and what was improper, they could not shake Margaret’s resolve. She was going, she told them, alone if she had to, no matter what anyone said, no matter how they argued over decorum. The man who had been her father’s oldest, dearest, most loyal friend was lying at the point of death, and the thought of it burned so bright and sharp in her chest that she surprised even herself with the firmness of her demand for the right to act on her own wishes. Five minutes before the train pulled out of the station, she found herself settled in a first-class carriage, sitting opposite Captain Lennox.

Margaret would always be glad she had made that trip, even though all she learned when she arrived was that he had died peacefully in the night, before Wallis had even posted the letter. She wandered slowly through his quiet, book-lined Fellow’s rooms, and forever after those rooms were tangled up in her most tender memories, tied to the thought of her father, and the one friend he had cherished so deeply. They had promised Edith before they left that if all had ended as they feared, they would return for dinner, so Margaret had to tear herself away from the little mementos of her father Mr. Bell had kept for decades, press a soft kiss to his still, kind face, and bid her quiet final farewell.

On the journey home, Captain Lennox fell asleep, and Margaret could cry freely at her leisure, thinking of this terrible, fatal year, and all the woe it had heaped upon her. This new loss did not erase the grief of the ones that had come before, but it tore open wounds that had barely started to scab over. But when she stepped through the door of the warm, lamplit Harley Street drawing room, she heard her aunt’s soft, tender voice, little Sholto trotted over to carefully carry Aunt Margaret’s cup of tea to her without spilling a drop, and there was Edith, pale and pretty, brimming with tender sympathy for her grief. Margaret roused herself out of that heavy, hopeless, superstitious fog of despair. She sank into Edith’s usual spot on the sofa, and by the time she went upstairs to dress, she could thank God that her dear old friend had been spared a long, lingering, painful illness.

“And down the sunny beach she paces slowly,
With many doubtful pauses by the way;
Grief hath an influence so hushed and holy.”

“Is Margaret not the heiress?” Edith whispered to her husband late that night, alone in their room after their sombre journey to Oxford. She had tugged his tall head down to her level, balancing on tiptoe, and made him swear not to think her greedy before she dared voice the question. Captain Lennox knew nothing of the matter: if he had ever heard a passing mention of it, he had long forgotten. He assumed a fellow of a tiny, obscure college could not have much to leave behind, joked that he had never expected Margaret to pay her share of household expenses anyway, and scoffed that two hundred and fifty pounds a year of independent income was absurd for a girl who never touched a drop of wine. Edith set her heels back on the carpet, a little sadder, her tiny romantic fancy of Margaret as a great wealthy heiress popped like a soap bubble.

A week later, she came prancing up to her husband, dropping him a deep, playful curtsy. “I was right, and you were wrong, my noble captain. Margaret has had a lawyer’s letter, and she is the residuary legatee. The small specific gifts add up to two thousand pounds, and the rest of the estate, at current booming Milton property values, comes to forty thousand.”

“Indeed? And how does she take this stroke of good fortune?”

“Oh, she acts as if she half expected it all along, though she had no idea the sum was so large. She went white as a sheet the second she read the full details, and says she is afraid of all that money. Which is nonsense, of course—she’ll get over that silly feeling soon enough. I left Mamma smothering her with congratulations and snuck away straight to tell you.”

By unspoken general consent, everyone assumed Henry Lennox would naturally act as Margaret’s informal legal adviser from that point onward. She was entirely unacquainted with every form of business procedure, so she turned to him for guidance in nearly every matter related to the estate. He selected her solicitor, brought her stacks of documents to sign, and was never happier than when walking her through the opaque, seemingly mystical jargon of legal contracts, explaining exactly what every clause and fine line meant.

“Henry,” Edith said one day, arch and teasing, “do you know what I hope and expect all these long closed-door conversations with Margaret will end in?”

“No, I don’t,” he replied, colouring bright red. “And I would thank you not to tell me.”

“Oh, very well, then. I suppose I don’t need to warn Sholto to stop inviting Mr. Montagu over to the house so often.”

“Just as you please,” he said, forcing a tone of cool indifference. “What you are imagining may or may not come to pass. But this time, before I commit myself, I will make absolutely sure my ground is solid. Invite whoever you like. Edith, this is not a thing to meddle in—if you interfere, you will ruin it entirely. She has been icy and distant with me for so long, sharp and unyielding as Queen Zenobia, and she is only just starting to thaw now. She has the makings of a Cleopatra in her, if only she were a little less pious.”

“For my part,” Edith said, a little sharp and mischievous, “I am very glad she is a Christian. I know so very few decent people who are!”

Margaret’s planned trip to Spain fell through that autumn, though she had clung to hope almost to the last that some lucky chance would bring Frederick to Paris, where she could easily travel to meet him with a respectable chaperone. Instead of the coast of Cadiz, she settled for Cromer, where Aunt Shaw, the Lennoxes, and the whole household were already bound for a holiday. They had gently pressed her to join them the whole time, making only half-hearted, lazy efforts to help arrange her separate travel plans, and in the end, Cromer was exactly what she needed: she was run ragged by grief and stress, and the bracing sea air soothed her body as much as her mind.

Among the hopes that had faded in the months after Mr. Bell’s death was the quiet, stubborn trust she had carried that Bell would pass the full, unvarnished truth of the Leonards incident on to Mr. Thornton—all the context of her brother’s long lost return, the desperate lie she had told to protect him, the months of guilt and shame she had carried afterward. She wanted Thornton’s opinion of her, no matter how harsh it had once been, to be rooted in full understanding of what she had done, and why. That small unburdening would have given her the rest she craved, the end to a restlessness she would otherwise carry her whole life, if she could not force herself to stop thinking of it. Now, so long after the events, there was no one left to tell the truth but herself, and she had no way to seek Thornton out to explain it. She tried to reason herself into accepting her fate, that millions of people lived misunderstood their whole lives, but her heart still ached with the quiet, foolish wish that one day—years and years from now, before he died at the very least—he would somehow learn how deeply she had been tempted, how little she had deserved his judgment. When she finally schooled herself to accept that wish would never be granted, she turned all her heart and strength to the life laid out before her, determined to make the very best of it.

She spent hours sitting on the pebbly shore, gazing intently at the waves as they dragged back and forth over the stones in ceaseless motion, or stared out at the distant silver heave of the open sea where it caught the light against the sky, listening half-unconsciously to the low, endless roar that sounded like a perpetual unwritten psalm rising from the water. It soothed her, and she could not have said how or why. She sat on the ground, hands clasped loosely around her knees, while her aunt wandered the town running small errands, and Edith and Captain Lennox rode for miles along the shore and across the inland lanes. Nannies trailing toddlers or pushing strollers sauntered past her again and again, whispering to each other, wondering what on earth she could possibly find to look at for hours, day after day. When the whole family gathered for dinner, Margaret was so quiet and lost in thought that Edith declared her moping, and leapt at her husband’s suggestion that they invite Henry Lennox to spend a week at Cromer when he returned from his Scotland trip in October.

All those long hours of quiet thinking let Margaret sort through every event of her past, untangle their roots and true significance, and shape a clear vision for her future. No one who did not care to look closely noticed, but a soft, steady peace had settled into her face, impossible to miss for anyone paying attention. Henry Lennox was completely stunned by the change when he first saw her.

“The sea has done Miss Hale an immense amount of good, I should say,” he remarked, after she had left the room shortly after his arrival. “She looks ten years younger than she did in Harley Street.”

“That’s the new bonnet I bought her!” Edith said, preening triumphantly. “I knew it would suit her the second I laid eyes on it.”

“I beg your pardon,” Mr. Lennox replied, in that half-indulgent, half-amused tone he always used with Edith. “But I think I can tell the difference between the charm of a dress and the charm of a woman. No mere bonnet could make Miss Hale’s eyes so bright and yet so soft, or her lips so warm and full, or her whole face glowing with such peace and light. She is like the Margaret Hale we knew at Helstone,” he dropped his voice, “and more, far more.”

From that moment, the clever, ambitious young barrister bent every one of his considerable powers to winning Margaret. He loved her soft, gentle beauty, and he saw the deep, latent strength of her sharp mind, which he was certain would easily align with all the grand, ambitious plans he held for his own career. He saw her fortune only as a natural part of the perfect, superb picture of who she was and the life they could share, though he was fully aware the sum would catapult the poor young barrister to a level of social and professional status he could never have reached on his own for decades. He had visited Milton on business related to her property holdings right after his Scotland trip, and as a skilled lawyer with an eye for contingencies, he saw clearly that the value of her land and houses in the booming industrial town was rising higher every single year. He was glad that their new dynamic of client and legal adviser was steadily erasing the awkward, painful memory of that botched, badly managed day at Helstone, when he had bungled his first proposal.

Margaret was only too happy to listen when he talked about Milton, even though he had not met any of the people she had cared for most deeply there. Her aunt and cousin had always dismissed Milton as coarse, ugly, and unpleasant, the very same feeling Margaret had once been ashamed to remember having in her first weeks in the town. But Henry’s admiration for the character of Milton and its people outstripped even Margaret’s own: he was captivated by their endless energy, their ferocious power, their indomitable courage in the face of every struggle, the bright, vivid intensity of their way of life. He never tired of talking of them, until Margaret, with her characteristic candour, gently pointed out that for all their noble, admirable grit, far too many of the goals they poured all that tireless, mighty effort into were selfish and material, a flaw that tainted all their greatness. Still, whenever every other topic paled for Margaret, and she gave only short, distant answers to idle chatter, all Henry had to do was ask about some small Darkshire custom, some quirk of Milton character, to bring the light back to her eyes and the glow back to her cheeks.

When they returned to London, Margaret kept the promise she had made to herself on the Cromer beach: she took full control of her own life. Before their seaside holiday, she had been as docile to her aunt’s rules as that scared little stranger who had cried herself to sleep in the Harley Street nursery her very first night there. But those hours of solemn, quiet thought had taught her that one day she would be held fully accountable for the life she had lived, and she had worked out the most difficult balance any woman ever strikes: how much of herself to yield to the loving authority of the people who cared for her, and how much to claim as her own, free to use for the work she felt called to do. Mrs. Shaw was unfailingly gentle and good-tempered, and Edith had inherited that same sweet domestic kindness. Margaret knew she had once had the sharpest temper of the three, her quick perceptions and overactive imagination making her hasty, her early isolation making her proud—but she had a soft, childlike sweetness of heart that had always made her irresistible even in her rare stubborn moods, and now, chastened even by what the world called her good fortune, she charmed her aunt into full acquiescence to her wishes. Margaret had won the right to follow her own ideas of duty.

“Only don’t get strong-minded,” Edith pleaded. “Mamma wants to buy you your very own footman, you are more than welcome to him—they are such a nuisance, after all. That’s the only thing I beg of you, darling: don’t turn strong-minded. Footman or no footman, don’t be one of those stern, serious bluestockings.”

“Don’t be afraid, Edith,” Margaret laughed. “I will faint dead away in the middle of the servants’ dinner the very first chance I get, and then, between Sholto playing with the fire and the baby screaming, you’ll be begging for a strong-minded woman who can handle any emergency.”

“And you won’t get too pious to joke and be merry with us?”

“Never. I shall be merrier than I have ever been, now that I get to live the life I choose.”

“And you won’t dress all in dull brown and dust-colour, to hide all the Milton dirt you’ll pick up wandering about those dreadful industrial parts, and make me buy all your pretty dresses for you?”

“Indeed I mean to pick out all my dresses for myself,” Margaret said. “You can come shopping with me if you like, but no one else can choose quite what suits me.”

“Oh, I was so afraid you’d give up every last vanity. I’m glad you’re keeping a few of them, just as little reminders of the old Margaret Hale we knew.”

“I am going to be exactly the same girl you have always known,” Margaret told her, “if only you and Aunt Shaw can make yourselves see it. I have no husband or children to give me natural daily duties, after all, so I shall simply make some good work for myself, beyond ordering my own new gowns.”

In their private little family conclave, made up of Edith, her mother, and her husband, they all agreed that Margaret’s new plans would only push her closer and closer to Henry Lennox. They kept her away from all other eligible young men among their acquaintance, and it soon became clear that Margaret did not care for the company of anyone outside their own small family but Henry. Other suitors, drawn to her beauty or the reputation of her fortune, drifted away quickly, put off by her quiet, uninterested gentle disdain, to chase after other less picky beauties, or other heiresses with even greater fortunes. Henry and Margaret grew slowly, steadily closer in their intimacy, and neither of them was the sort of person to let anyone peer too closely or remark on the quiet, tender relationship taking root between them.

“Here we go up, up, up; And here we go down, down, downee!” — NURSERY SONG

All across Milton, the mill chimneys puffed out plumes of unbroken gray smoke, while the endless roar of looms, the thunderous beat of steam engines, and the dizzying whirr of spinning spindles churned on without rest. Wood, iron, and steam labored on in their endless, senseless cycles, but their tireless, monotonous motion was matched by the crowds of mill workers, merchants, and traders who thronged the streets, every one of them driven by sharp, urgent purpose. No one loitered for pleasure: every face was etched with lines of eagerness or gnawing anxiety, men fought to grab every scrap of news they could find, and jostled each other aside on the Mart floor and the Exchange just as they jostled through life, caught in the cruel, selfish grind of cutthroat competition.

A heavy gloom hung over the entire town. Hardly any buyers came calling, and the few that did were eyed with deep suspicion by every seller, for credit had turned fragile overnight, even the most established firms at risk of being dragged down by the wave of collapses sweeping through the great port a few miles away, where shipping houses had toppled like dominoes. No Milton mill had failed yet, but everyone knew that the string of catastrophic losses that had come to light from bad speculations in America, and closer to home too, would cripple at least a handful of local trading houses. Day after day, every man’s face asked, if not his tongue: *What news? Who’s gone under? How will it hurt me?* When two or three men huddled to talk, they spoke first of the names of firms they knew were safe, no one daring to whisper out loud which houses they suspected might topple next—for idle, careless rumor could drag down a house that might otherwise have weathered the storm, and one collapsing firm would drag a dozen more after it.

“Thornton’s safe,” everyone said. “His business is huge, growing bigger every year—sharp as a tack, daring but prudent, that man is.” Then old Mr. Harrison, the lifelong croaker who had inherited his father’s fortune and never dared risk a single penny to expand his trade, would tug a neighbor aside, lean in to mutter in his ear: “Thornton’s business is big, right enough—but he poured every penny of his profits straight back into expanding it. No reserve capital stashed away. That new machinery of his, installed in the last two years? Cost him a fortune. A word to the wise, eh?”

But the truth was, Mr. Thornton was stretched thinner than he had ever been in his life, and the pain of it bit at the most vulnerable part of him: the fierce pride he held in the commercial reputation he had built entirely from scratch. He had always believed commerce gave every brave, honest, persevering man the chance to climb high, to build a name so solid it rang like gold across far-flung ports in the East and the West, where no one would ever know his face, but everyone would honor his word. His mother had read the old verse aloud to him as a boy, “Her merchants be like princes,” and it had felt like a trumpet call summoning him to the fight. He had spent years chasing that distant glory, barely noticing the people who lived and worked right alongside him in his own mills—until that day he had met Higgins, entirely outside the usual rigid master-and-workman dynamic, and the two men had looked each other eye to eye, and realized for the first time that “we have all of us one human heart.” It was the thin, sharp edge of a wedge that had split open his whole world: now, the fear of losing the connection he had built with the men he had only lately learned to see as full, complicated human beings, of having the quiet, tender experiments in better working conditions he had dreamed of nipped off before they could even be tested, had seeped into every quiet corner of his anxiety.

He traced all the trouble back to the strike a year and a half prior. Back then he had rushed to fulfill huge, high-value contracts, imported unskilled Irish labor to replace the striking hands, only to find their work clumsy and full of flaws, totally unfit to ship out from a firm that prided itself on selling nothing but top-quality goods. He had locked nearly all his capital up in new, expensive machinery, and bulk cotton he had bought at a premium for those unfulfilled contracts. Even after the strike ended, the losses lingered, and for a long time he had caught himself snapping irritably at Higgins for no cause at all, out of old, leftover resentment—until he forced himself to curb that anger, to seek Higgins out whenever business allowed, until all that resentment melted away, replaced by a quiet, mutual respect that let them disagree without malice, and see each other as more than just master and hand.

Now the full weight of the trade crash had arrived. The market had plummeted, the value of all his massive stock of cotton fell to nearly half what he had paid for it. No new orders came in, he could not even collect payment for orders he had already fulfilled, and the bills for that old bulk cotton purchase were coming due. Money was impossibly scarce, he could only borrow at usurious, ruinous interest rates, and there was no way he could sell off any of his assets to free up cash. But he did not despair. He worked day and night, planning for every possible emergency, stayed gentle and calm with the women of his household at home, spoke few words at the mill—though his hands knew him well enough now, saw the weight pressing down on him, and greeted his short, sharp orders with sympathy, not the old smoldering resentment they had once held for him.

“Th’ measter’s got a world o’ trouble on his plate,” Higgins said one night, after he heard Thornton snap a sharp demand to know why a neglected work order had not been followed through, and caught the soft, suppressed sigh Thornton heaved as he walked past the workroom. That night, Higgins and a fellow hand stayed hours after their shift, no one the wiser, to finish the neglected work, and Thornton never knew it was them, assuming the overlooker had seen to it himself.

A few days later, he ran straight into Thornton striding fast down Marlborough Street, deep in thought. “Measter,” he said, stopping him short, and Thornton looked up with a sharp, annoyed jolt, his mind far away. “Have yo’ heerd aught of Miss Marget lately? Miss Hale, th’ old parson’s daughter.”

“Miss Hale—oh, yes,” Thornton said, and suddenly all that wintry, careworn frost melted right off his face, as if a soft summer gale had blown all his anxieties away. His mouth was still set firm, but his eyes softened, warm and gentle as he looked at Higgins. “She’s my landlord now, you know. I get updates from her agent here every so often. She’s well, staying with friends—thank you for asking, Higgins.” That quiet, warm “thank you” lit up a new thought in Higgins’s sharp, observant head: he thought he saw a little glimmer of something deeper than he’d guessed, and he meant to follow it.

“And she’s not got married yet, measter?”
“Not yet. There’s talk of a match with a relation of her family, as far as I’ve heard.” Thornton’s face clouded over again.
“So she won’t be coming back to Milton, I reckon?”
“No.”
“Stop a minute, measter.” Higgins leaned in, confidential. “Is th’ young gentleman cleared, then? Master Frederick, her brother that came by when her mother died. Mary and me knew all about it, Mary worked in their house back then, we kept quiet, never breathed a word. I just wanted to know if he’s got his name cleared at last.”

Thornton stared at him, all the old uncertainty he had carried around about that night, about Margaret’s odd evasions, melting away like snow in spring. It had been her brother, all along. Of course it had. He had never for a second doubted her honor, but now he had his proof, a tiny little golden thread of comfort running through the dark, tangled web of all his misfortunes. He broke off from Higgins soon after, hurrying back to the pressing business he had been on his way to see, leaving Higgins baffled but not unhappy.

That very week, the final, crushing blow landed: his largest agent, the one handling all his American trade, went down with a whole string of other firms, toppling like a pack of cards. Thornton’s entire fortune was gone. Night after night he brought ledgers and papers up to his private study, sitting up long after everyone else in the house had gone to bed, none of them knowing he never slept at all. One dawn, gray light seeping through the cracks in the shuttered windows, the door opened and his mother walked in. She had not taken off her clothes or laid down to rest either. Their faces were wan and rigid, both worn thin from hours of silent, sleepless worry.

“Son John,” she said, her voice steady, even as it trembled. “Do you think I could sleep easy while you sit up here, eaten through with care? Tell me what it is.”

“Trade’s bad,” he said, lifting his head, holding it high and proud. “I won’t fail. I’m going to pay every last penny I owe. Not a single creditor will lose a farthing to me. But I’ll have to give up the business. I could save it, if I took the huge, high-risk speculation Watson offered me. If it succeeded, no one would ever know how close I came to ruin, I’d be richer than I ever was. But if it fails—”

“Go on,” she said, stepping forward, clutching his arm, her eyes bright with eager hope.

“If it fails, I’d be gambling all my creditors’ money just to save my own name. Honest men would be ruined for my petty pride. Mother, I’ve decided.” His voice was quiet, but unshakable. “It’s hard. I built this mill from nothing. I wanted to do such good by these men, give them better lives. Now all of it’s over, and I’m too old to start all over again with the same fire. But I’ll do it. You won’t grieve too much to leave this big house, will you, dear?”

“Not the house!” she cried, and burst into sobs, throwing her arms around his neck. “But to see you, my good, honest, hard-working boy, brought low when every scoundrel who gambles on other people’s luck gets rich—what kind of justice is there in that?”

“Mother,” he said, holding her gently, his voice soft. “You told me once, when father died, and we had nothing at all, to be brave, to trust. Don’t let the world have hardened us that much. Say those old brave words to me again.”

For a long minute she said nothing, sobbing convulsively, until she looked up and saw him sitting there, quiet, his face calm, and all her bitter rebellion melted away. She thanked God, fervent, that he was still hers, whole and unbroken. He went over and wrenched open the shutters, letting the pale, ruddy dawn light flood the room. The east wind was blowing sharp and cold, the unseasonable late spring frost lingering on, no one would be buying light summer cotton goods that year. All last hopes of a market rebound were dead.

Thornton’s brother-in-law Watson, furious that Thornton had refused to join his speculation or even borrow money from him, took the huge, high-risk gamble all on his own. He made a fortune, a massive, staggering sum that made him the talk of the Milton Exchange for nine whole days. Success turned him into a local legend, everyone calling him the wisest, most far-sighted man in the whole town. No one stopped to wonder that the very same gamble Thornton had refused to risk his creditors’ money on had been the path to sudden, glittering wealth. Thornton, meanwhile, quietly set to work hunting for a post as a mill manager, ready to start again from the bottom, steady, unbroken, the pride of his good name still intact.

“Bear up, brave heart! we will be calm and strong;
Sure, we can master eyes, or cheek, or tongue,
Nor let the smallest tell-tale sign appear
She ever was, and is, and will be dear.”

The July evening hung heavy and golden over the London house, the air thick with the scent of roses from the square garden below, no breeze stirring to lift the edge of a curtain or cool the flush of heat on unprotected skin. Edith burst into Margaret’s bedchamber first, breeches and riding habit still dusted with lane mud from her afternoon ride, and half an hour later returned, fully laced into her dinner gown, ribbons fluttering at her wrists.
